A company is setting up a secure and highly available managed workflow orchestration using Apache Airflow in AWS. The company chose Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (Amazon MWAA) for its data workflow orchestration.

A data engineer needs to establish an SSH connection to a remote Amazon EC2 instance from the company’s Amazon MWAA environment using the `SSHOperator` in a directed acyclic graph (DAG).

What should the data engineer do to meet the above requirements?

- Add the `apache-airflow-providers-snowflake` Python dependency on the web server and place it in the `dags`directory.
- **Install the `apache-airflow-providers-ssh` package on the web server via the `requirements.txt` file.**
    
    Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow is a managed orchestration service for Apache Airflow that you can use to set up and operate data pipelines in the cloud at scale. Apache Airflow is an open-source tool used to programmatically author, schedule, and monitor sequences of processes and tasks referred to as workflows.
    
    With Amazon MWAA, you can use Apache Airflow and Python to create workflows without having to manage the underlying infrastructure for scalability, availability, and security. Amazon MWAA automatically scales its workflow execution capacity to meet your needs. It also integrates with AWS security services to help provide you with fast and secure access to your data.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/amazon_mwaa_ssh_operator.png)
    
    You can use the SSHOperator in a directed acyclic graph (DAG) to connect to a remote Amazon EC2 instance from your Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow environment. You can also use a similar approach to connect to any remote instance with SSH access.
    
    In order to do this, you have to upload an SSH secret key (.pem) first to your environment’s dags directory on Amazon S3. Then, you install the necessary dependencies using requirements.txt and create a new Apache Airflow connection in the UI. Finally, you write a DAG that creates an SSH connection to the remote instance.
    
    You must add the following parameter to requirements.txt to install the apache-airflow-providers-ssh package on the web server.
    
    - `c https://raw.githubusercontent.com/apache/airflow/constraints-Airflow-version/constraints-Python-version.txt apache-airflow-providers-ssh`
    
    Once your environment updates and Amazon MWAA successfully installs the dependency, you will see a new SSH connection type in the UI.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Install the `apache-airflow-providers-ssh` package on the web server via the `requirements.txt` file:**
    
    The option that says: **Add the `apache-airflow-providers-snowflake` Python dependency on the web server and place it in the `dags` directory** is incorrect. You actually have to install the `apache-airflow-providers-ssh` package and not the `apache-airflow-providers-snowflake` Python dependency since this one is primarily used in AWS Secrets Manager when setting up an Apache Airflow Snowflake connection.
    
    The option that says: **Install the `apache-airflow-providers-ssh` package on the web server by configuring the `authorized_keys` file** is incorrect. While using the `apache-airflow-providers-ssh`package is right, configuring the `authorized_keys` file is wrong since this only contains the SSH-RSA public keys. The `apache-airflow-providers-ssh` package must be added to the requirements.txt file instead.
    
    The option that says: **Install the Apache Airflow base package and generate an Apache Airflow AWS connection URI string** is incorrect because this is only needed if you are configuring an Apache Airflow connection using an AWS Secrets Manager secret.
    
- Install the `apache-airflow-providers-ssh` package on the web server by configuring the `authorized_keys` file.
- Install the Apache Airflow base package and generate an Apache Airflow AWS connection URI string.

A startup has a Salesforce-powered SaaS application with a data warehouse running in Amazon Redshift. As part of its workflow, the company needs to set up an automated solution to continuously send encrypted data from its SaaS solution to its Amazon Redshift data warehouse. This process is required to meet the company’s data policy. Additionally, the data engineer must ensure that the company retains full control over its encrypted data.

Which of the following meets the requirements with the LEAST amount of operational overhead?

- Launch a user-defined function (UDF) in Amazon Redshift with a `Run on demand` flow trigger. Configure the UDF to synchronize the data exchange between the Salesforce solution and the Amazon Redshift data warehouse.
- Create a new workflow in Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (Amazon MWAA) and set up the Salesforce solution to forward the data to MWAA via the Java Database Connectivity (JDBC) connector in Amazon Redshift. Use an AWS managed keys for encrypting connection data.
- Set up an AWS Glue Workflow to orchestrate a Crawler and ETL Job between the Salesforce application and Amazon Redshift. Configure a rule using Amazon Eventbridge and define the event pattern that will trigger the AWS Glue Workflow as the target.
- **Use an Amazon AppFlow flow with a `Run on event` trigger. Configure it to ingest data from the Salesforce solution to the Redshift data warehouse. Use customer-managed key for encrypting connection data.**
    
    Amazon AppFlow is a fully-managed integration service that enables you to securely exchange data between software as a service (SaaS) applications, such as Salesforce, and AWS services, such as Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) and Amazon Redshift. For example, you can ingest contact records from Salesforce to Amazon Redshift or pull support tickets from Zendesk to an Amazon S3 bucket.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/AppFlow-DEA1-02.png)
    
    AppFlow relieves you from investing significant time and highly skilled developer staff to build and maintain custom API connectors for AWS services to exchange data with SaaS applications. Systems Administrators and business analysts can quickly implement most of the integrations they need without waiting months for IT to finish integration projects.
    
    A trigger determines how a flow runs. The following are the supported flow trigger types:
    
    **Run on demand** — Users manually run the flow as needed.
    
    **Run on event** — Amazon AppFlow runs the flow in response to an event from an SaaS application.
    
    **Run on schedule** — Amazon AppFlow runs the flow on a recurring schedule.
    
    Amazon AppFlow provides both AWS managed and customer managed keys for encrypting connection data and data stored in Amazon S3 when it is a destination. We recommend that you use a customer managed keys, as it puts you in full control over your encrypted data. When you choose a customer managed keys, Amazon AppFlow attaches a resource policy to the KMS key that grants it access to the KMS key.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use an Amazon AppFlow flow with a `Run on event` trigger. Configure it to ingest data from the Salesforce solution to the Redshift data warehouse. Use customer-managed key for encrypting connection data.**
    
    The option that says: **Launch a user-defined function (UDF) in Amazon Redshift with a `Run on demand` flow trigger. Configure the UDF to synchronize the data exchange between the Salesforce solution and the Amazon Redshift data warehouse** is incorrect because UDFs have certain limitations in terms of accessing external systems or services that are outside of AWS, such as a custom SaaS solution. In addition, a UDF is not capable of continuously sending data from the SaaS application to the Redshift data warehouse. It’s also important to note that the `Run on demand` flow trigger is commonly used in Amazon AppFlow and not in UDF.
    
    The option that says: **Create a new workflow in Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (Amazon MWAA) and set up the Salesforce solution to forward the data to MWAA via the Java Database Connectivity (JDBC) connector in Amazon Redshift. Use an AWS managed keys for encrypting connection data** is incorrect. Although using Amazon MWAA can satisfy the given requirement of continuously sending data from the SaaS application to Amazon Redshift, it still entails a lot of operational overhead to establish the end-to-end connection. Take note that the scenario explicitly asks for the least amount of overhead. Moreover, the use of an AWS managed keys does not fulfill the goal of providing the company with full control over its encrypted data. A customer-managed key should be used in this scenario instead.
    
    The option that says: **Set up an AWS Glue Workflow to orchestrate a Crawler and ETL Job between the Salesforce application and Amazon Redshift. Configure a rule using Amazon Eventbridge and define the event pattern that will trigger the AWS Glue Workflow as the target** is incorrect because AWS Glue Workflows is primarily used to create and visualize complex ETL activities. It is not for integrating an external SaaS solution with an AWS Service. Using Eventbridge requires an intermediary service to write the output data to Amazon Redshift. This solution would not continuously send data from the SaaS application to Amazon Redshift with the LEAST operational overhead.
    

A data engineering team manages 10 TB of data in an Amazon S3 bucket. The team must meet new data lifecycle and retention policies. Under the new guidelines, all newly stored data must be readily available for random occasional SQL queries. Data older than 3 years requires secure storage but must be accessible within 8 hours for compliance audits. Additionally, data exceeding a 10-year retention period should be permanently deleted.

Which solution will fulfill this requirement most cost-effectively?

- Configure Amazon RDS to store the newly added data. Create a Lambda function to periodically export the snapshots to the S3 Standard class. Set up lifecycle policies for transferring snapshots to S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval after 3 years and deleting data after 10 years.
- **Configure the new data to be stored in the S3 Standard-Infrequent Access (S3 Standard-IA) storage class. Use Amazon Athena for ad-hoc querying. Implement lifecycle rules for data archival to S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval after 3 years and deletion after 10 years.**
    
    **Amazon S3** offers different types of storage classes that users can choose based on the specific needs of their workload. Depending on their workload’s data access, resiliency, and performance requirements, these classes are tailored to provide the best storage options at the lowest possible costs. S3 storage classes are versatile and suitable for nearly any use case – including those with high-performance demands, data lakes, residency requirements, unknown or changing access patterns, or archival storage.
    
    One of these classes is the Amazon S3 Standard-Infrequent Access (S3 Standard-IA). Amazon S3 Standard-Infrequent Access (S3 Standard-IA) storage class is designed for data that is accessed less frequently but, when needed, requires rapid access. S3 Standard-IA offers the high durability, throughput, and low latency of S3 Standard but with a lower storage cost and a slightly higher retrieval fee. This storage class makes it an ideal solution for long-term storage and backups and a data store for disaster recovery files. Amazon Athena allows users to analyze data stored in Amazon S3 with SQL queries. It’s interactive and serverless, so there’s no infrastructure to manage. Users are charged only for the queries they run, making it an incredibly cost-effective solution.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-DEA1-03-lifecyle-rule.png)
    
    By implementing lifecycle rules in Amazon S3, you can easily manage your data throughout its lifecycle. S3 Lifecycle configuration rules allow you to transition objects to more cost-effective storage classes, such as S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval or S3 Glacier Deep Archive, for long-term storage and archiving purposes. Moreover, you have the option to set up rules that will automatically delete objects after a specific period, which helps you comply with data governance and regulatory requirements.
    
    Amazon S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval, previously known as S3 Glacier, is a cost-effective storage solution for archiving data. You can configure retrieval times from minutes to hours, which is suitable for accessing data for compliance audits within a 10-hour window. On the other hand, the S3 Glacier Deep Archive is designed for data that is accessed less frequently but needs to be retained for decades as part of digital preservation or regulatory compliance. It is the most affordable long-term archiving storage option available on AWS.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Configure the new data to be stored in the S3 Standard-Infrequent Access (S3 Standard-IA) storage class. Use Amazon Athena for ad-hoc querying. Implement lifecycle rules for data archival to S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval after 3 years and deletion after 10 years.**
    
    The option that says: **Set the S3 Intelligent-Tiering storage class for new data with a configuration towards Deep Archive Access for longer-term storage. Utilize Amazon Athena for ad-hoc querying. Configure S3 Intelligent Tiering to purge data after 10 years** is incorrect. Although using Amazon Athena to query data meets one of the requirements, transitioning to Deep Archive Access makes it impossible for data to be accessible within an 8-hour window. S3 Glacier Deep Archive is intended for long-term data storage that is accessed once or twice a year, with retrieval times typically ranging from 12 to 48 hours.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Redshift for data storage of the new data, then configure Redshift Spectrum to directly query data on Amazon S3. Implement lifecycle rules for data archival to Amazon S3 Glacier Deep Archive storage class. Set up another lifecycle rule to delete data longer than 10 years** is incorrect. Redshift is primarily a data warehousing solution for large-scale analytical workloads, not occasional SQL queries on recently ingested data. While Redshift Spectrum allows querying data in S3, maintaining a Redshift cluster incurs hourly charges, making it a less cost-effective solution. Moreover, although S3 Glacier Deep Archive is cheaper than Glacier Flexible Retrieval, it only allows retrieval times ranging from 12 to 48 hours.
    
    The option that says: **Configure Amazon RDS to store the newly added data. Create a Lambda function to periodically export the snapshots to the S3 Standard class. Set up lifecycle policies for transferring snapshots to S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval after 3 years and deleting data after 10 years** is incorrect. Amazon RDS is primarily a database service for relational databases and is not optimized for cost-effective data storage and retrieval for compliance and lifecycle management in the way S3 and its associated services are. Moreover, managing snapshots and exports introduces operational overhead that does not directly contribute to the goals of easy SQL analysis and compliance with lifecycle policies.
    
- Set the S3 Intelligent-Tiering storage class for new data with a configuration towards Deep Archive Access for longer-term storage. Utilize Amazon Athena for ad-hoc querying. Configure S3 Intelligent Tiering to purge data after 10 years.
- Use Amazon Redshift for data storage of the new data, then configure Redshift Spectrum to directly query data on Amazon S3. Implement lifecycle rules for data archival to Amazon S3 Glacier Deep Archive storage class. Set up another lifecycle rule to delete data longer than 10 years.

A financial company has several AWS accounts that fetch market data from several cryptocurrency exchanges. The company is planning to launch a centralized logging ingestion system that automatically converts the incoming application log files to Apache Parquet format and stores the logs in an Amazon S3 bucket for easier processing.

The data engineer has been tasked to ensure that the log files must be delivered in near real-time to provide accurate crypto market statistics.

Which of the following options can meet this requirement LEAST operational overhead?

- Modify the solution to forward the cryptocurrency log files to Amazon Kinesis Data Streams and install the Kinesis Client Library on an Auto Scaling group of Amazon EC2 instances. Configure the EC2 instances to fetch the stream records and automatically convert the log files to Apache Parquet. Store the processed log files in Amazon S3.
- Refactor the solution to send the cryptocurrency log files to Apache Hive on an Amazon EMR cluster. Launch a table from the log files by using a custom regular expression (regex). Set up an external table on Amazon S3 in Hive with the file format set to Apache Parquet and schedule a `HiveQL UNLOAD` query to persist the log files to the external Amazon S3 table.
- Use the Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (Amazon MWAA) to forward the log files to an Amazon S3 bucket which will automatically transform the logs into Apache Parquet format.
- **Set up the solution to send the cryptocurrency log files to Amazon Data Firehose. Set up the Data Firehose to trigger a Lambda function that converts the log files to Apache Parquet format and delivers the files to the centralized S3 bucket.**
    
    **Amazon Data Firehose** can convert the format of your input data from JSON to Apache Parquet or Apache ORC before storing the data in Amazon S3. Parquet and ORC are columnar data formats that save space and enable faster queries compared to row-oriented formats like JSON. If you want to convert an input format other than JSON, such as comma-separated values (CSV) or structured text, you can use AWS Lambda to transform it to JSON first.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/amazon_kinesis_parquet.png)
    
    Amazon Data Firehose is a fully managed service for delivering real-time streaming data to destinations such as Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3), Amazon Redshift, Amazon OpenSearch Service, Amazon OpenSearch Serverless, Splunk, and any custom HTTP endpoint or HTTP endpoints owned by supported third-party service providers, including Datadog, Dynatrace, LogicMonitor, MongoDB, New Relic, Coralogix, and Elastic.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Set up the solution to send the cryptocurrency log files to Amazon Data Firehose. Set up the Data Firehose to trigger a Lambda function that converts the log files to Apache Parquet format and delivers the files to the centralized S3 bucket.**
    
    The option that says: **Use the Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (Amazon MWAA) to forward the log files to an Amazon S3 bucket which will automatically transform the logs into Apache Parquet format** is incorrect because Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow is primarily used as a managed orchestration service for Apache Airflow and not for converting log files into Apache Parquet format.
    
    The option that says: **Modify the solution to forward the cryptocurrency log files to Amazon Kinesis Data Streams and install the Kinesis Client Library on an Auto Scaling group of Amazon EC2 instances. Configure the EC2 instances to fetch the stream records and automatically convert the log files to Apache Parquet. Store the processed log files in Amazon S3** is incorrect. Take note that the scenario explicitly mentioned that the solution should have the least operational overhead in terms of managing the AWS resources. Having an Auto Scaling group of Amazon EC2 instances entails a lot of management and upkeep, which do not conform to the aforementioned requirement.
    
    The option that says: **Refactor the solution to send the cryptocurrency log files to Apache Hive on an Amazon EMR cluster. Launch a table from the log files by using a custom regular expression (regex). Set up an external table on Amazon S3 in Hive with the file format set to Apache Parquet and schedule a `HiveQL UNLOAD` query to persist the log files to the external Amazon S3 table** is incorrect. Just like the previous option, using an EC2-based Amazon EMR cluster requires maintenance and certain operational tasks. Using Amazon EMR could be a possible solution if the option mentioned that it uses the Amazon EMR Serverless type.
    

A company gathers a large amount of data from user interactions via its user registration system, which is then stored in an Amazon S3 data lake. Some of this data contains personally identifiable information (PII). The company wants to automate the process of identifying new and existing data that contains sensitive personal information and mask it before any analysis.

The data engineer has been tasked to redact the PII automatically through a masking S3 Object Lambda function that’s powered by a prebuilt `ComprehendPiiRedactionS3ObjectLambda`Lambda function. In addition, the solution must also be capable of automatically triggering a notification when a piece of personally identifiable information is detected from the data lake and provide a detailed report on the identified data.

Which solution effectively meets these requirements with MINIMAL operational overhead?

- Utilize AWS Lake Formation to set up data access rules that automatically scan for personally identifiable information (PII). Trigger an AWS Lambda function to invoke the existing S3 Object Lambda masking function upon detection of sensitive data.
- Implement S3 Event notifications to trigger an AWS Lambda function that analyzes objects for personally identifiable information (PII) using custom scripts. Upon detecting PII, the Lambda function then invokes the existing S3 Object Lambda masking function
- Activate Amazon Inspector to regularly assess the S3 data lake for compliance with data protection standards, including PII detection. Use Amazon EventBridge to trigger the masking function based on Inspector findings.
- **Activate Amazon Macie within the AWS account for continuous monitoring and identification of personally identifiable information (PII) within the S3 data lake. Configure the Amazon EventBridge rule to capture Macie's findings and trigger the masking function as the designated target for this rule.**
    
    **Amazon Macie** is a security service that utilizes machine learning algorithms to automatically identify, classify, and secure sensitive data in AWS. Amazon Macie protects sensitive data, such as personally identifiable information (PII) (e.g., names, addresses, credit card numbers, and intellectual property), from unauthorized access. After identifying sensitive data, Macie generates detailed reports and alerts, which can be integrated with Amazon EventBridge for automated response actions.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/amazon_eventbridge_macie_findings_17jul2023.png)
    
    Using Amazon Macie in conjunction with Amazon EventBridge allows for the automation of the process to detect PII and trigger the masking application with minimal operational overhead. Macie’s continuous monitoring capability ensures both new and existing data in the S3 data lake are analyzed for PII, aligning perfectly with the corporation’s requirements for automating the identification and masking of personal information before analysis. Furthermore, the integration of Amazon Macie for PII detection and Amazon EventBridge for event-driven triggering of the masking application provides a seamless and automated workflow. This minimizes operational overhead while ensuring sensitive data is automatically masked, adhering to data protection standards.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Activate Amazon Macie within the AWS account for continuous monitoring and identification of personally identifiable information (PII) within the S3 data lake. Configure the Amazon EventBridge rule to capture Macie’s findings and trigger the masking function as the designated target for this rule.**
    
    The option that says: **Activate Amazon Inspector to regularly assess the S3 data lake for compliance with data protection standards, including PII detection. Use Amazon EventBridge to trigger the masking application based on Inspector findings** is incorrect because Amazon Inspector is primarily used for security assessments and vulnerability management of EC2 instances and applications, not for detecting PII in S3 objects.
    
    The option that says: **Implement S3 Event notifications to trigger an AWS Lambda function that analyzes objects for personally identifiable information (PII) using custom scripts. Upon detecting PII, the Lambda function then invokes the existing S3 Object Lambda masking function** is incorrect. This option involves significant operational overhead to develop, maintain, and scale custom scripts for personally identifiable information (PII) detection.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize AWS Lake Formation to set up data access rules that automatically scan for personally identifiable information (PII). Trigger an AWS Lambda function to invoke the existing S3 Object Lambda masking function upon detection of sensitive data** is incorrect. AWS Lake Formation is primarily used for managing secure data lake access and does not inherently provide automated PII detection capabilities like Amazon Macie.
    

A company uses AWS Glue to create a data pipeline for processing medical records in JSON format. The Glue job was initially configured with 20 Data Processing Units (DPUs), yet the execution time is still quite slow.

How can a data engineer determine the optimal DPU capacity for the Glue job?

- **Examine the past job runs in the `Job run monitoring` section of the AWS Glue console. Visualize the Glue job’s profiled metrics to determine the appropriate DPU capacity.**
    
    With AWS Glue, you only pay for the time your ETL job takes to run. You are charged an hourly rate based on the number of Data Processing Units (DPU) to run your ETL job. A Data Processing Unit can also be referred to as a worker, which is the processing power of your ETL job.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA1-06.png)
    
    The graph illustrates the maximum number of allocated executors, represented by a horizontal red line, which correlates with the number of Data Processing Units (DPUs) designated for the job; in this scenario, 10 DPUs are allocated. The AWS Glue console presents job metrics, including executor allocation based on DPUs for each job run. For in-depth analysis, select a job run and opt for ‘View run metrics’ to examine detailed performance data.
    
    You can use the Job Run Monitoring section in the AWS Glue console to determine the appropriate DPU capacity needed. The job monitoring section of the AWS Glue console uses the results of previous job runs to specify the proper DPU capacity.
    
    Hence, the correct option is: **Examine the past job runs in the Job run monitoring section of the AWS Glue console. Visualize the Glue job’s profiled metrics to determine the appropriate DPU capacity.**
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon CloudWatch Logs insights to review the log group associated with the Glue job. Query the logs for “DPU’ to optimize the appropriate number of DPUs** is incorrect. You can use the CloudWatch to monitor the AWS Glue performance. However, CloudWatch logs might not indicate the optimal number of DPUs needed, as they will inspect the aggregate job details and not help determine the appropriate DPU capacity.
    
    The option that says: **Visualize the Glue job to determine its appropriate number of DPUs in the AWS Glue console’s Extract, Transform, and load (ETL) section** is incorrect. While inspecting it can be helpful for a high-level overview of job configurations and operations, it does not offer in-depth analysis or visualization that can help to optimize DPU usage.
    
    The option that says: **Use a job bookmark to track the DPU consumed by the Glue job** is incorrect it is because job bookmarks in AWS Glue do not serve the purpose of tracking DPU consumption. Instead, job bookmarks are used to enable Glue jobs to process only new or updated data in subsequent runs by maintaining state information about the data previously processed.
    
- Use a job bookmark to track the DPU consumed by the Glue job.
- Use Amazon CloudWatch Logs insights to review the log group associated with the Glue job. Query the logs for "DPU' to optimize the appropriate number of DPUs.
- Visualize the Glue job to determine its appropriate number of DPUs in the AWS Glue console's Extract, Transform, and load (ETL) section.

A manufacturing company uses Amazon S3 as a data lake for collecting records from multiple sources. The company’s sales team frequently analyzes data from the past three months.

Moreover, the company generates a semi-annual report that includes data from the past six months. Even though there will be no further access to data older than 6 months, the company must retain it to comply with its data retention policy.

Which of the solutions will meet these requirements MOST cost-effectively? (Select TWO).

- Create a job that automatically copies the last three months of data to Amazon Redshift and unloads data older than three months to S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval. Use Amazon Redshift Spectrum to include the 6 months of data for semi-annual analysis.
- Implement an S3 lifecycle policy that moves data over 6 months old to S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval.
- **Implement an S3 lifecycle policy that moves data over 6 months old to S3 Glacier Deep Archive.**
- **Create a job that automatically copies the last three months of data to Amazon Redshift and unloads data older than three months to Amazon S3. Use Amazon Redshift Spectrum to include the 6 months of data for semi-annual analysis.**
- Implement Amazon QuickSight to analyze all the data stored in the S3 Data lake. Configure the QuickSight to go directly to the S3 data lake to enable real-time analytics of all the data.
- **Evaluation**
    
    Amazon Redshift is a fully managed cloud data warehouse that makes analyzing all your data simple and cost-effective. Using Amazon Redshift Spectrum can efficiently query and retrieve structured and semistructured data from files in Amazon S3 without having to load the data into Amazon Redshift Tables.
    
    Amazon S3 Glacier storage classes are purposely built for data archiving that provides the highest performance and the lowest cost archive storage in the cloud.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA1-07.png)
    
    There are two query requirements in the scenario:
    
    1. Regular queries against the most recent 3 months of data
    
    2. Occasional queries for the semi-annual report
    
    To solve the first requirement, we create an automated job that loads current data from the data lake to Amazon Redshift. We then use Amazon Redshift for running analytics. As for the 2nd requirement, since any data past 3 months would be rarely accessed, we can unload it to S3 instead of keeping it in the Redshift cluster to save on storage costs. Redshift Spectrum eliminates the need to move data to Redshift in order for it to be accessible within a cluster. Hence, you’d be able to use Redshift Spectrum to run queries directly against data in an S3 bucket. Redshift Spectrum uses the same resources you used on the Redshift database, and it only charges for data queried from Amazon S3.
    
    Finally, to comply with the data retention policy, you can create an S3 lifecycle policy that moves data older than 6 months to Glacier Deep Archive. S3 Glacier Deep Archive, offering the lowest-cost storage among the Glacier tiers, is ideal for long-term archived data accessed less than once a year and retrieved asynchronously.
    
    Hence, the correct options are:
    
    **– Create a job that automatically copies the last three months of data to Amazon Redshift and unloads data older than three months to Amazon S3. Use Amazon Redshift Spectrum to include the 6 months of data for semi-annual analysis.**
    
    - **Implement an S3 lifecycle policy that moves data over 6 months old to S3 Glacier Deep Archive.**
    
    The option that says: **Create a job that automatically copies the last three months of data to Amazon Redshift and unloads data older than three months to S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval. Use Amazon Redshift Spectrum to include the 6 months of data for semi-annual analysis** is incorrect because it is not possible to unload data directly from Amazon Redshift to S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval. Moreover, since Redshift Spectrum cannot query data in S3 Glacier, the proposed solution approach would not work for semi-annual analysis without restoring the entire data to S3.
    
    The option that says: **Implement an S3 lifecycle policy that moves data over 6 months old to S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval** is incorrect. While you can use S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval for archiving old data, Glacier Flexible Retrieval is more expensive than Deep Archive.
    
    The option that says: **Implement Amazon QuickSight to analyze all the data stored in the S3 Data lake. Configure the QuickSight to go directly to the S3 data lake to enable real-time analytics of all the data** is incorrect. Amazon QuickSight is a fast, easy-to-use tool for building visualizations and quick business insights from data. However, there might be better options for complex analytical queries, especially for large datasets like Data Lakes. There are more cost-effective ways to analyze the data than this one.
    

A data engineer at a healthcare analytics firm is tasked with enhancing patient outcome predictions using a machine learning (ML) model. The model integrates diverse datasets, including patient records, treatment histories, and clinical trial data. As the model is set to be deployed in a production environment, the engineer must guarantee the integrity and reliability of the data for ML-driven predictions. Additionally, the engineer needs to streamline the data preparation process for the model’s deployment.

Which solution will effectively meet these requirements?

- Implement Amazon SageMaker Processing to process the input data and output to Amazon S3.
- Utilize Amazon Redshift to aggregate and analyze the data, ensuring quality and completeness before feeding it into the model.
- Employ Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler for exploratory data analysis (EDA) to prepare the data.
- **Utilize Amazon SageMaker workflows, incorporating an Amazon SageMaker ML Lineage Tracking step for data preparation.**
    
    Amazon SageMaker ML Lineage Tracking offers a way to create and store information about the various steps in an ML workflow. This feature is integral for establishing model governance and audit standards, which are essential for ensuring the accuracy, completeness, and trustworthiness of the data used in ML decisions.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/ml_lineage_tracking.jpeg)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Utilize Amazon SageMaker workflows, incorporating an Amazon SageMaker ML Lineage Tracking step for data preparation.**
    
    The option that says: **Utilize Amazon Redshift to aggregate and analyze the data, ensuring quality and completeness before feeding it into the model** is incorrect. While Amazon Redshift is a powerful data warehousing service that provides capabilities for data aggregation and analysis, it does not directly address the specific need to automate data preparation or ensure data lineage tracking for ML model deployments.
    
    The option that says: **Employ Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler for exploratory data analysis (EDA) to prepare the data** is incorrect. SageMaker Data Wrangler is effective for conducting EDA and preparing data for ML models, but it does not inherently ensure the data’s accuracy, completeness, and trustworthiness for ML decisions.
    
    The option that says: **Implement Amazon SageMaker Processing to process the input data and output to Amazon S3** is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Processing is useful for various data operations, including processing and validation, but it does not inherently provide assurances about the data’s accuracy, completeness, and trustworthiness for ML decisions.
    

A data engineer at a multinational corporation is in charge of setting up a system for managing extensive organizational data. The system must be capable of granular access control at multiple levels: database, table, column, row, and cell. The solution must be implemented with minimal operational overhead.

Which option meets these requirements?

- Deploy a Hive metastore using Amazon EMR and manage user access through HiveQL data definition language statements.
- Implement an Amazon Aurora database as a catalog and control access using SQL GRANTs at different levels.
- Implement Amazon RDS with IAM database authentication to manage fine-grained access at various levels.
- **Establish a data lake and a data catalog using AWS Lake Formation, controlling access via Lake Formation data filters.**
    
    AWS Lake Formation is the optimal choice for setting up a centralized metadata storage solution that demands fine-grained access control. Utilizing Lake Formation data filters, a data engineer can enforce security measures at the database, table, column, row, and cell levels. This capability, coupled with its scalability and reliability, makes Lake Formation the ideal solution with minimal operational overhead.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/lake_formation.jpeg)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Establish a data lake and a data catalog using AWS Lake Formation, controlling access via Lake Formation data filters.**
    
    The option that says: **Implement Amazon RDS with IAM database authentication to manage fine-grained access at various levels** is incorrect. Although Amazon RDS supports IAM database authentication for access management, it does not inherently provide the level of detailed control (specifically at the row and cell levels) required in this scenario. This limitation makes it less suitable compared to Lake Formation for managing intricate permissions.
    
    The option that says: **Implement an Amazon Aurora database as a catalog and control access using SQL GRANTs at different levels** is incorrect. While Amazon Aurora allows extensive access control through SQL GRANTs, it lacks the native capability to manage permissions at the row and cell levels without additional tools or configurations. Furthermore, this approach involves significant operational management, especially if integrating with a Hive metastore.
    
    The option that says: **Deploy a Hive metastore using Amazon EMR and manage user access through HiveQL data definition language statements** is incorrect. Deploying a Hive metastore on Amazon EMR does not offer the necessary granularity for permissions control at the row and cell levels. Moreover, using HiveQL for access management does not align with the requirement for minimal operational overhead.
    

A company is developing an application to transform data using containers orchestrated by Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS), operating on Amazon EC2 nodes. In this setup, a data engineer faces the task of selecting an appropriate storage solution for the data before its transformation. Each container will process distinct datasets and subsequently store the transformed data in a data lake. Data sharing among the containers is prohibited.

Which storage solution offers the LOWEST latency for this scenario?

- Enable containers to establish a connection to Amazon DynamoDB Accelerator (DAX) within the application code.
- **Utilize an ephemeral volume provided by the node's RAM for the containers.**
    
    Using an ephemeral volume provided by the node’s RAM in Amazon EKS is the most efficient approach for achieving the lowest latency. Amazon EKS, a managed Kubernetes service, orchestrates container deployment on nodes, which can be EC2 instances or managed through AWS Fargate. Ephemeral volumes, tied to the pod’s lifecycle, can swiftly access the node’s local resources like drives or RAM. Since the requirement does not involve data sharing between containers and focuses on low-latency access, local storage like node RAM is ideal.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/ephemeral_storage_k8s.jpeg)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Utilize an ephemeral volume provided by the node’s RAM for the containers.**
    
    The option that says: **Enable containers to establish a connection to Amazon DynamoDB Accelerator (DAX) within the application code** is incorrect. Although DAX, a write-through cache for DynamoDB, offers low-latency access to stored items, it requires network calls for storage access. This external storage setup introduces more latency compared to local node storage.
    
    The option that says: **Have containers use a `PersistentVolume` backed by Amazon EFS, managed through AWS Controllers for Kubernetes (ACK)** is incorrect. While AWS Controllers for Kubernetes (ACK) allow for efficient management of AWS services within the Kubernetes environment, using a PersistentVolume backed by Amazon EFS introduces higher latency. This is due to the network calls required for accessing EFS, making it less suitable for scenarios where low latency is crucial.
    
    The option that says: **Enable containers to establish a connection to Amazon MemoryDB for Redis within the application code** is incorrect. While MemoryDB provides low-latency in-memory database services, it requires network connectivity for data access. Like DAX, being external to the node means increased latency compared to local storage options.
    
- Have containers use a `PersistentVolume` backed by Amazon EFS, managed through AWS Controllers for Kubernetes (ACK).
- Enable containers to establish a connection to Amazon MemoryDB for Redis within the application code.

A data engineer is developing an application that involves sending data for transformation to an Amazon Simple Queue Service (Amazon SQS) queue. Multiple Amazon Elastic Container Service (ECS) tasks will poll and consume messages from the queue. The engineer must ensure that each message is processed once before being deleted.

Which events can lead to the removal of messages from an SQS queue? (Select THREE.)

- **A `DeleteMessage` API call was made to Amazon SQS.**
- **A purge operation was performed on the queue.**
- An application receives a message without deleting it.
- The visibility timeout expires on a message.
- The configuration for a queue is edited.
- **The `maxReceiveCount` for a message was exceeded.**
- **Evaluation**
    
    In Amazon SQS, several events can lead to the removal of messages, which is crucial for ensuring the queue’s efficiency and reliability. A DeleteMessage API call is a direct method to remove a message from the queue, typically after it has been processed by a consumer. This ensures that messages are not processed more than necessary. Reaching the maxReceiveCount for a message is another way messages are removed; this occurs when a message has been received a specified number of times but not deleted, indicating processing issues and usually results in the message being sent to a dead-letter queue. Furthermore, performing a purge operation on the queue instantly clears all messages, useful for resetting or troubleshooting the queue.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/dea_sqs_purge.jpg)
    
    Therefore, the correct answers are:
    
    **– A `DeleteMessage` API call was made to Amazon SQS.**
    
    **– The `maxReceiveCount` for a message was exceeded.**
    
    **– A purge operation was performed on the queue.**
    
    The option that says: **An application receives a message without deleting it** is incorrect. Merely receiving a message does not remove it from the queue. Instead, it triggers a visibility timeout, during which the message is not visible to other consumers but remains in the queue.
    
    The option that says: **The visibility timeout expires on a message** is incorrect. The expiry of the visibility timeout makes the message available to other consumers again but does not remove it from the queue.
    
    The option that says: **The configuration for a queue is edited** is incorrect. Editing a queue’s configuration can change its behavior and settings but does not directly lead to message removal from the queue.
    

A healthcare company has recently started using Amazon Glue DataBrew for data preparation tasks. A dataset containing patient information, which includes Personally Identifiable Information (PII), is given for processing. To comply with data privacy regulations, the PII data needs to be masked before further processing. The technique chosen for this task should replace the actual PII data with other authentic-looking values to maintain the overall structure and statistical distribution of the data.

Which of the following data-masking techniques should be used?

- Nulling out or deletion
- Decryption
- Probabilistic encryption
- **Substitution**
    
    **Substitution** is a data-masking technique that involves replacing actual PII data with other authentic-looking values. This technique is useful in maintaining the overall structure and statistical distribution of the data, which is important for testing or development purposes. For example, a patient’s real name in a healthcare dataset could be replaced with a fictitious, but real-sounding name. This way, the sensitive information is protected, but the data remains useful for analysis or testing.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/masking-technique-011724.jpg)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Substitution.**
    
    **Decryption** is a process used to convert encrypted data back into its original form. It requires a specific key, which is used to decrypt the encrypted data. In the context of data privacy, decryption would be used to access the original data, not to mask or protect it.
    
    **Probabilistic encryption** is a type of encryption that produces different ciphertext each time it’s applied, even for the same plaintext. This means that if you encrypt the same data twice, you’ll get two different ciphertexts. While this adds an extra layer of security, it’s not the same as replacing PII data with other authentic-looking values. It’s a way to protect data, but it doesn’t maintain the same structure or statistical distribution of the data.
    
    **Nulling out or deletion** is a data-masking technique where a particular field is replaced with a null value or the column is deleted entirely. While this does protect sensitive information, it doesn’t replace the PII data with other authentic-looking values. Instead, it removes the data entirely, which can impact the utility of the dataset for testing or development purposes.
    

A data engineer is assigned to build a data lake for a company’s business data stored on Amazon S3. The data, saved in the Apache Parquet format, will be utilized for complex analytic tasks. The company anticipates that users will need granular access to the datasets.

Which of the following will meet these requirements in the shortest amount of time and in the MOST cost-effective manner?

- Leverage Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (Amazon MWAA) to orchestrate and schedule data processing workflows. Ensure data security by assigning appropriate access controls.
- Using AWS Lake Formation, register the S3 bucket where the data resides. To secure the data, assign granular permissions to different users.
- **Execute an AWS Glue crawler to establish a data catalog for existing data and register its Amazon S3 path location on Lake Formation. For data security, allocate granular permissions to various users.**
    
    **AWS Lake Formation** is a service that makes it easy to set up a secure data lake in days. A data lake is a centralized, curated, and secured repository that stores all your data, both in its original form and prepared for analysis. A data lake enables you to break down data silos and combine different types of analytics to gain insights and guide better business decisions.
    
    Amazon S3 forms the storage layer for Lake Formation. If you already use S3, you typically begin by registering existing S3 buckets that contain your data. Lake Formation creates new buckets for the data lake and imports data into them. AWS always stores this data in your account, and only you have direct access to it.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws-lake-formation-glue-crawler.png)
    
    AWS Lake Formation is integrated with AWS Glue, which you can use to create a data catalog that describes available datasets and their appropriate business applications. Lake Formation lets you define policies and control data access with simple “grant and revoke permissions to data” sets at granular levels. You can assign permissions to IAM users, roles, groups, and Active Directory users using federation. You specify permissions on catalog objects (like tables and columns) rather than on buckets and objects.
    
    AWS Lake Formation also provides blueprints that you can run for loading and cataloging data. A blueprint allows you to import data from MySQL, Postgres, SQL Server, MariaDB, and Oracle databases running in Amazon RDS or hosted in Amazon EC2. With Lake Formation, you can import data from MySQL, Postgres, SQL Server, MariaDB, and Oracle databases running in Amazon RDS or hosted in Amazon EC2. You can also import from on-premises databases by connecting with Java Database Connectivity (JDBC).
    
    It was indicated in the scenario that data in Parquet format is already existing in Amazon S3. As a result, you don’t need to utilize a Lake Formation blueprint.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Execute an AWS Glue crawler to establish a data catalog for existing data and register its Amazon S3 path location on Lake Formation. For data security, allocate granular permissions to various users.**
    
    The option that says: **Leverage Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (Amazon MWAA) to orchestrate and schedule data processing workflows. Ensure data security by assigning appropriate access controls** is incorrect because while Amazon MWAA can orchestrate and schedule data processing workflows, it does not provide a solution for creating a data lake or managing granular access to datasets. These tasks would need to be handled separately, which could increase complexity and cost.
    
    The option that says: **Using AWS Lake Formation, register the S3 bucket where the data resides. To secure the data, assign granular permissions to different users** is incorrect. You should register the Amazon S3 path of the bucket where your data is stored and not the bucket name.
    
    The option that says: **Import data from Amazon S3 using an AWS Lake Formation blueprint. Subsequently, accord users granular Lake Formation permissions as needed** is incorrect because Amazon S3 is not a valid import source in a Lake Formation blueprint.
    
- Import data from Amazon S3 using an AWS Lake Formation blueprint. Subsequently, accord users granular Lake Formation permissions as needed.

A company uses a variety of SaaS applications and AWS services. The data engineering team has been tasked with integrating and automating data flows between Salesforce and Amazon Redshift.

The team needs to transfer new Salesforce event data to Redshift every day but also requires the ability to manually trigger the data flow for debugging and testing purposes. The data transferred should only include records that have been updated or newly created since the last successful flow run.

Which Amazon AppFlow Flow Trigger should be used?

- **Incremental transfer schedule-triggered flows**
    
    When you select incremental transfer, Amazon AppFlow transfers only the records that have been added or changed since the last successful flow run. This is achieved by selecting a source timestamp field to specify how Amazon AppFlow identifies new or changed records. For example, if you have a Created Date timestamp field, you can instruct Amazon AppFlow to transfer only newly-created records (and not changed records) since the last successful flow run.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/appflow-011824.png)
    
    The first schedule-triggered flow will pull 30 days of past records at the time of the first flow run. To transfer records created or modified over a different time range other than the past 30 days at the time of the first flow run, you can set up the flow to be triggered on demand. You can then use the filter option to pull records over the desired time range. After the on-demand flow runs and pulls the initial set of records, you can edit the flow to be triggered on schedule so that subsequent flow runs transfer incremental data.
    
    Optionally, you can add a time offset to the time range for the incremental transfer. The flow run will import records that were created or changed between the previous flow run and the specified offset prior to the current flow run. This feature can be used to accommodate any latencies in the source systems in timestamping changes to records.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Incremental transfer schedule-triggered flows.**
    
    **On-demand flows** is incorrect. These flows are manually run as needed. This option would not be suitable for the given scenario because the requirement is to transfer new Salesforce event data to Redshift every day automatically, not manually.
    
    **Event-triggered flows** is incorrect. Amazon AppFlow runs these flows based on a specified change event in the source application. This option is not suitable for the given scenario because the requirement is to transfer data every day, not just when a change event occurs.
    
    **Full transfer schedule-triggered flows** is incorrect. Amazon AppFlow runs these flows based on the schedule that you specify during the flow setup. When you select full transfer, Amazon AppFlow transfers a snapshot of all records at the time of the flow run from the source to the destination. This option is not suitable for the given scenario because the requirement is to transfer only the records that have been added or changed since the last successful flow run, not all records.
    
- On-demand flows
- Full transfer schedule-triggered flows
- Event-triggered flows

A company that provides streaming services operates several microservices. These handle various tasks such as recommending content, managing user profiles, and collecting feedback. Each microservice consumes TLS-encrypted streaming data from a specific Amazon Managed Streaming for Apache Kafka (Amazon MSK) topic.

However, after the deployment of a new recommendation algorithm, the microservice for content recommendation started receiving data meant for feedback collection. This has led to inconsistencies in the user experience and skewed analytics.

What solution would ensure that each microservice can only access data from its designated topic?

- **Set up Amazon MSK to utilize Kafka access control lists (ACLs). Restrict the permissions of each microservice to only its respective Amazon MSK topic.**
    
    **Amazon Managed Streaming for Apache Kafka (Amazon MSK**) is a fully managed service that enables you to build and run applications that use Apache Kafka to process streaming data. Amazon MSK provides the control-plane operations, such as those for creating, updating, and deleting clusters. It lets you use Apache Kafka data-plane operations, such as those for producing and consuming data. It runs open-source versions of Apache Kafka. This means existing applications, tooling, and plugins from partners and the Apache Kafka community are supported without requiring changes to application code.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/amazon-msk-architecture-diagram.png)
    
    Apache Kafka’s Access Control Lists (ACLs) offer a highly granular approach to permissions management. They provide a mechanism to regulate which applications can read or write to a certain topic. They follow the format “Principal P is [Allowed/Denied] Operation O From Host H on any Resource R matching ResourcePattern RP”. If RP doesn’t match a specific resource R, then R has no associated ACLs, and therefore, no one other than super users is allowed to access R. To change this Apache Kafka behavior, you set the property `allow.everyone.if.no.acl.found` to true. Amazon MSK sets it to true by default. This means that with Amazon MSK clusters, if you don’t explicitly set ACLs on a resource, all principals can access this resource. If you enable ACLs on a resource, only the authorized principals can access it. If you want to restrict access to a topic and authorize a client using TLS mutual authentication, add ACLs using the Apache Kafka authorizer CLI.
    
    In the scenario, after deploying a new recommendation algorithm, the content recommendation microservice began receiving data intended for feedback collection. This led to inconsistencies in user experience and skewed analytics. The solution to guarantee that each microservice can only access data from its assigned topic is to configure Amazon MSK to use Kafka access control lists (ACLs). By limiting the permissions of each microservice to its respective Amazon MSK topic only, the company can ensure that each microservice only accesses the data it is supposed to, thereby preventing any cross-contamination of data across different microservices. This approach provides a robust and secure method for managing access to data in a microservices architecture.
    
    Therefore, the correct answer is: **Set up Amazon MSK to utilize Kafka access control lists (ACLs). Restrict the permissions of each microservice to only its respective Amazon MSK topic.**
    
    The option that says: **Update the security group configurations associated with each Elastic Network Interface (ENI) of the Kafka brokers within the cluster. Add a security group rule that allows specific microservices to access their designated brokers** is incorrect. Security groups in AWS are used to control inbound and outbound network traffic to AWS resources, and they operate at the level of network interfaces. While they can restrict which IP addresses can connect to the Kafka brokers, they can’t control access at the level of individual topics. Therefore, this wouldn’t prevent a misconfigured microservice from consuming data from the wrong topic.
    
    The option that says: **Modify the partitioning key in the producer application to prevent data intended for feedback collection from being written to the content recommendation topic** is incorrect. The partitioning key determines how messages are distributed among the various partitions within a Kafka topic. Modifying the partitioning key in the producer application would change the distribution of the messages, but it would not alter or restrict which microservices can access which topics
    
    The option says: **Deploy individual source connectors for each microservice using Kafka Connect and modify the connector configurations to link each to its specific Amazon MSK topic** is incorrect. Kafka Connect is designed to bridge Kafka with other systems, such as databases or search systems. Its main purpose is to enable data flow between Kafka and other platforms, not to enforce data access controls
    
- Deploy individual source connectors for each microservice using Kafka Connect and modify the connector configurations to link each to its specific Amazon MSK topic.
- Update the security group configurations associated with each Elastic Network Interface (ENI) of the Kafka brokers within the cluster. Add a security group rule that allows specific microservices to access their designated brokers.
- Modify the partitioning key in the producer application to prevent data intended for feedback collection from being written to the content recommendation topic.

A company is utilizing multiple AWS accounts for various departments. The corporation wants to streamline its logging queries across all accounts with minimal setup and management overhead.

Which of the following would provide the most efficient solution for the corporation’s requirements?

- Configure AWS Lake Formation for centralized logging across all accounts.
- Manually establish a user-managed, centralized data lake using Amazon S3 for each account.
- **Use AWS CloudTrail Lake for centralized logging across all accounts.**
    
    **AWS CloudTrail Lake** simplifies activity log analysis by integrating collection, storage, optimization, and query in the same product. By consolidating these features into one environment, CloudTrail Lake eliminates the need for separate data processing pipelines that span across teams and products.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/cloudtrail-lake-013124.png)
    
    AWS CloudTrail Lake provides an SQL-based query interface, allowing you to easily analyze your logs using standard SQL queries. This is particularly useful for auditing, security investigation, and operational troubleshooting purposes. CloudTrail Lake also features sample queries to help you get started with writing queries for common scenarios, such as collecting a user’s activity, that can accelerate security investigations. With immutable event data storage and a default retention period of seven years, CloudTrail Lake can meet the compliance requirements of most users. CloudTrail Lake can also collect events from multiple AWS regions and accounts.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use AWS CloudTrail Lake for centralized logging across all accounts.**
    
    The option that says: **Configure AWS Lake Formation for centralized logging across all accounts** is incorrect because AWS Lake Formation is primarily used for managing, securing, and sharing data for analytics and machine learning. It is not specifically designed for centralized logging across multiple AWS accounts. It would require more setup and management overhead compared to AWS CloudTrail Lake.
    
    The option that says: **Manually establish a user-managed, centralized data lake using Amazon S3 for each account** is incorrect because manually establishing a user-managed, centralized data lake using Amazon S3 for each account would require significant effort and time. It would involve setting up data replication from multiple AWS accounts, which is not as efficient as using AWS CloudTrail Lake.
    
    The option that says: **Deploy Amazon Athena for centralized logging across all accounts** is incorrect because Amazon Athena, while it can be used to query logs, is not a centralized logging solution. You would still need a way to aggregate logs from multiple AWS accounts into a centralized location, which Amazon Athena does not provide by itself. Therefore, it’s not the most efficient solution for the corporation’s requirements.
    
- Deploy Amazon Athena for centralized logging across all accounts.

An e-commerce company has implemented real-time inventory management using a DynamoDB table with multiple data partitions. This solution updates inventory levels as customers place orders on the website. However, during peak shopping hours, the data engineer has noticed an increase in throttled items in the DynamoDB table, which is degrading the system’s performance. This causes delays in inventory updates.

To address these problems, the company needs to:

1. Continuously monitor and diagnose the DynamoDB table’s access patterns.
2. Analyze traffic trends during different times of the day.
3. Identify the most frequently accessed partition keys.

What steps should the data engineer take to address these issues? (Select TWO.)

- Use Amazon Athena to analyze the access patterns of the DynamoDB table.
- Use Amazon QuickSight to visualize the access patterns of the DynamoDB table.
- **Redesign the DynamoDB table to use a high cardinality key to distribute the read and write operations as evenly as possible.**
- **Use Amazon CloudWatch Contributor Insights to find the most accessed and throttled items in the DynamoDB table.**
- Increase the provisioned read and write capacity units of the DynamoDB table.
- **Evaluation**
    
    Amazon CloudWatch Contributor Insights is a diagnostic tool that provides a summarized view of your DynamoDB table’s traffic trends. It helps you identify the most frequently accessed partition keys, which can be useful in diagnosing throttling issues. By identifying the most accessed and throttled items, the data engineer can gain insights into the table’s access patterns and take appropriate actions to mitigate the throttling issues.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/contributor-insights-030724.png)
    
    In DynamoDB, data is distributed across multiple partitions based on the partition key. If a few partition keys receive a majority of the read and write traffic, this can lead to hot partitions, where these partitions receive a disproportionately high volume of traffic. This can result in throttling issues. By redesigning the table to use a high cardinality key, the read and write operations can be distributed more evenly across all the partitions, reducing the likelihood of hot partitions and throttling.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Use Amazon CloudWatch Contributor Insights to find the most accessed and throttled items in the DynamoDB table.**
    
    **– Redesign the DynamoDB table to use a high cardinality key to distribute the read and write operations as evenly as possible.**
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon QuickSight to visualize the access patterns of the DynamoDB table** is incorrect. While Amazon QuickSight is a powerful visualization tool, it does not provide the real-time diagnostic capabilities needed to identify and address throttling issues in a DynamoDB table. Its primary function is to provide a visual representation of your data rather than real-time diagnostics or performance optimization.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Athena to analyze the access patterns of the DynamoDB table** is incorrect because Amazon Athena is just a query service that makes it easy to analyze data in Amazon S3 and other data sources using standard SQL. It’s not designed to analyze access patterns of a DynamoDB table.
    
    The option that says: **Increase the provisioned read and write capacity units of the DynamoDB table** is incorrect because while this might help in some cases, it’s not a guaranteed solution. If the workload is unevenly distributed across the partitions (due to a low cardinality key), increasing capacity may only lead to higher costs if it’s not properly scaled based on the actual traffic patterns.
    

A data engineering team in a retail company needs to ensure the consistency and accuracy of its product inventory data. They must validate that the “Stock Quantity” column always contains positive integer values and the “Product Category” column should not have any null or empty values.

Which AWS service and method would be the most effective for implementing these data quality checks with minimal operational effort?

- **Use AWS Glue DataBrew to create a ruleset that defines data quality rules for the specific requirements of the "Stock Quantity" and "Product Category" columns.**
    
    AWS Glue DataBrew offers a streamlined solution for data quality management, particularly beneficial in scenarios requiring precise and automated data validation. Its ability to define specific data quality rules within a ruleset makes it an optimal choice for scenarios like ensuring inventory data accuracy. In the context of the given scenario, DataBrew can effectively validate that the “Stock Quantity” remains a positive integer and that the “Product Category” column is free of null or empty values. This functionality is crucial for maintaining the integrity of inventory data in retail, where accurate stock monitoring and categorization are vital.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/databrew.jpg)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use AWS Glue DataBrew to create a ruleset that defines data quality rules for the specific requirements of the “Stock Quantity” and “Product Category” columns.**
    
    The option that says: **Leverage AWS Glue ETL jobs to write custom scripts for checking and enforcing the data quality constraints on the dataset** is incorrect. This approach, while feasible, demands more complex scripting and operational management, making it less efficient compared to the streamlined, rule-based system of DataBrew.
    
    The option that says: **Implement AWS Lambda functions to trigger on data updates, validating and correcting the data as per the specified rules** is incorrect because it involves additional development and operational complexities. This method is not as straightforward for predefined data quality checks as using DataBrew’s ruleset feature.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize AWS Glue DataBrew recipes for data transformation, adding steps to validate and rectify data quality issues in the dataset** is incorrect. Recipes in DataBrew are more suited for data transformation tasks and may not offer the direct and focused approach of a ruleset for specific data quality validations.
    
- Leverage AWS Glue ETL jobs to write custom scripts for checking and enforcing the data quality constraints on the dataset.
- Implement AWS Lambda functions to trigger on data updates, validating and correcting the data as per the specified rules.
- Utilize AWS Glue DataBrew recipes for data transformation, adding steps to validate and rectify data quality issues in the dataset.

A game retail company manages user purchase data in a MySQL database on Amazon RDS. They frequently query and analyze the most recent three months of data, amounting to several terabytes. The company also needs to store older data outside the database, yet access it for quarterly trend reports, which involves joining it with recent data.

Which of the following options will provide optimal performance and a cost-effective solution based on the requirements?

- **Migrate historical data to Amazon S3. Set up a daily transfer of current data from Amazon RDS to Amazon Redshift. Use Redshift for regular queries and Redshift Spectrum to join the historical data in S3 with current data.**
    
    **Amazon Redshift Spectrum** stands out as a highly efficient solution for querying data across Amazon S3 and Amazon Redshift. It leverages the benefits of low-cost storage and open data formats, along with Redshift’s advanced query optimization and fast access to local disks. Redshift Spectrum’s serverless architecture aligns with Amazon Athena’s, negating the need for resource provisioning and management.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/redshift_spectrum-1_15OCT2023.gif)
    
    In this scenario, two distinct query requirements are identified:
    
    1. Regular analytics on the most recent three months of data.
    2. Periodic queries for quarterly reports, encompassing both historical and recent data.
    
    To meet the first requirement, a regular transfer of current data from the MySQL database to Amazon Redshift is established, taking advantage of Redshift’s powerful analytics capabilities. For the second requirement, given the infrequent access to historical data, storing it in S3 and querying via Redshift Spectrum is a more cost-effective approach than keeping it within Redshift. This method avoids data migration costs and utilizes Redshift’s allocated resources for optimized query performance.
    
    Thus, the optimal solution is: **Migrate historical data to Amazon S3. Set up a daily transfer of current data from Amazon RDS to Amazon Redshift. Use Redshift for regular queries and Redshift Spectrum to join the historical data in S3 with current data.**
    
    The option that says: **Synchronize a year’s worth of data on an Amazon RDS read replica. Move the older historical data to an Amazon S3 bucket for long-term storage. Create an AWS Glue Data Catalog and use Amazon Athena to join the historical and current data for report generation**is incorrect. This approach is inefficient as it requires maintaining a full year’s worth of data on an RDS read replica, which can be costly and doesn’t leverage the optimized analytical capabilities of a dedicated analytics service like Amazon Redshift. Additionally, while Amazon Athena is effective for querying S3 data, it necessitates data movement between RDS and S3, which can be time-consuming and adds complexity.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue to perform ETL and incrementally load a year’s worth of data into an Amazon Redshift cluster. Run the regular queries against this cluster. Create an AWS Glue Data Catalog of the data in Amazon S3 and use Amazon Athena to join the historical and current data to generate the reports** is incorrect. In this method, there is an unnecessary duplication of effort and resources. Loading recent data into a Redshift cluster and then moving it again to S3 for Athena queries is inefficient. Redshift Spectrum would be a more streamlined and cost-effective solution since it can query data directly in S3 without the need for additional data movement.
    
    The option that says: **Configure an Amazon Aurora database to handle real-time data. Archive older data to an S3 bucket. Utilize Amazon QuickSight for analytics, combining current data in Aurora with historical data for comprehensive reporting** is incorrect. While Amazon Aurora is a powerful database for transactional workloads, it is not optimized for the kind of large-scale analytical processing described in the scenario. Moreover, using Amazon QuickSight for analytics across Aurora and S3 introduces complexity in managing data across different storage solutions and may not offer the same level of performance as Redshift, especially for large-scale data joining and analytical queries.
    
- Configure an Amazon Aurora database to handle real-time data. Archive older data to an S3 bucket. Utilize Amazon QuickSight for analytics, combining current data in Aurora with historical data for comprehensive reporting.
- Use AWS Glue to perform ETL and incrementally load a year’s worth of data into an Amazon Redshift cluster. Run the regular queries against this cluster. Create an AWS Glue Data Catalog of the data in Amazon S3 and use Amazon Athena to join the historical and current data to generate the reports.
- Synchronize a year’s worth of data on an Amazon RDS read replica. Move the older historical data to an Amazon S3 bucket for long-term storage. Create an AWS Glue Data Catalog and use Amazon Athena to join the historical and current data for report generation.

A data analytics company is using AWS Glue for their ETL jobs to process and transform large-scale data sets. The team is experiencing irregularities and inefficiencies in their job execution times and is seeking to optimize it. To solve this, the team wants to accurately and effectively identify the root causes of these AWS Glue jobs.

Which method should the team use to gather the MOST detailed data and analyses for these operations?

- Use AWS CloudTrail's Data Events to monitor and log data access and API activity for the AWS Glue ETL jobs.
- Configure AWS X-Ray to trace and profile the AWS Glue job executions.
- **Profile and monitor AWS Glue operations using AWS Glue Monitoring and the AWS Glue Job profiler through CloudWatch.**
    
    AWS Glue is a managed ETL service that facilitates the extraction, transformation, and loading of data for analysis. It automates and simplifies data integration processes, making it easier to prepare and consolidate data for analytics.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/AWS_Glue_Monitoring_01DEC23.png)
    
    AWS Glue offers a Job run monitoring feature that gives a quick overview of job runs, including status, timestamps, and other key details. For deeper insights, the AWS Glue Job Profiler goes a step further, offering detailed metrics such as execution time, data processing rates, and memory usage. These metrics are crucial for pinpointing performance bottlenecks and fine-tuning resource utilization in ETL jobs.
    
    When these features are integrated with Amazon CloudWatch, they create dedicated log groups for each Glue job. These logs in CloudWatch provide a granular view of job performance. For example, by analyzing log data, teams can track how long specific data transformation tasks take or identify recurring errors in job runs.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/AWS_Glue_Cloudwatch_01DEC23.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Profile and monitor AWS Glue operations using AWS Glue Monitoring and the AWS Glue Job profiler through CloudWatch.**
    
    The option that says **Use AWS CloudTrail’s Data Events to monitor and log data access and API activity for the AWS Glue ETL jobs** is incorrect because AWS CloudTrail’s Data Events are primarily used for recording data plane activities of some AWS resources, such as object uploads to Amazon S3, Lambda Function invocations, or item deletions in a DynamoDB table. While they can log some API activity for AWS Glue, they are not designed for in-depth performance monitoring or analysis of Glue jobs, especially in comparison to the detailed metrics available through the AWS Glue job profiler and CloudWatch.
    
    The option that says **Query the AWS Glue Data Catalog using Athena for real-time monitoring of Glue job metrics and performance analysis** is incorrect because it is inefficient for real-time performance monitoring. Querying the AWS Glue Data Catalog with Athena provides access to metadata but does not offer the same level of real-time, detailed job execution metrics and analysis capabilities as the AWS Glue job profiler and CloudWatch.
    
    The option that says **Configure AWS X-Ray to trace and profile the AWS Glue job executions** is incorrect because AWS X-Ray is primarily used for analyzing and debugging distributed applications, including microservices, not specifically for profiling AWS Glue job performance.
    
- Query the AWS Glue Data Catalog using Athena for real-time monitoring of Glue job metrics and performance analysis.

A marketing agency runs daily sales campaigns and stores the results in their Amazon S3 data lake. They want to implement a new platform that allows analysts to visually verify data quality against set standards before any transformation or analysis.

Which set of actions can fulfill the requirements with the least amount of management overhead?

- Implement AWS Kinesis Data Firehose for streaming S3 data lake contents to an Amazon OpenSearch cluster. Use OpenSearch Dashboards for visualizing data quality metrics.
- Set up an Amazon EMR cluster with Apache Spark and Apache Zeppelin. Conduct a daily transfer of S3 data to the cluster's Hadoop Distributed File System (HDFS). Use Spark MLlib for quality checks and Zeppelin notebooks for result visualization.
