- Develop AWS Glue ETL jobs using the Deequ Spark library for daily data validation. Store ETL outputs in S3 and instruct analysts to use Amazon Athena for querying and visualizing data quality metrics.
- **Connect the S3 data lake as a data set in AWS Glue DataBrew and create a profiling job for evaluating data quality metrics.**
    
    AWS Glue DataBrew is a visual data preparation tool that helps data analysts with data preparation tasks such as data profiling, cleaning, and normalizing all without the need to write any code.
    
    You can create your own data quality rules to validate business requirements for specific datasets. These rules enable you to check for issues such as duplicate values in columns or to ensure uniqueness between columns. You can manage these rules by grouping them into rule sets and applying them during profile jobs. The system displays the results on a dashboard, offering a clear and concise view of how well the data complies with the set business rules.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/aws_glue_databrew_data_profiling.jpg)
    
    Since AWS Glue DataBrew is serverless, you do not need to provision or manage any infrastructure to use it. With AWS Glue DataBrew, you can easily and quickly create and execute data preparation recipes through a visual interface. The service automatically scales up or down based on the size and complexity of your data, so you only pay for the resources you use.
    
    Therefore, the correct answer is: **Connect the S3 data lake as a data set in AWS Glue DataBrew and create a profiling job for evaluating data quality metrics.**
    
    The option that says: **Implement AWS Kinesis Data Firehose for streaming S3 data lake contents to an Amazon OpenSearch cluster. Use OpenSearch Dashboards for visualizing data quality metrics** is incorrect. This approach involves the unnecessary complexity of streaming data from S3 using Kinesis Data Firehose when the data is already stored in the S3 data lake. Moreover, OpenSearch is more oriented towards real-time data streaming and visualization, making it an overkill solution for the scenario.
    
    The option says: **Develop AWS Glue ETL jobs using the Deequ Spark library for daily data validation. Store ETL outputs in S3 and instruct analysts to use Amazon Athena for querying and visualizing data quality metrics** is incorrect. Deequ is an open-source library built on top of Apache Spark to verify data quality at scale. While it excels at unit testing data for quality constraints, it’s not primarily tailored for visual data profiling.
    
    The option that says: **Set up an Amazon EMR cluster with Apache Spark and Apache Zeppelin. Conduct a daily transfer of S3 data to the cluster’s Hadoop Distributed File System (HDFS). Use Spark MLlib for quality checks and Zeppelin notebooks for result visualization** is incorrect. Deploying and managing an Amazon EMR cluster typically involves more administrative overhead. Additionally, using Spark’s MLlib for data quality assessments in this scenario introduces complexity that may be unnecessary, as the requirement is for visual data profiling and not machine learning.
    

A financial company has an Amazon S3 bucket that is used as a data lake for various cryptocurrency data sent from crypto exchanges across the globe. The DevOps team notices inconsistencies in data upload times across different regions.

The company aims to guarantee high availability and fast access to the S3 bucket data across all regions as well as ensure data sovereignty. The challenge lies in accomplishing these objectives with minimal management overhead.

Which set of actions can fulfill the requirements with the LEAST amount of management overhead?

- Use Amazon S3 Express One Zone storage class for the bucket and use S3 Cross-Region Replication (CRR) to replicate the data to buckets in each region.
- **Enable S3 Transfer Acceleration on the bucket and use S3 Cross-Region Replication (CRR) to replicate the data to buckets in each region.**
    
    **Amazon S3 Transfer Acceleration** is a bucket-level feature that enables fast, easy, and secure transfers of files over long distances between your client and your Amazon S3 bucket. It takes advantage of Amazon CloudFront’s globally distributed edge locations. As the data arrives at an edge location, data is routed to Amazon S3 over an optimized network path. This results in faster data transfers and reduced latency, which is crucial for the scenario’s requirement of fast access retrieval times.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/s3-transfer-acceleration-120823.png)
    
    **Amazon S3 Cross-Region Replication (CRR)** is a feature that automatically replicates data between buckets across different AWS Regions. This helps to reduce latency by allowing users to access a copy of the data that is closer to them. It also addresses the requirement of data sovereignty, as data is stored within the specific regions where the company operates.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/s3-crr-120823.png)
    
    Both S3 Transfer Acceleration and S3 Cross-Region Replication are fully managed services, which means that AWS handles all the heavy lifting, and you don’t have to worry about the operational burden of scaling, patching, and maintaining servers or software. This allows you to focus on your core business and applications, thereby fulfilling the requirement of the least amount of management overhead.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Enable S3 Transfer Acceleration on the bucket and use S3 Cross-Region Replication (CRR) to replicate the data to buckets in each region.**
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon S3 Express One Zone storage class for the bucket and use S3 Cross-Region Replication (CRR) to replicate the data to buckets in each region** is incorrect. Amazon S3 Express One Zone is a high-performance, single-Availability Zone storage class that’s purpose-built to deliver consistent single-digit millisecond data access for one specific Availability Zone only. This type of storage class won’t suit the scenario since the data sources are sending data from all over the globe. While S3 CRR can help with data sovereignty and availability, the use of S3 Express One Zone does not meet the requirement for fast transfer times from a group of global sources.
    
    The option that says: **Enable S3 Transfer Acceleration on the bucket and use AWS Direct Connect to establish a dedicated network connection from each region to AWS** is incorrect because while S3 Transfer Acceleration can improve access times, AWS Direct Connect does not address the requirement of data sovereignty as it does not replicate the data to different regions. AWS Direct Connect establishes a dedicated network connection from your premises to AWS, but it does not automatically replicate data across regions.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon S3 Express One Zone storage class for the bucket and use AWS Direct Connect to establish a dedicated network connection from each region to AWS** is incorrect because ****an Amazon S3 Express One Zone is not suitable for a solution where the data producers are sending files from different parts of the world. Take note that this particular storage class is only fast in a given Availability Zone and AWS Direct Connect does not address the requirement of data sovereignty as it does not replicate the data to different regions.
    
- Use Amazon S3 Express One Zone storage class for the bucket and use AWS Direct Connect to establish a dedicated network connection from each region to AWS.
- Enable S3 Transfer Acceleration on the bucket and use AWS Direct Connect to establish a dedicated network connection from each region to AWS.

A company has a data warehouse solution running in an Amazon Redshift cluster in AWS. The company’s data engineer runs the following query in Redshift:

`select current_schema(), userid from sales_data where order_date = '2025-01-01' limit 1;`

Upon running the query, there was an error in the output that says:

`INFO: Function "current_schema()" not supported`

`ERROR: Specified types or functions (one per INFO message) not supported on Redshift tables.`

What is the MOST likely root cause of this issue?

- **The `current_schema()` is a leader node–only function in Redshift and can’t be run with a query that references a Redshift `sales_data` user table that resides on the compute nodes.**
    
    Amazon Redshift has two types of nodes: **leader nodes** and **compute nodes**.
    
    **Compute Nodes**: These nodes store user-created tables and process queries. They handle data storage, filtering, and computations.
    
    **Leader Node**: This node manages query execution, distributes work to compute nodes, and stores system tables. It also manages external communication and aggregates the results from the compute nodes. Certain functions, including `current_schema()`, are **leader node–only functions**, meaning they can only be executed on the leader node.
    
    This node manages query execution, distributes work to compute nodes, and stores system tables.
    
    **System Tables**: Redshift has several system tables that store metadata and information about the cluster, databases, schemas, tables, and users. These tables are typically prefixed with “PG_” or “STV_”.
    
    **User-Defined Tables**: These are the tables created by users to store their data in Redshift.
    
    ![Redshift Compute Leader Node](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td_redshift_compute_leader_node_30-08-24.png)
    
    The sales_data table in the query is a user-created table, which resides on the compute nodes. Redshift does not allow leader node–only functions, like current_schema(), to be executed in queries that reference user-created tables on the compute nodes, as this would require the function to run on compute nodes, which is not supported.
    
    Therefore, the error occurs because the current_schema() function, which can only run on the leader node, is used in a query that references the sales_data user table, which is stored on the compute nodes.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **The `current_schema()` is a leader node–only function in Redshift and can’t be run with a query that references a Redshift `sales_data`user table that resides on the compute nodes.**
    
    The option that says: **The `current_schema()`is a compute node–only function in Redshift and can’t be run with a query that references a Redshift `sales_data` user table that resides on the leader nodes** is incorrect because the `current_schema` is actually a leader-only function and not for compute node-only.
    
    The option that says: **The `current_schema()`is a compute node–only function in Redshift and can’t be run with a query that references a Redshift `user_id` column that resides on the compute nodes** is incorrect. Similar to the previous option, the `current_schema` is actually a leader-only function and not for compute node-only. Additionally, the error message in the question refers primarily to the `sales_data` table, not a `user_id` table.
    
    The option that says: **The `current_schema()`is a leader node–only function in Redshift and can’t be run because it does not reference a table** is incorrect simply because the issue is not due to the absence of a table reference but because of the leader node function being used with a table from the compute nodes.
    
- The`current_schema()` is a compute node–only function in Redshift and can’t be run with a query that references a Redshift `user_id` column that resides on the compute nodes.
- The `current_schema()` is a compute node–only function in Redshift and can’t be run with a query that references a Redshift `sales_data` user table that resides on the leader nodes.
- The `current_schema()` is a leader node–only function in Redshift and can’t be run because it does not reference a table.

A retail company handles a large amount of data in their Amazon Redshift. The company wants to find all the product names that start with “TAD” or “BAG.” Additionally, they want to process the data, which will be used for creating reports in the fastest way possible.

What query would meet the requirements?

- `SELECT * FROM products WHERE product_name ~ '^TAD|BAG';`
- `SELECT * FROM products WHERE product_name !~ '^TAD|BAG';`
- `SELECT * FROM products WHERE product_name SIMILAR TO '(TAD|BAG)%';`
- **`SELECT * FROM products WHERE product_name LIKE 'TAD%' OR product_name LIKE 'BAG%';`**
    
    A pattern-matching operator searches a string for a pattern specified in the conditional expression and returns true or false depending on whether it finds a match. Amazon Redshift uses three methods for pattern matching:
    
    `LIKE` Operator where it compares a string expression, and its pattern-matching covers the entire string.
    
    `SIMILAR TO` Operator where it matches a string expression with a SQL standard regular expression pattern.
    
    `POSIX` Operator that provides a more powerful means for pattern matching since it can match any portion of the string and performs a case-sensitive match.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA1-24.png)
    
    Using `SIMILAR TO` or `POSIX` operators are computationally expensive when doing regular expression matching. Furthermore, `LIKE` the command performs way better compared to the other operators as it does not use `REGEX`, which causes potential drawbacks.
    
    Hence, The correct option is: **`SELECT * FROM products WHERE product_name LIKE 'TAD%' OR product_name LIKE 'BAG%';`**
    
    The option that says: **`SELECT * FROM products WHERE product_name ~ '^TAD|BAG';`** is incorrect. This query uses `POSIX` regex with the `~` operator to match product names. While this archives the same result as the `LIKE` operator, using `REGEX` is generally slower and computationally expensive.
    
    The option that says: **`SELECT * FROM products WHERE product_name SIMILAR TO '(TAD|BAG)%';`** is incorrect. This query uses the `SIMILAR TO` operator, an SQL standard, for pattern matching. However, this is less efficient and computationally expensive as this operator internally translates to a `REGEX`, causing performance drawbacks.
    
    The option that says: **`SELECT * FROM products WHERE product_name !~ '^TAD|BAG';`** is incorrect. This query uses the `POSIX` regex operator for negation pattern matching. This operator will find product names that do not match the specific pattern, making it incorrect to find patterns. Additionally, `REGEX`operators are less efficient for pattern matching than the `LIKE` operator.
    

A company operates a MySQL database in a data center. Recently, they have decided to replicate their data into an Amazon S3 bucket for analysis. The company must ensure that ongoing changes in its database are reflected in the target S3 bucket.

Which solution can satisfy the requirements? (Select TWO).

- Use CSV format when writing the data into the S3 bucket.
- Use AWS Lambda to trigger data replication on a daily basis from the MySQL database to the S3 bucket.
- **Use Apache Parquet format when writing the data into the S3 bucket.**
- **Use AWS Database Migration Service (DMS) to migrate the database to S3 with Full Load + change data capture (CDC).**
- Use AWS Database Migration Service (DMS) to migrate the database to S3 with change data capture (CDC) Only.
- **Evaluation**
    
    AWS Database Migration Service(AWS DMS) is a cloud service that makes it possible to migrate relational database, data warehouses, NoSQL databases, and other data stores.
    
    You can use AWS DMS to transfer data to Amazon S3 from any database source. When Amazon S3 is employed as a target in an AWS DMS task, both full load and change data capture(CDC) data are written to a comma-separated value (.csv) format by default. However, to ensure more compact storage and faster query performance, you may opt to have the data written in Apache Parquet(.parquet) format.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/awsdmsques.png)
    
    When using Full load + CDC in AWS DMZ Task, the task migrates existing data and then updates the target database based on changes to the source database.
    
    Hence, the correct options are:
    
    **– Use AWS Database Migration Service (DMS) to migrate the database to S3 with Full Load + CDC.**
    
    **– Use Apache Parquet format when writing the data into the S3 bucket.**
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Database Migration Service (DMS) to migrate the database to S3 with CDC Only** is incorrect it is because, by default, When using Amazon S3 as a target in an AWS DMS task, both full load and CDC data are used. However, choosing the CDC needs to pay more attention to the initial full load of the existing data.
    
    The option that says: **Use CSV format when writing the data into the S3 bucket** is incorrect. While you can use CSV format when writing the data, there are better choices for optimal query performance, especially when dealing with extensive data.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Lambda to trigger data replication on a daily basis from the MySQL database to the S3 bucket** is incorrect. While AWS Lambda can be used for data movement tasks, its batch-processing nature could lead to significant delays in reflecting the latest data changes. Additionally, the manual effort required to manage and scale the replication gives an additional amount of operational overhead.
    

A data engineer is designing a serverless data pipeline to process streaming JSON data and deliver it to a highly available, persistent storage. The data is being ingested into an Amazon Kinesis Data Stream. Before storage, the JSON data must be converted into a columnar format with a buffering interval of 60 seconds. The solution must enable data analysts to execute SQL queries directly on the transformed data and use existing Business Intelligence (BI) tools to visualize the results.

Which of the following solutions meets the requirements?

- Store the JSON records in Amazon S3. Trigger an AWS Lambda function through S3 Put events to transform the data into Apache ORC. Run queries directly on S3 with Amazon Athena. Set up Athena Java Database Connectivity (JDBC) connector to connect to the BI tool.
- Utilize Amazon Managed Streaming for Apache Kafka (Amazon MSK) to ingest the streaming data. Configure an MSK consumer to process and transform the data into Apache Parquet format, subsequently storing it in Amazon Redshift. Set up a Redshift Java Database Connectivity (JDBC) Driver to connect to the BI tool.
- **Use Amazon Data Firehose to stream and transform the JSON records to Apache Parquet. Deliver the results in Amazon S3. Define the schema of transformed data in an AWS Glue Data Catalog database. Run queries directly on S3 with Amazon Athena. Set up Athena Java Database Connectivity (JDBC) connector to connect to the BI tool.**
    
    **Amazon Data Firehose** is a fully managed service that delivers real-time streaming data to destinations such as Amazon S3, Amazon Redshift, Amazon OpenSearch Service, and others. It can automatically ingest and load streaming data into these data stores without any need for ETL processes.
    
    The **AWS Glue Data Catalog** is a centralized repository for structural and operational metadata for all of your data assets. You can save a dataset’s table definition and physical location, add business-relevant features, and track how the data has changed over time.
    
    The **Athena JDBC Connector** allows connecting BI tools, reporting tools or custom applications to Amazon Athena using standard JDBC. This enables running SQL queries and fetching results from Athena tables using common SQL tools without needing to use AWS CLI or SDKs.
    
    ![Amazon Data Firehose](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Amazon_Kinesis_Firehose_14March2024.png)
    
    To fulfill the requirement, the data engineer should utilize Amazon Data Firehose to stream JSON records from the Kinesis data stream to destinations like Amazon S3. This process seamlessly integrates with AWS Glue for schema definition and the transformation of data into the Parquet format without the need for custom coding, subsequently delivering the transformed data to an S3 bucket. The schema is efficiently managed in the AWS Glue Data Catalog, serving as a repository for metadata. Amazon Athena enables SQL querying of the data stored in S3, scalable for exabyte-scale analysis. Additionally, the Athena JDBC connector facilitates connectivity with existing Business Intelligence (BI) tools for visualization. This serverless approach streamlines the transformation to Parquet, effectively meeting the requirements.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use Amazon Data Firehose to stream and transform the JSON records to Apache Parquet. Deliver the results in Amazon S3. Define the schema of transformed data in an AWS Glue Data Catalog database. Run queries directly on S3 with Amazon Athena. Set up Athena Java Database Connectivity (JDBC) connector to connect to the BI tool.**
    
    The option that says: **Store the JSON records in Amazon S3. Trigger an AWS Lambda function through S3 Put events to transform the data into Apache ORC. Run queries directly on S3 with Amazon Athena. Set up Athena Java Database Connectivity (JDBC) connector to connect to the BI tool** is incorrect. This approach does not primarily support real-time processing as it requires data to be stored before transformation, and the asynchronous invocation of Lambda functions based on S3 events does not guarantee a fixed buffering interval.
    
    The option that says: **Store the JSON records in Amazon S3. Configure an S3 Event notification to invoke an AWS Glue job that uses Glue Parquet writer for converting data into Apache ORC. Load the result into an Amazon RDS PostgreSQL database. Set up a PostgreSQL Java Database Connectivity (JDBC) Driver to connect to the BI tool** is incorrect. While S3 event notifications can typically publish events to AWS Lambda, Amazon SNS, and Amazon SQS, it cannot directly trigger AWS Glue jobs. Moreover, loading data into RDS for querying with BI tools, though feasible, deviates from a serverless approach, potentially increasing operational complexity and cost.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize Amazon Managed Streaming for Apache Kafka (Amazon MSK) to ingest the streaming data. Configure an MSK consumer to process and transform the data into Apache Parquet format, subsequently storing it in Amazon Redshift. Set up a Redshift Java Database Connectivity (JDBC) Driver to connect to the BI tool** is incorrect. This architecture introduces complexities associated with managing Kafka and Redshift, which can increase operational overhead and cost. Moreover, this solution steps away from a purely serverless approach, as Redshift requires cluster management and scaling considerations.
    
- Store the JSON records in Amazon S3. Configure an S3 Event notification to invoke an AWS Glue job that uses Glue Parquet writer for converting data into Apache ORC. Load the result into an Amazon RDS PostgreSQL database. Set up a PostgreSQL Java Database Connectivity (JDBC) Driver to connect to the BI tool.

An e-commerce company has observed new accounts being created with minor alterations in names and emails based on a list of flagged accounts. Their existing system cannot detect these subtle variations. The updated account records are stored in an Amazon S3 bucket for inspection.

A data engineer must develop a solution for identifying accounts that could match a known suspicious account.

Which solution would meet the requirements?

- **Implement a `FindMatches` transform in AWS Glue to identify potentially fraudulent user accounts.**
    
    AWS Glue FindMatches Machine Learning (ML) Transform enables you to identify duplicate or matching records in your dataset, even when the records do not have a common unique identifier and no fields match exactly. This will not require writing any code or knowing how machine learning works.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/aws_glue_findmatches_ml_transform.png)
    
    FindMatches can be useful in many different problems, such as:
    
    **– Matching customers**: Linking customer records across different customer databases, even when many customer fields do not match exactly across the databases (e.g., different name spelling, address differences, missing or inaccurate data, etc).
    
    **– Matching products**: Matching products in your catalog against other product sources, such as product catalog against a competitor’s catalog, where entries are structured differently.
    
    **– Improving fraud detection**: Identifying duplicate customer accounts, determining when a newly created account is (or might be) a match for a previously known fraudulent user.
    
    **– Other matching problems**: Match addresses, movies, parts lists, etc. In general, if a human being could look at your database rows and determine that they were a match, there is a really good chance that the FindMatches transform can help you.
    
    In the scenario where there’s a need to detect subtle variations in names and emails from flagged data, using AWS Glue FindMatches Transform offers a direct solution. This would allow the company to spot discrepancies without the intricacies of setting up custom models or understanding how Machine Learning works. Custom machine learning can be expensive and tricky. With FindMatches, you avoid the time and cost of setting up and tuning your own model.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Implement a `FindMatches` transform in AWS Glue to identify potentially fraudulent user accounts.**
    
    The option that says: **Execute SQL queries via Amazon Athena using the `REGEXP_LIKE`function to search for keywords** is incorrect. While `REGEXP_LIKE` allows for pattern matching, it requires precise regex patterns to be effective. This approach could miss variations that don’t fit the predefined patterns and might not scale well for complex variations without extensive manual effort to update and maintain these patterns.
    
    The option that says: **Create an AWS Glue PySpark job. Use PySpark’s regular expression function to compare new accounts against the list of flagged accounts** is incorrect. Regular expressions work for pattern matching but might not be the best tool for catching subtle variations in data.
    
    The option that says: **Use Redshift Spectrum with the `SQL LIKE` operator to search for similarities in data** is incorrect. The `LIKE`operator works on patterns and is typically used for exact substrings or basic wildcard matching. When it comes to variations in names and emails, a pattern might not always capture all discrepancies. Thus, this approach might miss subtle variations and generate false negatives.
    
- Use Redshift Spectrum with the `SQL LIKE` operator to search for similarities in data.
- Execute SQL queries via Amazon Athena using the `REGEXP_LIKE` function to search for keywords.
- Create an AWS Glue PySpark job. Use PySpark’s regular expression function to compare new accounts against the list of flagged accounts.

A video streaming company is preparing a dataset to train a predictive model. The dataset contains null values, irrelevant data, and duplicates. The goal is to improve data quality so that the company can accurately classify subscribers into those likely to churn and those likely to stay.

Which solution can meet the company’s needs with LEAST operational overhead?

- Implement SageMaker Canvas for automatic data cleansing and organization. Configure a prediction model using multi-class text classification within SageMaker Canvas to label the data.
- Utilize SageMaker HyperPod for data cleansing, then apply a SageMaker built-in algorithm for the creation of a binary classification model.
- **Implement SageMaker Canvas's automated data cleansing and preparation features for optimal data structuring. Configure to use the built-in binary classification model within SageMaker Canvas for prediction.**
    
    **Amazon SageMaker** is a service that simplifies building, training, and deploying machine learning models. SageMaker is a platform that facilitates the creation of high-quality machine learning models by abstracting away the complexity typically associated with each process step. It offers an integrated Jupyter Notebook environment for data exploration and preprocessing, optimized and ready-to-use algorithms, and a fully managed deployment service. This enables practitioners to focus more on solving the problem than on the operational overhead of machine learning pipelines.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Amazon_SageMakerCanvas_for_BinaryClassification_7March2024.png)
    
    **SageMaker Canvas** is a feature of Amazon SageMaker that represents a no-code approach to machine learning. It makes machine learning accessible to users of all technical backgrounds. With a user-friendly interface, it automates data preparation, model selection, training, and deployment. This allows users to create predictive models without needing to write any code. Canvas simplifies the entire process of applying machine learning, from data cleaning to making predictions.
    
    In building a model, SageMaker Canvas offers different types of models, which are the following:
    
    **Numeric prediction** (*regression*) model is used to predict numeric data such as the price of houses based on features like square footage.
    
    **Categorical prediction** (*classification*) model is mainly used in categorizing data into groups, which also have two categorical prediction model types:
    
    **2 category prediction** (also known as *binary classification* in machine learning) is used when you have two categories you want to predict for your data, such as determining whether a customer will churn.
    
    **3+ category prediction** (*multi-class classification*) is used to predict three or more categories, such as a customer’s loan status based on past payments.
    
    **Time series forecasting** is useful for making predictions over a period of time, such as sales predictions for the next quarter.
    
    **Image prediction** (*single-label image classification*) model can be used for image labeling.
    
    **Text prediction** (*multi-class text classification*) If you have a collection of customer reviews for a particular product and you want to categorize the passages of text to determine whether customers had a positive, negative, or neutral opinion about the product, you can utilize this model to assign a label to each passage of text. This approach can help you analyze the sentiment of the reviews and gain valuable insights about the product’s performance.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Implement SageMaker Canvas’s automated data cleansing and preparation features for optimal data structuring. Configure to use the built-in binary classification model within SageMaker Canvas for prediction.**
    
    The option that says: **Implement SageMaker Canvas for automatic data cleansing and organization. Configure a prediction model using multi-class text classification within SageMaker Canvas to label the data** is incorrect. Although SageMaker Canvas is useful for data cleaning and organization, the primary function of multi-class text classification is to analyze labeled text data and predict labels for unlabeled text passages. This doesn’t align with the company’s requirement to categorize subscribers into two groups.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize SageMaker HyperPod for data cleansing, then apply a SageMaker built-in algorithm for the creation of a binary classification model** is incorrect. SageMaker HyperPod is only ideal for provisioning resilient clusters to run machine learning workloads and develop models like foundation models (FMs), large language models (LLMs), and diffusion models. Thus, it does not fulfill the company’s requirement to build a predictive model that will forecast subscriber discontinuation.
    
    The option that says: **Deploy SageMaker Data Wrangler for data cleansing, then use SageMaker Autopilot to train a binary classification model** is incorrect. SageMaker Data Wrangler and SageMaker Autopilot are indeed powerful tools for data preparation and model training. While these tools offer extensive functionality, they also introduce more overhead in terms of setting up, configuration, and operation. This can increase the effort and time required to develop the predictive model, unlike SageMaker Canvas, which offers a more streamlined, no-code solution for users who may not have deep technical expertise in machine learning.
    
- Deploy SageMaker Data Wrangler for data cleansing, then use SageMaker Autopilot to train a binary classification model.

A bank is currently using Amazon Redshift as its data warehouse. The IT Lead requires the Data Warehouse to prevent read and write access while inserting data into the Redshift.

How can the Data Engineer meet the above requirement?

- **Use a `LOCK` command at the beginning of a transaction before inserting data.**
    
    The `Lock` command restricts access to the database table. This command enables the acquisition of a table-level lock in “ACCESS EXCLUSIVE” mode. It waits, if necessary, for any conflicting locks to be released. Explicitly locking a table in this way causes other transactions or sessions to wait when attempting to read or write to the table.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/LockCommand.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use a `LOCK`command at the beginning of a transaction before inserting data.**
    
    The option that says: **Set up an IAM Policy to restrict access whenever inserting data into the Redshift** is incorrect. While setting up an IAM Policy for Redshift may restrict user access to the Redshift cluster, it does not explicitly target the prevention of read and write access during data insertion. IAM policies focus more on controlling who can access the Redshift cluster rather than managing access at the operation level during data insertion.
    
    The option that says: **Implement Serializable Isolation level for Inserting Transactions** is incorrect. Setting the transaction isolation level to `SERIALIZABLE` provide the highest isolation level by ensuring that transactions are entirely isolated. However, this does not explicitly lock tables for other users. It only guarantees that concurrent transactions are executed in a way as if they were serialized.
    
    The option that says: **Create a separate schema in Redshift dedicated to data-loading operations**is incorrect. While you can control access to the schema very tightly, This may increase the operational overhead instead of using the LOCK command in Redshift.
    
- Create a separate schema in Redshift dedicated to data-loading operations.
- Implement Serializable Isolation level for Inserting Transactions.
- Set up an IAM Policy to restrict access whenever inserting data into the Redshift.

A medical company manages patient records in an Amazon S3 bucket. The company is planning to implement a data lifecycle and retention policy for compliance purposes. The policy must follow the requirements below:

- Newly created records must allow immediate access for occasional queries.
- Records older than 5 years must be securely archived yet retrievable for legal and medical review within 24 hours.
- Records older than 15 years must be securely deleted from S3.

Which solution satisfies these requirements in the MOST cost-effective way? (SELECT TWO)

- **Create a lifecycle rule to migrate the records to S3 Glacier Flexible retrieval after 5 years. Create another rule to delete the records automatically after 15 years.**
- Create a lifecycle rule to migrate the records to Amazon S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval after 5 years. Create another rule to delete the records automatically after 15 years.
- Use Amazon S3 Standard Storage class for newly created records. Use Amazon Athena for querying records in place on S3.
- Use S3 Intelligent-Tiering to manage the records automatically based on access patterns.
- **Use Amazon S3 Infrequent Access storage class. Query the records in place on Amazon S3 using Amazon Athena.**
- **Evaluation**
    
    S3 Standard-Infrequent Access is designed for data accessed less frequently but requires rapid access when needed. It offers a lower storage price point than S3 Standard, making it an economical choice for storing data that are not accessed daily but are important enough to require rapid access when requested.
    
    Amazon Athena allows users to perform SQL queries directly on data stored in S3 without moving the data to a separate data analysis platform.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/S3tiersComparison.png)
    
    As records age, their access frequency typically decreases. Migrating older records to S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval after five years can significantly reduce storage costs while keeping the data accessible.
    
    Creating a lifecycle rule for data archiving and data retention will help reduce storage costs.
    
    Hence, the correct options are:
    
    **– Use Amazon S3 Infrequent Access storage class. Query the records on Amazon S3 using Amazon Athena.**
    
    **– Create a lifecycle rule to migrate the records to S3 Glacier Flexible retrieval after five years. Create another rule to delete the records automatically after 15 years.**
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon S3 Standard Storage class for newly created records. Use Amazon Athena for querying records in place on S3** is incorrect. While using S3 Standard Storage is a valid storage solution, it’s less cost-effective than S3 IA for storing data that are queried occasionally.
    
    The option that says: **Create a lifecycle rule to migrate the records to Amazon S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval after 5 years. Create another rule to delete the records automatically after 15 years** is incorrect. Although you can archive data to S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval and access it within milliseconds, the requirement in the scenario permits retrieval within a 5-hour window. With this grace period, you can opt for a much cheaper storage solution, like S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval.
    
    The option that says: **Use S3 Intelligent-Tiering to manage the records automatically based on access patterns** is incorrect. S3 Intelligent Tiering is more suitable for storing data with unknown access patterns. In the scenario, the data lifecycle and access patterns are clearly defined, eliminating the need for S3 Intelligent Tiering. Additionally, S3 Infrequent Access (IA) incurs monitoring and automation charges, potentially leading to additional costs.
    

A company has created an ETL Pipeline using Amazon Glue that loads data from S3 into a Redshift table. The Glue Job uses the `TRUNCATE` command to refresh the table before inserting new data. Things are running without issues until they are receiving jobs that are getting stuck. Upon investigation, the table is under an `AccessShareLock`, caused by unexpected queries from one of their applications.

Which of the steps can a Data Engineer use to resolve this problem? (SELECT TWO)

- **Use `pg_terminate_backend` to terminate the session that is holding the lock.**
- **Run a query to identify sessions that are holding locks.**
- Use the `EXPLAIN` command to analyze and optimize the query execution plan to prevent locks.
- Use the `REINDEX` command on the table to rebuild indexes and attempt to clear the lock.
- Use the `VACCUM` command to clean the table and release the lock.
- **Evaluation**
    
    Locking is a protection mechanism that controls how many sessions can access a table at the same time. Locking also determines which operations can be performed in those sessions. Most relational databases use row-level locks. However, Amazon Redshift uses table-level locks. You might experience locking conflicts if you perform frequent DDL statements on user tables or DML queries.
    
    Amazon Redshift has three lock modes:
    
    **AccessExclusiveLock:** Acquired primarily during DDL operations, such as `ALTER TABLE`, `DROP`, or `TRUNCATE`. AccessExclusiveLock blocks all other locking attempts.
    
    **AccessShareLock:** Acquired during `UNLOAD`, `SELECT`, `UPDATE`, or `DELETE` operations. AccessShareLock blocks only `AccessExclusiveLock`attempts. AccessShareLock doesn’t block other sessions that are trying to read or write on the table.
    
    **ShareRowExclusiveLock:** Acquired during `COPY`, `INSERT`, `UPDATE`, or `DELETE` operations. `ShareRowExclusiveLock` blocks `AccessExclusiveLock` and other `ShareRowExclusiveLock` attempts but doesn’t block `AccessShareLock`attempts.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA1-31.jpg)
    
    In this scenario, `TRUNCATE` is frequently executed on the table. DDL statements like `TRUNCATE` attempts an `AccessExclusiveLock` on a table. If a process tries to acquire `AccessExclusiveLock` on a table and there are ongoing queries that holds `AccessShareLock`, `TRUNCATE` would have to wait until the `AccessShareLock` is released, causing the Glue job to hang.
    
    To solve a locking problem, identify the session (PID) that is holding the lock and then terminate the session. If the session doesn’t terminate, reboot your cluster.
    
    Hence, the correct options are:
    
    **– Run a query to identify sessions that are holding locks.**
    
    **– Use `pg_terminate_backend` to terminate the session that is holding the lock.**
    
    The option that says: **Use the `VACCUM`command to clean the table and release the lock** is incorrect. `VACCUM` command Re-sorts rows and reclaims space in a specified table or all tables in the current database. While it improves query performance, it does not directly address or release active locks caused by rogue queries.
    
    The option that says: **Use the `EXPLAIN`command to analyze and optimize the query execution plan to prevent locks** is incorrect. This is because The `EXPLAIN` command is designed to help maximize query execution plans. However, this command focuses more on the preventive side, helping to identify performance issues before they occur. This option does not offer a solution for releasing the lock.
    
    The option that says: **Use the `REINDEX`command on the table to rebuild indexes and attempt to clear the lock** is incorrect. `REINDEX`rebuilds indexes to ensure efficient data retrieval. In contrast, this improves the performance of the database. I do not directly help with the issue of releasing the locks.
    

A company is using Amazon Athena to analyze three years’ worth of data totaling over 50 GB. The data is stored in Amazon S3 and is highly partitioned by date. However, as the size of data grows, the queries take longer to complete and often time out.

How can a Data Engineer resolve this issue with the LEAST operational overhead?

- Use Query Result Reuse in Athena to bypass the need to re-scan the data.
- Implement Athena User-Defined Functions(UDF) to incorporate custom logic into SQL queries.
- Use Athena Create Table As Select(CTAS) to aggregate and reorganize data into fewer, larger partitions.
- **Use Partition Projection in Athena to directly calculate which partition to access based on the query criteria.**
    
    Amazon Athena is an interactive query service that makes it easy to analyze data directly from Amazon S3 using Standard SQL. In partition projection, Athena calculates partition values and locations using the table properties that you configure directly on your table in AWS Glue.
    
    Creating a complex structure can slow down query processing due to the overhead of loading and parsing partition metadata; as mentioned, the data is partitioned by date across three years. By enabling partition projection, Athena can bypass the traditional metadata loading process. Instead, it will directly calculate which partition to access based on the query criteria using rules and patterns specified in the table definition.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA1-32%20(1).png)
    
    This method will drastically reduce the initiation time of the query. It will narrow the data scanned to only relevant partitions, leading to better query performance and eliminating timeouts with minimal operational overhead. This method will also negate the need for manually updating or maintaining partition metadata, offering a streamlined approach to handling large-scale, partitioned datasets.
    
    Hence, the best option is: **Use Partition Projection in Athena to directly calculate which partition to access based on the query criteria.**
    
    The option that says: **Implement Athena User-Defined Functions(UDF) to incorporate custom logic into SQL queries** is incorrect because Athena User-Defined Functions (UDFs) offer the flexibility of custom query processing. However, they do not inherently improve query performance on large datasets, mainly when dealing with partitioned data. Additionally, UDFs can add more complexity and operational overhead, resulting in more performance issues.
    
    The option that says: **Use Athena Create Table As Select(CTAS) to aggregate and reorganize data into fewer, larger partitions** is incorrect because Athena’s CTAS feature allows for creating optimized tables based on query results. However, this solution needs additional steps to manage the generated tables that do not directly solve the issue of slow queries due to the need to scan highly partitioned data. This solution will not improve the query performance with the least operational overhead.
    
    The option that says: **Use Query Result Reuse in Athena to bypass the need to re-scan the data** is incorrect because Query Result Reuse in Amazon Athena stores query outcomes, enabling faster retrieval for identical future queries by avoiding data re-scanning. This solution can speed up repeated queries on static data, offering cost and time savings. However, it doesn’t solve the initial slow query times caused by navigating a highly partitioned dataset. This solution will not improve the query performance with the least operational overhead.
    

A manufacturing company manages IoT devices that collect and store records on Amazon Timestream and Amazon Redshift. These databases contain a vast amount of crucial information that needs to be analyzed using SQL. However, the analysis process requires the company to combine or join data from both the Timestream and Redshift tables.

What solution meets the requirements with the LEAST operational overhead?

- **Use AWS Athena Federated Query to execute SQL queries that span both Amazon Timestream and Redshift tables.**
    
    Amazon Athena Federated Query allows you to query data from various sources using AWS Lambda-based connectors. The connectors integrate with data sources like Amazon CloudWatch Logs, DynamoDB, and relational databases like MySQL, enabling SQL queries to span different data locations.
    
    You can create custom connectors using the Athena Query Federation SDK, enhancing query flexibility and data access management. The connectors return data in Apache Arrow format, supporting multiple programming languages for broad compatibility.
    
    ![Amazon Athena](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/AmazonAthena.png)
    
    Athena Federated Query allows SQL queries across multiple data sources, including Amazon Timestream and Redshift. It uses AWS Lambda-based connectors to execute complex queries without data movement or replication. You can analyze IoT device data alongside corporate data stored in Redshift efficiently.
    
    Hence, the best option is: **Use AWS Athena Federated Query to execute SQL queries that span both Amazon Timestream and Redshift tables.**
    
    The option that says: **Use Redshift Federated Query to access and query data from Timestream and Redshift tables together** is incorrect because Redshift Federated Query allows you to query external databases directly. However, Amazon Timestream is not currently supported as a source since Timestream is a specialized time-series database requiring specific query capabilities not addressed by Redshift’s federated querying features. These features are more suitable for traditional relational databases accessed via JDBC.
    
    The option that says: **Use Redshift Spectrum to query data in Timestream and join it with the Redshift table** is incorrect because Redshift Spectrum extends Redshift queries to S3 data, allowing for the analysis of large volumes of data stored in S3 without importing it into Redshift. However, it does not facilitate querying data from Amazon Timestream. Therefore, it is impossible to directly join data from Timestream and Redshift tables as required for this IoT data analysis scenario.
    
    The option that says: **Export data from Amazon Timestream to Amazon S3. Then, use the `COPY` command to load it into the Amazon Redshift table. Query the newly imported data alongside existing tables within Redshift** is incorrect because this approach introduces significant operational overhead. It requires manual steps to export, store, and then load the data, making the process cumbersome and time-consuming. With Amazon Athena Federated Query, the data stays within their corresponding database and you can get to query them.
    
- Export data from Amazon Timestream to Amazon S3. Then, use the `COPY`command to load it into the Amazon Redshift table. Query the newly imported data alongside existing tables within Redshift.
- Use Redshift Federated Query to access and query data from Timestream and Redshift tables together.
- Use Redshift Spectrum to query data in Timestream and join it with the Redshift table.

A data engineer executes a weekly AWS Glue job, sourcing data from an Amazon S3 bucket. This bucket receives regular data uploads from a Lambda function, organized in a path format by year, month, and day.

The data engineer must create a solution to update the AWS Glue Data Catalog as soon as new partitions are added to the bucket.

Which solution will achieve this objective?

- **Implement a code within the Lambda function to automatically invoke the Boto3 `create_partition` API in AWS Glue.**
    
    **AWS Glue** is a managed ETL service that simplifies data preparation and analytics. The AWS Glue Data Catalog is a central repository for metadata regarding data utilized in ETL jobs within AWS Glue. This catalog is critical for managing the schema and structure of data in various storage mediums, including Amazon S3. Crawlers within AWS Glue are designed to scan data stores, identify data schema, and populate the Data Catalog with metadata tables describing the data, facilitating seamless ETL processes.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/AWS_GLUE_create_partition_March-7-202.png)
    
    Boto3, AWS’s SDK for Python, offers various methods to interact with AWS services programmatically, including AWS Glue. Through the Boto3 AWS Glue client, developers can directly invoke operations such as `create_partition`, allowing for real-time updates to the AWS Glue Data Catalog as soon as data is written to S3, without the need for manual intervention or waiting for scheduled crawlers.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Implement a code within the Lambda function to automatically invoke the Boto3 `create_partition` API in AWS Glue.**
    
    The option that says: **Configure a scheduled AWS Glue crawler that runs every day** is incorrect. This approach involves updating the AWS Glue Data Catalog at scheduled intervals, which may not meet the real-time needs after an ETL job. Although AWS Glue crawlers can effectively discover new and existing data in S3 and create or update the Data Catalog, the once-a-week schedule may cause a delay in reflecting new data partitions. This delay can affect the availability of data for analysis and querying.
    
    The option that says: **Set the `enableUpdateCatalog` option to `true`within the Glue job’s script** is incorrect. This option is only helpful if you want to update the Data Catalog with the output data from the job. It won’t automatically detect and add new partitions from the input data source.
    
    The option that says: **Run the `MSCK REPAIR TABLE` command directly from the Amazon Athena query editor after new data is added** is incorrect. While this command can repair and add new partitions to the Data Catalog, it is not an automated solution. Running this command manually from the Athena query editor requires manual intervention and may miss new partitions if not executed promptly after data is added to the S3 bucket.
    
- Configure a scheduled AWS Glue crawler that runs every day.
- Run the `MSCK REPAIR TABLE`command directly from the Amazon Athena query editor after new data is added.
- Set the `enableUpdateCatalog` option to `true` within the Glue job’s script.

An organization plans to ingest data from external software-as-a-service (SaaS) applications and store it in an Amazon S3 bucket. The organization will then analyze the data using Amazon Redshift.

Which AWS service would meet this requirement with the LEAST operational overhead?

- Amazon EventBridge
- AWS Step Functions
- Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (Amazon MWAA)
- **Amazon AppFlow**
    
    **Amazon AppFlow** is a fully managed integration service that enables secure, private data transfer between AWS services and SaaS applications with minimal operational overhead. It allows for the direct integration of SaaS applications like Salesforce, ServiceNow, Slack, and many others with AWS services.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/dea1-35-applow-diagram-from-aws-022724.png)
    
    Amazon AppFlow simplifies the process of securely transferring data between SaaS applications and AWS services, thereby significantly reducing the operational overhead associated with data integration tasks. It supports various data transformation capabilities, which means that data can be formatted and transformed as needed during the transfer process, ensuring that the data stored in Amazon S3 is ready for analysis in Amazon Redshift.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Amazon AppFlow.**
    
    The option that says: **Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (Amazon MWAA)**is incorrect because Amazon MWAA is a managed service for orchestrating complex workflows using Apache Airflow. It requires more operational overhead compared to Amazon AppFlow for the use case of simply transferring data from SaaS applications to AWS services.
    
    The option that says: **AWS Step Functions** is incorrect because AWS Step Functions is primarily used for coordinating multiple AWS services into serverless workflows. While it can handle complex workflows involving AWS services and external systems, it introduces unnecessary complexity and operational overhead for the specific task of transferring data from SaaS applications to Amazon S3.
    
    The option that says: **Amazon EventBridge** is incorrect because Amazon EventBridge is a serverless event bus service designed for application integration and event-driven computing. Although it can route data from various sources to AWS services, it is not specifically optimized for the direct transfer of data from SaaS applications to AWS services like Amazon AppFlow is.
    

A marketing analyst is utilizing Amazon Athena to analyze campaign performance data hosted on an S3 bucket. The analyst has created an external table in Athena to aggregate the data, containing metrics like campaign names, impressions, clicks, and orders. The aim is to pinpoint which marketing campaigns yielded the highest order counts and filter out any campaigns that generated less than 100 orders.

To achieve this objective, the analyst drafts the following initial query:

`SELECT campaign, SUM(orders) AS total_ordersFROM campaign_performanceGROUP BY campaign`

The analyst must ensure that only the top-performing campaigns are included in the final report.

Which adjustment should the analyst make to the query?

- Append `HAVING SUM(clicks) > 100`following the `GROUP BY` clause.
- **Append `HAVING SUM(orders) > 100`followed by `ORDER BY total_orders DESC;` after the GROUP BY clause.**
    
    **Data distribution in Amazon Athena** pertains to the organization of data stored in Amazon S3 when running interactive queries using standard SQL. Unlike traditional databases, Athena allows for the analysis of vast datasets without the need for database loading or server maintenance.
    
    The `HAVING` clause, when used in conjunction with the `GROUP BY` clause allows for the filtering of groups based on specific conditions applied to aggregate functions like `SUM`, `COUNT`, `AVG`, etc. This clause is applied after groups are formed, enabling the filtering of groups based on aggregated values. It’s important to note that any column referenced in the `HAVING`clause must be part of the `GROUP BY` clause or an aggregate function.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA1-36-having-clause-athena-query-030524.png)
    
    The correct strategy for refining the query to identify top-performing campaigns involves using the `HAVING` clause to filter grouped results based on the aggregate function `SUM(orders)`. The addition of `ORDER BY total_orders DESC;` ensures the presentation of results in descending order of total orders, facilitating a quick assessment of the highest-performing campaigns.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Append `HAVING SUM(orders) > 100` followed by `ORDER BY total_orders DESC;` after the `GROUP BY`clause.**
    
    The option that says: **Append `HAVING SUM(clicks) > 100` following the `GROUP BY` clause** is incorrect as it filters campaigns based on the number of clicks instead of orders, which does not align with the goal of measuring campaign success by orders generated.
    
    The option that says: **Include a `WHERE orders > 100` condition before the `GROUP BY`clause** is incorrect because the `WHERE` clause filters individual rows before aggregation, which is not suitable when filtering based on the sum of orders per campaign.
    
    The option that says: **Insert `LIMIT 100` at the end of the query to limit the number of results** is incorrect as this approach just limits the number of campaigns returned rather than filtering campaigns based on the sum of orders generated.
    
- Insert `LIMIT 100` at the end of the query to limit the number of results.
- Include a `WHERE orders > 100`condition before the `GROUP BY` clause.

A data engineer needs to perform a one-time, ad-hoc query to retrieve specific columns from a large dataset stored in an Amazon S3 bucket. The dataset contains 25 columns in Apache Parquet format. The solution should be efficient and avoid introducing any operational overhead or the need for managing new infrastructure

Which of the following solutions would fulfill this requirement?

- Configure an S3 Object Lambda access point that filters and retrieves only the necessary columns from the Parquet files.
- **Utilize Amazon Athena to perform `SQL SELECT` queries to fetch specific columns.**
    
    **Amazon Athena** is a user-friendly query service that allows users to analyze data stored in Amazon S3 by using familiar SQL queries. It operates in a serverless environment, which means there’s no need to manage any infrastructure, and users only pay for the queries they execute. This makes Athena a highly efficient and cost-effective solution for ad-hoc queries or one-time operations, as it automatically scales resources based on demand.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-athena-select-column-query-03-10-2024.png)
    
    By utilizing Amazon Athena, a data engineer can efficiently retrieve specific columns from a large Apache Parquet dataset stored in Amazon S3 without introducing additional operational overhead. This is particularly beneficial since Athena can query data directly from S3, eliminating the need for data loading or complex ETL processes. This feature significantly reduces the effort and time typically associated with moving or copying data to another storage or processing system.
    
    Moreover, Athena’s native support for the Apache Parquet columnar format makes it an ideal choice for analytical workloads. Parquet is optimized for retrieving specific columns, allowing data engineers to access the information they need quickly and efficiently. Additionally, since Athena uses standard SQL syntax, data engineers and analysts who are already familiar with SQL can easily query and analyze the data without having to learn a new query language or framework.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Utilize Amazon Athena to perform `SQL SELECT` queries to fetch specific columns.**
    
    The option that says: **Create a Lambda function to read S3 data and format it in a pandas dataframe. Use Python to run a `SQL SELECT`statement on the dataframe to query specific columns** is incorrect. This method requires setting up a Lambda function and writing code, which introduces operational overhead and may not be suitable for a one-time query operation. Additionally, processing large datasets within a Lambda function may not be efficient due to the typically limited resources and execution time constraints of Lambda functions.
    
    The option that says: **Configure an AWS Glue DataBrew project to ingest Apache Parquet files from the S3 bucket and perform column-specific queries** is incorrect. While DataBrew can ingest Parquet files from S3 and perform column-specific queries, configuring a project involves multiple steps and generating recipes. For a one-time column retrieval from Parquet in S3, DataBrew’s capabilities will only introduce unnecessary complexity and overhead compared to more direct solutions like Athena SQL queries.
    
    The option that says: **Configure an S3 Object Lambda access point that filters and retrieves only the necessary columns from the Parquet files** is incorrect. While S3 Object Lambda could retrieve specific columns, it typically serves for data transformation rather than querying specific columns directly and is not the most straightforward approach for ad-hoc querying.
    
- Create a Lambda function to read S3 data and format it in a pandas dataframe. Use Python to run a `SQL SELECT`statement on the dataframe to query specific columns.
- Configure an AWS Glue DataBrew project to ingest Apache Parquet files from the S3 bucket and perform column-specific queries.

A manufacturing company uses Amazon Redshift for its operational database and data warehousing. The company aims to streamline the process of updating its Amazon Redshift materialized views routinely through automation.

Which solution requires the MINIMAL operational overhead to fulfill this requirement?

- **Execute scheduled refresh commands using the query editor v2 in Amazon Redshift.**
    
    **Amazon Redshift** is a managed data warehousing service that allows easy data analysis using SQL and existing BI tools. Amazon Redshift enables you to process massive amounts of structured data with advanced query optimization, columnar storage, and parallel processing, making it easy to run complex analytic queries.
    
    Additionally, Amazon Redshift service allows users to create and manage materialized views, which are stored or cached views of a query’s result set for faster access. Redshift supports various automation options, including AWS services and external tools, to automate the updates of these views.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/TD_ScheduledRefresh_DEA-C01.png)
    
    The query editor v2 in Amazon Redshift can execute SQL commands directly within the Redshift console, including commands to refresh materialized views. These commands can be scheduled to execute automatically and can be set up directly in the Redshift environment. This provides a straightforward method to automate materialized view updates without additional infrastructure or services, thereby minimizing operational overhead.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Execute scheduled refresh commands using the query editor v2 in Amazon Redshift.**
    
    The option that says: **Implement an AWS Step Function workflow to orchestrate the automation of materialized view updates** is incorrect. AWS Step Functions is a powerful service for coordinating complex workflows involving multiple tasks and services, but it is not well-suited for running simple operations that occur entirely within Amazon Redshift. In this case, refreshing materialized views is a self-contained task within the Redshift environment, and using Step Functions would be an unnecessary overhead.
    
    The option that says: **Configure an AWS Glue workflow specifically to refresh the materialized views** is incorrect. While Glue can connect to Redshift as a data source or target, using it solely for refreshing materialized views would require additional coding, job setup, and potentially moving data in and out of Redshift, which adds operational complexity.
    
    The option that says: **Launch an AWS Lambda user-defined function (UDF) to trigger updates within Amazon Redshift for the materialized views** is incorrect. Using AWS Lambda to refresh materialized views in Amazon Redshift adds complexity and requires custom code development and maintenance. It is less operationally efficient than Redshift’s native scheduling capabilities.
    
- Launch an AWS Lambda user-defined function (UDF) to trigger updates within Amazon Redshift for the materialized views.
- Configure an AWS Glue workflow specifically to refresh the materialized views.
- Implement an AWS Step Function workflow to orchestrate the automation of materialized view updates.

A data engineer is building a pipeline in which an AWS Glue job first transforms data from a MySQL database and loads it into an S3 bucket. Following this, three Lambda functions process the data in parallel. The data engineer is looking for a way to orchestrate this workflow with the least amount of operational overhead.

Which would fulfill these requirements?

- Configure an Apache Airflow workflow on Amazon EC2. Design a directed acyclic graph (DAG) where the sequence begins with the invocation of the Lambda function and proceeds to the execution of the AWS Glue job.
- Configure an Apache Airflow workflow on Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (EKS). Design a directed acyclic graph (DAG) where the sequence begins with the invocation of the Glue job followed by the execution of the Lambda functions.
- **Create a state machine in AWS Step Functions to manage the execution sequence of the Glue job and Lambda functions.**
    
    AWS Step Functions allows the coordination of multiple AWS services into serverless workflows. By designing a state machine, AWS Step Functions can manage the execution order, dependency tracking, and error handling of the tasks, which in this scenario include an AWS Lambda function and an AWS Glue job. This service provides a visual interface to monitor the steps of the workflow, significantly reducing the management overhead compared to managing infrastructure or writing custom orchestration code.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/codepipeline_statemachine.png)
    
    In this scenario, the data engineer can create task states in Step Functions for the Glue job and Lambda functions. Step Functions has AWS SDK integration, allowing direct Glue API calls to run a Glue job for data transformation and to invoke multiple Lambda functions for data processing. You can configure a parallel state to execute the Lambda functions independently of each other concurrently. This approach significantly reduces operational complexity and accelerates the overall process.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create a state machine in AWS Step Functions to manage the execution sequence of the Glue job and Lambda functions.**
    
    The option that says: **Configure an Apache Airflow workflow on Amazon EC2. Design a directed acyclic graph (DAG) where the sequence begins with the invocation of the Lambda function and proceeds to the execution of the AWS Glue job** is incorrect. This approach introduces significant operational overhead by requiring the management of Apache Airflow on EC2 instances, which is not necessary when using AWS Step Functions.
    
    The option that says: **Create an AWS Glue workflow that orchestrates the sequence of initiating the Glue job and triggering the Lambda functions** is incorrect because Glue workflow is primarily designed for orchestrating Glue jobs and crawler schedules rather than coordinating external services like Lambda functions.
    
    The option that says: **Configure an Apache Airflow workflow on Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (EKS). Design a directed acyclic graph (DAG) where the sequence begins with the invocation of the Glue job followed by the execution of the Lambda functions** is incorrect. Deploying Apache Airflow on Amazon EKS introduces complexity primarily associated with Kubernetes management. Although Amazon EKS can simplify the management of containerized applications, using it just for workflow orchestration of external services is an overkill. Therefore, this option does not satisfy the requirements.
    
- Create an AWS Glue workflow that orchestrates the sequence of initiating the Glue job and triggering the Lambda functions.

A data engineering team is building a central data catalog and metadata management system for various data sources on AWS. The data includes semistructured and unstructured data formats, such as CSV logs, JSON files, .xml files, and NoSQL data in Amazon DocumentDB (with MongoDB compatibility). The team seeks an automated solution to continuously update the data catalog and monitor changes in source metadata with the least manual intervention.

Which solution requires the LEAST amount of operational overhead to fulfill these requirements?

- Configure AWS Glue jobs to extract schema details and store the schema data in Amazon S3.
- Implement MongoDB as the data catalog. Create a Lambda function to collect and update metadata in MongoDB. Schedule the function to run at regular intervals.
- Utilize Amazon DocumentDB (with MongoDB compatibility) as the data catalog and create AWS Lambda functions to collect and refresh metadata in the DocumentDB database. Set a cron job to execute the Lambda functions periodically.
- **Utilize the AWS Glue Data Catalog as the central metadata repository. Set up Glue crawlers on a schedule to regularly crawl the data sources and refresh the metadata catalog.**
    
    The **AWS Glue Data Catalog** is a fully managed, serverless, and cloud-optimized metadata repository that integrates seamlessly with other AWS services. It provides a unified view of all your data assets, making it easier to discover, search, and manage data across various AWS data stores.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-DEA1-40-GlueCrawlers-Mar-14-2024.png)
    
    AWS Glue crawlers automatically scan your data sources, extract schema information, and populate the Glue Data Catalog with metadata tables, which can be used for data discovery, extract, transform, and load (ETL) jobs, and query execution. This process significantly reduces manual efforts and ensures that your data catalog is always up-to-date with the latest schema changes.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Utilize the AWS Glue Data Catalog as the central metadata repository. Set up Glue crawlers on a schedule to regularly crawl the data sources and refresh the metadata catalog.**
    
    The option that says: **Implement MongoDB as the data catalog. Create a Lambda function to collect and update metadata in MongoDB. Schedule the function to run at regular intervals** is incorrect. This option requires custom development and manual setup for metadata management, which is less efficient than using AWS Glue’s automated capabilities.
    
    The option that says: **Configure AWS Glue jobs to extract schema details and store the schema data in Amazon S3** is incorrect. While this could work, it would require you to write custom scripts. This not only adds to the development effort but also to the maintenance effort, as any changes in data formats or structures would necessitate updates to your scripts. Glue jobs is more suitable for transformation jobs rather than inferring schema.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize Amazon DocumentDB (with MongoDB compatibility) as the data catalog and create AWS Lambda functions to collect and refresh metadata in the DocumentDB database. Set a cron job to execute the Lambda functions periodically** is incorrect. Amazon DocumentDB (with MongoDB compatiblity) is primarily a document database and not specialized for metadata management across various data sources. It would require more manual configuration and lacks the seamless integration with data cataloging features provided by AWS Glue.
    

A data engineering team manages a DynamoDB table configured with provisioned capacity mode. The table experiences a highly predictable traffic pattern, with peaks occurring at midnight every day. These peaks are followed by a period of low activity in the morning, which gradually transitions to normal levels for the remainder of the day.

The data engineering team wants to maintain the application’s performance during peak times and save costs during low usage periods.

Which solution meets this requirement?

- Update the table’s class to DynamoDB Standard Infrequent Access (DynamoDB Standard-IA).
- Switch to an on-demand capacity mode for the table.
- Set the provisioned capacity to match the highest observed demand during peak periods.
- **Update the table’s capacity to use AWS Application Auto Scaling based on minimum and maximum utilization.**
    
    **Amazon DynamoDB** allows for two capacity modes for managing throughput costs: provisioned and on-demand. In provisioned capacity mode, the user specifies the number of reads and writes per second as required by the application. This mode is cost-effective for predictable workloads where the throughput does not frequently change. On-demand capacity mode, on the other hand, offers flexible billing and automatically accommodates the table’s workload without requiring manual scaling.
    
    **AWS Application Auto Scaling** enables automatic adjustment of resources to maintain consistent, predictable performance at the lowest possible cost. It supports scheduling, which allows for capacity planning based on known usage patterns. This feature is particularly useful for applications with predictable load patterns.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA1-41-dynamodb-auto-scaling-030524.png)
    
    Implementing **AWS Application Auto Scaling for DynamoDB** allows for the dynamic adjustment of provisioned capacity to align with the application’s workload patterns, ensuring high performance during peak times and cost savings during off-peak times. This approach leverages the predictable nature of the workload, enhancing resource utilization efficiency without the need for manual intervention or the costs associated with maintaining excessive provisioned capacity.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Update the table’s capacity to use AWS Application Auto Scaling based on minimum and maximum utilization.**
    
    The option that says: **Set the provisioned capacity to match the highest observed demand during peak periods** is incorrect. In a provisioned capacity mode, you pay for the specified capacity regardless of actual usage. Therefore, matching capacity to peak demand without adjusting for periods of low activity results in paying for unneeded capacity during those lower periods, which is not cost-effective.
    
    The option that says: **Update the table’s class to DynamoDB Standard Infrequent Access (DynamoDB Standard-IA)** is incorrect. DynamoDB Standard-IA is mainly used in scenarios where data is accessed less frequently, offering lower storage costs while maintaining the same performance levels as the standard storage class. However, reading from and writing to these tables costs more than the Standard class, making it pricier for tables that are frequently used, such as the one in the scenario.
    
    The option that says: **Switch to an on-demand capacity mode for the table** is incorrect. The on-demand capacity mode charges for each read or write, unlike the provisioned mode, where you pay for a set capacity. For tables with predictable traffic, using provisioned capacity often works out cheaper. This is because you can set your capacity to cover the expected throughput, avoiding the costs tied to the per-action pricing of the on-demand mode.
    

An organization is migrating its Apache Hadoop cluster from a data center to an Amazon EMR cluster. They want to adopt a serverless architecture for managing their data catalog, which is currently managed through an Apache Hive metastore within their local Hadoop cluster. The metastore records must be stored in persistent data storage.

What is the MOST cost-effective solution to fulfill these requirements?

- Configure an Amazon Aurora MySQL database as an external Hive metastore for the EMR cluster.
- Launch a new Hive metastore in the EMR cluster and transfer existing on-premises data to it. Configure AWS DataSync for ongoing replication of the EMR Hive metastore changes to an Amazon S3 bucket.
- Migrate the on-premises Hive metastore to Amazon S3 using AWS Database Migration Service (AWS DMS).
- **Create AWS Data Catalog tables as an external Hive metastore for the EMR cluster.**
    
    In an Amazon EMR cluster, Hive records metastore information in a MySQL database on the primary node’s file system by default. The metastore contains a description of the table and the underlying data on which it is built, including the partition names, data types, and so on. When a cluster terminates, all cluster nodes shut down, including the primary node. When this happens, local data is lost because node file systems use ephemeral storage. If you need the metastore to persist, you must create an **external metastore**that exists outside the cluster.
    
    You have two options for an external metastore:
    
    **1. AWS Glue Data Catalog**
    
    **2. Amazon RDS or Amazon Aurora**
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/external-metastore-for-Hive-012624-1.png)
    
    The AWS Glue Data Catalog provides a centralized metadata repository for clients running Apache Hive and Apache Spark. This serverless repository eliminates the need for operational tasks like scaling, patching, and managing servers, which aligns well with the organization’s goal to adopt a serverless architecture. The AWS Glue Data Catalog can seamlessly integrate with Amazon EMR, providing a managed Hive metastore service that simplifies the process of cataloging, searching, and querying metadata.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create AWS Data Catalog tables as an external Hive metastore for the EMR cluster.**
