    The option that says: **Migrate the on-premises Hive metastore to Amazon S3 using AWS Database Migration Service (AWS DMS)** is incorrect. Amazon S3 can store Hive data, but using it as a standalone metastore is not standard practice. This is because S3, as an object storage service, lacks native support for database-like operations, schema management, and querying capabilities that are typical of a metastore. Metastores depend on structured schema information to manage and query metadata about the data stored, usually in relational databases or specialized metadata management services like the AWS Glue Data Catalog.
    
    The option that says: **Configure an Amazon Aurora MySQL database as an external Hive metastore for the EMR cluster** is incorrect. While Amazon Aurora MySQL is a valid external metastore, it requires managing a database instance, which contradicts the requirement of a serverless solution.
    
    The option that says: **Launch a new Hive metastore in the EMR cluster and transfer existing on-premises data to it. Configure AWS DataSync for ongoing replication of the EMR Hive metastore changes to an Amazon S3 bucket**is incorrect. Local Hive metastores within EMR clusters are vulnerable to data loss during node failures, making them not a persistent storage solution. Moreover, AWS DataSync can only monitor and synchronize file changes, not database changes.
    

A company uses an Amazon Redshift cluster with KEY distribution for its data warehouse. This cluster has multiple nodes, but one node is using more CPU than the others, which have normal CPU usage during business hours.

Due to a limited budget, the data engineering team seeks a solution to distribute the workload evenly across the cluster without increasing the number of nodes.

Which adjustment should the data engineering team make to achieve the desired distribution?

- **Identify the largest dimension table and alter its distribution key to `EVEN`.**
    
    **Data distribution in Amazon Redshift** refers to how data is stored across the nodes and slices within a cluster. When you load data into a Redshift table, the rows are distributed and stored across these slices according to the table’s distribution style.
    
    **KEY distribution styles:** The rows are distributed based on the values in a specified distribution key column. Rows with the same value are stored together. This helps optimize queries that filter or join on the distribution key column.
    
    The different key concepts in Amazon Redshift are:
    
    **Distribution key:** This column is used to distribute the rows across nodes in the cluster. It is specified when creating a table using the `DISTKEY` clause.
    
    **Sort key:** This column is used to order or sort the rows stored on each node. It is specified using the `SORTKEY` clause. Multiple columns can be used as sort keys.
    
    **Primary key:** This uniquely identifies each row in the table. It consists of the distribution key as the partition key and can optionally include a sort key.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA-1-43-KEY-DISTSTYLE-022724.jpg)
    
    The data engineering team should adjust the distribution key to correct the load imbalance across the cluster nodes. The aim is to achieve a more balanced distribution of queries and storage across all nodes to prevent any single node from becoming a bottleneck. A well-chosen `DISTKEY` ensures that the data is distributed uniformly across all slices of each node, which helps in achieving an even distribution of query load. If the current `DISTKEY` is leading to high CPU usage, it suggests that it is not evenly distributing the data due to its low cardinality or because it doesn’t align well with the query patterns. Choosing a column with a more evenly distributed range of values for the `DISTKEY`can solve this issue.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Identify the largest dimension table and alter its distribution key to `EVEN`.**
    
    The option that says: **Use columns that are frequently used in `JOIN` conditions as the sort key** is incorrect. Sort keys optimize query performance by determining the order of data stored and are mainly used to improve the speed of range-restricted queries, not to balance CPU load across nodes. Therefore, adjusting sort keys would not address the issue of uneven distribution.
    
    The option that says: **Scale up the size of the node experiencing high CPU utilization** is incorrect because scaling up a single node is not possible in Redshift, as the cluster’s nodes are uniform. Moreover, the aim is to balance the load without changing the node count or their configurations.
    
    The option that says: **Identify the column that is most commonly referenced in the `WHERE`clause of `SELECT` statements. Then, assign this column as the new primary key** is incorrect. This would only improve query performance, not necessarily solve the uneven distribution of workload across the cluster.
    
- Identify the column that is most commonly referenced in the `WHERE`clause of `SELECT` statements. Then, assign this column as the new primary key
- Use columns that are frequently used in `JOIN` conditions as the sort key.
- Scale up the size of the node experiencing high CPU utilization.

A company’s data engineering team needs to process and analyze terabytes of IoT data stored in Amazon S3. The structure of the data changes periodically as often as the IoT device gets upgraded.

The team is required to find a solution that includes data cataloging and schema management to ensure consistent access and analysis capabilities.

Which option satisfies the mentioned requirements in the MOST cost-effective manner?

- Set up an Amazon Athena workgroup. Leverage Apache Spark through Athena for data exploration in S3, granting the team access to workgroup schemas and tables.
- Set up AWS Glue Data Catalog, Schema Registry, and Lambda functions with Redshift Data API. Manage data ingestion into Amazon Redshift Serverless via AWS Step Functions.
- **Set up an AWS Glue Data Catalog. Define a schema using AWS Glue Schema Registry, and orchestrate data ingestion into Amazon Redshift Serverless via AWS Glue workflows.**
    
    **AWS Glue** is a service that facilitates the extraction, transformation, and loading (ETL) of data to prepare it for analytics. It is fully managed and offers visual workflows along with a Python library to easily perform data transformations at scale.
    
    The **AWS Glue Data Catalog** is a centralized metadata repository that stores table definitions, physical locations of data, and business attributes and tracks change over time. It provides a unified interface to query metadata across data sources. When AWS Glue ETL jobs run, they use the data catalog to understand data formats and ensure correct transformations.
    
    The **AWS Glue Schema Registry** tracks schema changes to tables in the data catalog. It allows schemas to evolve over time while maintaining compatibility with existing analytics, queries, and applications. This enables schema-on-read for analytics tools like Athena, EMR, and Redshift Spectrum.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA1-44-awsglue-data-catalog-030624.png)
    
    **Amazon Redshift Serverless** is a cloud-based, fully managed data warehouse service that allows you to easily analyze your data using standard SQL and existing BI tools. It’s designed to be fast and cost-effective, making it ideal for petabyte-scale data analysis.
    
    To meet the requirements for cost-effective data cataloging, schema management, and analytics, the recommended solution involves utilizing the AWS Glue Data Catalog for organizing and querying vast amounts of dynamic IoT data stored in S3, ensuring consistent access for analysis. The AWS Glue Schema Registry is employed to manage evolving schemas of IoT data in a version-controlled manner during device upgrades. AWS Glue workflows orchestrate the periodic ingestion of data from S3 into Amazon Redshift Serverless, enabling scalable analytics capabilities without the need for provisioning and managing dedicated Redshift clusters. With Redshift Serverless, customers benefit from a pay-for-what-you-use model, which is more cost-effective for handling large and fluctuating IoT datasets. This entire serverless solution leverages native AWS services, minimizing both cost and complexity by eliminating the need for additional infrastructure setup and maintenance.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Set up an AWS Glue Data Catalog. Define a schema using AWS Glue Schema Registry, and orchestrate data ingestion into Amazon Redshift Serverless via AWS Glue workflows.**
    
    The option that says: **Set up an Amazon Redshift provisioned cluster. Leverage Amazon Redshift Spectrum to query data in S3, and handle data ingestion via Redshift stored procedures** is incorrect. This option involves unnecessary complexity and higher costs associated with managing a provisioned cluster and does not leverage the serverless capabilities that can lead to cost savings.
    
    The option that says: **Set up an Amazon Athena workgroup. Leverage Apache Spark through Athena for data exploration in S3, granting the team access to workgroup schemas and tables** is incorrect. While Athena provides serverless querying capabilities, this option lacks the comprehensive data management and schema consistency features provided by AWS Glue Schema Registry and Data Catalog, which are essential for handling evolving data structures efficiently.
    
    The option that says: **Set up AWS Glue Data Catalog, Schema Registry, and Lambda functions with Redshift Data API. Manage data ingestion into Amazon Redshift Serverless via AWS Step Functions** is incorrect. This option introduces unnecessary components like AWS Lambda functions for data ingestion into Amazon Redshift Serverless and AWS Step Functions for orchestration. While these services can be used, they require custom code development, maintenance, and incur additional costs for execution and data transfer.
    
- Set up an Amazon Redshift provisioned cluster. Leverage Amazon Redshift Spectrum to query data in S3, and handle data ingestion via Redshift stored procedures.

An organization uses an Amazon S3 bucket located in the `us-east-1` region to store legal documents. The organization wants to log all write operations performed on this bucket. These logs must be stored in a separate S3 bucket, also in the `us-east-1`region.

How can this be achieved in the LEAST operational effort?

- Track write-only management events in AWS CloudTrail from the source S3 bucket. Filter them without a specific prefix and send the logs to the destination S3 bucket.
- **In AWS CloudTrail, configure a trail that logs write-only data events from the source S3 bucket. Apply filters to capture events from the source bucket's Arn and direct these logs to the destination S3 bucket.**
    
    **AWS CloudTrail** enables auditing, security monitoring, and operational troubleshooting by tracking user activity and API usage. CloudTrail logs, continuously monitors, and retains account activity related to actions across your AWS infrastructure, giving you control over storage, analysis, and remediation actions.
    
    AWS CloudTrail’s **management events** provide visibility into management or control plane operations that are performed on AWS resources, such as creating, modifying, or deleting resources. This includes API calls and console sign-ins. Management events are enabled by default when you create a trail.
    
    AWS CloudTrail’s **data events** (also known as “data plane operations”) provide insights into resource or data plane operations performed within resources, such as S3 object uploads and downloads or Lambda function executions. These are often high-volume activities. Data events are disabled by default, and you need to explicitly configure the trail to record them for supported resources. Recording data events incur additional costs.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/s3-data-events-022624.png)
    
    Given the scenario’s requirement for minimal operational effort and direct logging of write operations to another S3 bucket in the same region, the best solution is to utilize AWS CloudTrail’s data event logging capabilities. This approach automates the logging process directly, without the need for intermediary services or additional programming, aligning perfectly with the need for operational simplicity and direct logging.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **In AWS CloudTrail, configure a trail that logs write-only data events from the source S3 bucket. Apply filters to capture events from the source bucket’s Arn and direct these logs to the destination S3 bucket.**
    
    The option that says: **Create an S3 Event Notification that triggers a Lambda function in response to write-related activities on the source S3 bucket. Configure the Lambda function to write events to a Kinesis Data Firehose steam, which then saves logs into the designated S3 bucket** is incorrect. It introduces unnecessary complexity and operational overhead in writing and maintaining the Lambda function, compared to the direct logging capability provided by CloudTrail.
    
    The option that says: **Track write-only management events in AWS CloudTrail from the source S3 bucket. Filter them without a specific prefix and send the logs to the destination S3 bucket** is incorrect because management events do not include the detailed data-level operations (such as write operations) that are captured by data events, making it less suitable for this specific logging requirement.
    
    The option that says: **Create an S3 Event Notifications to trigger a Lambda function for all actions on the source S3 bucket and directly log events into the destination S3 bucket** is incorrect. While operationally simple, it does not leverage the built-in capabilities of CloudTrail for direct and automated logging of S3 bucket activities, thus not fulfilling the requirement as effectively as the CloudTrail data events option.
    
- Create an S3 Event Notification that triggers a Lambda function in response to write-related activities on the source S3 bucket. Configure the Lambda function to write events to a Kinesis Data Firehose steam, which then saves logs into the designated S3 bucket.
- Create an S3 Event Notifications to trigger a Lambda function for all actions on the source S3 bucket and directly log events into the destination S3 bucket.

A data engineer is using a MySQL database as a central metadata store. This database holds Hive records, which serve as the schema for SQL queries run in Amazon Athena and processes on Amazon EMR. The data engineer wants to migrate the metadata store to a solution requiring less operational effort.

Which would meet this requirement?

- Leverage the local Hive metastore on an EMR cluster.
- Provisioned AWS Lake Formation together with Amazon EMR.
- Create a metastore on an Amazon Aurora for MySQL DB instance.
- **Use AWS Glue Data Catalog as the central metadata repository.**
    
    The **AWS Glue Data Catalog** is a central metadata repository, encompassing structural and operational metadata for data assets across various data sources. It provides a consolidated interface for maintaining and accessing information about data formats, schemas, and origins. When running an AWS Glue ETL (Extract, Transform, Load) job, this catalog is employed to understand data details, ensuring the precision of the data transformation process.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/AWS_Glue_Data_Catalog_table_15March2024.png)
    
    In the scenario, by using AWS Glue Data Catalog as the central metadata repository, you eliminate the need to manage separate metastores or manually configure connections, reducing operational overhead. As a managed service, AWS Glue Data Catalog provides scalability, reliability, and security out of the box, further decreasing the administrative effort required from data engineers.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use AWS Glue Data Catalog as the central metadata repository.**
    
    The option that says: **Provisioned AWS Lake Formation together with Amazon EMR** is incorrect because AWS Lake Formation is just used to simplify and secure your data lake. While it integrates with Amazon EMR and provides capabilities for managing metadata, its primary focus is on setting up, securing, and managing data lakes rather than serving as a dedicated metadata repository. It’s more about data lake governance and less about being a direct solution for managing Hive metadata specifically.
    
    The option that says: **Leverage the local Hive metastore on an EMR cluster** is incorrect. This approach ties the metadata management closely to the lifecycle of the EMR cluster. This would require managing the EMR cluster’s scaling, security, and availability, which can increase operational effort rather than reduce it.
    
    The option that says: **Create a metastore on an Amazon Aurora for MySQL DB instance** is incorrect. While Amazon Aurora offers an improved, managed database solution compared to standard MySQL installations with high availability and scalability, it still requires operational effort in terms of database management, scaling, and maintenance. This option might reduce some operational efforts but does not fully leverage the serverless and integrated capabilities of AWS services designed for metadata management.
    

A financial company is developing a data lake using Amazon S3. The company plans to utilize services such as Amazon Athena, Amazon Elastic MapReduce (EMR), and Amazon Redshift Spectrum to meet its data analysis needs.

The company has several departments that need access to the data lake. Each department should only access parts of the data relevant to their work, with permissions set at both the column and row levels to meet their specific needs.

How can the company meet this requirement in the LEAST amount of operational overhead?

- Utilize Apache Ranger through Amazon Elastic MapReduce (EMR) to manage access at the row and column level. Integrate Amazon S3 for data lake storage and provide data access via Apache Pig.
- **Utilize Amazon S3 for data lake storage and leverage AWS Lake Formation for row and column-level access control. Enable data access via AWS Lake Formation.**
    
    **AWS Lake Formation is a central hub on Amazon S3 for storing structured and unstructured data until needed. It manages permissions and access for data stored in S3. Lake Formation provides granular control over data access and a unified Storage API for secure data operations. Key features include encryption, private endpoints, and audit logs for enhanced security and isolation of the data lake within S3.**
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/dea1-47-data-lake%20diagram-022624.jpg)
    
    Lake Formation also offers centralized data governance through the AWS Glue Data Catalog, employing row and column-level access controls for precise access management. This ensures fine-grained control over data accessibility. Amazon Athena and Redshift Spectrum can then query S3 data while adhering to Lake Formation’s permissions, thereby maintaining proper access control. Apache Pig on Amazon ElasticMap Reduce can also leverage Lake Formation permissions to securely access data from the S3 data lake. Lastly, integration with Apache Ranger enables centralized access management, where Ranger policies are applied to data access via Apache Pig, and other tools.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Utilize Amazon S3 for data lake storage and leverage AWS Lake Formation for row and column-level access control. Enable data access via AWS Lake Formation.**
    
    The option that says: **Utilize Amazon S3 for the data lake’s storage and implement S3 access policies for row and column access restrictions. Facilitate data access directly via Amazon S3** is incorrect because S3 access policies alone cannot provide the granular row-level and column-level access control required by the scenario. While S3 offers robust data storage capabilities, it lacks the finer access controls available through AWS Lake Formation, which are essential for detailed data access management as described.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize Apache Ranger through Amazon Elastic MapReduce (EMR) to manage access at the row and column level. Integrate Amazon S3 for data lake storage and provide data access via Apache Pig** is incorrect. Apache Ranger manages access in Hadoop environments but doesn’t fully utilize AWS’s capabilities for managing data lake security. AWS Lake Formation provides centralized access control across multiple services, making it a better choice.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize Amazon Redshift for data lake storage with Redshift policies for access control. Access data via Apache Spark and Amazon Athena federated queries** is incorrect. This option is not aligned with the scenario’s focus on building a data lake with Amazon S3 and managing access with AWS Lake Formation. Although Amazon Redshift is a powerful data warehousing solution, it is not intended to serve as the primary storage for a data lake. Furthermore, using Redshift for data lake storage would not provide the same level of granular access control or the ease of management offered by AWS Lake Formation when used in conjunction with Amazon S3.
    
- Utilize Amazon S3 for the data lake's storage and implement S3 access policies for row and column access restrictions. Facilitate data access directly via Amazon S3.
- Utilize Amazon Redshift for data lake storage with Redshift policies for access control. Access data via Apache Spark and Amazon Athena federated queries.

A logistics company analyzes transportation and delivery data to optimize route planning and improve delivery times. For its testing phase, the company uses Amazon S3 to store logistics metrics in .csv format and leverages Amazon Athena for data querying. The datasets are organized by date within the S3 bucket. As data volumes grow, the company seeks strategies to optimize query performance and storage efficiency.

Which two actions should be taken to meet these requirements most effectively? (Select TWO)

- Store the .csv files in S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval. Add randomized string as a prefix for S3 objects.
- **Reformat the .csv data into a more query-efficient format like Apache Parquet. Use the predicate pushdown technique for efficient data retrieval.**
- Implement a data lake architecture using AWS Lake Formation to automate data cataloging and optimize query execution.
- **Ensure the AWS region where the S3 bucket is located is the same as the region where the Athena queries are run.**
- Apply a lifecycle policy to the S3 bucket to archive older .csv files to S3 Glacier for cost savings.
- **Evaluation**
    
    **Amazon Athena** provides an interactive query service, allowing users to examine information in Amazon S3 using conventional SQL. With Athena, you can point to your data stored in S3 and begin using standard SQL to run ad-hoc queries and get results. Athena is serverless, so you have no infrastructure to set up or manage.
    
    ![Amazon Athena - Query editor](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Amazon_Athena_14March2024.png)
    
    The two actions that should be taken to meet the requirements most effectively are: Converting CSV data into a columnar format such as Apache Parquet significantly improves performance. This conversion enables Athena to process queries more efficiently by reading only the necessary columns, thereby reducing the amount of data scanned and lowering costs.
    
    Secondly, ensuring that the AWS region for the S3 data storage matches the region where Athena queries are executed minimizes network latency, leading to faster query performance. These approaches underscore the importance of strategic data organization and regional considerations in maximizing the cost-effectiveness and scalability of data analytics using AWS Athena and S3, enabling a more streamlined and effective management of large data sets and complex analytics operations.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Ensure the AWS region where the S3 bucket is located is the same as the region where the Athena queries are run.**
    
    **– Reformat the .csv data into a more query-efficient format like Apache Parquet. Use the predicate pushdown technique for efficient data retrieval.**
    
    The option that says: **Implement a data lake architecture using AWS Lake Formation to automate data cataloging and optimize query execution** is incorrect because it only simplifies the setup and management of a secure data lake, including data cataloging and optimization of query execution; it might introduce additional complexity and overhead for an application focused on enhancing operational efficiency. Therefore, this solution does not address the specific need for query performance optimization and storage efficiency in the scenario described.
    
    The option that says: **Store the .csv files in S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval. Add randomized string as a prefix for S3 objects** is incorrect. S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval is primarily designed for data that is accessed less frequently but requires rapid access when needed. While it can help reduce storage costs, it incurs higher retrieval costs compared to the Standard S3 storage class. Adding a custom prefix for S3 objects can provide some level of performance improvement but not as effective as reformatting the data into a more query-efficient format like Apache Parquet.
    
    The option that says: **Apply a lifecycle policy to the S3 bucket to archive older .csv files to S3 Glacier for cost savings** is incorrect. Using S3 Glacier means that you would have to restore the data before it can be queried, which adds a significant delay and complexity to the process. This approach is not conducive to optimizing query performance; it’s primarily a cost-saving measure for data that is rarely accessed.
    

An e-commerce company is experiencing a significant increase in sales during the holiday season. Due to this surge, database queries processing customer orders are taking longer than usual. This is causing delays in the checkout process.

The company’s primary database is hosted on Amazon RDS for SQL server, and the workload is write-intensive. To provide customers with a better checkout experience, the company needs to address the high CPU utilization of their RDS instance.

Which possible solutions can help solve the issue effectively? (Select TWO.)

- Add more tables and indexes by altering the database structure.
- Schedule weekly manual reboots of the RDS DB instance.
- **Transition to a DB instance with greater CPU and memory resources.**
- **Identify and optimize high CPU usage queries using Amazon RDS Performance Insights.**
- Cache the database queries to reduce loading time.
- **Evaluation**
    
    **Amazon RDS Performance Insights** is a useful feature that gathers and analyzes important performance metrics from an Amazon RDS database instance, such as database load and SQL queries executed. It presents these metrics in an easy-to-understand visual format over a period of time to help you analyze your database load and identify any potential bottlenecks. To use Performance Insights, you must enable it when creating an RDS instance. By default, it provides seven days of metric history.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/dea1-49-rds-performance-insight-030624.png)
    
    An instance upgrade in RDS is the process of upgrading the underlying database engine or version of an existing RDS database instance. It is important to upgrade instances for the following reasons:
    
    Newer versions provide bug fixes, security enhancements, performance improvements, and new features introduced by the database vendor. This helps keep the database secure and optimized over time.
    
    Upgrading helps maintain support status as database vendors stop supporting older versions after a certain time period.
    
    Future-proofing applications by upgrading to a version compatible with planned features in newer RDS instances or the database engine.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/dea1-49-rds-instance-upgrade-030624.png)
    
    The company should first optimize the problematic queries identified using Performance Insights. However, since this is a write-intensive workload during peak season, upgrading the RDS instance to a larger size would also be needed to scale up the database capacity and meet the demands of processing higher order volumes. Together, these solutions can help improve database performance and provide customers with a seamless checkout experience.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Identify and optimize high CPU usage queries using Amazon RDS Performance Insights.**
    
    **– Transition to a DB instance with greater CPU and memory resources.**
    
    The option that says: **Add more tables and indexes by altering the database structure** is incorrect. While indexing can help improve read query performance, it’s not beneficial for write-intensive workloads. In fact, excessive indexing can lead to increased overhead during insert, update, and delete operations, potentially aggravating CPU and write latency issues.
    
    The option that says: **Schedule weekly manual reboots of the RDS DB instance** is incorrect. Regularly rebooting the database instance does not directly impact long-term CPU utilization trends. While it might temporarily alleviate some issues, it does not address the root cause of high CPU usage.
    
    The option that says: **Cache the database queries to reduce loading time** is incorrect. Caching is generally more beneficial for read-heavy workloads. Since the database in question is write-intensive, caching would have a negligible impact on reducing CPU utilization.
    

A company needs to optimize the performance of an Amazon Redshift table containing two years of customer purchase data. Currently, the table uses an interleaved sort key on the `country_id` column. Due to weekly data additions, the distribution of data has become uneven, leading to slower queries and less available storage.

Which `VACUUM` command will effectively reclaim disk space and improve query performance?

- `VACUUM SORT ONLY`
- `VACUUM DELETE ONLY`
- `VACUUM FULL`
- **`VACUUM REINDEX`**
    
    **VACUUM** is a command in Amazon Redshift that optimizes query performance by reclaiming disk space from deleted or updated rows, rebuilding indexes, and reorganizing tables. The main VACUUM commands are:
    
    **`VACUUM FULL`** – Reclaims disk space, rebuilds indexes, and re-sorts all rows in the table. This is the default `VACUUM` operation in Redshift.
    
    **`VACUUM DELETE ONLY`** – Only reclaims disk space occupied by deleted rows without re-sorting data. This is faster than VACUUM FULL but does not optimize query performance.
    
    **`VACUUM REINDEX`** – Analyzes the distribution of values in interleaved sort key columns and performs a full VACUUM operation, including re-sorting. Used when interleaved sort keys are skewed.
    
    **`VACUUM SORT ONLY`** – This command performs a sort-only operation on the table without reclaiming disk space. It is useful when rows are unsorted, but space is not an issue.
    
    Amazon Redshift automatically performs **`VACUUM DELETE ONLY`** operations in the background to reclaim space from deleted rows.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA1-50-Redshift-Vacuum-Command-030724.png)
    
    The best VACUUM command for the company to use is **`VACUUM REINDEX`**. This command will re-sort the entire table based on the interleaved sort key, restoring optimal sort order and improving query performance by analyzing and rebalancing the distribution of values in the sort key columns. Since the sort key distribution is significantly uneven/skewed, using REINDEX instead of a standard VACUUM will be more effective. Additionally, VACUUM REINDEX will reclaim disk space from deleted or updated records fulfilling another of the scenario’s requirements.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **`VACUUM REINDEX`**
    
    The option that says: **`VACUUM FULL`** is incorrect because, although it reclaims disk space and might improve overall table health, it does not specifically focus on the nuanced needs of interleaved sort key optimization, nor does it offer the most effective solution for analyzing sort keys in skewed data distributions.
    
    The option that says: **`VACUUM DELETE ONLY`**is incorrect as it is primarily designed to free up space from deleted rows rather than focusing on optimizing sort keys or analyzing their order
    
    The option that says: **`VACUUM SORT ONLY`** is incorrect. This command will simply re-sort rows within each block but won’t necessarily reclaim disk space.
    

A gaming company seeks a system to monitor player activities and interactions across various online platforms in real-time. The desired system must be capable of ingesting streaming data from game events formatted as nested JSON. It must also keep the query latency under single-digit milliseconds to provide quick updates for game dashboards and analytics.

How can the company minimize operational overhead while meeting these requirements effectively?

- **Ingest event data to Amazon Kinesis Data Streams. Create an application for consuming data and loading results in Amazon DynamoDB for querying.**
    
    **Amazon Kinesis Data** Streams is a service that continuously captures and stores streaming data records in real-time. It can ingest massive amounts of data from hundreds of sources, such as application logs, social media feeds, website clickstreams, etc.
    
    – Producers like applications, servers, devices, etc., can continuously send data records to a data stream in Kinesis.
    
    – Consumers, like custom applications, can then process/analyze the streaming data by reading data records from the stream.
    
    – Applications can use the Kinesis Client Library for reading data from streams.
    
    Processed records can then be stored in other AWS services like DynamoDB, Redshift, S3, etc.
    
    **Amazon DynamoDB** is a fully managed NoSQL database that offers single-digit millisecond performance at any scale. It is a document and key-value database that provides predictable performance with seamless scalability.
    
    – It allows data to be stored and queried in JSON format, which makes it suitable for storing processed records from Kinesis streams.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/dea1-51-KinesisDataStream-Game-Event-Data-2-030724.png)
    
    To best fulfill the gaming company’s requirements, it’s recommended that Amazon Kinesis Data Streams be utilized to ingest game event data. With its ability to handle streaming data from numerous sources at high throughput, Kinesis Data Streams is an ideal choice for this task. Subsequently, storing the results in Amazon DynamoDB allows for efficient querying. DynamoDB’s capability to store data in nested JSON format aligns well with the needs of the project, while its fast and predictable performance ensures swift queries. With DynamoDB being a fully managed NoSQL database service, latency for queries can be maintained under 10 milliseconds, meeting the necessary performance criteria.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Ingest event data to Amazon Kinesis Data Streams. Create an application for consuming data and loading results in Amazon DynamoDB for querying.**
    
    The option that says: **Ingest event data to a self-managed Apache Kafka hosted in EC2 instances. Analyze the data with Amazon Redshift** is incorrect. This solution would require managing Kafka clusters on EC2 instances, which adds operational overhead. Additionally, Redshift is better suited for batch analytics rather than low-latency queries.
    
    The option that says: **Publish the data to an Amazon Aurora Serverless database and use native SQL queries** is incorrect. While Amazon Aurora Serverless can automatically scale, it is mainly a relational database service, which makes it less suitable for handling nested JSON data and low-latency queries at scale.
    
    The option that says: **Publish game event data to an Amazon SQS queue. Create an AWS Lambda function to process data in batches** is incorrect. Although SQS queues are great for decoupling components, they introduce latency that may prevent the single-digit millisecond response times from being achieved. Moreover, processing data in batches could further delay the availability of data for real-time analysis and dashboard updates.
    
- Publish game event data to an Amazon SQS queue. Create an AWS Lambda function to process data in batches.
- Ingest event data to a self-managed Apache Kafka hosted in EC2 instances. Analyze the data with Amazon Redshift.
- Publish the data to an Amazon Aurora Serverless database and use native SQL queries.

A company has centralized its data storage in an Amazon S3 bucket. Within this repository, specific datasets include personally identifiable information (PII). Given the diversity of user groups needing access to raw data, a data engineer must ensure that each group can only access the PII relevant to them.

Which solution would satisfy the requirement in the LEAST amount of operational overhead?

- **Register the Amazon S3 location in AWS Lake Formation. Utilize Lake Formation's data filters to manage access for different users. Query data using Amazon Athena.**
    
    **AWS Lake Formation** streamlines the process of setting up a secure data lake in AWS. It provides fine-grained access control to sensitive data stored in Amazon S3 through a simple request or revoke process, allowing organizations to define who has access to specific aspects of their data. When integrated with **Amazon Athena**, a serverless interactive query service, Lake Formation enables column-level security (CLS) by leveraging data filters. This approach significantly reduces the effort required to manage access to PII data, as it directly associates user access levels with their IAM roles, eliminating the need for complex custom solutions or manual intervention.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Amazon_Athena_with_AWS_Lake_Formation_7March2024.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Register the Amazon S3 location in AWS Lake Formation. Utilize Lake Formation’s data filters to manage access for different users. Query data using Amazon Athena.**
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue to create ETL jobs that transform and move the data into Amazon Redshift. Apply row-level security in Redshift for different user groups** is incorrect. This approach introduces additional complexity, such as the need for ETL jobs and data transfer into Redshift. Implementing row-level security in Redshift adds overhead in terms of managing the data pipeline, handling transformations, and dealing with ongoing data movement. It’s not as streamlined as using Lake Formation and Athena.
    
    The option that says: **Create a query interface using Amazon Athena to execute backend data queries. Create user groups in Amazon Cognito and assign permission levels based on user-specific PII access requirements** is incorrect because it mainly suggests building a custom solution, which inherently requires more effort than using AWS’s managed services designed for security and access control. While integrating Athena and Cognito is feasible, the development, maintenance, and scalability of custom UIs typically demand significantly more resources.
    
    The option that says: **Provision IAM roles with granular access permissions and associate these roles with IAM user groups. Attach an IAM policy to the group that restricts access at the column level** is incorrect because it primarily uses IAM roles and identity-based policies for column-level access, which isn’t directly supported by IAM for S3 resources.
    
- Create a query interface using Amazon Athena to execute backend data queries. Create user groups in Amazon Cognito and assign permission levels based on user-specific PII access requirements.
- Use AWS Glue to create ETL jobs that transform and move the data into Amazon Redshift. Apply row-level security in Redshift for different user groups.
- Provision IAM roles with granular access permissions and associate these roles with IAM user groups. Attach an IAM policy to the group that restricts access at the column level.

A data engineering team needs to develop an ETL(Extract, Transform, and Load) pipeline to handle data inflow from ten distinct source systems. These systems generate files in CSV, JSON, and Apache Parquet formats. These files, varying in size from 5 MB to 15 GB, are stored in a single Amazon S3 bucket every 20 minutes.

The pipeline must dynamically adjust to schema changes while processing and loading the data into ten specific tables in an Amazon Redshift database.

Which solutions will MOST effectively meet these requirements? (Select TWO.)

- Initiate an AWS Glue job every 20 minutes via Amazon EventBridge Schedule. Process the data with an Apache Spark DataFrame and load it into Amazon Redshift tables.
- **Initiate a Glue workflow through an S3 event notification with an AWS Lambda function. Trigger an on-demand Glue crawler followed by a Glue job using Apache Spark DataFrame to process and load data into Amazon Redshift upon schema detection.**
- **Initiate an AWS Glue workflow job every 20 minutes via Amazon EventBridge rules. Use a Glue crawler to detect schema modifications and then process and load data into Amazon Redshift with Apache Spark DataFrame after schema validation.**
- Use an Amazon EMR cluster with Apache Hive to process the data every 20 minutes and load it into Amazon Redshift tables.
- Create two AWS Lambda functions: one to trigger a Glue crawler upon new S3 file uploads and another to execute a Glue job processing data with Apache Spark DataFrame and loading it into Amazon Redshift. The second function is coordinated by an Amazon EventBridge rule after the crawler's successful execution.
- **Evaluation**
    
    **AWS Glue workflows** enable the orchestration of complex ETL tasks by linking multiple jobs and crawlers, allowing for automated data processing sequences. These workflows can be initiated based on predefined schedules or in response to specific events, facilitating flexible and efficient data management strategies.
    
    **Amazon EventBridge** (formerly CloudWatch Events) is a serverless event bus service that connects AWS services, applications, and devices. Using simple rules, events in one service can be used to trigger actions in another.
    
    **Apache Spark DataFrames** are distributed data collections arranged into named columns that enable parallel SQL operations on massive datasets. Glue jobs can use Spark DataFrames to process and convert data.
    
    **S3 event notifications** allow you to execute a Lambda function anytime an object is created or updated in an S3 bucket. Once new data arrives in S3, this can be used to start a Glue process for near-real-time ETL.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/AWS_Glue_Workflow_&_Amazon_EventBridge_13March2024.png)
    
    AWS Glue is a managed ETL service that facilitates the preparation and loading of data for analytics. It can automatically discover the schema of the data through Glue crawlers, making it ideal for environments where the data schema can change over time. The integration with Amazon EventBridge or S3 event notifications allows for automating these ETL jobs in response to data arriving in S3, adhering to the requirement of handling files every 20 minutes. Processing data with Apache Spark DataFrame before loading it into Amazon Redshift is an efficient method for handling large datasets and transforming them as needed.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Initiate an AWS Glue workflow job every 20 minutes via Amazon EventBridge rules. Use a Glue crawler to detect schema modifications and then process and load data into Amazon Redshift with Apache Spark DataFrame after schema validation.**
    
    **– Initiate a Glue workflow through an S3 event notification with an AWS Lambda function. Trigger an on-demand Glue crawler followed by a Glue job using Apache Spark DataFrame to process and load data into Amazon Redshift upon schema detection.**
    
    The option that says: **Initiate an AWS Glue job every 20 minutes via Amazon EventBridge Schedule. Process the data with an Apache Spark DataFrame and load it into Amazon Redshift tables** is incorrect because it primarily lacks dynamic schema adaptation. While leveraging AWS Glue and Amazon EventBridge for periodic data processing and loading, this option does not include a mechanism like a Glue crawler for detecting and adapting to schema changes in the source data. Schema changes are critical in this scenario, and by handling them dynamically, the ETL process could succeed when unexpected changes occur.
    
    The option that says: **Create two AWS Lambda functions: one to trigger a Glue crawler upon new S3 file uploads and another to execute a Glue job processing data with Apache Spark DataFrame and loading it into Amazon Redshift. The second function is coordinated by an Amazon EventBridge rule after the crawler’s successful execution** is incorrect because it only introduces unnecessary complexity and potential points of failure. While this solution addresses schema changes using a Glue crawler, coordinating multiple AWS Lambda functions adds complexity and could lead to inefficiencies. The process might be more streamlined and reliable than directly using Glue workflows, which are designed to handle such ETL tasks more elegantly.
    
    The option that says: **Use an Amazon EMR cluster with Apache Hive to process the data every 20 minutes and load it into Amazon Redshift tables**is incorrect because it does not efficiently address dynamic schema changes and involves more complex setup and maintenance compared to AWS Glue-based solutions.
    

An e-commerce company stores customer profile data in Amazon S3. An AWS Glue crawler runs monthly to update the metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog.

Business analysts use Amazon Athena to query customer data. The results of the query are visualized on Amazon QuickSight dashboards, which are refreshed daily.

The company wants to reduce the cost of running SQL queries in Athena.

Which solution will fulfill these requirements with the LEAST amount of operational overhead?

- Convert stored data in S3 to a more efficient format, such as ORC, to reduce query execution times.
- **Enable Amazon Athena's query result caching feature, setting the cache duration to 24 hours.**
    
    **Amazon Athena** is a user-friendly query service that allows you to analyze data in Amazon S3 by using common SQL queries. Athena operates in a serverless environment, which means that you do not have to manage any infrastructure, and you only pay for the queries that you execute.
    
    **Amazon QuickSight** is a fully managed, serverless BI service for visualizing data and getting business insights. Users can easily connect QuickSight to their data sources and build interactive dashboards and reports.
    
    **AWS Glue** is a fully managed ETL service that automatically crawls data, infers schemas, and stores metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog. Developers can write ETL scripts with Glue to prepare and move data.
    
    **AWS Glue crawlers** connect to data sources, extract schemas using classifiers, and populate the AWS Glue Data Catalog with metadata about tables, partitions, and file formats. Crawlers can run periodically to detect new or changed data.
    
    The **AWS Glue Data Catalog** is a centralized metadata repository that stores information about data assets across various data sources. It provides a unified interface to query schemas, formats, and source details.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA1-54-REUSE-QUERY-ATHENA-030624.png)
    
    By enabling reused query results in Athena with a maximum age of 1 day (24 hours), Athena can reuse prior query results from the same day rather than re-running queries each time the QuickSight dashboard refreshes. This avoids unnecessary data scans and reduces costs. The analysts still get updated reports based on the latest full dataset without provisioning any additional infrastructure.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Enable Amazon Athena’s query result caching feature, setting the cache duration to 24 hours.**
    
    The option that says: **Implement a lifecycle policy in S3 to transition data to S3 Intelligent Tiering after 30 days** is incorrect because this action would not reduce the operational overhead associated with running queries in Athena or impact the cost of query executions directly.
    
    The option that says: **Convert stored data in S3 to a more efficient format, such as ORC, to reduce query execution times** is incorrect. This approach is a valid way of reducing the amount of data scanned by Athena queries, thus reducing the cost of queries. However, initial effort is required to convert the data format, and maintaining the process for new data may involve ongoing operational overhead.
    
    The option that says: **Activate Amazon QuickSight’s in-memory calculation to minimize the number of Athena queries** is incorrect. While this option can reduce the need to run new Athena queries for some types of data analysis, it primarily affects how QuickSight processes the data after it has been retrieved. This option might lower the overall expenses of the solution, but it does not directly decrease the costs associated with Athena, which is the primary concern.
    
- Implement a lifecycle policy in S3 to transition data to S3 Intelligent Tiering after 30 days.
- Activate Amazon QuickSight's in-memory calculation to minimize the number of Athena queries.

A retail company needs to improve SQL query efficiency in its data warehouse, which uses Amazon Redshift. They cannot expand the cluster due to cost constraints.

The company’s data is in various tables with some having thousands of megabytes of data, while others are smaller and updated infrequently.

Which strategy is the most effective for meeting these requirements?

- Ensure that primary and foreign keys are defined for each table while maintaining an EVEN distribution style across all tables.
- Change the distribution style of larger tables to ALL distribution and set primary and foreign keys across all tables.
- Change the distribution style of small tables to KEY distribution and set primary and foreign keys across all tables
- **Change the distribution style of small tables to ALL distribution and set primary and foreign keys across all tables.**
    
    **Data distribution in Amazon Redshift** refers to how data is stored across the nodes and slices within a cluster. When you load data into a Redshift table, the rows are distributed and stored across these slices according to the table’s distribution style.
    
    The different distribution styles in **Amazon Redshift** are:
    
    **EVEN distribution** – The rows are distributed evenly across all nodes and slices in a round-robin fashion, regardless of the values in any particular column. This is used when the table doesn’t participate in joins or when there is no clear choice of key distribution column.
    
    **KEY distribution** – The rows are distributed based on the values in a specified distribution key column. Rows with the same value are stored together physically. This helps optimize queries that filter or join on the distribution key column.
    
    **ALL distribution** – A copy of the entire table is stored on each node, so every row is collocated for any join query involving that table. However, it requires more storage and load time compared to EVEN or KEY. Used for relatively static tables.
    
    **AUTO distribution** – Redshift automatically assigns the optimal distribution style based on table size and characteristics. It initially uses ALL and may later change to KEY or EVEN, depending on how the table grows.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/dea1-55-distkey_all_diagram.jpg)
    
    Considering the described scenario, the solution that best meets the requirement involves using the ALL distribution style for the small, infrequently updated table. Changing the smaller dimension tables to ALL distribution would replicate these tables to all nodes, allowing them to be colocated with larger fact tables during joins. Furthermore, setting the primary keys on dimension tables and referencing these as foreign keys in fact tables allows Redshift to leverage these constraints for query optimization.
    
    Using ALL distribution for relevant small tables improves query performance without requiring the company to expand its cluster due to cost constraints. Lastly, ALL distribution is well-suited for smaller tables that see infrequent changes as it avoids data redistribution during updates.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Change the distribution style of small tables to ALL distribution and set primary and foreign keys across all tables.**
    
    The option that says: **Ensure that primary and foreign keys are defined for each table while maintaining an EVEN distribution style across all tables** is incorrect because the EVEN distribution is not optimal for small, infrequently updated tables that are frequently joined with large tables. It can lead to unnecessary data redistribution during joins, which can degrade performance.
    
    The option that says: **Change the distribution style of larger tables to ALL distribution and set primary and foreign keys across all tables** is incorrect. Distributing large tables with an ALL distribution style would significantly increase storage requirements and could slow down query performance due to the additional data being processed on each node.
    
    The option that says: **Change the distribution style of small tables to KEY distribution and set primary and foreign keys across all tables** is incorrect. While KEY distribution can be effective for distributing data based on the values of a specific column (usually a join key), using ALL for small tables is generally more efficient as it removes the need for redistribution during joins, leading to faster query execution.
    

A company gathers observations on different animal species and compiles them into a CSV file with attributes such as `Animal_ID`, `Animal_Name`, `Scientific_Name`, `Status` and many others.

The company aims to transform this data into a flat JSON format using AWS Glue DataBrew, where each key represents a column name and its corresponding value from the rows. In the output, the original order of the columns from the CSV does not matter.

Which of the following can meet the requirements with the LEAST amount of coding effort?

- Use the `NEST_TO_ARRAY`transformation.
- **Use the `NEST_TO_MAP` transformation.**
    
    **AWS Glue DataBrew** is a visual data preparation tool that makes it easy to clean and normalize data for analysis and reporting without the need for writing code. It provides a broad set of transformations that can be applied to data sets to prepare them for analytics.
    
    A **DataBrew recipe** is a set of data transformation steps that are applied sequentially to clean, normalize, and prepare data. Here are some of those recipe steps:
    
    **NEST_TO_ARRAY** converts selected columns into an array while maintaining their order and typecasting them into a common data type.
    
    **NEST_TO_MAP** converts user-selected columns into key-value pairs, with each key representing the column name and the corresponding row value as the value. Column order is not preserved, and different column data types are typecast to a common type.
    
    **UNNEST_MAP** separates a map column into key-value pairs, generating a row for each pair. It only works for one map column level.
    
    **UNNEST_ARRAY** function extracts array elements into separate rows, allowing only one level of an array column to be unnested.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/dea1-56-databrew-nest-to-map-2-030624.png)
    
    The `NEST_TO_MAP` transformation takes each row of the DataFrame and nests the column values into a map using the column names as keys. This converts the data into `JSON` format where each row becomes a JSON object, meeting the needs described in the question.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use the `NEST_TO_MAP` transformation.**
    
    The option that says: **Use the `NEST_TO_ARRAY`transformation** is incorrect because this transformation simply converts data into an array, not into a key-value pair structure required for `JSON`.
    
    The option that says: **Use the `UNNEST_MAP`transformation** is incorrect as UNNEST_MAP is more suitable for expanding map data into separate columns, not for creating nested JSON structures from flat data.
    
    The option that says: **Use the `UNNEST_ARRAY`transformation** is incorrect because this transformation is primarily used to expand arrays into separate rows or columns, which does not align with the goal of converting to a `JSON`structure.
    
- Use the `UNNEST_MAP` transformation.
- Use the `UNNEST_ARRAY` transformation.

A data engineer manages a workflow where financial records are continuously uploaded to Amazon S3. These records contain confidential information that needs encryption measures to enhance security. Only a select group of employees must have control over the encryption keys.

How can the data engineer fulfill the requirements with the LEAST operational overhead?

- Store encryption keys in an AWS CloudHSM cluster. Create an IAM policy to limit access. Encrypt/decrypt objects using CloudHSM for S3 write operations.
- Encrypt the records via Server-side Encryption with Customer-provided keys (SSE-C). Limit access to the keys responsible for encryption.
- **Encrypt the records via Server-side Encryption with AWS KMS keys (SSE-KMS). Use KMS key policies to define the users who can access the encryption keys.**
    
    Amazon S3 provides several methods to encrypt objects stored at rest:
    
    **S3-managed encryption (SSE-S3):** With this method, Amazon S3 encrypts your data using keys that it manages and controls. This is the default encryption for all new objects. Amazon handles the encryption, decryption, and key management for you.
    
    **SSE-C (Server-Side Encryption with Customer-Provided Keys):** You manage and control the encryption keys, while Amazon S3 performs the encryption and decryption operations on your behalf. You have complete control over the keys, but Amazon handles the encryption/decryption process.
    
    **Server-Side Encryption with AWS Key Management Service (SSE-KMS):** Provides an additional layer of security by integrating with AWS KMS for centralized key management and access controls. KMS encrypts the data keys, which S3 then uses to encrypt the objects.
    
    **Client-Side Encryption:** You can encrypt objects client-side before uploading to S3 using libraries like the Amazon S3 Encryption Client. This gives you complete control over the encryption process.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA1-57-SSE-KMS-Diagram-022924.jpg)
    
    Encrypting files with confidential customer information in Amazon S3 requires a solution that not only secures the data but also allows for precise encryption key control. AWS Key Management Service (KMS) meets these needs by enabling users to create, manage, and control the use of encryption keys across AWS services and their applications.
    
    AWS KMS integrates with S3 to support server-side encryption using KMS keys. When uploading data to S3, you specify the KMS key to be used for encryption; S3 then encrypts the file before storing it. KMS key policies primarily control who can access the keys and delineate the permissions regarding what actions they can perform with these keys. Through these policies, the data engineer can precisely delegate which employees can use the keys for encrypting and decrypting the financial records stored in S3.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Encrypt the records via Server-side Encryption with AWS KMS keys (SSE-KMS). Use KMS key policies to define the users who can access the encryption keys.**
    
    The option that says: **Store encryption keys in an AWS CloudHSM cluster. Create an IAM policy to limit access. Encrypt/decrypt objects using CloudHSM for S3 write operations** is incorrect. In AWS CloudHSM, you’re responsible for managing the hardware security module that generates and stores your keys. This involves significant operational overhead, including hardware provisioning, scaling, and maintenance. While CloudHSM provides a high level of security, it’s more complex and costly compared to managing keys with AWS KMS.
    
    The option that says: **Encrypt the records via Server-side Encryption with Customer-provided keys (SSE-C). Limit access to the keys responsible for encryption** is incorrect because SSE-C requires the customer to manage and secure the encryption keys outside of AWS. This increases the complexity and risk, as the customer is responsible for these keys’ secure generation, storage, and rotation.
    
    The option that says: **Encrypt the records via Server-side Encryption with Amazon S3 managed keys (SSE-S3). Create an IAM policy to control access to the S3-managed keys** is incorrect because SSE-S3 provides a simple and managed encryption solution using Amazon S3 managed keys. However, it does not offer the same level of control or auditing over the encryption keys as SSE-KMS, making it less suitable for scenarios requiring strict access control to encryption keys.
    
- Encrypt the records via Server-side Encryption with Amazon S3 managed keys (SSE-S3). Create an IAM policy to control access to the S3-managed keys.

A corporation has a vast amount of data stored in Amazon S3 buckets. The access patterns for this data are both unpredictable and frequently change. The corporation wants to optimize storage costs while ensuring that data retrieval times remain within milliseconds.

Which of the following strategies would be the most suitable for this scenario with the LEAST operational overhead?

- Use S3 Storage Lens to monitor the `BucketSizeBytes` metric and transition data to S3 Glacier Deep Archive if the total size of a bucket exceeds a certain threshold.
- **Create an Intelligent-Tiering Archive configuration and choose the default access tier.**
    
    Amazon S3 Intelligent-Tiering is a storage class designed to optimize costs by automatically moving data between two access tiers (frequent and infrequent) based on changing access patterns. When you upload data to S3 Intelligent-Tiering, it is automatically stored in the Frequent Access tier, which is optimized for data that is accessed frequently. If an object hasn’t been accessed for 30 consecutive days, S3 Intelligent-Tiering moves it to the Infrequent Access tier, which is less expensive. If an object in the Infrequent Access tier is accessed later, it is automatically moved back to the Frequent Access tier. This automatic tiering allows you to optimize storage costs without impacting data retrieval times or requiring operational overhead.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/s3-intelligent-tiering-030624.png)
    
    In the context of the question, the corporation’s data access patterns are unpredictable and variable, which makes S3 Intelligent-Tiering an ideal choice. Regardless of how frequently or infrequently the data is accessed, S3 Intelligent-Tiering ensures that all data can be retrieved within milliseconds, meeting the corporation’s requirements. Furthermore, because S3 Intelligent-Tiering automatically moves data between access tiers based on access patterns, it requires the least operational overhead compared to other options that involve manual intervention or monitoring with S3 Storage Lens.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create an Intelligent-Tiering Archive configuration and choose the default access tier.**
    
    The option that says: **Create an Intelligent-Tiering Archive configuration, and choose the Deep Archive Access tier** is incorrect because the Deep Archive Access tier is not designed for data that needs to be retrieved within milliseconds. It is meant for long-term archiving of data that is rarely accessed.
    
    The option that says: **Use S3 Storage Lens to monitor the `NumberOfObjects` metric and transition data to S3 One Zone-Infrequent Access if the number of objects in a bucket exceeds a certain threshold** is incorrect because it suggests transitioning data based on the number of objects in a bucket, not on access patterns. S3 Storage Lens is used here to monitor the `NumberOfObjects` metric, which provides the total number of objects stored in a bucket. However, this does not take into account the access patterns of the data, which are unpredictable and variable in this scenario.
    
    The option that says: **Use S3 Storage Lens to monitor the `BucketSizeBytes` metric and transition data to S3 Glacier Deep Archive if the total size of a bucket exceeds a certain threshold** is incorrect because it suggests transitioning data based on the total size of a bucket, not on access patterns. S3 Storage Lens is used here to monitor the `BucketSizeBytes`metric, which provides the total size of a bucket in bytes. However, this does not take into account the access patterns of the data, which are unpredictable and variable in this scenario.
    
- Use S3 Storage Lens to monitor the `NumberOfObjects` metric and transition data to S3 One Zone-Infrequent Access if the number of objects in a bucket exceeds a certain threshold.
- Create an Intelligent-Tiering Archive configuration, and choose the Deep Archive Access tier.

A data engineer at a large organization is setting up an AWS Glue job to transform and load data from various sources into an Amazon Redshift data warehouse. To ensure security and access management, the engineer needs to store and access the necessary credentials securely.

What is the most secure and best practice for the engineer to manage these credentials?

- Use AWS Parameter Store to store the credentials. Grant the Glue job’s IAM role permission to retrieve parameters.
- Pass the credentials as job parameters when invoking the AWS Glue job.
- Create a configuration file containing the credentials and upload it to an Amazon S3 bucket. Update the bucket policy to grant the Glue job access to the file.
- **Use AWS Secrets Manager to store the credentials. Grant the Glue job’s IAM role permission to retrieve secret values.**
    
    AWS Secrets Manager is a robust service designed specifically for managing access to applications, services, and IT resources. It provides a secure and scalable method to store and manage database credentials, API keys, and other secrets. This service is particularly useful in scenarios where there is a need to securely handle sensitive information such as credentials. It allows you to protect access to your applications by enabling you to easily rotate, manage, and retrieve these secrets throughout their lifecycle.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/secrets-manager-030624.png)
    
    When setting up an AWS Glue job, it is important to manage credentials securely. AWS Secrets Manager integrates seamlessly with other AWS services and provides a centralized service to manage secrets. By storing credentials in AWS Secrets Manager, they are kept secure and can be programmatically retrieved when needed. This eliminates the need to hardcode sensitive information in plain text, which is a common security bad practice.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/glue-job-to-access-the-credentials-030624.png)
    
    In addition, AWS Secrets Manager allows for fine-grained access control to secrets using AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) policies. This means that you can control which AWS Glue jobs or other AWS resources have permission to access these secrets. This level of control is important in maintaining the principle of least privilege, a key security best practice.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use AWS Secrets Manager to store the credentials. Grant the Glue job’s IAM role permission to retrieve secret values.**
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Parameter Store to store the credentials. Grant the Glue job’s IAM role permission to retrieve parameters** is incorrect. Systems Manager Parameter Store is also a viable option, but it is generally recommended for storing configuration data rather than sensitive credentials. It’s not the best choice for managing credentials in this scenario. AWS Secrets Manager is more suited for this purpose as it offers additional security features tailored for credential management, like automatic secrets rotation.
    
    The option that says: **Create a configuration file containing the credentials and upload it to an Amazon S3 bucket. Update the bucket policy to grant the Glue job access to the file** is incorrect because storing sensitive information like credentials in a plain text configuration file in an Amazon S3 bucket is not a secure practice. Even though you can control access to the S3 bucket using IAM roles, it does not provide the same level of protection and management features as AWS Secrets Manager.
    
    The option that says: **Pass the credentials as job parameters when invoking the AWS Glue job** is incorrect because hardcoding sensitive information like credentials as job parameters can lead to security risks. These parameters are also logged in AWS CloudTrail, making it a less secure option.
    

A company runs a complex analytics workload every week on an Amazon Redshift cluster. The workload includes the following tasks:

1. Launching a new Redshift cluster.
2. Performing processing and loading data to an Amazon S3 bucket.
3. Terminating the Redshift cluster.

The company wants to minimize operational overhead and ensure that all these tasks are handled automatically. The solution must also be able to handle the complexity of the analytics functions.

Which solution offers the LEAST operational overhead while meeting these requirements?

- **Use Amazon Redshift Serverless to run the analytics workload.**
    
    Amazon Redshift Serverless is a fully managed data warehouse service that automatically scales compute resources up or down to meet the demands of your workload. This means that it can handle the processing of large amounts of data without requiring manual intervention to adjust compute capacity. This automatic scaling and task management significantly reduce operational overhead, making it an ideal solution for the company’s needs.
    
    ![Amazon Redshift Serverless](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/redshift-serverless-030624.png)
    
    Furthermore, Amazon Redshift Serverless is designed to handle complex analytics functions. It uses machine learning to deliver consistently fast performance, even for complex queries and large datasets. This means that it can efficiently manage the company’s complex analytics workload.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use Amazon Redshift Serverless to run the analytics workload.**
    
    The option that says: **Run Apache Oozie on Amazon EC2 to process the analytics workload** is incorrect because Apache Oozie is a workflow scheduler primarily designed for Hadoop ecosystems. Running Oozie on EC2 requires complex setup, including infrastructure provisioning, configuration of dependencies, and ongoing instance management. This results in high operational overhead, which does not meet the company’s goal to reduce manual operations.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Athena to process the analytics workload** is incorrect. While Athena is serverless and has low operational overhead, its use of pooled resources can lead to variable performance, especially for complex analytic workloads that demand consistent and high-performance computing resources.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Redshift Spectrum to run the analytics workload** is incorrect. Redshift Spectrum is simply an extension of a Redshift cluster that allows you to directly query data on S3. Amazon Redshift Spectrum still needs an existing Redshift cluster to function and does not operate on its own.
    
- Use Amazon Athena to process the analytics workload.
- Run Apache Oozie on Amazon EC2 to process the analytics workload.
- Use Amazon Redshift Spectrum to run the analytics workload.

A company that provides a geolocation service gathers large datasets of longitude and latitude coordinates representing customer interactions. These datasets are saved in a CSV document, which can reach sizes of up to 3GB. The document is then uploaded to Amazon S3 for storage and analysis.

The data engineering team needs to merge the latitude and longitude fields and then analyze the data to find the total number of unique customer entries in these daily records.

How can the team satisfy the requirements in the LEAST amount of development effort?

- **Construct a recipe with AWS Glue DataBrew to calculate the distinct count of customers by using the `COUNT_DISTINCT` function.**
    
    **AWS Glue DataBrew** is a user-friendly tool that simplifies data preparation by allowing users to clean, transform, and profile data without writing any code. With DataBrew, users can easily visualize, clean, and normalize data directly from data stores like Amazon S3, Amazon Redshift, and Amazon RDS.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/dea1-61-aws-databrew-count_distinct-from-aws-022924.png)
    
    AWS Glue DataBrew makes it easier for data engineering teams to perform tasks like merging fields and aggregating counts. With the `COUNT_DISTINCT` function, identifying unique customers is just a few clicks away, eliminating the need for custom code or scripts. The recipe and results can be saved and reused for ongoing analysis of daily records with minimal effort.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Construct a recipe with AWS Glue DataBrew to calculate the distinct count of customers by using the `COUNT_DISTINCT` function.**
    
    The option that says: **Write a PySpark script for counting distinct entries and run it in an Amazon EMR Serverless application** is incorrect. Although EMR Serverless involves less overhead in managing an EMR cluster, you’d still need to write the code compared to using AWS Glue DataBrew, which offers a more straightforward, no-code approach to accomplish the task.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue Crawlers to infer the schema of source data. Write an AWS Glue Spark job to perform the unique customer count** is incorrect. AWS Glue Crawlers are used to discover and profile data, create metadata, and make the data searchable in the AWS Glue Data Catalog. AWS Glue DataBrew allows direct connection to Amazon S3 without needing to infer the schema using Glue crawlers. Also, this option entails more development effort due to having to code a Spark job.
    
    The option that says: **Configure an AWS Lambda function to execute a Python script that reads the S3 file, processes the concatenation, and uses a distinct count algorithm** is incorrect. AWS Lambda allows running code in response to triggers without provisioning or managing servers. However, processing large files (up to 3GB, as mentioned) directly within Lambda may be challenging due to its limitations in execution time and memory. It would require careful management of resources and potentially splitting the file into smaller chunks, increasing the complexity and operational overhead.
    
- Write a PySpark script for counting distinct entries and run it in an Amazon EMR Serverless application.
- Use AWS Glue Crawlers to infer the schema of source data. Write an AWS Glue Spark job to perform the unique customer count.
- Configure an AWS Lambda function to execute a Python script that reads the S3 file, processes the concatenation, and uses a distinct count algorithm.

A biomedical company has various medical devices that send health metrics to Amazon Kinesis Data Streams.

The company wants to analyze current versus historical data and display the insights on a dashboard in near real-time. To achieve this, a data engineer must set up a pipeline that stores incoming data streams in an Amazon Redshift Serverless data warehouse.

Which solution will fulfill these requirements with LEAST operational overhead?

- Ingest the data to Amazon Redshift by first staging the data in Amazon S3 and using the `COPY` command.
- Ingest the data into an Amazon Aurora database and use the zero-ETL integration to sync it with the Amazon Redshift database.
- Create an Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose stream using Kinesis Data Streams as the source and Amazon Redshift as the destination.
- **Stream the data to the Amazon Redshift database using Amazon Redshift Streaming Ingestion.**
    
    **Amazon Kinesis Data Streams** is a fully managed service that can continuously capture and store terabytes of data per hour from hundreds of thousands of sources.
    
    **Amazon Redshift Streaming Ingestion** allows ingesting streaming data from Kinesis data streams into an Amazon Redshift data warehouse in near real-time for analytics. It can ingest hundreds of megabytes of data per second with low latency.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA1-62-redshift-streaming-ingestion-030524.png)
    
    By utilizing Amazon Redshift’s Streaming Ingestion, the company can directly ingest data from Amazon Kinesis Data Streams into an Amazon Redshift materialized view, bypassing the need for additional data processing layers or staging areas like S3 and directly ingesting data into Redshift. For the given scenario of streaming biomedical equipment data, the steps would be:
    
    Stream the data from biomedical equipment into an Amazon Kinesis data stream.
    
    Define an external schema in Redshift pointing to the Kinesis data stream using `CREATE EXTERNAL SCHEMA`.
    
    Create a materialized view to consume the streaming data from the external schema using `CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW`.
    
    Periodically refresh the materialized view using `REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW` to load new data from the stream into Redshift tables.
    
    This enables near real-time analytics on the streaming biomedical data as it continuously gets ingested into Redshift with low latency using streaming ingestion. The data engineer can then run queries on both the incoming streaming data as well as historical data for analysis.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Stream the data to the Amazon Redshift database using Amazon Redshift Streaming Ingestion.**
    
    The option that says: **Create an Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose stream using Kinesis Data Streams as the source and Amazon Redshift as the destination** is incorrect.
