While Kinesis Data Firehose supports data streaming into Amazon Redshift, it involves staging data in Amazon S3 before loading it into Redshift, which can introduce additional latency compared to direct streaming ingestion.
    
    The option that says: **Ingest the data to Amazon Redshift by first staging the data in Amazon S3 and using the `COPY` command** is incorrect. This method requires data to be first stored in S3, followed by a batch loading process using the COPY command. It’s less efficient for real-time data processing needs due to the delay in batch processing.
    
    The option that says: **Ingest the data into an Amazon Aurora database and use the zero-ETL integration to sync it with the Amazon Redshift database** is incorrect. Although Amazon Aurora offers zero-ETL integration with Amazon Redshift, it is not directly related to streaming data ingestion from Amazon Kinesis Data Streams. Moreover, this approach leads to extra operational overhead from unnecessarily managing two databases.
    

A data engineer wants to use Amazon QuickSight to visualize the results of Amazon Athena queries, which are delivered in an S3 bucket. However, the data engineer encountered a permission-related error message when they attempted to access the QuickSight dashboard.

What could be the underlying reasons for this error? (Select THREE.)

- **The data engineer's IAM user does not have the required read and write access to the S3 bucket.**
- **QuickSight is not authorized to decrypt the data in the S3 bucket, preventing data access.**
- Athena data tables have not been properly cataloged, leading to potential access issues within QuickSight.
- Amazon Athena's query performance optimization settings are not properly configured.
- QuickSight does not have a configured connection to Athena.
- **QuickSight is not authorized to access the S3 bucket containing the data.**
- **Evaluation**
    
    The following are common reasons for getting **Access denied** errors when you use Amazon Athena as a data source in Amazon QuickSight:
    
    – Your QuickSight account doesn’t have the required permission to access the Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) bucket.
    
    – Your data file is encrypted with an AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS) key.
    
    – You don’t have the necessary AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) permissions policy assigned to you.
    
    – The Amazon S3 bucket doesn’t exist, or the IAM role used to query the data doesn’t have the required S3 permissions.
    
    – You don’t have the necessary service control policies (SCPs) assigned to you. (For QuickSight accounts that use AWS Organizations)
    
    – Your QuickSight user or group doesn’t have AWS Lake Formation permissions. (For Athena accounts that use Lake Formation)
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Troubleshoot_connecting_to_Amazon_Athena_29FEB2024%20(2).png)
    
    QuickSight uses a service role to communicate with other AWS services like Amazon Athena and Amazon S3 on your behalf. If you are being denied access to a QuickSight dashboard, it’s most likely due to insufficient permissions related to the QuickSight service role. For the data engineer to successfully run queries in Athena, their IAM user must have the necessary permissions to access the S3 bucket where Athena stores the query results, as well as permissions to decrypt the data if the S3 bucket is encrypted. IAM provides managed policies that you can use to avoid guessing required permissions and save time when granting access.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– QuickSight is not authorized to access the S3 bucket containing the data.**
    
    **– QuickSight is not authorized to decrypt the data in the S3 bucket, preventing data access.**
    
    **–The data engineer’s IAM user does not have the required read and write access to the S3 bucket.**
    
    The option that says: **QuickSight does not have a configured connection to Athena** is incorrect because it simply addresses the configuration for data querying between QuickSight and Athena, not the permissions issue directly. Therefore. this option is not related to the error.
    
    The option that says: **Athena data tables have not been properly cataloged, leading to potential access issues within QuickSight** is incorrect. This option primarily focuses on the organization and accessibility of data within Athena. While cataloging data tables is crucial for data querying, the permissions error encountered in QuickSight is tied to access rights, not data organization. Proper cataloging would not resolve a permissions denial error when attempting to access data.
    
    **The option that says: Amazon Athena’s query performance optimization settings are not properly configured is incorrect because it only addresses Athena’s query performance optimization rather than permissions. Athena’s query performance optimization settings pertain to the efficiency and speed of data processing and have no direct impact on permissions or access rights.**
    

An enterprise exports JSON and CSV files from a Software-as-a-Service (SaaS) application to an Amazon S3 bucket. Additionally, they have various internal services that store data in Amazon RDS for MySQL, SQL Server, and Amazon DynamoDB.

The data engineering division must create a system that allows data scientists to execute queries across all these data storage options using an SQL-like syntax.

Which solution will satisfy these prerequisites while minimizing operational overhead?

- Utilize AWS Glue to crawl data sources, with metadata stored in the AWS Glue Data Catalog. Transform JSON format data to Apache Parquet or .csv using AWS Glue jobs, storing transformed data in S3. Query both original and transformed data using Amazon Athena.
- Configure Amazon RDS to directly query data using federated queries for RDS, DynamoDB, and S3. Store JSON and .csv data in S3 and use SQL Server-linked servers for cross-database queries.
- Utilize AWS Glue to crawl data sources, saving metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog. Run SQL queries using Redshift spectrum.
- **Use AWS Glue to crawl the data sources and store the metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog. With Amazon Athena, use SQL and PartiQL to query structured and semi-structured data respectively.**
    
    AWS Glue crawlers are designed to discover and catalog data from various data sources automatically, creating and updating tables in the AWS Glue Data Catalog. This catalog serves as a central metadata repository, which data engineers and scientists can use to understand the structure and schema of their data across multiple data stores. The process of using crawlers simplifies the discovery of data, as it automatically infers the schema of the data and categorizes it accordingly, which is crucial for efficiently managing and querying large datasets.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Amazon_Athena_&_AWS_Glue_Crawler_5March2024.png)
    
    By leveraging Amazon Athena, which supports both SQL and PartiQL, queries can be executed across structured and unstructured data, respectively. PartiQL, an extension of SQL, simplifies querying unstructured data like JSON by offering built-in functions for easy navigation and parsing. Additionally, you can continue using native SQL from structured data sources such as relational database systems, ensuring compatibility and ease of use across the entire dataset.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use AWS Glue to crawl the data sources and store the metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog. With Amazon Athena, use SQL and PartiQL to query structured and semi-structured data respectively.**
    
    The option that says: **Utilize AWS Glue to crawl data sources, saving metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog. Run SQL queries using Redshift spectrum** is incorrect because it simply introduces more complexity and cost compared to Athena for querying across diverse data sources without the need for data transformation. Although Redshift Spectrum allows querying data in S3, it does not meet the requirements given in the scenario.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize AWS Glue to crawl data sources, with metadata stored in the AWS Glue Data Catalog. Transform JSON format data to Apache Parquet or .csv using AWS Glue jobs, storing transformed data in S3. Query both original and transformed data using Amazon Athena** is incorrect because this option primarily involves additional operational overhead for data transformation. It requires configuration for AWS Glue Jobs and handling data formats, which is unnecessary for the requirement to query data in its original format.
    
    The option that says: **Configure Amazon RDS to directly query data using federated queries for RDS, DynamoDB, and S3. Store JSON and .csv data in S3 and use SQL Server-linked servers for cross-database queries** is incorrect because Amazon RDS federated queries simply do not natively support querying across DynamoDB and S3 in the manner described. This option introduces significant complexity and misrepresents the capabilities of federated queries and SQL Server-linked servers in the context of AWS services.
    

A data engineer is trying to use AWS Glue Interactive Sessions in Amazon SageMaker Studio for data preparation tasks. The data engineer has successfully created a SageMaker IAM role with trust policies for both Amazon SageMaker and AWS Glue services.Despite this setup, the data engineer is being denied access when attempting to initiate an AWS Glue Interactive Session

How can the data engineer resolve the issue?

- Ensure the data engineer’s IAM user has permission to perform the `sts:AssumeRole` and `sagemaker:AddAssociation` actions.
- Add the `AWSGlueServiceRole`managed policy to the IAM user of the data engineer.
- Attach the `AmazonSageMakerFullAccess`managed policy to the SageMaker’s execution role.
- **Attach the `AwsGlueSessionUserRestrictedServiceRole`managed policy to the SageMaker’s execution role.**
    
    Amazon SageMaker Studio is an integrated development environment (IDE) for machine learning that provides every tool needed to build, train, and deploy machine learning models. SageMaker offers a user-friendly interface for executing ML workflows, making its tools accessible across various integrated development environments (IDEs). Additionally, It conducts operations on your behalf by leveraging other AWS services. You need to assign permissions to enable SageMaker to utilize these services and access the corresponding resources. This permission is achieved through an AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) execution role, granting SageMaker the necessary permissions.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/SageMaker_Execution_Role_7March2024.png)
    
    AWS Glue Interactive Sessions provides a programmatic and visual interface for building and testing ETL scripts for data preparation. To use it in Amazon SageMaker Studio, the SageMaker execution role needs to have the necessary permissions for AWS Glue. This can be achieved by attaching the `AwsGlueSessionUserRestrictedServiceRole`managed policy to the SageMaker execution role.
    
    The `AwsGlueSessionUserRestrictedServiceRole`policy grants the required permissions for creating and managing AWS Glue Interactive Sessions, as well as accessing the AWS Glue Data Catalog and other related resources.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Attach the `AwsGlueSessionUserRestrictedServiceRole`managed policy to the SageMaker’s execution role.**
    
    The option that says: **Attach the `AmazonSageMakerFullAccess` managed policy to the SageMaker’s execution role.** is incorrect. This policy just grants all actions across Amazon SageMaker, which is not required for the specific task of using AWS Glue Interactive Sessions.
    
    The option that says: **Ensure the data engineer’s IAM user has permission to perform the `sts:AssumeRole` and `sagemaker:AddAssociation` actions** is incorrect. While `sts:AssumeRole` is required to assume an IAM role, this permission is not specifically needed for using AWS Glue Interactive Sessions. Additionally, the `sagemaker:AddAssociation` permission is only used for associating resources with a SageMaker notebook instance or studio, but it’s not directly related to AWS Glue Interactive Sessions.
    
    The option that says: **Add the `AWSGlueServiceRole` managed policy to the IAM user of the data engineer** is incorrect because this policy is typically used to grant AWS Glue the permissions it needs to execute jobs and does not apply to the SageMaker execution role’s permissions for AWS Glue Interactive Sessions.
    

A Data Engineer at a large e-commerce company is managing a DynamoDB table that stores product data from various categories. The partition key currently in use is `CATEGORY_NAME`. However, this has led to some partitions receiving more requests than others, causing imbalanced I/O and potential throttling.

Which of the following strategies should the engineer implement to balance the throughput and prevent “hot” partitions?

- **Create a new DynamoDB table using a more specific partition key, for instance, `PRODUCT_ID`.**
    
    In Amazon DynamoDB, data is distributed across multiple partitions based on the partition key. Each partition can handle a certain amount of read and write capacity. If the workload is not evenly distributed across partitions, some partitions (known as “hot” partitions) may receive a higher volume of traffic than others, leading to throttling and inefficient use of provisioned capacity.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/adaptive-capacity-022924.png)
    
    The partition key currently in  use is `CATEGORY_NAME`. However, if the data within a category is heavily accessed, it can lead to imbalanced I/O and potential throttling. This is because all items with the same `CATEGORY_NAME` are stored in the same partition, and if that category is popular, it can lead to a “hot” partition.
    
    The solution is to create a new DynamoDB table using a more specific partition key, such as `PRODUCT_ID`. `PRODUCT_ID` is likely to have a higher cardinality (i.e., more unique values) than `CATEGORY_NAME`, which means the data will be spread across more partitions. This results in a more even distribution of data and requests, reducing the likelihood of “hot” partitions.
    
    By using `PRODUCT_ID` as the partition key, each product would have its own partition. This means that even if one product is very popular, the high traffic will be isolated to that product’s partition and will not impact the performance of other partitions. This strategy ensures efficient use of your provisioned I/O capacity and prevents throttling.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create a new DynamoDB table using a more specific partition key, for instance, `PRODUCT_ID`.**
    
    The option that says: **Create a local secondary index (LSI) on `PRODUCT_ID` in a new DynamoDB database** is incorrect. An LSI does not change the underlying partition key or the distribution of data across partitions. It merely provides an alternative way of querying by allowing you to create an index on non-key attributes and query the data based on those attribute.
    
    The option that says: **Create a new DynamoDB table called `DEPARTMENT_ID` with a more general partition key** is incorrect. The issue with this approach is that departments could still have an imbalanced number of products, leading to potential “hot” partitions. For example, some departments might have a large number of products, while others might have fewer products. This could result in a similar situation as the current `CATEGORY_NAME` partition key, where certain partitions receive a disproportionate amount of requests
    
    The option that says: **Create a new DynamoDB table with a Global Secondary Index (GSI) on `PRODUCT_NAME`** is incorrect because GSIs simply allow you to create a new view of the data where you can use a different partition key and sort key. However, creating a GSI on `PRODUCT_NAME` would not address the issue of imbalanced I/O on the main table. GSIs are best used for improving the flexibility of queries, not for balancing throughput on the main table.
    
- Create a local secondary index (LSI) on `PRODUCT_ID` in a new DynamoDB database.
- Create a new DynamoDB table with a Global Secondary Index (GSI) on `PRODUCT_NAME`.
- Create a new DynamoDB table called `DEPARTMENT_ID` with a more general partition key.

A data engineering team is automating the process to extract, transform, and load (ETL) gzip compressed JSON files from an Amazon S3 bucket. The gzip files are uploaded daily, with each having a total size of less than 30 MB. The goal is to implement the ETL pipeline in the most cost-effective way.

Which action should be taken to meet this requirement?

- Utilize AWS Glue on Apache Spark, writing the logic in Scala for data transformation.
- Use AWS Glue for Ray to write Python scripts for data transformation.
- Utilize AWS Glue on Apache Spark, using PySpark for data transformation.
- **Use an AWS Glue job using Python Shell and pandas for data transformation.**
    
    **AWS Glue** is a serverless data integration service that makes it easier to discover, prepare, and combine data for analytics, machine learning (ML), and application development. AWS Glue provides all the capabilities needed for data integration, so you can start analyzing your data and putting it to use in minutes instead of months. AWS Glue provides both visual and code-based interfaces to make data integration easier. Users can more easily find and access data using the AWS Glue Data Catalog. Data engineers and ETL (extract, transform, and load) developers can visually create, run, and monitor ETL workflows.
    
    AWS Glue offers different engines on which you can run jobs, providing flexibility for various data processing needs:
    
    **Spark:** AWS Glue on Apache Spark is designed for complex ETL jobs that require processing large volumes of data. Spark’s distributed computing model enables high-performance data transformation and analysis across clusters of computers. It’s ideal for tasks that need to handle big data sets, perform extensive data manipulation, or require the use of Spark’s rich ecosystem of libraries.
    
    **Ray:** AWS Glue for Ray is optimized for highly parallel and compute-intensive tasks. Ray is a framework that allows for easy scaling of applications from a laptop to a cluster, making it suitable for machine learning workflows, real-time data processing, and tasks that require concurrency.
    
    **Python Shell:** AWS Glue’s Python Shell is best suited for light to medium data transformation tasks that do not necessitate the distributed computing capabilities of Spark or Ray. It allows for simpler, script-based ETL jobs that can be executed without the overhead of setting up a Spark or Ray environment.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA2-28-glue-python-shell-030524.png)
    
    With AWS Glue, you only pay for the time that your ETL job takes to run. There are no resources to manage, no upfront costs, and you are not charged for startup or shutdown time. AWS charges an hourly rate based on the number of data processing units (DPUs) used to run your ETL job. A single standard DPU provides 4 vCPU and 16 GB of memory, whereas a high-memory DPU (M-DPU) provides 4 vCPU and 32 GB of memory.
    
    In simple ETL tasks, such as the given scenario of processing small gzip files, using AWS Glue’s Python Shell proves highly cost-effective. The data engineering team can opt to allocate only 1/16th of a DPU for the ETL job. This approach allows them to execute their tasks economically, paying only for the minimal computing power required and avoiding the higher expenses associated with more resource-intensive options like Spark or Ray.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use an AWS Glue job using Python Shell and pandas for data transformation.**
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue for Ray to write Python scripts for data transformation** is incorrect because Ray is generally used for large-scale parallel data processing, which might be overkill for processing files under 30 MB. Moreover, jobs that use AWS Glue for Ray are more expensive to run than those using Python Shell.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize AWS Glue on Apache Spark, using PySpark for data transformation** is incorrect. AWS Glue with Apache Spark using PySpark is a powerful option, but it’s not the most cost-effective for small files under 30MB. Spark is more suited for handling large-scale data processing, and the overhead it introduces might not be necessary for smaller files.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize AWS Glue on Apache Spark, writing the logic in Scala for data transformation** is incorrect. Similar to the other incorrect option, this solution runs on the Spark engine, which might be more than necessary for small file processing and can increase costs.
    

A multinational corporation has recently started using Amazon Redshift for its data warehousing needs. The company’s database contains a large `sales` table that stores all the transactions and a smaller `products` table containing product details. The `sales` table is frequently joined with the `products` table for generating various reports.

The management team has noticed that some queries are taking longer than expected to return results, especially those involving joins between the `sales` and `products` tables. The task is to optimize the performance of these queries.

Which distribution style should be recommended to optimize the performance of the join queries?

- Use the `EVEN` distribution style for both `sales` and `products` tables to distribute the rows evenly across all nodes in the cluster.
- Use the `ALL` distribution style for both `sales` and `products` tables to replicate all rows across all nodes in the cluster.
- Use the `KEY` distribution style for both `sales` and `products` tables based on the `product_id` and `id` columns respectively to distribute the rows across the nodes in the cluster.
- **Use the `KEY` distribution style for the `sales` table based on the `product_id` column and the `ALL`distribution style for the `products`table to replicate it across all nodes in the cluster.**
    
    Amazon Redshift is a data warehousing service that makes it simple and cost-effective to analyze all your data using standard SQL. One of the key features of Redshift is its ability to handle large amounts of data and complex queries by distributing data and query execution across multiple nodes. The method by which Redshift distributes the data across the nodes is called the distribution style. There are three types of distribution styles in Redshift: `KEY`, `ALL`, and `EVEN`.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/redshift-table-design-distkey-030524.gif)
    
    The `KEY` distribution style distributes the data across the nodes based on the values in one column. The rows with the same value in the distribution key column are located on the same node. This is beneficial when two tables are frequently joined on the distribution key column, as it allows for fast, local joins. The `ALL` distribution style replicates the entire table on every node. This is beneficial for small tables that are frequently joined with large tables, as it allows for fast, local joins. However, it uses a lot of storage space and can slow down data loading and update operations. The `EVEN` distribution style (the default) distributes the rows evenly across all nodes without considering the join patterns. This could lead to a lot of network I/O during the join operations, which could slow down the query performance.
    
    In the given scenario, the `sales` table is a large table that stores all the sales transactions and the `products` table is a smaller table that contains product details. The `sales` table is frequently joined with the `products` table to generate various reports. Therefore, using the `KEY` distribution style for the `sales` table based on the `product_id` column and the `ALL` distribution style for the `products` table to replicate it across all nodes in the cluster would be beneficial. This would ensure that the rows with the same product ID are located on the same node, allowing for fast, local joins and that the entire `products` table is available on every node.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use the `KEY` distribution style for the `sales` table based on the `product_id` column and the `ALL` distribution style for the `products` table to replicate it across all nodes in the cluster.**
    
    The option that says: **Use the `EVEN` distribution style for both `sales` and `products` tables to distribute the rows evenly across all nodes in the cluster** is incorrect because the `EVEN` distribution style simply distributes the rows evenly across all nodes in the cluster without considering the join patterns. This could lead to a lot of network I/O during the join operations, which could slow down the query performance.
    
    The option that says: **Use the `KEY` distribution style for both `sales` and `products` tables based on the `product_id` and `id` columns respectively to distribute the rows across the nodes in the cluster** is incorrect. While this approach can ensure related data resides on the same node, it does not consider the size disparity between the tables. For very large and smaller tables, replicating the smaller table across tables can further reduce the need for cross-node data movement during joins, optimizing performance more than matching `KEY`distributions alone.
    
    The option that says: **Use the `ALL` distribution style for both `sales` and `products` tables to replicate all rows across all nodes in the cluster** is incorrect. Using the `ALL` distribution style for both tables would result in a lot of storage space being used, as every row from both tables would be replicated on every node. This could also slow down the data loading and update operations. This distribution style is typically recommended for small dimension tables that are frequently joined with large fact tables.
    

A company is using Amazon Redshift for its data warehousing needs. The company noticed that over 5 TB of its stored content is infrequently accessed. The company wants to optimize costs and storage space for this data.

Which of the following options should the company choose to meet its requirements?

- Create an external table in Amazon Redshift Spectrum and load the data from Amazon Redshift using the `COPY`command.
- Store infrequently accessed data in Amazon Redshift.
- Use the `COPY` command to export data from Amazon Redshift to Amazon S3.
- **Use the `UNLOAD` command to export data from Amazon Redshift to Amazon S3 in Parquet format.**
    
    The `UNLOAD` command in Amazon Redshift allows you to unload the result of a query to Amazon S3. This command is particularly useful for exporting data that is not frequently accessed, thereby saving storage space in Redshift and reducing costs.
    
    The `UNLOAD` command supports exporting data in various formats, including CSV, JSON, and Parquet. Parquet is an efficient open columnar storage format for analytics. It is up to 2x faster to unload and consumes up to 6x less storage in Amazon S3 compared to text formats. This makes Parquet a good choice for storing large volumes of infrequently accessed data.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/unload-command-022524.png)
    
    Amazon S3 is a cost-effective storage service. The cost of storing data in S3 depends on the amount of data stored, the duration of storage, and the storage class. By exporting infrequently accessed data from Redshift to S3, the company can optimize its storage costs.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use the `UNLOAD`command to export data from Amazon Redshift to Amazon S3 in Parquet format.**
    
    The option that says: **Use the `COPY` command to export data from Amazon Redshift to Amazon S3** is incorrect because the `COPY` command is used to load data into Amazon Redshift from data files from an Amazon DynamoDB table. It’s not used to export data from Amazon Redshift.
    
    The option that says: **Store infrequently accessed data in Amazon Redshift** is incorrect because storing infrequently accessed data in Amazon Redshift can be expensive as Redshift is designed for high-performance analysis and not for storing infrequently accessed data.
    
    The option that says: **Create an external table in Amazon Redshift Spectrum and load the data from Amazon Redshift using the `COPY`command** is incorrect. Redshift Spectrum is simply a feature that allows you to query data directly in S3 using Redshift SQL queries without having to load the data into Redshift. You would typically use Redshift Spectrum to query data already in S3. Once the data is in S3, you can create external tables that reference this data. Redshift Spectrum does not have its own built-in storage; rather, it serves as a bridge for querying external data.
    

A tech startup uses various AWS services to collect, process, and store user activity data for its mobile payment service. Its data engineering team is tasked with designing a cost-effective solution utilizing Amazon S3, Amazon DynamoDB, and Amazon QuickSight.

The data from the last 60 days must be fetched from the DynamoDB table and displayed on a custom QuickSight dashboard for monthly analysis. The older user activity data must be archived in a data lake for 2 years before being purged. During this period, the startup is expected to occasionally query the data using Amazon Athena.

Which combination of steps provides the MOST cost-effective solution? (Select THREE.)

- **Use the Time to Live (TTL) feature to automatically delete items older than 60 days from the DynamoDB table.**
- Archive older user activity data using Amazon Redshift Serverless instead of Amazon S3. Fetch data using the Amazon Athena SQL query editor.
- Use Amazon EMR Serverless to query the data in the S3 data lake. Set the S3 Lifecycle policy to transition the older user activity data to the S3 Glacier Deep Archive storage class.
- Store all user activity data indefinitely in the DynamoDB table. Configure the table to use the DynamoDB Standard-Infrequent Access (Standard-IA) table class.
- **Set up an AWS Glue job to extract the last 60 days of data from the DynamoDB table and load it into Amazon QuickSight for visualization.**
- **Configure an Amazon S3 Lifecycle policy to transition user activity data older than 60 days to the S3 Infrequent Access (S3-IA) storage class and then permanently delete it after 2 years.**
- **Evaluation**
    
    Amazon DynamoDB TTL is a feature that allows you to define a per-item timestamp to determine when an item is no longer needed. Once the timestamp expires, the item is marked for deletion from your table. This is a cost-effective way to reduce storage usage by deleting items that are no longer needed. In this scenario, setting a TTL of 60 days for items in the DynamoDB table would ensure that only the most recent 60 days of data are stored in the table.
    
    AWS Glue is a fully managed ETL (extract, transform, and load) service that makes it easy to prepare and load your data for analytics. You can create and run an ETL job with a few clicks in the AWS Management Console. AWS Glue generates the ETL code that is customizable, reusable, and portable. Once your ETL job is ready, you can schedule it to run on AWS Glue’s fully managed, scale-out Apache Spark environment. AWS Glue provides a console and API operations to set up and manage your extract, transform, and load (ETL) workload. It also provides a Jupyter Notebook server you can use to develop your ETL scripts interactively.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/tutorialsdojo_data_catalog_dynamoDB.png)
    
    Amazon S3 offers storage management features that let you organize your data and configure finely-tuned access controls to meet specific business, organizational, and compliance requirements. Amazon S3 is designed for 99.999999999% (11 9’s) of durability and stores data for millions of applications for companies worldwide. S3 Lifecycle policies make transitioning data from S3 to Archive or deleting old data versions simple and cost-effective.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Use the Time to Live (TTL) feature to automatically delete items older than 60 days from the DynamoDB table.**
    
    **– Set up an AWS Glue job to extract the last 60 days of data from the DynamoDB table and load it into Amazon QuickSight for visualization.**
    
    **– Configure an Amazon S3 Lifecycle policy to transition user activity data older than 60 days to the S3 Infrequent Access (S3-IA) storage class and then permanently delete it after 2 years.**
    
    The option that says: **Archive older user activity data using Amazon Redshift Serverless instead of Amazon S3. Fetch data using the Amazon Athena SQL query editor** is incorrect because Amazon Redshift is primarily a data warehousing solution designed for online analytic processing (OLAP) and not as a data lake. The scenario explicitly mentions that the older user activity data must be archived in a data lake and not in a data warehouse. Amazon S3 is cheaper than Redshift for long-term data storage, making it a more cost-effective choice for archiving older user activity data.
    
    The option that says: **Store all user activity data indefinitely in the DynamoDB table. Configure the table to use the DynamoDB Standard-Infrequent Access (Standard-IA) table class** is incorrect because DynamoDB charges you for read and write capacity, as well as the amount of data stored; thus, storing data indefinitely will effectively increase the operating cost of the solution. Using a Standard-Infrequent Access (Standard-IA) table class is more suitable for data that is meant to be stored for a long time and not for items that are meant to expire in a period of 60 days. It’s more cost-effective to move older data to cheaper storage solutions like S3 and enable the DynamoDB table’s TTL feature for automatic data deletion.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon EMR Serverless to query the data in the S3 data lake. Set the S3 Lifecycle policy to transition the older user activity data to the S3 Glacier Deep Archive storage class** is incorrect. Although Amazon EMR can be used for querying data in an S3 data lake, the scenario specifies that the startup intends to use Amazon Athena for this purpose; therefore, using EMR would be unnecessary. While storing the data in S3 Glacier Deep Archive provides the cheapest storage option, it would entail a significant operational overhead since you have to restore the Glacier objects before you can access them.
    

A company has established a data pipeline to handle large volumes of data. The pipeline consists of an AWS Glue job that pulls data from a source S3 bucket, transforms it, and sends the result to a target S3 bucket.

A data engineer must configure permissions for the Glue job to access these S3 buckets, which reside in the same AWS account.

Which of the following options would meet the requirements with the LEAST amount of configuration effort?

- Use S3 Access Control Lists (ACLs) to manage permissions.
- Create an IAM user for AWS Glue and attach policies for S3 access.
- **Create an IAM role with necessary S3 permissions and assign this role to the AWS Glue service.**
    
    AWS Glue is a fully managed extract, transform, and load (ETL) service that makes it easy to prepare and load your data for analytics. To perform its operations, AWS Glue needs to interact with other AWS services on your behalf. This is achieved by using an IAM role that has the necessary permissions. When you create an IAM role for AWS Glue, you grant your IAM role permissions that AWS Glue can assume when calling other services on your behalf. This includes access to Amazon S3 for any sources, targets, scripts, and temporary directories that you use with AWS Glue.
    
    By creating a new IAM service role specifically for AWS Glue and attaching the necessary policies to this role, you can grant the necessary permissions to AWS Glue without affecting other services or users. This approach also aligns with the principle of least privilege, which is a best practice in IAM. This principle encourages you to grant only the permissions required to perform a task.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/glue-iam-role-012324.jpg)
    
    This approach allows you to separate the permissions for the source and destination S3 buckets by attaching different policies to the IAM role. For example, you could attach a policy that grants read access to the source bucket and a separate policy that grants write access to the destination bucket. This way, you can meet the requirement of separating the permissions for the source and destination S3 buckets.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create an IAM role with necessary S3 permissions and assign this role to the AWS Glue service.**
    
    The option that says: **Create an IAM user for AWS Glue and attach policies for S3 access** is incorrect. Glue jobs are designed to assume an IAM role. It’s not possible to assign IAM user’s credentials to AWS Glue.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon S3 bucket policies to grant access to AWS Glue** is incorrect. While this is a viable approach, using S3 bucket policies to grant access to the AWS Glue service requires separate bucket policies to be configured for both the source and target S3 buckets. With an IAM role, you can centralize the permission management, simplifying the configuration process.
    
    The option that says: **Use S3 Access Control Lists (ACLs) to manage permissions** is incorrect because ACLs are less flexible and harder to manage than IAM roles. ACLs provide a broad level of access control, but they don’t provide the same level of granularity as IAM roles. This can make it difficult to adhere to the principle of least privilege, as you might end up granting more permissions than necessary. Additionally, managing ACLs can be complex and error-prone, especially if you have many S3 buckets or objects.
    
- Use Amazon S3 bucket policies to grant access to AWS Glue.

A company that uses AWS Lake Formation for its data lake solution is in the process of consolidating all marketing data into a centralized data lake. During the process, the team encounters a `NotAuthorized` error while trying to create a new database and data catalog within Lake Formation. This error hinders the team from properly managing the data lake and creating databases.

What additional permissions, roles, or policies should the team be granted to resolve the issue? (Select TWO.)

- `IAMFullAccess`
- **Data Lake Administrator**
- Lake Formation Data Catalog Full Access
- **Database Creator**
- Lake Formation Data Analyst
- **Evaluation**
    
    The Data Lake Administrator role is an important permission in AWS Lake Formation. This permission allows a user to perform several important tasks related to managing a data lake. These tasks include registering Amazon S3 locations, accessing the Data Catalog, creating databases, creating and running workflows, granting Lake Formation permissions to other users, and viewing AWS CloudTrail logs. Without this permission, a user would not be able to perform these tasks, which are essential for managing a data lake in AWS Lake Formation.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/aws-lake-formation-permissions-030424.png)
    
    The database creator permission is another important permission in AWS Lake Formation. This permission specifically allows a user to create new databases within the data lake. Creating databases is a fundamental task when managing a data lake, as databases are used to organize and store the data within the lake. Without this permission, a user would not be able to create new databases, which could hinder their ability to manage the data lake effectively.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    – **Data Lake Administrator**
    
    – **Database Creator**
    
    The option that says: **`IAMFullAccess`** is incorrect. While this IAM policy provides broad permissions across AWS services, it does not specifically grant the necessary permissions to manage a data lake and create databases in AWS Lake Formation.
    
    The option that says: **Lake Formation Data Analyst** is incorrect because this role just represents a user who can run queries against the data lake such as Amazon Athena. However, this role does not necessarily have permission to manage the data lake or create databases, which are required to resolve the “NotAuthorized” error in this scenario.
    
    The option that says: **Lake Formation Data Catalog Full Access** is incorrect because this permission allows a user to access the data catalog in AWS Lake Formation. However, this permission does not include the ability to create databases or manage the data lake, which is required to resolve the “NotAuthorized” error in the given scenario.
    

A company is migrating data from its various on-premises file systems into an Amazon S3 data lake managed via the AWS Lake Formation service. To enhance analytics capabilities, the data engineer is tasked with creating a data ingestion pipeline that not only transfers data efficiently but also splits the data across multiple Amazon S3 buckets in near real-time to support different analytical workloads.

Which of the following is the MOST operationally efficient solution that the data engineer should implement?

- Initially transfer the data using AWS Snowcone and implement S3 Replication Time Control (S3 RTC) to manage the replication of data across S3 buckets efficiently.
- Use Amazon Data Firehose for real-time data streaming and automatic distribution to multiple S3 buckets.
- Set up S3 Batch Operations for bulk data management and distribution across multiple S3 buckets.
- **Utilize AWS DataSync for streamlined and fast data transfer to various S3 buckets.**
    
    AWS DataSync is specifically designed to accelerate the transfer of vast volumes of data from on-premises storage systems to AWS storage services such as Amazon S3. It supports fast, secure, and reliable data migration, making it an ideal choice for projects that require the distribution of data across multiple S3 buckets in near real-time. DataSync automates the replication process, optimizes the data transfer over the network, and seamlessly integrates with Amazon S3, allowing for the direct specification of multiple target buckets.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/AWS_DataSync_for_Amazon_S3_1March2024.png)
    
    To transfer data to or from your Amazon S3 bucket, you need to create an AWS DataSync transfer location. DataSync uses this location as a source or destination for transferring data. Keep in mind that DataSync needs access to the S3 bucket that you’re transferring to or from. To do this, you must create an AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) role that DataSync assumes with the permissions required to access the bucket. You can then specify this role when creating your Amazon S3 location for DataSync.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Utilize AWS DataSync for streamlined and fast data transfer to various S3 buckets.**
    
    The option that says: **Set up S3 Batch Operations for bulk data management and distribution across multiple S3 buckets** is incorrect because it is primarily used for operations on existing S3 objects rather than the initial upload and real-time distribution of data. It does not meet the requirement for real-time data ingestion and distribution.
    
    The option that says: **Initially transfer the data using AWS Snowcone and implement S3 Replication Time Control (S3 RTC) to manage the replication of data across S3 buckets efficiently** is incorrect. Physically transferring data via an AWS Snowcone device is not necessary since the transfer can be fully done online. Moreover, the S3 RTC feature simply focuses on enhancing the replication speed and predictability of S3 objects within AWS, which does not directly address the need for initial data migration and near real-time distribution to multiple buckets.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Data Firehose for real-time data streaming and automatic distribution to multiple S3 buckets** is incorrect. Although the Amazon Data Firehose service is capable of handling real-time data streaming and can automatically load streaming data into Amazon S3, this service is primarily designed for streaming use cases rather than bulk data migrations from on-premises environments. Thus, this solution does not fully meet the requirements for this specific scenario.
    

A large e-commerce company is leveraging an Amazon Kinesis stream to ingest real-time data from its website, such as user clicks and page views. A major sale event has been launched recently, which significantly increased the volume of data flowing into the Kinesis stream. The stream is unable to handle the increased data volume, causing data loss and increased latency.

Which of the following actions should be taken to handle the increased data volume and ensure that the Kinesis stream can ingest the data in real-time? (SELECT TWO.)

- Implement Step Scaling to the Kinesis Data Stream.
- Decrease the number of shards in the Kinesis stream to reduce complexity using the `MergeShard` command.
- Replace the data stream with Amazon Data Firehose instead.
- **Enable Kinesis Data Streams Enhanced Fan-Out and HTTP/2 data retrieval to reduce latency.**
- **Increase the number of shards in the Kinesis stream to handle the increased data volume using the `SplitShard`command.**
- **Evaluation**
    
    **Amazon Kinesis Data Streams**supports *resharding*, which lets you adjust the number of shards in your stream to adapt to changes in the rate of data flow through the stream. Resharding is considered an advanced operation.
    
    There are two types of resharding operations: shard split and shard merge. In a shard split, you divide a single shard into two shards. In a shard merge, you combine two shards into a single shard. Resharding is always *pairwise* in the sense that you cannot split into more than two shards in a single operation, and you cannot merge more than two shards in a single operation. The shard or pair of shards that the resharding operation acts on are referred to as *parent* shards. The shard or pair of shards that result from the resharding operation are referred to as *child* shards.
    
    The `SplitShard` command is used to increase the number of shards in a Kinesis data stream, which can help handle increased data volume. Each shard in a Kinesis data stream provides a fixed amount of capacity, so adding more shards increases the stream’s capacity to ingest and transport data.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/resharding-012424.png)
    
    Enhanced Fan-Out is a feature in Kinesis Data Streams that provides each consumer with its own 2 MB per second of dedicated bandwidth per shard, which can help handle increased data volume. Additionally, the HTTP/2 data retrieval API can reduce latency, making data delivery from producers to consumers faster.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/enhanced_fan-out-012424.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Increase the number of shards in the Kinesis stream to handle the increased data volume using the `SplitShard` command.**
    
    **– Enable Kinesis Data Streams Enhanced Fan-Out and HTTP/2 data retrieval to reduce latency.**
    
    The option that says: **Decrease the number of shards in the Kinesis stream to reduce complexity using the `MergeShard` command** is incorrect. The `MergeShard` command is used to reduce the number of shards in a Kinesis data stream. Reducing the number of shards would decrease the stream’s capacity to ingest and transport data, which is not suitable when the data volume has increased.
    
    The option that says: **Implement Step Scaling to the Kinesis Data Stream** is incorrect. While step scaling is a concept in AWS for adjusting capacity based on demand, it is not directly applicable to Kinesis Data Streams. Kinesis Data Streams are scaled using shards, and the number of shards can be adjusted using the `SplitShard` and `MergeShard` commands.
    
    The option that says: **Replace the data stream with Amazon Data Firehose instead** is incorrect because the throughput of Data Firehose is not exceptionally higher than Kinesis Data Streams. In fact, the throughput of an Amazon Kinesis data stream is designed to scale without limits by increasing the number of shards within a data stream.
    

A company is leveraging Amazon Redshift for its data warehousing needs. The data engineering team is tasked with running an exploratory data analysis (EDA) to prepare the data for a predictive model. The team needs to query relevant information from the Redshift cluster and export the results for further processing. The connection details for the Redshift cluster must be securely stored and retrieved.

Which of the following approaches would meet the requirements? (SelectTWO.)

- **Configure AWS Secrets Manager to store the connection details of the Redshift cluster. Use the `AWS SDK for pandas`to retrieve the secret values and connect to the Redshift cluster.**
- **Leverage Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler to query the necessary information from the Redshift cluster. After the exploratory data analysis (EDA) is complete, export the results to Amazon S3 for further processing.**
- Launch an Amazon EMR cluster and run an Apache Spark job to query relevant information from the Redshift cluster. Export the results to Amazon S3 for further processing.
- Use AWS Lambda to run the EDA queries on the Redshift cluster. Store the results in DynamoDB for further processing.
- Configure AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store to store the connection details of the Redshift cluster. Use Amazon Redshift Data API to connect to the Redshift cluster.
- **Evaluation**
    
    **Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler** reduces the time it takes to aggregate and prepare data for machine learning (ML) from weeks to minutes. With SageMaker Data Wrangler, you can simplify the process of data preparation and feature engineering, and complete each step of the data preparation workflow, including data selection, cleansing, exploration, and visualization from a single visual interface. After the exploratory data analysis (EDA) is complete, exporting the results to Amazon S3 for further processing aligns with the requirements of securely storing and retrieving data for predictive modeling. Amazon S3 has a simple web services interface that you can use to store and retrieve any amount of data at any time from anywhere on the web.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/sagemaker-data-wrangler-012924.jpg)
    
    AWS Secrets Manager protects access to your applications, services, and IT resources without the upfront investment and ongoing maintenance costs of operating your own infrastructure. This service enables you to easily rotate, manage, and retrieve database credentials, API keys, and other secrets throughout their lifecycle.
    
    **AWS SDK** **for** **pandas** (previously known as **awswrangler**) is an open-source Python project that bridges the pandas library with AWS data and analytics services, effectively expanding pandas’ capabilities into the AWS cloud ecosystem. This tool makes it straightforward to work with various AWS services (e.g, Athena, Glue, Redshift, Secrets Manager, S3) directly from pandas DataFrames. This includes support for a range of file formats such as Parquet, CSV, JSON, and EXCEL. By leveraging the AWS SDK for pandas, developers can write more efficient and streamlined code for a wide range of tasks, such as exploratory data analysis (EDA), data transformation, and loading (ETL) processes.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Leverage Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler to query the necessary information from the Redshift cluster. After the exploratory data analysis (EDA) is complete, export the results to Amazon S3 for further processing.**
    
    **– Configure AWS Secrets Manager to store the connection details of the Redshift cluster. Use the `AWS SDK for pandas`to retrieve the secret values and connect to the Redshift cluster.**
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Lambda to run the EDA queries on the Redshift cluster. Store the results in DynamoDB for further processing** is incorrect because DynamoDB is a NoSQL database service, which is not suitable for storing EDA results that are typically structured and relational in nature.
    
    The option that says: **Configure AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store to store the connection details of the Redshift cluster. Use Amazon Redshift Data API to connect to the Redshift cluster** is incorrect. While you can use the AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store to store sensitive data like database credentials, it does not have a native integration with Redshift Data API, unlike Secrets Manager. In AWS, Secrets Manager is more preferable for storing database credentials due to its automated secrets rotation and tight integration with AWS database services.
    
    The option that says: **Launch an Amazon EMR cluster and run an Apache Spark job to query relevant information from the Redshift cluster. Export the results to Amazon S3 for further processing** is incorrect. While EMR and Spark are powerful tools for big data processing, they are not designed to interact directly with Amazon Redshift for the purpose of running EDA.
    

A manufacturing company is in the process of establishing a cloud-based data analytics solution using an Amazon Redshift data warehouse. This new setup will empower the company to conduct an in-depth bi-weekly analysis of its production efficiency and inventory levels.

Due to strict regulatory requirements, the company seeks an effective method to oversee and document all activities within its Amazon Redshift resource. The aim is to systematically record SQL queries, user actions, and alterations to databases and database objects for audit and traceability purposes.

Which solution would satisfy these requirements with the LEAST amount of operational overhead involved?

- Integrate Amazon CloudWatch Logs with Amazon Redshift to collect Redshift SQL query logs. Utilize Amazon CloudWatch alarms and Amazon SNS to alert on any anomalies.
- Parse Amazon CloudWatch Logs from the Redshift cluster using an AWS Lambda function. Store logs in Amazon OpenSearch Service for detailed analysis.
- Utilize the AWS Config for continuous monitoring of Amazon Redshift clusters' logging features to maintain compliance with regulations.
- **Enable Amazon Redshift's built-in audit logging feature to capture detailed database transactions, including SQL query executions and user activities. Integrate it with an AWS CloudTrail to enhance tracking of Redshift API calls.**
    
    **Amazon Redshift** keeps a record of all connections and user activities in your database, which is commonly known as database auditing. These logs are essential for security and troubleshooting purposes. They are stored in Amazon S3 buckets, which provide easy access and data security features for those who monitor database activities.
    
    Amazon Redshift creates three types of log files containing different information:
    
    **Connection log** – records all authentication attempts, connections, and disconnections.
    
    **User log** – keeps track of changes to database user definitions.
    
    **User activity log** – records each query before it is run on the database.
    
    The user activity log is handy for troubleshooting since it provides information about the types of queries the users and the system perform in the database.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-DEA2-35-Amazon-Redshift-Audit_logging.png)
    
    While the connection and user log contain the same information as the system tables, the log files provide an easier way to retrieve and review the information. You need database permissions to query the system tables, while the log files rely on Amazon S3 permissions. Viewing the information in log files instead of querying system tables also helps reduce the impact of interacting with the database.
    
    AWS CloudTrail and Amazon Redshift integration offers detailed records of Redshift API calls, including API caller identity, time, source IP address, request parameters, and response elements. This integration provides a comprehensive audit trail that includes the actions taken within the Redshift cluster and API interactions that affect the cluster’s configuration or data query. The built-in audit logging and CloudTrail integration create a robust monitoring solution that secures and audits a Redshift environment, ensuring compliance obligations are met, and data is safeguarded. This dual approach simplifies audit log management and enhances the visibility of operations within and around the Redshift clusters, making it easier to take a proactive stance on security and compliance.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Enable Amazon Redshift’s built-in audit logging feature to capture detailed database transactions, including SQL query executions and user activities. Integrate it with an AWS CloudTrail to enhance tracking of Redshift API calls.**
    
    The option that says: **Parse Amazon CloudWatch Logs from the Redshift cluster using an AWS Lambda function. Store logs in Amazon OpenSearch Service for detailed analysis** is incorrect because using AWS Lambda and integrating it with Amazon OpenSearch Service for parsing and storing logs can be complex and require custom development and maintenance. Moreover, CloudWatch Log entries for Redshift do not entirely capture SQL query executions, user activities, and other things that are fully covered by the built-in Audit Logging feature.
    
    The option that says: **Integrate Amazon CloudWatch Logs with Amazon Redshift to collect Redshift SQL query logs. Utilize Amazon CloudWatch alarms and Amazon SNS to alert on any anomalies** is incorrect. This approach mainly focuses on collecting SQL query logs and creating alerts for anomalies, which covers only a part of the monitoring requirements. However, it doesn’t fulfill the need to document all user activities and changes made to databases and their objects in a way that is easy to audit for compliance purposes. Using the built-in Audit Logging feature is still the best option than using a combination of CloudWatch Logs and Alarms.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize the AWS Config for continuous monitoring of Amazon Redshift clusters’ logging features to maintain compliance with regulations** is incorrect. AWS Config is a service for assessing and monitoring AWS resource configurations over time, but it doesn’t capture detailed transactional audit logs in Redshift. It may not be suitable for the company’s need to analyze production efficiency and inventory levels.
    

A data engineer is working on a machine learning project that requires real-time feature access for model predictions and batch feature access for training models. The engineer decides to use Amazon SageMaker Feature Store for managing the features.

Which mode of Amazon SageMaker Feature Store should the engineer choose to meet the requirements of both real-time feature access and batch feature access?

- Batch
- **Online and Offline**
    
    In Feature Store, features are stored in a collection called a *feature group*. You can visualize a feature group as a table in which each column is a feature, with a unique identifier for each row. In principle, a feature group is composed of features and values specific to each feature. A `Record` is a collection of values for features that correspond to a unique `RecordIdentifier`. Altogether, a `FeatureGroup` is a group of features defined in your `FeatureStore` to describe a `Record`.
    
    You can use Feature Store in the following modes:
    
    **Online** – In online mode, features are read with low latency (milliseconds) reads and used for high throughput predictions. This mode requires a feature group to be stored in an online store.
    
    **Offline** – In offline mode, large streams of data are fed to an offline store, which can be used for training and batch inference. This mode requires a feature group to be stored in an offline store. The offline store uses your S3 bucket for storage and can also fetch data using Athena queries.
    
    **Online and Offline** – This includes both online and offline modes.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Amazon-SageMaker-Feature-Store-012624.png)
    
    You can ingest data into feature groups in Feature Store in two ways: streaming or in batches. When you ingest data through streaming, a collection of records are pushed to the Feature Store by calling a synchronous `PutRecord` API call. This API enables you to maintain the latest feature values in the Feature Store and to push new feature values as soon as an update is detected.
    
    Alternatively, Feature Store can process and ingest data in batches. You can author features using Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler, create feature groups in Feature Store, and ingest features in batches using a SageMaker Processing job with a notebook exported from Data Wrangler. This mode allows for batch ingestion into the offline store. It also supports ingestion into the online store if the feature group is configured for both online and offline use.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Online and Offline.**
    
    **Online** is incorrect because it only provides low-latency real-time access to features, which is suitable for model predictions. However, it does not support batch feature access, which is needed for training models.
    
    **Offline** is incorrect because it only supports batch feature access, which is suitable for training models. However, it does not provide real-time feature access, which is needed for model predictions.
    
    **Batch** is incorrect because there is no “Batch” mode in the Amazon SageMaker Feature Store. The Offline store provides batch feature access, but it does not provide real-time feature access.
    
- Offline
- Online

A company is building a serverless data pipeline to process and analyze streaming data with AWS. The pipeline mainly consists of several Lambda functions orchestrated by AWS Step Functions. The company seeks a way to create, manage, and deploy its pipeline with minimal overhead.

Which solution meets these requirements?

- Configure AWS Systems Manager (SSM) to automate the management and deployment of serverless components.
- Leverage AWS CodeBuild for compiling source code, running tests, and producing software packages ready to deploy.
- Utilize AWS CloudFormation to define and deploy serverless resources through infrastructure such as Code (IaC).
- **Use AWS Serverless Application Model (SAM) to define, package, and deploy serverless applications and resources.**
    
    The **AWS Serverless Application Model (AWS SAM)** is an essential framework that simplifies the process of developing serverless applications. It is an open-source tool that uses a concise syntax to represent the fundamental components of serverless architectures, such as functions, APIs, databases, and event source mappings. With AWS SAM, developers can define their serverless applications using a minimal amount of code by using YAML for the modeling process. The integration of AWS SAM with AWS CloudFormation provides an effortless way of defining crucial elements of serverless applications, including Amazon API Gateway APIs, AWS Lambda functions, and Amazon DynamoDB tables.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-DEA2-63-AWS-SAM.png)
    
    In the scenario, using SAM leads to writing more concise code for serverless resources like AWS Lambda and AWS Step Functions. This conciseness simplifies infrastructure management and deployment. Additionally, SAM enables local testing of Lambda functions, which speeds up development by allowing for fast iterations and immediate feedback without deploying to AWS. This set of features streamlines the development and deployment process of serverless data pipelines.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use AWS Serverless Application Model (SAM) to define, package, and deploy serverless applications and resources.**
    
    The option that says: **Utilize AWS CloudFormation for defining and deploying serverless resources through Infrastructure as Code (IaC)** is incorrect. While it’s a valid solution writing native CloudFormation resources requires more verbose and detailed definitions compared to AWS SAM, especially when it comes to serverless resources like Lambda functions and Step Functions.
    
    The option that says: **Configure AWS Systems Manager (SSM) to automate the management and deployment of serverless components** is incorrect. AWS Systems Manager (SSM) is just a management service that helps you automatically apply operational actions across your AWS resources. This service primarily focuses on operational insights, management, and resource automation once deployed and not on deploying serverless applications.
    
    The option that says: **Leverage AWS CodeBuild for compiling source code, running tests, and producing software packages ready to deploy** is incorrect. AWS CodeBuild focuses on handling the building stage of your application lifecycle rather than defining, packaging, and deploying serverless resources.
    

A large-scale data analytics company is migrating their big data platform to Amazon Web Services (AWS). They have chosen to use Amazon Elastic MapReduce (EMR) for processing large datasets and Amazon Aurora as a Hive metastore.

The company’s data engineers need to configure Amazon EMR to use Amazon Aurora as a Hive metastore. This setup will allow the metadata to persist even after the EMR cluster is terminated, enabling cost savings and improved query performance.

Which of the following steps need to be taken? (SELECT THREE.)

- Ensure that you do not write to the same metastore table concurrently.
- **Set JDBC configuration values in hive-site.xml.**
- **Create a MySQL or PostgreSQL Aurora database.**
- **Modify security groups to allow JDBC connections.**
- Set up cross-account access for EMR Serverless and AWS Glue Data Catalog.
- Configure the AWS Glue Data Catalog.
- **Evaluation**
    
    Amazon EMR uses an external database to store metadata about the tables, schema, and other Hive data structures. The company’s data engineers would need to create an Amazon Aurora database (either MySQL or PostgreSQL) that will serve as the Hive metastore. This is a crucial step because the Hive metastore is a central repository of Hive metadata. It persists metadata for Hive tables and partitions in a relational database, and provides high availability, and ease of use.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/external-metastore-for-Hive-012624-1.png)
    
    Hive uses JDBC to connect to the metastore database. The data engineers would need to modify the security groups of both the Amazon EMR cluster and the Amazon Aurora database to allow these connections. This is important because the security group acts like a virtual firewall that controls the traffic for one or more instances. When you launch an instance, you can associate one or more security groups with the instance. You add rules to each security group that allow traffic to or from its associated instances.
    
    Set JDBC configuration values in hive-site.xml. This involves creating a configuration file called `hiveConfiguration.json` containing edits to `hive-site.xml`. This file specifies the JDBC connection parameters that Hive uses to connect to the metastore database. The data engineers would need to set the appropriate JDBC configuration values in this file to point to the Amazon Aurora database. This is necessary because the `hive-site.xml` file is where Hive reads the configuration for the metastore connection, among other settings. The JDBC connection parameters that Hive uses to connect to the metastore database are defined in this file.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Create a MySQL or PostgreSQL Aurora database.**
    
    **– Modify security groups to allow JDBC connections.**
    
    **– Set JDBC configuration values in hive-site.xml.**
    
    The option that says: **Configure the AWS Glue Data Catalog** is incorrect because it is not relevant when using Amazon Aurora as the Hive metastore. AWS Glue Data Catalog is an alternative to using an external Hive metastore, but it’s not used in conjunction with Aurora for this purpose.
    
    The option that says: **Set up cross-account access for EMR Serverless and AWS Glue Data Catalog** is incorrect because it is also not relevant when using Amazon Aurora as the Hive metastore. This would be necessary if you were using the AWS Glue Data Catalog and needed to access it from a different AWS account, but it’s not applicable in this scenario.
    
    The option that says: **Ensure that you do not write to the same metastore table concurrently** is incorrect because this is just a cautionary step to avoid data conflicts when sharing metastore information between two clusters. While it’s good practice to avoid concurrent writes, it’s not a step in the process of configuring Amazon EMR to use Amazon Aurora as a Hive metastore. It’s more of a usage guideline after the setup is complete. The data engineers would need to be aware of this guideline when using the Hive metastore, but it’s not a step they would need to take to set up the metastore.
    

A company needs to periodically assess air quality changes in a particular city. The company recently launched a serverless data pipeline on AWS that feeds data into an Amazon QuickSight dashboard.

After a month, the data engineering team noticed that the dashboard data was obsolete and did not refresh due to the data pipeline’s failure to execute every week. To solve this issue, the team must put in place a system to orchestrate the data workflow properly.

Which solution can fulfill these requirements with the LEAST operational effort?

- Implement Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (MWAA) for both orchestration and weekly task scheduling.
- **Utilize AWS Step Functions for workflow orchestration and Amazon EventBridge for the weekly scheduling of tasks.**
    
    **AWS Step Functions** serves as a serverless function orchestrator, enabling the design and execution of complex workflows across AWS services. It simplifies the automation of data pipelines by managing tasks, ensuring they execute in order, handling errors, and retrying as needed. This service is instrumental in orchestrating tasks that transform, analyze, and store data, providing visibility and control over each execution step.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/AWS_Step_Functions_&_Amazon_EventBridge_1March2024.png)
    
    **Amazon EventBridge** offers a serverless event bus that connects applications with data from various sources. It schedules and triggers workflows automatically, such as those orchestrated by AWS Step Functions, based on a predefined schedule using cron or rate expressions. EventBridge facilitates the automation of the data pipeline’s execution on a weekly basis without the need for manual intervention, aligning with the requirement for operational simplicity.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Utilize AWS Step Functions for workflow orchestration and Amazon EventBridge for the weekly scheduling of tasks.**
    
    The option that says: **Implement Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (MWAA) for both orchestration and weekly task scheduling** is incorrect because it simply introduces a higher operational and maintenance overhead compared to the serverless solutions provided by AWS Step Functions and Amazon EventBridge, making it less ideal for the specified requirements.
    
    The option that says: **Configure Amazon S3 Events to trigger an AWS Lambda function, which in turn schedules an AWS Glue job weekly** is incorrect because it only complicates the workflow with unnecessary intermediaries and manual setup steps, deviating from the goal of operational simplicity and automation. Using a combination of AWS Step Functions and Amazon EventBridge should suffice.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS CodePipeline for orchestration tasks, complemented by Amazon EventBridge for the pipeline’s weekly scheduling** is incorrect because the AWS CodePipeline service is primarily tailored more towards continuous integration and delivery rather than data pipeline orchestration. This makes it less suitable for the task compared to the more tailored and integrated capabilities of AWS Step Functions and EventBridge for data workflows.
    
- Configure Amazon S3 Events to trigger an AWS Lambda function, which in turn schedules an AWS Glue job weekly.
- Use AWS CodePipeline for orchestration tasks, complemented by Amazon EventBridge for the pipeline's weekly scheduling.

A company is planning to migrate its centralized metadata store for Apache Hive to AWS. The metadata store exposes S3 objects as tables that can be used by all data analytics teams running their own Amazon EMR clusters. Each team has its own metadata for running different SQL queries using Hive.

Which solution would be the most suitable for this task?

- Use Amazon Athena to query data directly from S3 using standard SQL.
- Use EMR File System (EMRFS) as the external metastore for Hive.
- **Use AWS Glue Data Catalog as the external metastore for Hive.**
    
    **AWS Glue Data Catalog** is a fully-managed service that serves as a centralized metadata repository. It can be used as a drop-in replacement for the Apache Hive Metastore, providing a unified metadata repository across various services. This is crucial when you have multiple data analytics teams each running their own Amazon EMR clusters and using their own metadata for running different SQL queries using Hive.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/emr-aws-glue-data-catalog-hive-011024.png)
    
    One of the key features of AWS Glue Data Catalog is its ability to crawl data sources to discover schemas and populate your Catalog with new and modified table and partition definitions. This means that it can automatically keep the metadata up-to-date, reducing the need for manual management.
    
    Furthermore, AWS Glue Data Catalog maintains schema versioning. This means that it keeps track of all changes to the metadata, allowing you to easily revert to a previous version if necessary.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use AWS Glue Data Catalog as the external metastore for Hive.**
    
    The option that says: **Use EMR File System (EMRFS) as the external metastore for Hive** is incorrect because EMRFS is an Amazon S3-compatible file system that you can use with Amazon EMR. It is not designed to serve as an external metastore for Hive.
    
    The option that says: **Use EMR notebook to extract metadata from your Hive metastore, then load the metadata and update the AWS Glue Data Catalog** is incorrect because EMR notebooks are primarily used for creating and sharing documents that contain live code, equations, visualizations, and narrative text, not for extracting metadata from Hive metastore.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Athena to query data directly from S3 using standard SQL** is incorrect because Amazon Athena allows you to query data directly from S3 using standard SQL, bypassing the need for a traditional data warehouse. However, this does not provide a centralized metadata store solution.
    
- Use EMR notebook to extract metadata from your Hive metastore, then load the metadata and update the AWS Glue Data Catalog.

A data engineer uses an on-premises application to process messages from an Amazon Simple Queue Service (SQS) queue. The application has retry logic implemented to address message processing failures.

However, due to a network outage, many messages failed to process and ended up in the dead letter queue. The engineer wants to mitigate the impact of similar network disruptions in the future.

Which solution should the engineer implement to meet the requirement?

- Adjust the `receiveMessageWaitTimeSeconds`setting for the SQS queue.
- Decrease the `visibilityTimeout`setting for the SQS queue.
- Increase the `delaySeconds` setting for the SQS queue.
- **Increase the `maxReceiveCount` setting for the SQS queue.**
    
    **Amazon SQS** is a fully managed message queuing service that provides a virtual queue acting as a buffer between the components of an application. It enables you to decouple and scale microservices, distributed systems, and serverless applications.
    
    **`DelaySeconds`** setting refers to the delay period for a delay queue. This sets how long any messages sent to the queue remain invisible to consumers after being added to the queue. The default is 0 seconds, and the maximum is 15 minutes.
    
    **`VisibilityTimeout`** is the period of time during which Amazon SQS prevents other consuming components from receiving and processing a message after it has been received from a queue (but not yet deleted). This helps prevent a message from being received and processed more than once.
    
    **`MaxReceiveCount`** refers to the number of times a message can be received from the queue before being deleted. When the limit is reached, the message will be sent to the dead letter queue. The default Maximum received is 10.
    
    **`ReceiveMessageWaitTimeSeconds`** is an optional parameter that sets the duration (in seconds) for waiting for a message to arrive in the queue.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/dea2-65-amazon-sqs-maxreceivecount-MAR-08-24.png)
    
    To reduce the number of premature messages sent to the DLQ, the engineer should increase the `MaxReceiveCount` setting for the SQS queue (In the AWS Management Console, it is the same with the option labeled “Maximum receives”). By increasing the `MaxReceiveCount`, it allows the on-premises application to retry processing messages more times before they are sent to the DLQ. This gives the application more opportunities to successfully process messages even during brief network issues.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Increase the `MaxReceiveCount` setting for the SQS queue.**
    
    The option that says: **Increase the `DelaySeconds` setting for the SQS queue** is incorrect because delaying message delivery won’t effectively handle transient failures. It merely postpones the delivery of messages without providing additional attempts for message processing.
