    The option that says: **Decrease the `VisibilityTimeout` setting for the SQS queue** is incorrect. Lowering this timeout can lead to messages becoming available to other consumers too quickly before the initial processing attempt can be retried, exacerbating the problem.
    
    The option that says: **Adjust the `ReceiveMessageWaitTimeSeconds` setting for the SQS queue** is incorrect. While long polling can reduce the number of empty responses from SQS by waiting for messages to arrive, it does not influence the retry behavior or the movement of messages to the DLQ.
    

A financial company uses an Amazon Aurora database for its loan approval system. When a loan application’s status is updated in the database, different processing systems must be triggered to execute various tasks.

Which option would best meet the requirements?

- Create an Amazon EventBridge rule to detect changes in loan status within the database. Configure the rule to trigger a Lambda function to send approval events to an Amazon SQS queue. Update the systems to read from the queue.
- Configure Amazon Aurora to publish messages directly on an SNS topic upon loan approval. Subscribe a Lambda function to this topic. Configure the function to forward messages to an Amazon SQS queue for processing by the systems.
- **Create a stored procedure or native function that invokes a Lambda function upon loan status updates. Configure the Lambda function to forward the approval event to an Amazon SQS queue for subsequent processing actions of the systems.**
    
    You can leverage AWS Lambda functions to manage data changes. When a loan application is approved, the corresponding data needs to be removed from the current applications and forwarded to a distributed processing system. This can be achieved by invoking an AWS Lambda function from an Amazon Aurora MySQL-Compatible Edition DB cluster using a native function or a stored procedure. This approach is particularly useful when you want to integrate your Aurora MySQL database with other AWS services. For instance, you might want to capture data changes whenever a row in a table is modified in your database.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/auroara-lambda-sqs-032224-1.png)
    
    In this scenario, you can set up a trigger that activates a Lambda function whenever a loan is approved. The Lambda function can then be programmed to remove the approved loan data from the current applications in the Aurora database and send the removed loan data to an Amazon Simple Queue Service (SQS) queue. The distributed processing system can then consume the loan data from the SQS queue at its own pace, effectively achieving the requirement of forwarding approved loan data from the Aurora database to the processing system.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create a stored procedure or native function that invokes a Lambda function upon loan status updates. Configure the Lambda function to forward the approval event to an Amazon SQS queue for subsequent processing actions of the systems.**
    
    The option that says: **Set up an RDS event subscription to trigger a Lambda function upon loan status update. Configure the Lambda function to send the approval event to an Amazon SQS queue. Update the systems to read from the queue** is incorrect. RDS Event Subscriptions can only notify you of operational events such as instance status changes. They can’t monitor table-level events like inserts or updates, which the scenario requires.
    
    The option that says: **Create an Amazon EventBridge rule to detect changes in loan status within the database. Configure the rule to trigger a Lambda function to send approval events to an Amazon SQS queue. Update the systems to read from the queue** is incorrect. EventBridge doesn’t directly monitor changes at the data level within Aurora databases. It would require some mechanism to emit those changes as events, which isn’t natively supported in Amazon Aurora.
    
    The option that says: **Configure Amazon Aurora to publish messages directly on an SNS topic upon loan approval. Subscribe a Lambda function to this topic. Configure the function to forward messages to an Amazon SQS queue for processing by the systems** is incorrect. While Amazon Aurora supports invoking AWS Lambda functions based on database activity, it’s not capable of directly publishing events to SNS topics without a Lambda function acting as an intermediary.
    
- Set up an RDS event subscription to trigger a Lambda function upon loan status update. Configure the Lambda function to send the approval event to an Amazon SQS queue. Update the systems to read from the queue.

A company has expanded its AWS infrastructure, utilizing Amazon S3 for data storage and Amazon Athena for query execution. With growth, the complexity and volume of data have increased, leading to the adoption of Amazon EMR for advanced data processing using Apache Spark and machine learning libraries.

The data collection is cataloged on both a Hadoop Distributed File System (HDFS) via an Apache Hive metastore and a custom AWS Glue Data Catalog. The data engineering team faces the challenge of designing a durable data store that utilizes both storage systems.

To achieve a centralized data management strategy, which approach should the data engineering team adopt with the LEAST migration effort?

- Centralize all data in Amazon S3. Configure Amazon EMR to integrate with AWS Glue Data Catalog and adopt Spark SQL for Athena queries. Maintain the use of Amazon EMR for ongoing data operations.
- **Migrate all data from Apache Hive to an Amazon S3 bucket to create a centralized data source data and utilize AWS Glue Data Catalog for metadata storage. Leverage Amazon EMR and Athena for data processing.**
    
    The AWS Glue Data Catalog serves as a centralized metadata repository, containing references to data employed as both origins and destinations for extract, transform, and load (ETL) tasks within AWS Glue. This catalog facilitates more efficient management of data sources, further enhanced by its compatibility with Amazon EMR, Amazon Athena, and other AWS services. Leveraging Amazon EMR, which supports various processing frameworks such as Apache Spark, expands capabilities for advanced data processing tasks, including batch processing, streaming, and machine learning.
    
    A database in the AWS Glue Data Catalog is a container that holds tables. You use databases to organize your tables into separate categories. Databases are created when you run a crawler or add a table manually. The database list in the AWS Glue console displays descriptions for all your databases.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/tutorialsdojo_aws_glue_17MAR2024_data_catalog.png)
    
    The latest versions of Amazon EMR allow you to configure Apache Hive to use the AWS Glue Data Catalog as its metastore. This configuration is recommended when you require a persistent metastore or a metastore shared by different clusters, services, applications, or AWS accounts.
    
    Companies might want to create AWS Glue Data Catalog tables manually and then keep them updated with AWS Glue crawlers. Crawlers running on a schedule can add new partitions and update the tables with any schema changes. This also applies to tables migrated from an Apache Hive metastore.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Migrate all data from Apache Hive to an Amazon S3 bucket to create a centralized data source data and utilize AWS Glue Data Catalog for metadata storage. Leverage Amazon EMR and Athena for data processing.**
    
    The option that says: **Consolidate all data in HDFS by moving the data from Amazon S3. Transition Athena queries to Presto on Amazon EMR. Continue utilizing Amazon EMR for data processing** is incorrect because it simply overlooks the primary concern of data durability. HDFS, when used within EMR, is considered an ephemeral storage that could potentially lead to data loss in the event of cluster termination. Additionally, this approach entails significant migration effort to shift from Athena to Presto, increasing complexity without addressing the need for a centralized data catalog.
    
    The option that says: **Centralize all data in Amazon S3. Configure Amazon EMR to integrate with AWS Glue Data Catalog and adopt Spark SQL for Athena queries. Maintain the use of Amazon EMR for ongoing data operations** is incorrect because it mainly suggests migrating Athena queries to Spark SQL. While integrating Amazon S3 with AWS Glue Data Catalog and Amazon EMR is beneficial, this move is not necessary as Athena can directly support SQL queries on data stored in S3 without the need for conversion to Spark SQL, making this step unnecessary and potentially adding complexity.
    
    The option that says: **Move all data stored in Amazon S3 to HDFS. Convert Athena queries to Spark SQL, and use EMR File System (EMRFS) for data processing tasks** is incorrect because it primarily relies on HDFS for centralized data storage, which does not offer the same level of durability and scalability as Amazon S3. This option also requires converting Athena queries to Spark SQL, which adds unnecessary migration effort and complexity without providing a clear benefit over using Athena for direct queries on data stored in Amazon S3. Moreover, EMRFS simply provides the convenience of storing persistent data in Amazon S3 for Amazon EMR and not for running data processing tasks.
    
- Move all data stored in Amazon S3 to HDFS. Convert Athena queries to Spark SQL, and use EMR File System (EMRFS) for data processing tasks.
- Consolidate all data in HDFS by moving the data from Amazon S3. Transition Athena queries to Presto on Amazon EMR. Continue utilizing Amazon EMR for data processing.

A company runs an on-premises data processing system that uses an Apache Hadoop cluster to execute various batch processing jobs written in several open-source frameworks. These frameworks include Apache Spark, Apache MXNet, Apache HBase, Apache Hudi, Presto, et cetera. Due to an anticipated surge in data storage needs, the corporation is considering migrating to AWS to leverage enhanced storage scalability and durability while continuing to utilize its existing job scripts.

Which solution will effectively fulfill these criteria at the LOWEST cost?

- Migrate to Amazon EMR and centralize data storage on Amazon S3. Utilize persistent EMR clusters for job execution.
- **Transition the on-premises Apache Hadoop cluster to Amazon EMR. Centralize the data storage on Amazon S3 and initiate transient EMR clusters for task execution.**
    
    **Amazon EMR** is a managed service that allows you to process big data workloads using popular frameworks such as Apache Hadoop, Spark, HBase, and others. By migrating your on-premises Apache Hadoop cluster to Amazon EMR and storing your data centrally on Amazon S3, you can take advantage of AWS services’ scalability, durability, and cost-effectiveness. Amazon S3 is a highly durable and scalable storage service that provides a cost-effective solution for storing large amounts of data. Utilizing Amazon EMR allows you to execute existing job scripts with minimal modifications while leveraging the managed scaling and cluster management features. Additionally, Amazon EMR supports transient clusters that can be created on demand for specific jobs and terminated once the job is completed. This feature can offer significant cost savings over persistent clusters that run continuously.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-DEA2-34-Amazon-EMR.png)
    
    Initiating transient EMR clusters for task execution aligns to minimize costs while meeting the surge in data storage and processing requirements. Transient clusters are created for the duration of specific tasks and terminated afterward, eliminating the cost of running idle resources. This approach, combined with storing data in Amazon S3, takes full advantage of AWS’s pay-as-you-go pricing model, ensuring that storage is scalable and cost-efficient.
    
    Centralizing data in Amazon S3 also simplifies data management and enhances data durability and availability. This solution meets the company’s immediate needs for scalability and durability. It does so in the most cost-effective manner possible, leveraging the best of what AWS offers for big data processing.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Transition the on-premises Apache Hadoop cluster to Amazon EMR. Centralize the data storage on Amazon S3 and initiate transient EMR clusters for task execution.**
    
    The option that says: **Migrate to Amazon EMR and centralize data storage on Amazon S3. Utilize persistent EMR clusters for job execution** is incorrect. Using persistent EMR clusters for job execution is more expensive than using transient clusters that are terminated after the jobs are completed.
    
    The option that says: **Transition to Amazon EMR with a complete data migration to Hadoop Distributed File System (HDFS). Expand the cluster with additional core nodes as storage demands increase** is incorrect. This approach involves higher costs due to the need for additional core nodes to expand the cluster, and it doesn’t utilize the cost-effectiveness and scalability of Amazon S3 for storage.
    
    The option that says: **Move into Amazon EMR. Configure to store the active data within HDFS and archival data in Amazon S3. Utilize Apache Hive for table integration across S3 and HDFS** is incorrect. Although Amazon S3 is used for archival storage, Hadoop Distributed File System (HDFS) requires EMR cluster resources to maintain active data; thus, it leads to higher costs compared to a solution that fully utilizes Amazon S3 for storage.
    
- Move into Amazon EMR. Configure to store the active data within HDFS and archival data in Amazon S3. Utilize Apache Hive for table integration across S3 and HDFS.
- Transition to Amazon EMR with a complete data migration to Hadoop Distributed File System (HDFS). Expand the cluster with additional core nodes as storage demands increase.

A data engineer is working with a company that heavily relies on big data analytics. The company uses an Amazon EMR cluster running Apache Hive to query data stored on Hadoop Distributed File System (HDFS). The data on HDFS is stored in a partitioned format in a table named `sales_data`, partitioned by date (e.g., year=2024/month=01/day=01).

When a new batch of data for the date 2024/01/02 is added directly to HDFS using the `hadoop fs -put` command, it is noticed that the new partition is not visible through Hive queries on the `sales_data` table.

What action can be taken to resolve this issue and make the new partition visible in Hive queries?

- Run `ALTER TABLE sales_data DROP PARTITION (year='2024', month='01', day='02')`, then recreate the partition.
- Restart the EMR cluster.
- Delete and re-upload the data to HDFS.
- **Run `MSCK REPAIR TABLE sales_data`.**
    
    The `MSCK REPAIR TABLE` command is used to synchronize the metadata of a table with the actual data layout in the file system. When new data is added directly to HDFS, Hive is not aware of the new partitions. This is because Hive requires metadata about partitions to be updated in its metastore, which doesn’t happen when data is added directly to HDFS.
    
    Running `MSCK REPAIR TABLE` scans the file system for new partitions that were added after the table was created. It then compares the partitions in the table metadata and the partitions in the file system. If new partitions are present in the file system that are not in the table metadata, it adds those partitions to the metadata and to the Hive table.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/msck-repair-table-011124.png)
    
    When you add physical partitions, the metadata in the catalog becomes inconsistent with the layout of the data in the file system, and information about the new partitions needs to be added to the catalog. To update the metadata, you run `MSCK REPAIR TABLE` so that you can query the data in the new partitions.
    
    It’s important to note that `MSCK REPAIR TABLE` only adds partitions to metadata; it does not remove them. To remove partitions from metadata after the partitions have been manually deleted in the file system, you would run the command `ALTER TABLE table-name DROP PARTITION`.
    
    In the scenario, the data engineer has added a new batch of data for the date 2024/01/02 directly to HDFS using the `hadoop fs -put` command. However, the new partition is not visible through Hive queries. By running the `MSCK REPAIR TABLE` command, the data engineer can update the Hive metastore with the metadata of the new partition, making it visible in Hive queries.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Run `MSCK REPAIR TABLE sales_data`.**
    
    The option that says: **Run `ALTER TABLE sales_data DROP PARTITION (year='2024', month='01', day='02')`, then recreate the partition** is incorrect because dropping and recreating the partition would mean deleting and recreating the data, which could be time-consuming and resource-intensive. Moreover, this method does not address the root cause of the issue, which is that Hive is not aware of partitions if they are directly added to HDFS. Therefore, even if the partition is dropped and recreated, the same issue could occur in the future when new data is added directly to HDFS.
    
    The option that says: **Delete and re-upload the data to HDFS** is incorrect because this action does not solve the issue as Hive still needs the metadata of the new partitions to be updated in its metastore. Simply deleting and re-uploading the data does not update the metadata in Hive’s metastore. Therefore, even after re-uploading the data, the new partition would still not be visible in Hive queries.
    
    The option that says: **Restart the EMR cluster** is incorrect. Restarting the EMR cluster might seem like a potential solution to some issues, but in this case, it does not address the root cause of the problem. The issue lies in the fact that Hive is not aware of the new partitions added directly to HDFS, and restarting the EMR cluster does not make Hive aware of these new partitions.
    

A data engineering team uses a custom-built application to generate sales reports for a company. The data is hosted in Amazon Redshift, and the application logic executes 30 SQL statements sequentially when generating the report. The team wants to simplify the operation and improve the network traffic between the application and the Redshift database.

Which of the following would be the MOST effective solution?

- Use Amazon Redshift Spectrum to run the SQL statements in parallel.
- Use Amazon Redshift User-defined functions to encapsulate the SQL statements.
- **Use Amazon Redshift Stored Procedures to encapsulate the SQL statements.**
    
    In Amazon Redshift, stored procedures serve as a mechanism to encapsulate logic for various operations, such as data transformation and validation, as well as business-specific logic. This encapsulation allows for a more streamlined and efficient process, particularly when dealing with multiple SQL statements. By bundling these statements into a single stored procedure, the number of round trips between the custom-built application and the database can be significantly reduced, thereby simplifying the operation and enhancing network traffic efficiency.
    
    Stored procedures are stored directly in the database, making them accessible to any user with the appropriate privileges. They offer a level of flexibility not found in user-defined functions (UDFs), as they can incorporate not only SELECT queries but also data definition language (DDL) and data manipulation language (DML). Unlike UDFs, stored procedures do not necessarily need to return a value.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/stored-procedure-022824.png)
    
    Stored procedures in Amazon Redshift are used to encapsulate logic for data transformation, data validation, and business-specific logic. By combining multiple SQL steps into a stored procedure, you can reduce round trips between your custom-built application and the database. This would simplify the operation and improve the network traffic between the custom-built application and Redshift.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use Amazon Redshift Stored Procedures to encapsulate the SQL statements.**
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Redshift Spectrum to run the SQL statements in parallel** is incorrect because Amazon Redshift Spectrum is only useful when querying data directly against Amazon S3. Moreover, the idea of running SQL statements in parallel is more about optimizing query performance across distributed data rather than simplifying operational processes or reducing the application-database interaction overhead.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Redshift User-defined functions to encapsulate the SQL statements** is incorrect. UDFs are typically used to encapsulate frequently used calculations or transformations within SQL queries, not for managing a sequence of data retrieval tasks. UDFs execute within the context of a single SQL query and cannot manage the flow control of multiple sequential operations.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Redshift ML to predict the results of the SQL statements** is incorrect because Amazon Redshift ML is simply a feature for training and applying machine learning models using familiar SQL commands. Predicting the outcome of a sequence of SQL statements isn’t within its scope. Additionally, when generating reports with SQL queries, the aim is to absolute values from the actual database data, not to predict outcomes as with machine learning models.
    
- Use Amazon Redshift ML to predict the results of the SQL statements.

An e-commerce company stores customer data in an Amazon Redshift cluster hosted in a private VPC. The company aims to create visual charts of sales rankings by product category and customer demographics. To achieve this, the company’s data engineer must configure Amazon QuickSight to connect to the Redshift cluster securely.

How can the data engineer meet the requirements in the LEAST amount of effort?

- **Set up a VPC connection in QuickSight. Create inbound and outbound rules in the security groups for both services to allow traffic between them.**
    
    You can create a VPC connection in Amazon QuickSight to securely access private databases, such as an Amazon Redshift cluster, directly within your VPC. This setup is ideal for scenarios where your databases must be accessible only from certain IP addresses or within a secure environment, ensuring your data remains isolated from the public internet. When QuickSight connects to your VPC, it creates elastic network interfaces (ENIs) to facilitate a secure channel of communication directly to your resources, like a Redshift cluster, without traversing the public internet.
    
    You can connect Amazon QuickSight to the same VPC where your Amazon Redshift cluster is located. Once connected, you’ll need to configure the security groups associated with both QuickSight and the Amazon Redshift cluster. The security groups act as virtual firewalls, controlling the traffic that can enter and leave the network interfaces.
    
    In the Redshift security group’s configuration, the inbound rule must allow TCP traffic on port 5439 (the default port number of Redshift) from the QuickSight security group, enabling QuickSight to initiate connections to the Redshift cluster. The outbound rule should permit all TCP traffic to the QuickSight security group, allowing Redshift to send responses back to QuickSight.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/redshift-security-group-032724.png)
    
    For QuickSight’s security group, the inbound rules need to allow incoming responses from Redshift’s port range, and the outbound rules must permit initiating queries to the Redshift cluster on port 5439.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/quicksight-security-group-032724.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Set up a VPC connection in QuickSight. Create inbound and outbound rules in the security groups for both services to allow traffic between them.**
    
    The option that says: **Create an outbound rule in the security group of the Redshift cluster to allow traffic to Quicksight’s IP address. Establish a VPN connection between the Redshift cluster and Quicksight and modify the network ACLs to allow traffic between them** is incorrect. Establishing a VPN connection is not necessary in this scenario. The use of VPNs is more applicable to scenarios where you are connecting on-premise resources to AWS resources, not for connecting AWS services within the same region.
    
    The option that says: **Create an inbound rule in the security group of the Redshift cluster to allow traffic from Quicksight’s IP address. Establish a Direct Connect connection between the Redshift cluster and Quicksight and modify the route tables to allow traffic between them** is incorrect. Similar to the other incorrect option, this introduces complexity that isn’t necessary for connecting QuickSight to Redshift. Direct Connect is primarily used to create a dedicated, private connection between your on-premises data center and AWS. QuickSight, being a fully managed AWS service, does not require Direct Connect to access Redshift within AWS.
    
    The option that says: **Set up a VPC connection in QuickSight. Establish a VPC peering connection between the Redshift cluster and Quicksight’s VPC. Create inbound and outbound rules in the security groups for both services to allow traffic between them** is incorrect. While this method is technically viable, VPC peering adds unnecessary complexity to the given requirement. Since QuickSight can connect to Redshift through a simpler VPC connection setup, introducing VPC peering is not the most straightforward approach. It would be useful in scenarios where a Redshift cluster and QuickSight need to communicate across different VPCs.
    
- Create an outbound rule in the security group of the Redshift cluster to allow traffic to Quicksight’s IP address. Establish a VPN connection between the Redshift cluster and Quicksight and modify the network ACLs to allow traffic between them.
- Create an inbound rule in the security group of the Redshift cluster to allow traffic from Quicksight’s IP address. Establish a Direct Connect connection between the Redshift cluster and Quicksight and modify the route tables to allow traffic between them.
- Set up a VPC connection in QuickSight. Establish a VPC peering connection between the Redshift cluster and Quicksight’s VPC. Create inbound and outbound rules in the security groups for both services to allow traffic between them.

A large multinational corporation has an existing Amazon Redshift cluster that contains sensitive data. The company’s security policy mandates that all sensitive data be encrypted, and the encryption keys must be rotated every 90 days. The policy also states that the company must have control over the rotation of these keys.

Which combination of steps would comply with the company’s security policy? (Select TWO.)

- Enable automatic key rotation for the default Redshift AWS KMS key.
- **Implement a custom solution for rotating keys using AWS Lambda and Amazon EventBridge.**
- **Modify the existing Redshift cluster to use a customer-managed AWS KMS key for encryption.**
- Enable automatic key rotation for the customer-managed KMS key.
- Create a new Redshift cluster using the default Redshift AWS KMS key for encryption.
- **Evaluation**
    
    Amazon Redshift supports encryption at rest and in transit. For encryption at rest, it uses keys managed through AWS Key Management Service (KMS). When you create a table in Redshift, you can choose to encrypt your data at rest by selecting an encryption key. If you don’t choose a key, Redshift takes the default key for the account.
    
    There are two types of keys you can use with KMS:
    
    1. AWS-managed keys: These are the default keys that AWS creates, owns, and manages on your behalf.
    2. Customer-managed keys: These are the keys that you create, own, and manage.
    
    According to the company’s security policy, all sensitive data must be encrypted, and the encryption keys must be rotated every 90. The policy also states that the company must have control over the rotation of these keys. By choosing to use a customer-managed AWS KMS key for encryption, the company has control over the encryption keys and its rotation period.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/key-rotation-auto-kms-022924.png)
    
    AWS KMS allows you to enable automatic key rotation for your customer-managed keys, which automatically generates new cryptographic material for your key every year. To rotate a key manually, you would create new cryptographic material and associate it with the key yourself. This process involves generating a new version of the key while retaining the ability to access the previous versions, including all primary and replica key versions. Manual rotation provides direct control over the timing and process of the rotation, aligning with specific security policies that require such control. Additionally, this process can be automated using services such as AWS Lambda and Amazon EventBridge, allowing for custom rotation schedules and procedures that meet specific organizational requirements.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Modify the existing Redshift cluster to use a customer-managed AWS KMS key for encryption.**
    
    **– Implement a custom solution for rotating keys using AWS Lambda and Amazon EventBridge.**
    
    The option that says: **Create a new Redshift cluster using the default Redshift AWS KMS key for encryption** is incorrect. You don’t have to recreate a Redshift cluster to enable encryption. You can modify existing clusters to change their encryption settings, including applying encryption to previously unencrypted clusters. Moreover, using the default Redshift KMS key won’t allow the company to manage the keys directly or control their rotation
    
    The option that says: **Enable automatic key rotation for the default Redshift AWS KMS key** is incorrect. It’s not possible to control the key rotation of AWS-managed keys. The rotation of these keys are automatically handled by AWS.
    
    The option that says: **Enable automatic key rotation for the customer-managed KMS key** is incorrect. When you enable automatic key rotation, your custom-managed keys are rotated every year. This won’t satisfy the requirement of rotation the keys every 90 days.
    

A data engineering team has developed a stored procedure in Amazon Redshift to streamline data processing tasks. A data analyst, already a user of the Redshift database, needs permission to run this stored procedure for data analysis purposes.

How can the team grant this access while adhering to the principle of least privilege?

- Grant the data analyst the `REFERENCES`privilege on the procedure.
- Grant the data analyst the `USAGE`privilege on the schema containing the procedure.
- Grant the data analyst the `INSERT`, `UPDATE`, `DELETE`privileges on the table used by the procedure.
- **Grant the data analyst the `EXECUTE`privilege on the procedure.**
    
    A **stored procedure in Amazon Redshift** is a piece of SQL code that has been pre-written and can be re-executed multiple times. Stored procedures allow encapsulating logic for data transformation, validation, and business rules.
    
    Here are some permissions related to stored procedures in Amazon Redshift:
    
    **EXECUTE** permission allows users to run a stored procedure. This is the main permission required to allow users to execute procedures. `GRANT EXECUTE` is used to provide this permission.
    
    **INSERT, UPDATE,** and **DELETE** permissions on underlying tables or views allow read/write access.
    
    **USAGE** permission on schemas is required to allow creating and managing procedures within that schema.
    
    **ALTER** permission on the procedure allows modifying the procedure definition after creation.
    
    **REFERENCES** permission allows using the procedure name in SQL statements like CALL.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA2-60-STORED-PROCEDURE-Diagram-022824.jpg)
    
    To provide the data analyst permission to call the stored procedure, the data engineering team can create a role in Amazon Redshift and grant EXECUTE permission on a stored procedure to the role using the `GRANT` statement. Add the data analyst to the role to allow them to execute the stored procedure and perform data analysis tasks while restricting access to the procedure only. This gives the data engineering team control over database permissions and access.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Grant the data analyst the `EXECUTE` privilege on the procedure.**
    
    The option that says: **Grant the data analyst the `REFERENCES` privilege on the procedure** is incorrect. The `REFERENCES` privilege is used in the context of creating a foreign key constraint and does not apply to the execution of stored procedures. It would not enable the data analyst to execute the stored procedure.
    
    The option that says: **Grant the data analyst the `USAGE` privilege on the schema containing the procedure** is incorrect. While `USAGE` on a schema grants the ability to access objects within that schema, it does not specifically allow for the execution of stored procedures. `USAGE` might be necessary in addition to `EXECUTE` for certain scenarios, but on its own, it does not grant permission to execute stored procedures.
    
    The option that says: **Grant the data analyst the `INSERT`, `UPDATE`, `DELETE` privileges on the table used by the procedure** is incorrect. These privileges just allow users to perform table data manipulation operations, such as adding, modifying, or removing data. However, they do not provide permission to execute stored procedures. These privileges are unrelated to the execution of stored procedures and would not enable the data analyst to run the stored procedure.
    

A multinational corporation is working on a large-scale data processing project. The project will continuously consume streaming data from an Apache Kafka cluster, transform the data in-flight, and deliver it into an Amazon S3 bucket. The tasks involved in this project are complex and long-running, often taking over 15 minutes to complete.

Which AWS service would be the best choice to handle these long-running tasks?

- **AWS Glue**
    
    AWS Glue is a fully managed extract, transform, and load (ETL) service that makes it easy for users to prepare and load their data for analytics. It’s designed to handle complex tasks that often take over 15 minutes to complete, which aligns with the requirements of the scenario.
    
    ![AWS Glue](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/AWSGlueJobs.png)
    
    You can create streaming extract, transform, and load (ETL) jobs that run continuously and consume data from streaming sources like Amazon Kinesis Data Streams, Apache Kafka, and Amazon Managed Streaming for Apache Kafka (Amazon MSK). The jobs cleanse and transform the data and then load the results into Amazon S3 data lakes or JDBC data stores.
    
    In the scenario where a streaming ETL is required, AWS Glue is the best choice because it’s designed for continuous streaming data processing. It can handle long tasks by transforming and loading data into Amazon S3 in real-time, which matches the project’s needs for handling streaming data from Apache Kafka and delivering it efficiently to S3.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **AWS Glue.**
    
    **AWS Lambda** is incorrect because it is a serverless compute service that runs your code in response to events and automatically manages the underlying compute resources for you. However, AWS Lambda functions have a maximum execution time of 15 minutes. This makes it unsuitable for tasks that often take over 15 minutes to complete, as specified in the scenario.
    
    **Amazon Data Firehose** is incorrect because it is a fully managed service that delivers real-time streaming data to destinations such as Amazon S3, Amazon Redshift, Amazon OpenSearch Service, and Splunk. While it typically supports data transformation through AWS Lambda, the complexity and duration of the tasks described in the scenario exceed the capabilities of simple transformations, which is the 15-minute execution limit of AWS Lambda functions.
    
    **AWS Batch** is incorrect because it primarily enables developers, scientists, and engineers to easily and efficiently run hundreds of thousands of batch computing jobs on AWS. However, it cannot directly consume data from a streaming source. It is more suited for scenarios where workloads are not continuous streams but rather discrete batches of data that can be processed independently.
    
- AWS Lambda
- Amazon Data Firehose
- AWS Batch

A company is using AWS Glue for ETL jobs and Athena for querying data stored in S3. The corporation has strict security policies and requires fine-grained access control over AWS Glue resources.

There are two departments in the corporation, Finance and HR. Each department has its own set of ETL jobs and databases in AWS Glue. The requirement is that the Finance department should only have access to run its own ETL jobs and query its own databases. The same applies to the HR department.

Which of the following approaches would be the most appropriate to implement this requirement?

- Use Amazon Athena workgroups to control access to AWS Glue resources.
- Use Amazon S3 bucket policies to control access to AWS Glue resources.
- Use AWS Glue Data Catalog encryption settings to control access to databases and ETL jobs.
- **Use AWS Glue Data Catalog resource policies to manage access at a granular level.**
    
    AWS Glue Catalog resource policies allow you to manage access at a granular level. You can specify permissions at the database, table, and column level. This is particularly useful in scenarios where you need to restrict access to specific databases or tables within AWS Glue, such as the scenario described in the question.
    
    In the given scenario, the corporation has two departments, Finance and HR, each with its own set of ETL jobs and databases in AWS Glue. The requirement is that each department should only have access to run its own ETL jobs and query its own databases. This level of access control can be achieved using AWS Glue Catalog resource policies.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/GlueFineGrain-012524.png)
    
    With AWS Glue Catalog resource policies, you can define fine-grained access to the AWS Glue data catalog by using resource-level permissions in IAM policies. These policies can restrict access to different portions of the catalog based on users, roles, or applied at a resource level. This allows you to provide granular control over which users can access the various metadata definitions in your data lake.
    
    In addition, it’s important to note that the AWS Glue Data Catalog policies define the access to the metadata, and the S3 policies define the access to the content itself. You can restrict which metadata operations can be performed, such as GetDatabases, GetTables, CreateTable, and others, using identity-based policies (IAM). You can also restrict which data catalog objects those operations are performed on.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use AWS Glue Data Catalog resource policies to manage access at a granular level.**
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon S3 bucket policies to control access to AWS Glue resources**is incorrect because Amazon S3 bucket policies are used to manage access to S3 buckets and objects, not AWS Glue resources.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue Data Catalog encryption settings to control access to databases and ETL jobs** is incorrect because encryption settings are used for data security, not for managing access to AWS Glue resources.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Athena workgroups to control access to AWS Glue resources** is incorrect because Amazon Athena workgroups are used to control access to Athena queries, not to control access to AWS Glue resources.
    

A multinational e-commerce company is ingesting data from various sources into an Amazon Aurora database. The database currently stores 15 GB of data and is growing daily by 100 GB. The company’s data engineering team is tasked with performing near real-time predictive analysis.

Which of the following options would be the most efficient and require the least operational effort for analyzing the data?

- Use Amazon Data Firehose to stream data from Amazon Aurora to Amazon Redshift for analysis.
- Export the data from Amazon Aurora to Amazon S3, then use Amazon Athena for analysis.
- Use an AWS Glue for Spark to extract data from Amazon Aurora into Amazon Redshift for analysis.
- **Use the zero-ETL integration feature of Amazon Aurora with Amazon Redshift for analysis.**
    
    Leveraging Amazon Aurora’s zero-ETL integration with Amazon Redshift allows direct analysis of the data without the need for ETL jobs. This feature enables near real-time analytics and machine learning on petabytes of transactional data.
    
    With zero-ETL integration, transactional data from Aurora is made available in Amazon Redshift within seconds of being written into Aurora. This enables near real-time analytics on large volumes of data.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/zero-etl-integration-011224.png)
    
    This integration removes the need to build and manage complex data pipelines that perform extract, transform, and load (ETL) operations. This significantly reduces the operational effort required to analyze the data.
    
    The zero-ETL integration allows data from multiple Aurora database clusters to be replicated to a single Amazon Redshift data warehouse. This enables unified analytics across multiple applications and data sources.
    
    When using both Amazon Aurora Serverless v2 and Amazon Redshift Serverless, there’s no need to manage any infrastructure. This further reduces the operational burden.
    
    With the Aurora zero-ETL integration with Amazon Redshift, you can use Amazon Redshift’s comprehensive analytical capabilities, such as built-in machine learning, materialized views, data sharing, and federated access to multiple data stores and data lakes.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use the zero-ETL integration feature of Amazon Aurora with Amazon Redshift for analysis.**
    
    The option that says: **Export the data from Amazon Aurora to Amazon S3, then use Amazon Athena for analysis** is incorrect because it involves additional steps of exporting data to S3 and then using Athena for analysis. This process would be slower and time-consuming than using zero-ETL integration.
    
    The option that says: **Use an AWS Glue for Spark to extract data from Amazon Aurora into Amazon Redshift for analysis** is incorrect. While AWS Glue for Spark can write directly to Amazon Redshift, the process of moving data to Redshift involves staging the data into Amazon S3 first. This additional step isn’t near real-time and is slower than zero-ETL integration.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Data Firehose to stream data from Amazon Aurora to Amazon Redshift for analysis** is incorrect because Amazon Aurora is not a valid source of Data Firehose. This means that you cannot directly transfer newly inserted data from an Aurora database to Amazon Redshift.
    

A company stores terabytes of data in an Amazon S3 bucket, which is accessed by multiple applications. The company uses a bucket policy to manage permissions. As application requirements evolve, the engineering team has to update the bucket policy accordingly. This process causes access issues to some applications due to misconfigurations.

The company needs a way to control access for each application without disrupting the others.

Which solution will meet this requirement with the LEAST operational overhead?

- Utilize Amazon S3 Lifecycle policies to manage permissions based on the data lifecycle.
- Create individual IAM roles for each application and grant the necessary permissions to access the bucket.
- **Set up dedicated Amazon S3 Access Points for each application.**
    
    **Amazon S3 Access Points** are a feature of Amazon S3 that simplifies managing data access for shared datasets. With Access Points, you can create multiple access points for a single S3 bucket, with each access point having a unique hostname and access policy tailored for a specific use case or application.
    
    Each access point can be configured with a custom access policy granting only the necessary permissions for its intended use.
    
    Access points allow separating access control for different applications rather than managing everything through a single bucket policy.
    
    Network controls can be applied to access points to restrict access to only requests from a specific VPC.
    
    Access points can be used to grant cross-account access while still retaining control over the bucket and data through the bucket policy.
    
    Aliases are automatically generated for each access point, making them interchangeable with bucket names in AWS APIs and CLI.
    
    Common S3 operations can be performed by replacing the bucket name with the access point ARN or alias.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/dea2-56-s3-accesspoint-030724.png)
    
    The company can meet its requirement of controlling access for each application without disrupting the others by setting up dedicated Amazon S3 Access Points for different applications to streamline access management.
    
    A custom policy attached to each Access Point will grant only the necessary permissions for that specific application.
    
    As application requirements change, only the policy of that application’s Access Point needs updating, avoiding disruptions to other applications.
    
    This prevents issues caused by other applications from being affected when one application’s permissions need to be updated. Developers no longer need to change the overall bucket policy frequently as their application ecosystem evolves.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Set up dedicated Amazon S3 Access Points for each application.**
    
    The option that says: **Create individual IAM roles for each application and grant the necessary permissions to access the bucket** is incorrect. This approach makes managing and auditing permissions more complicated and time-consuming because you need to grant correct permissions for each app’s IAM role. It also increases security risks, as one might give applications access to more AWS services than they need.
    
    The option that says: **Implement Amazon S3 Object Lock to manage permissions more securely** is incorrect because S3 Object Lock is designed to prevent objects from being deleted or overwritten for a fixed amount of time or indefinitely, which is not directly related to simplifying permission management or preventing disruptions due to policy misconfigurations.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize Amazon S3 Lifecycle policies to manage permissions based on the data lifecycle** is incorrect because S3 Lifecycle policies are used to manage objects’ lifecycle, such as transitioning objects to less expensive storage classes or automatically deleting them after a certain period, and do not directly help with permission management or preventing configuration errors.
    
- Implement Amazon S3 Object Lock to manage permissions more securely.

A data engineer needs to perform data analytics on datasets that originate from various sources. The engineer wants to use Apache Spark to process these datasets and interactively visualize the results.

Which solution will meet this requirement with LEAST operational overhead?

- Use Amazon EMR Studio notebooks
- Use AWS Glue Studio
- **Use Athena Notebooks**
    
    **Amazon Athena Notebooks** is an interactive coding environment where you can write and execute Apache Spark code using Python. Athena notebooks allow chaining together SQL queries, calculations, and visualizations.
    
    **Apache Spark** is a distributed processing framework used for big data analytics. It utilizes in-memory computing techniques for fast performance on large datasets.
    
    Using **Apache Spark in Amazon Athena Notebooks** allows you to run Spark interactively on data from various sources without having to provision or manage infrastructure. You can write Python code in Athena notebooks to query data, perform Spark transformations and actions, and visualize results. Athena handles the automatic scaling of resources for your Spark jobs based on requirements.
    
    Data can be queried directly from data lakes in Amazon S3, databases, etc., using Athena’s metadata cataloging.
    
    Notebooks provide a Jupyter-like interface to develop and run Spark code and applications.
    
    Resources are serverless – you only pay for the actual notebook/query executions without any upfront costs.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/dea2-57-athena-notebook-MAR-07-24.png)
    
    Based on the provided requirements, the most cost-effective solution for the data engineer to implement would be Amazon Athena Notebooks. Amazon Athena is a serverless solution, eliminating the need for infrastructure provisioning and management. It enables querying data directly from various sources, eliminating the need for data migration and satisfying the requirement to analyze data from different sources. Additionally, Athena’s integration with Apache Spark allows for interactive data analytics and visualization through Python notebooks, chaining together SQL queries, calculations, and visualizations for exploration purposes. Athena’s automatic scaling of compute resources ensures faster notebook execution without the hassle of resource sizing. Most importantly, with Athena, the data engineer only pays for the actual queries and notebooks run, with no upfront costs or minimum commitments, making it highly cost-effective for the required interactive analysis.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use Athena Notebooks.**
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon EMR Studio notebooks** is incorrect because, although EMR notebooks support Apache Spark and interactive data analysis, they require provisioning and managing an EMR cluster, which can be more costly than a serverless solution like Athena.
    
    The option that says: **Use Athena query editor** is incorrect because the Athena query editor is designed for running SQL queries and does not directly support interactive data visualization or analytics using Apache Spark.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue Studio** is incorrect because AWS Glue Studio is primarily used for creating, running, and monitoring ETL jobs, not for interactive data analysis and visualization with Apache Spark.
    
- Use Athena query editor

A financial company recently migrated its cryptocurrency data warehouse on-premises to AWS and has chosen Amazon Redshift as its data storage solution. The company’s collection of digital currency information can either be in a semistructured or nested data format stored in Redshift as a `SUPER`data type. The data engineering team’s task is to query the large dataset stored in the Redshift cluster with less operational overhead.

Which of the following would meet the requirements with the LEAST amount of effort?

- Query the dataset by using string functions such as `SUBSTRING`, `CHAR_LENGTH`, `UPPER`, `LOWER`, et cetera
- Query the dataset by using SQL aggregate functions such as `COUNT`, `SUM`, `AVG`, `MIN`, `MAX`, et cetera
- **Query the dataset by using dot and bracket notation to access nested data in JSON columns.**
    
    Amazon Redshift supports querying nested data in JSON columns using dot and bracket notation. This allows you to access and query nested data efficiently, which is especially useful when dealing with large datasets. For example, if you have a JSON column `data` with nested fields `field1` and `field2`, you can access the nested data using the following syntax: `data.field1` or `data['field1']`. This approach provides the least operational overhead, making it the most suitable for the given task. It allows you to access the required data directly without having to process the entire JSON object, thereby reducing the computational resources required for the query.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/redshift_nested_data_query_16MAR2024.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Query the dataset by using dot and bracket notation to access nested data in JSON columns.**
    
    The option that says: **Query the dataset by using string functions such as `SUBSTRING`, `CHAR_LENGTH`, `UPPER`, `LOWER`, et cetera** is incorrect. While string functions can be used to manipulate and query data, they may not provide the least operational overhead when dealing with large datasets. These functions are more suited for specific data manipulation tasks rather than efficient querying of large datasets.
    
    The option that says: **Query the dataset by using SQL pattern matching operators like `LIKE`, `SIMILAR TO`, or `POSIX` expressions** is incorrect because the SQL pattern matching operators are primarily used for finding specific patterns within a particular table or database only. This is not commonly used to query semistructured or nested datasets. Moreover, they can be computationally expensive and may not provide the least operational overhead when querying large datasets.
    
    The option that says: **Query the dataset by using SQL aggregate functions such as `COUNT`, `SUM`, `AVG`, `MIN`, `MAX`, et cetera** is incorrect because SQL aggregate functions are mostly used to perform calculations on a set of values and return a single value. While they are useful for data analysis tasks, they may not provide the least operational overhead when querying large datasets. These functions are more suited for data analysis tasks rather than efficiently querying large datasets.
    
- Query the dataset by using SQL pattern matching operators like `LIKE`, `SIMILAR TO`, or `POSIX` expressions.

A data engineer is designing an ETL workflow that would export 5 TB of data to Amazon S3 every day. The data is sourced from a variety of platforms, including Oracle DB, Cassandra, Amazon Aurora, and Apache Kafka, with the schemas being frequently updated.

To address this, the data engineer needs a robust solution capable of schema detection for these heterogeneous data sources.

Which solution has the LEAST operational overhead for this requirement?

- Develop a solution that incorporates Amazon Athena for schema detection, executing ETL tasks through SQL queries against S3 data.
- **Utilize AWS Glue crawlers for schema detection and AWS Glue Spark jobs for data transformation.**
    
    **AWS Glue** is a fully managed extract, transform, and load (ETL) service that makes it easy for customers to prepare and load data for analytics. It provides capabilities for schema detection, ETL jobs, and crawlers that make it useful for working with dynamic or changing schemas.
    
    **AWS Glue crawlers** automatically detect schemas for data sources. Glue crawlers scan data sources, detect schemas, and store the associated metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog. This enables Glue to handle changing schemas without requiring code changes. The crawlers run periodically to detect new or changed data.
    
    **AWS Glue jobs** can then be used to process the data. Glue jobs are defined using Python or Scala scripts that reference the schemas and metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog. This allows Glue jobs to work with changing schemas without needing redeploys as the underlying data changes.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA2-26-aws-glue-detect-schema-022824.jpg)
    
    Given the requirements for schema detection, ETL capabilities, and the need to handle dynamic schemas, AWS Glue stands out as the most suitable option. It offers a managed environment that significantly reduces the operational overhead of managing infrastructure for ETL processes. Glue Crawlers automatically detect and adapt to schema changes, ensuring that the ETL process remains robust against evolving data structures. Glue Jobs then allow for the transformation and loading of data into Amazon S3 within the required timeframe.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Utilize AWS Glue crawlers for schema detection and AWS Glue Spark jobs for data transformation.**
    
    The option that says: **Configure AWS Data Pipeline to orchestrate data movement and processing tasks, leveraging PySpark in AWS Lambda for ETL operations** is incorrect. AWS Data Pipeline is a web service for orchestrating data movements and processing tasks. While it can integrate with AWS Lambda for custom processing logic, using PySpark within Lambda poses limitations on execution time and resource allocation, which may not be suitable for processing 5 TB of data daily.
    
    The option that says: **Create an Amazon Redshift cluster as a staging area. Use a stored procedure for schema identification and data transformation** is incorrect. Using Amazon Redshift as a staging area would require significant engineering effort due to the necessity of adapting disparate data formats, schemas, and types to Redshift’s structured, columnar storage system. Moreover, a stored procedure is just a set of SQL statements saved together as a single executable entity. While it can execute complex logic, it’s not inherently designed for dynamic schema detection across diverse data sources.
    
    The option that says: **Develop a solution that incorporates Amazon Athena for schema detection, executing ETL tasks through SQL queries against S3 data** is incorrect. Athena is primarily a query service that does not inherently provide ETL capabilities or schema detection in the manner required for this scenario. It is designed for querying data directly in S3 using SQL, not for the ETL process itself.
    
- Configure AWS Data Pipeline to orchestrate data movement and processing tasks, leveraging PySpark in AWS Lambda for ETL operations.
- Create an Amazon Redshift cluster as a staging area. Use a stored procedure for schema identification and data transformation.

A company stores CSV records in an Amazon S3 bucket. These records are queried using the Amazon Athena query editor. The company wants to be able to run SQL queries to add and delete data.

Which combination of steps would meet the requirements with the LEAST development overhead? (Select TWO.)

- Use Amazon Redshift Serverless for inserting and deleting records.
- Convert the CSV records into an Apache Hudi format. Run an AWS Glue crawler to generate a Hudi table.
- **Use Amazon Athena for inserting and deleting records.**
- Convert the CSV records into a Delta Lake format. Create a Delta Lake table using Amazon Athena.
- **Create an Apache Iceberg table using Amazon Athena and insert the current table into it.**
- **Evaluation**
    
    Apache Iceberg is an open table format for very large analytic datasets. Iceberg manages large collections of files as tables, and it supports modern analytical data lake operations such as record-level insert, update, delete, and time travel queries. The Iceberg specification allows seamless table evolution, such as schema and partition evolution, and is designed for optimized usage on Amazon S3.
    
    Amazon Athena is an interactive query service that makes it easy to analyze data in Amazon S3 using standard SQL. Athena supports read, time travel, write, and DDL queries for Apache Iceberg tables that use the Apache Parquet format for data and the AWS Glue catalog for their metastore.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Iceberg-030124.png)
    
    There are different ways of creating an Iceberg table, such as directly defining the table schema with a CREATE TABLE statement, then populating it with existing data using the INSERT INTO statement. Alternatively, you can also have a Glue Crawler to crawl your data and automatically create the table for you. Once established, Iceberg table data can be directly managed in Amazon Athena through INSERT, UPDATE, and DELETE queries. These operations on Iceberg tables produce new snapshots with each transaction, enabling querying of table states at different points in time through Iceberg’s time travel capability.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Create an Apache Iceberg table using Amazon Athena and insert the current table into it.**
    
    **– Use Amazon Athena for inserting and deleting records.**
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Redshift Serverless for inserting and deleting records** is incorrect. While Amazon Redshift Serverless allows you to run SQL queries without managing the underlying infrastructure, it involves more development effort due to moving or copying data from Amazon S3 into Redshift.
    
    The option that says: **Convert the CSV records into a Delta Lake format. Create a Delta Lake table using Amazon Athena** is incorrect. Delta Lake provides ACID transactions, scalable metadata handling, and unifies streaming and batch data processing. However, Athena only supports read queries against Delta Lake tables.
    
    The option that says: **Convert the CSV records into an Apache Hudi format. Run an AWS Glue crawler to generate a Hudi table** is incorrect. Although Hudi tables are capable of handling upserts, deletes, and incremental data processing, Athena lacks support for the insertion of new Hudi data into a Hudi table.
    

A large e-commerce company has an Amazon Redshift data warehouse that contains about 300 TB of consumer data. The Redshift cluster is updated every 6 hours by importing newly gathered consumer activity data. Read-only queries are executed throughout the day to generate product patterns used to curate the company’s website homepage. However, there is a particular long-running query that runs for more than two hours every hour, causing other short-running queries to get queued up or take a longer time to execute.

Which of the following would be the most cost-effective solution to optimize query execution?

- Expand the number of nodes in the Redshift cluster to accommodate the increased load.
- Activate Short Query Acceleration (SQA) to give priority to short-running queries.
- **Enable auto Concurrency Scaling mode and set up a distinct Workload Manager (WLM) queue for the long-running query.**
    
    Amazon Redshift’s Concurrency Scaling feature allows you to automatically add additional cluster capacity to handle an increase in concurrent queries. This feature is designed to support thousands of concurrent users and concurrent queries with consistently fast query performance. When you turn on concurrency scaling, Amazon Redshift automatically adds additional cluster capacity to process an increase in both read and write queries. This means that users see the most current data, whether the queries run on the main cluster or a concurrency-scaling cluster.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/concurrency-scaling-011124-2204.png)
    
    By configuring a separate WLM queue for the long-running query, you can ensure that this query does not block other queries from executing. Amazon Redshift workload management (WLM) enables users to flexibly manage priorities within workloads so that short, fast-running queries won’t get stuck in queues behind long-running queries. When you run a query, WLM assigns the query to a queue according to the user’s user group or by matching a query group that is listed in the queue configuration with a query group label that the user sets at runtime.
    
    This approach is cost-effective because you are charged for concurrency-scaling clusters only for the time they’re actively running queries.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Enable auto Concurrency Scaling mode and set up a distinct Workload Manager (WLM) queue for the long-running query.**
    
    The option that says: **Expand the number of nodes in the Redshift cluster to accommodate the increased load** is incorrect. Increasing the number of nodes can improve performance by providing more resources to handle the load. However, this approach might not be the most cost-effective solution, as it involves additional costs for the extra nodes. It’s also worth noting that simply adding more nodes may not solve the problem if the issue is related to query optimization or workload management.
    
    The option that says: **Activate Short Query Acceleration (SQA) to give priority to short-running queries** is incorrect. While SQA might improve the execution time of other, shorter queries, it doesn’t directly address the issue with the long-running query. The long-running query would still take the same amount of time to execute and could still cause performance issues when it’s running.
    
    The option that says: **Adjust the WLM settings to terminate the long-running query if it exceeds two hours of execution time** is incorrect. While terminating long-running queries can free up resources for other queries, it might not be the best approach if the long-running query is critical for your business operations. It’s also important to note that terminating a query does not address the root cause of why the query is taking a long time to run. A better approach might be to optimize the query or adjust the workload management settings to better handle long-running queries.
    
- Adjust the WLM settings to terminate the long-running query if it exceeds two hours of execution time.

A Philippines-based company ship orders globally. These orders are placed in an Amazon Simple Queue Service (SQS) queue. They have an AWS Step Functions workflow that exclusively processes international orders. This workflow should only be executed when an order’s data does not contain the ‘PH’ country code.

Which of the following options would satisfy the requirements in the LEAST amount of development effort?

- Create an Amazon EventBridge rule with an event pattern that matches the `SendMessage` SQS actions. Select the Step Functions workflow as the target. Implement a filtering logic in the Step functions workflow.
- **Create a pipe in Amazon EventBridge Pipes with the SQS queue as the source. Configure an event pattern that filters non-PH orders. Select the Step Functions workflow as the target.**
    
    Amazon EventBridge Pipes connects sources to targets. Pipes are intended for point-to-point integrations between supported sources and targets, with support for advanced transformations and enrichment. It reduces the need for specialized knowledge and integration code when developing event-driven architectures, fostering consistency across your company’s applications. To set up a pipe, you choose the source, add optional filtering, define optional enrichment, and choose the target for the event data.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/aws-eventbridge-pipes-config-03082024.jpg)
    
    In this scenario, the goal is to only run the Step Functions workflow for orders not marked with the ‘PH’ country code, avoiding unnecessary executions. By using Amazon EventBridge Pipes with an “anything-but” event filter, we can directly target only those orders. This setup filters out orders before they trigger the workflow, ensuring that only international orders proceed for processing.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create a pipe in Amazon EventBridge Pipes with the SQS queue as the source. Configure an event pattern that filters non-PH orders. Select the Step Functions workflow as the target.**
    
    The option that says: **Create an Amazon EventBridge rule with an event pattern that matches the `SendMessage` SQS actions. Select the Step Functions workflow as the target. Implement a filtering logic in the Step functions workflow** is incorrect. With this approach, the workflow would be triggered for each order, regardless of the country code, leading to unnecessary executions of the workflow. Moreover, relying on the `SendMessage` action in Amazon EventBridge for processing orders may not be reliable due to potential delays or the asynchronous nature of the message delivery system in Amazon SQS.
    
    The option that says: **Filter orders based on the SQS queue length, considering longer queues to have more relevant orders** is incorrect because the length of the SQS queue (the number of messages in the queue) is not a reliable indicator of an order’s relevance to the workflow. Also, this approach will not filter orders containing non-PH country codes.
    
    The option that says: **Create an AWS Lambda function with an event source mapping for SQS. Configure an event pattern that filters non-PH orders. Write the function’s code to invoke the Step Functions workflow.** is incorrect. While feasible, this solution demands extra development effort because it involves using a Lambda function as an intermediary to invoke the Step Functions. This is in contrast to using EventBridge Pipes, which provides a more straightforward approach.
    
- Filter orders based on the SQS queue length, considering longer queues to have more relevant orders.
- Create an AWS Lambda function with an event source mapping for SQS. Configure an event pattern that filters non-PH orders. Write the function’s code to invoke the Step Functions workflow.

A data engineering team has a task to configure an ETL job using AWS Glue DynamicFrame to manage data in a partitioned AWS Glue table. The table is optimized with partition indexes to enhance query efficiency. The team aims to refine the ETL operation by selectively processing necessary data within a DynamicFrame, using partition indexes to boost query performance and minimize costs.

Which of the following is the MOST cost-effective solution that fulfills these requirements?

- Utilize pushdown predicates to filter partitions upfront. Minimize data load by applying filters to the partition metadata within DynamicFrames.
- Aggregate all input files from the source table into a unified in-memory partition before executing queries to simplify the data processing workflow.
- **Optimize the process using server-side filtering with the catalog partition predicates during DynamicFrame creation.**
    
    **AWS Glue is a powerful ETL service that easily moves data between different data stores. By using AWS Glue, you can easily create and manage ETL jobs to transfer data from various sources, such as Amazon S3, Amazon RDS, and Amazon Redshift. Additionally, AWS Glue enables you to transform your data as needed to fit your specific needs. One of the key advantages of AWS Glue is its automatic schema discovery and mapping, which allows you to map data from different sources with different schemas easily.**
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-Dynamic-Frame-Partitions.png)
    
    AWS Glue’s support for server-side filtering with catalog partition predicates directly during the creation of DynamicFrames is a powerful feature for optimizing ETL processes. This capability allows the ETL job to selectively process only the necessary data by utilizing the metadata catalog’s partition indexes. Doing so significantly reduces the volume of data that needs to be read and processed, leading to reductions in execution time and cost.
    
    Server-side filtering applies filter predicates against the partition metadata stored in the AWS Glue Data Catalog. This means that before the data is even loaded into the DynamicFrame, AWS Glue can narrow down the data to just the relevant partitions based on the criteria specified by the ETL job. This approach is more efficient than client-side filtering, where data is first loaded into memory before being filtered, and it leverages the AWS Glue catalog’s capabilities to minimize data scan and processing.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Optimize the process using server-side filtering with the catalog partition predicates during DynamicFrame creation.**
    
    The option that says: **Utilize pushdown predicates to filter partitions upfront. Minimize data load by applying filters to the partition metadata within DynamicFrames** is incorrect. Pushdown predicates are less efficient than catalog partition predicates when used directly during DynamicFrame creation. This is because pushdown predicates apply filters after the DynamicFrame has been created, leading to higher initial data loads before the filter is applied.
    
    The option that says: **Transform the DynamicFrame into a Spark SQL DataFrame for efficient partitioned writing into Amazon S3** is incorrect because this approach just focuses on the writing process rather than optimizing the reading process by filtering unnecessary data early on.
    
    The option that says: **Aggregate all input files from the source table into a unified in-memory partition before executing queries to simplify the data processing workflow** is incorrect. This approach would likely increase costs and processing time due to the large volume of data being loaded into memory, contrary to the goal of minimizing costs.
    
- Transform the DynamicFrame into a Spark SQL DataFrame for efficient partitioned writing into Amazon S3.

A company utilizes Amazon Redshift for its primary data warehousing needs tied to an e-commerce application. Recently, they added a new SQL report for their sales data, which needs to compare specific columns from this year’s sales against historical data. Data older than one year is stored in an Amazon S3 bucket. This new report has resulted in slower performance and increased query times. The company needs a solution to enhance system performance and speed up these queries.

What is the MOST efficient solution to meet these requirements?

- Run the `VACUUM DELETE` command in Redshift to clean up and optimize the data before running the report. Afterward, execute the `SQL COPY`command to load the data from Amazon S3 into Redshift. Adjust the report to pull data from the latest and newly imported tables.
- Run a `SQL JOIN` operation with Amazon Redshift Spectrum to merge the most recent data with the S3 external table data into a new table. Adjust the report to access data from this newly formed table.
- Run the `SQL UNLOAD` command to move data from the sales table to a new external table in Amazon S3. Adjust the report to utilize Amazon Redshift Spectrum for querying the newly created table and the existing tables in Amazon S3.
- **Use the report’s SQL statement to retrieve data directly from Amazon S3. Save these results in a Redshift materialized view. Adjust the report to refresh using `SQL REFRESH` and then extract data from the materialized view.**
    
    Amazon Redshift Spectrum allows you to efficiently query and retrieve structured and semistructured data from files in Amazon S3 without having to load the data into Amazon Redshift tables. Redshift Spectrum queries employ massive parallelism to run very fast against large datasets. Much of the processing occurs in the Redshift Spectrum layer, and most of the data remains in Amazon S3. Like Amazon Athena, Redshift Spectrum is serverless, and there’s nothing to provision or manage. You just pay for the resources you consume for the duration of your Redshift Spectrum query. Like Amazon Redshift itself, you get the benefits of a sophisticated query optimizer, fast access to data on local disks, and standard SQL.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/redshift_spectrum-1_15OCT2023.gif)
    
    Amazon Redshift offers several features to enhance query performance, such as result caching, concurrency scaling, and materialized views. Materialized views in Redshift provide a powerful way to optimize query performance for repeated and predictable query workloads. They precompute and store the results of a query, which improves the speed of data retrieval for complex queries that are run frequently. This makes them particularly useful for scenarios like the one described, where data from historical records stored in Amazon S3 is regularly queried alongside newer data. Redshift can use materialized views to serve query results from precomputed data, reducing the compute resources required and accelerating query performance, thus enabling near real-time analytics on large datasets.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use the report’s SQL statement to retrieve data directly from Amazon S3. Save these results in a Redshift materialized view. Adjust the report to refresh using `SQL REFRESH` and then extract data from the materialized view.**
    
    The option that says: **Run the `VACUUM DELETE`command in Redshift to clean up and optimize the data before running the report. Afterward, execute the `SQL COPY` command to load the data from Amazon S3 into Redshift. Adjust the report to pull data from the latest and newly imported tables** is incorrect. Although this option suggests a good practice, optimizing data using `VACUUM DELETE`, copying data from Amazon S3 using the SQL COPY command is resource-extensive and may not be the most efficient approach, especially when the data is already in Amazon S3. Always remember that Amazon Redshift already has a suite of features that allows it to execute specific operations for data hosted in Amazon S3.
    
    The option that says: **Run a `SQL JOIN`operation with Amazon Redshift Spectrum to merge the most recent data with the S3 external table data into a new table. Adjust the report to access data from this newly formed table** is incorrect. While Redshift Spectrum effectively allows for querying data in S3 without moving it into Redshift, this approach relies heavily on the continuous execution of SQL JOINs across Redshift and S3. This approach can introduce significant query overhead, especially when the datasets or queries are complex because it must dynamically handle large volumes of data across the two services, which can cause delays in the query execution time.
    
    The option that says: **Run the `SQL UNLOAD`command to move data from the sales table to a new external table in Amazon S3. Adjust the report to utilize Amazon Redshift Spectrum for querying the newly created table and the existing tables in Amazon S3** is incorrect. This involves loading and unloading data in and out of Amazon Redshift and Amazon S3, which consumes many unnecessary resources. If you already host your data in Amazon S3, it is best to query it directly from Amazon Redshift instead of moving data between Redshift and S3. Additionally, this option introduces complexity with multiple tables and data transfer steps.
    

A data engineer is tasked with regularly updating their Amazon Redshift data warehouse. Every month, the data engineer receives a large batch of new data. This data often contains updates to existing records, and the goal is to ensure that the data in Redshift is always up-to-date while minimizing the impact on query performance.
