Which strategy should be chosen to ensure efficient data ingestion?

- Use the `INSERT` command to load the updated data into a temporary table. Then, run the `UPSERT` command to insert new records and update existing ones in the main table.
- **Create a temporary table for the updated data. Use the `MERGE` operation on the temporary table and the main data warehouse. When no match is detected, use the `INSERT` operation to insert values from the temporary table into the main data warehouse. When a match is detected, use the `UPDATE` operation to update the main data warehouse with values from the temporary table.**
    
    Amazon Redshift is a cloud-based data warehouse that you can use to perform data analysis. One of the features of Amazon Redshift is the support for the `MERGE` operation, which uses a temporary staging table. This operation is particularly useful when you have a large batch of updated data that often contains updates to existing records, and you want to ensure that the data in Redshift is always up-to-date while minimizing the impact on query performance.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/MERGE-redshift-031024.png)
    
    The `MERGE` operation works by comparing the records in two tables based on a specified match condition. When the `MERGE` operation detects a match between the records in the two tables, it performs an `UPDATE` operation. This means that the existing records in the main table are updated with the values from the matching records in the temporary table.
    
    On the other hand, when the `MERGE` operation does not detect a match, it performs an `INSERT` operation. This means that the new records from the temporary table are inserted into the main table.
    
    This strategy of using the `MERGE` operation ensures efficient data ingestion because it minimizes the number of separate operations that need to be performed. Instead of having to perform `INSERT` and `UPDATE` operations separately, you can perform them both at once using the `MERGE` operation. This also minimizes the impact on query performance, as the database doesn’t have to process separate `INSERT` and `UPDATE` commands.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create a temporary table for the updated data. Use the `MERGE`operation on the temporary table and the main data warehouse. When no match is detected, use the `INSERT` operation to insert values from the temporary table into the main data warehouse. When a match is detected, use the `UPDATE` operation to update the main data warehouse with values from the temporary table** is correct because it involves creating a temporary table and using the `MERGE` operation. When no match is detected, the `INSERT` operation is used to add new records from the temporary table to the main data warehouse. When a match is detected, the `UPDATE` operation is used to update the existing records in the main data warehouse with the new data. This ensures that the data in Redshift is always up-to-date while minimizing the impact on query performance.
    
    The option that says: **Load the updated data into a temporary table using the `INSERT` command. Run an AWS Glue job that applies the Drop Duplicates transformation to remove any duplicate records in the temporary table** is incorrect. While loading the data into a temporary table and using an AWS Glue job to remove duplicates might ensure that the data in the temporary table is unique, this strategy does not account for handling updates to existing records in the main table. Additional steps would be required to compare and merge this data back into the main table.
    
    The option that says: **Use the `INSERT` command to load the updated data into a temporary table. Then, run the `UPSERT` command to insert new records and update existing ones in the main table** is incorrect because Amazon Redshift does not natively support the `UPSERT` command. However, you could achieve similar functionality using a combination of Amazon services and big data frameworks. For instance, Amazon EMR, a managed service that runs big data frameworks including Hudi, supports record-level operations, including `INSERT`, `UPDATE`, `UPSERT`, and `DELETE`.
    
    The option that says: **Load the updated data into a temporary table using the `INSERT`command. Then, use the `DELETE` command to remove any records in the main table that also exist in the temporary table. After that, use the `INSERT` command to insert new records from the temporary table into the main table** is incorrect. This approach involves deleting existing records that match those in the new batch before inserting the new data. This strategy can be very disruptive and might lead to data unavailability or integrity issues if not managed carefully.
    
- Load the updated data into a temporary table using the `INSERT` command. Then, use the `DELETE` command to remove any records in the main table that also exist in the temporary table. After that, use the `INSERT` command to insert new records from the temporary table into the main table.
- Load the updated data into a temporary table using the `INSERT` command. Run an AWS Glue job that applies the Drop Duplicates transformation to remove any duplicate records in the temporary table.

A data engineering team is using AWS Glue for their ETL jobs as part of a larger data analytics solution. They are leveraging CloudWatch Logs Insights to analyze various types of logs from their AWS Glue jobs to optimize job performance and troubleshoot issues.

Which of the following combinations of metrics and logs can be directly monitored and analyzed in CloudWatch Logs Insights?

- Apache Spark error logs, CPU usage, and Data Processing Unit (DPU) usage.
- **Apache Spark job progress bar, Apache Spark job logs, driver logs, and executor logs.**
    
    AWS Glue provides real-time, continuous logging for AWS Glue jobs. You can view real-time **Apache Spark job logs** in Amazon CloudWatch, including **driver logs, executor logs, and an Apache Spark job progress ba**r. Viewing real-time logs provides you with a better perspective on the running job.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/aws-glue-logs-013024.jpg)
    
    When you start an AWS Glue job, it sends the real-time logging information to CloudWatch (every 5 seconds and before each executor termination) after the Spark application starts running. You can view the logs on the AWS Glue console or the CloudWatch console dashboard.
    
    The continuous logging feature includes the following capabilities:
    
    – Continuous logging with a default filter to reduce high verbosity in the logs
    
    – Continuous logging with no filter
    
    – A custom script logger to log application-specific messages
    
    – A console progress bar to track the running status of the current AWS Glue job
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Apache Spark job progress bar, Apache Spark job logs, driver logs, and executor logs.**
    
    The option that says: **Apache Spark error logs, CPU usage, and Data Processing Unit (DPU) usage** is incorrect. CPU and Data Processing Unit (DPU) usage are metrics that cannot be directly monitored in CloudWatch Logs Insights. DPUs are a measure of computation power used by AWS Glue and do not produce logs that can be analyzed by CloudWatch Logs Insights.
    
    The option that says: **Quantum Processing unit (QPU), system logs, and CPU usage** is incorrect. QPU refers to computing resources, specifically within the context of Amazon Braket, which is a service for exploring and experimenting with quantum computing. QPUs are not relevant to the log analysis of AWS Glue jobs. Moreover, CPU usage is monitored through CloudWatch Metrics rather than Logs Insights, which focuses on the analysis of log data.
    
    The option that says: **Apache Spark error logs, driver logs, and network usage** is incorrect. While driver logs can be monitored and analyzed in CloudWatch Logs Insights, Apache Spark error logs and network usage can’t be directly monitored in CloudWatch Logs Insights. Error logs provide information about errors that occurred during the execution of the Spark job, but they are not specific to AWS Glue and are not directly monitored by CloudWatch Logs Insights. Network usage is a system-level metric that is not directly related to the execution of the AWS Glue job.
    
- Quantum Processing unit (QPU), system logs, and CPU usage.
- Apache Spark error logs, driver logs, and network usage.

A company is developing a data processing pipeline for their e-commerce platform. The pipeline requires the execution of Python code every Monday at 9:00 AM UTC to analyze customer behavior data stored in S3 buckets that serve as a data lake. This data includes information such as page views, product clicks, and purchase history. The solution should prioritize operational efficiency.

Which of the following options would be most suitable for this scenario? (Select TWO.)

- **Use Amazon EventBridge to execute the Python code every Monday at 9:00 AM UTC.**
- Use AWS Cloud9 to write, run, and debug the Python code.
- **Use AWS Lambda to execute the Python code.**
- Use AWS Cloud Development Kit (CDK) to define cloud infrastructure in code and provision it through AWS CloudFormation.
- Use AWS CloudShell to execute the Python code.
- **Evaluation**
    
    AWS Lambda is a serverless computing service that lets you run your code without provisioning or managing servers. In the given scenario, you can upload your Python code to AWS Lambda, which takes care of all the capacity, scaling, patching, and administration of the infrastructure to run your code. This allows you to focus on analyzing customer behavior data rather than managing infrastructure, which aligns with the requirement to prioritize operational efficiency.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/eventbridge-032024.png)
    
    Amazon EventBridge is a serverless event bus service that makes it easy to connect applications together using data from your own applications, Software-as-a-Service (SaaS) applications, and AWS services. You can set up a rule in EventBridge to trigger your AWS Lambda function every Monday at 9:00 AM UTC. This way, your Python code gets executed automatically at the specified time each week.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Use AWS Lambda to execute the Python code.**
    
    **– Use Amazon EventBridge to execute the Python code every Monday at 9:00 AM UTC.**
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Cloud Development Kit (CDK) to define cloud infrastructure in code and provision it through AWS CloudFormation** is incorrect. While CDK can be used to create the infrastructure needed for the pipeline (including Lambda functions and EventBridge rules), it doesn’t directly execute Python code as part of a data processing pipeline. It’s more about setting up the infrastructure.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Cloud9 to write, run, and debug the Python code** is incorrect because AWS Cloud9 is just a cloud-based integrated development environment (IDE) that lets you write, run, and debug your code with just a browser. It can’t be used to execute Python code on schedule as part of a data processing pipeline.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS CloudShell to execute the Python code** is incorrect because AWS CloudShell is simply a browser-based shell intended for interactive use and does not support scheduling or automation.
    

A company plans to migrate its on-premises data to the AWS Cloud. This on-premises data is mainly used for data analytics and is on an NFS file share. Post-migration, the company utilizes Lambda functions to analyze the data further. This data should still be in an NFS file system shared with all Lambda functions to access across all concurrent runs.

Which solution would satisfy these requirements?

- Migrate the data to Amazon Elastic Block Store (EBS) volumes and enable access to these EBS volumes from each Lambda function.
- **Migrate the data to Amazon Elastic File System (EFS) and configure each Lambda function to mount the EFS for access.**
    
    **Amazon Elastic File System** is a scalable file storage service you can integrate with AWS Lambda or other compute options. Amazon EFS is a serverless solution that allows your compute resources in AWS to share data without provisioning or managing storage capacity and performance.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/EFS_7JAN2024.png)
    
    Amazon EFS offers a seamless, scalable, and fully managed NFS file system that perfectly aligns with the requirements for shared access and concurrent processing capabilities needed for AWS Lambda functions. While other storage solutions offer access sharing, Amazon EFS is the best solution for the scenario since the company must host the migrated data in an NFS file system.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Migrate the data to Amazon Elastic File System (EFS) and configure each Lambda function to mount the EFS for access.**
    
    The option that says: **Migrate the data to the local storage of each Lambda function for quick and seamless access** is incorrect. Remember, the local storage in Lambda functions is ephemeral; Lambda erases any data stored in its local storage as soon as the invocation ends. Additionally, this local storage does not support the NFS protocol.
    
    The option that says: **Migrate the data to Amazon Elastic Block Store (EBS) volumes and enable access to these EBS volumes from each Lambda function** is incorrect primarily because you cannot mount an EBS volume on a Lambda Function. EBS volumes offer block storage, and you can only mount EBS volumes on EC2 instances, which do not support the NFS protocol.
    
    The option that says: **Migrate the data to Amazon FSx for Windows File Server and configure each Lambda function to connect to the FSx file share for access** is incorrect. Although it is possible for multiple Lambda functions to access an Amazon FSx for Windows File Server, Amazon FSx for Windows File Server only supports the SMB protocol, not NFS. It would help to look at other Amazon FSx file system types that support NFS protocol, such as FSx for NetApp ONTAP and FSx for OpenZFS.
    
- Migrate the data to the local storage of each Lambda function for quick and seamless access.
- Migrate the data to Amazon FSx for Windows File Server and configure each Lambda function to connect to the FSx file share for access.

A data engineering team at a large retail company is planning to use AWS Glue for its data pipeline. The team needs to process approximately 500GB of data daily, which is stored in Amazon S3 and partitioned by date. The team plans to run Glue jobs every hour and is considering enabling autoscaling to optimize the number of DPUs.

What steps should the data engineering team take to estimate the right number of DPUs for Glue?

- Use the Glue Job Editor's Data Preview feature to analyze data transformations visually and estimate the optimal number of DPUs required for job execution.
- **Implement job metrics in Glue to estimate the number of data processing units (DPUs). Based on the profiled metrics, adjust the value of the maximum capacity job parameter.**
    
    **AWS Glue** provides a feature called job metrics, which can be used to estimate the number of DPUs that can be used to scale out an AWS Glue job. This feature is particularly useful in understanding the resource utilization of your jobs and can help in making informed decisions about scaling.
    
    ![DPUs Monitoring](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/dpus-monitoring-013024.png)
    
    When you run a job, AWS Glue provides metrics such as the total number of actively running executors, the number of completed stages, and the number of maximum needed executors. These metrics can give you insights into whether your job is under-provisioned or over-provisioned.
    
    For example, if the number of maximum needed executors is significantly higher than the number of active executors, it indicates that the job is under-provisioned. In such a case, you can increase the maximum capacity job parameter, which effectively increases the number of DPUs allocated to the job.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Implement job metrics in Glue to estimate the number of data processing units (DPUs). Based on the profiled metrics, adjust the value of the maximum capacity job parameter.**
    
    The option that says: **Allocate the maximum number of DPUs** is incorrect because allocating the maximum number of DPUs for all jobs, regardless of their complexity or data size, can lead to unnecessary costs. It’s important to match the resources to the requirements of each job.
    
    The option that says: **Use the Glue Job Editor’s Data Preview feature to analyze data transformations visually and estimate the optimal number of DPUs required for job execution** is incorrect. Data Preview is only valuable in developing and debugging ETL scripts by allowing you to see a preview of the data transformations. However, it won’t help estimate the optimal number of DPUs for job execution.
    
    The option that says: **Configure Glue job bookmarks to track and analyze data processing progress. Use this information to adjust the number of DPUs needed for future job runs** is incorrect. Job bookmarks is simply used for tracking data that has already been processed in a job, thereby preventing redundant processing. They don’t provide insights into the optimal number of DPUs required.
    
- Configure Glue job bookmarks to track and analyze data processing progress. Use this information to adjust the number of DPUs needed for future job runs.
- Allocate the maximum number of DPUs.

A company is leveraging its extensive dataset to train a machine learning (ML) model for predictive analytics. The dataset includes sensitive personally identifiable information (PII), which should be excluded from the ML model. In compliance with security standards, the data also needs to be encrypted using AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS). A data engineer is tasked with choosing the right AWS services to prepare and provide this data to the ML model.

Which combination of steps will meet these requirements in the MOST cost-effective manner? (Select TWO.)

- Load the dataset into Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler to encode the PII, then proceed with model training.
- Deliver the data to an Amazon OpenSearch Service cluster encrypted with AWS KMS.
- **Store the data in an Amazon S3 bucket and enable security features with AWS KMS server-side encryption (SSE-KMS).**
- **Implement AWS Glue DataBrew to manage the data intake process and employ a Glue DataBrew recipe job to conceal the PII within the dataset.**
- Use Amazon EMR to process and transform the data, applying custom scripts to mask PII before usage in the ML model.
- **Evaluation**
    
    AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS) is a managed service that enables you to easily create and control cryptographic keys used to encrypt your data. These keys are used across a variety of AWS services to provide encryption of stored data.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Encrypted_S3_30NOV2023.png)
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/KMS_30NOV2023.png)
    
    Server-Side Encryption with AWS KMS (SSE-KMS) is a feature where AWS manages both the encryption process and the cryptographic keys. When you use Amazon S3 with SSE-KMS, for example, your data is automatically encrypted as it is written to S3 and decrypted when accessed, using keys managed in KMS. This process provides an additional layer of security by involving the management of encryption keys. **This satisfies the encryption requirement of the problem.**
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/SageMakerGlue_30NOV2023.png)
    
    AWS Glue DataBrew is a visual data preparation tool that simplifies the process of cleaning and normalizing data. It features data masking capabilities, which are crucial for handling PII. DataBrew can be configured to identify and mask PII, ensuring that sensitive information is obscured or removed from datasets before they are used in machine learning models or analytics. This ensures compliance with regulations that mandate the protection of PII. This satisfies the PII masking requirement of the problem.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Store the data in an Amazon S3 bucket and enable security features with AWS KMS server-side encryption (SSE-KMS).**
    
    **– Implement AWS Glue DataBrew to manage the data intake process and employ a Glue DataBrew recipe job to conceal the PII within the dataset.**
    
    The option that says: **Deliver the data to an Amazon OpenSearch Service cluster encrypted with AWS KMS** is incorrect. Although Amazon OpenSearch Service with AWS KMS encryption ensures data security, it is more costly due to its search and analytics capabilities, which are not necessary for simply delivering data to an ML model.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon EMR to process and transform the data, applying custom scripts to mask PII before usage in the ML model** is incorrect. Although Amazon EMR is capable of managing large datasets and executing custom scripts for tasks like PII masking, its cost-effectiveness is diminished due to its design for more extensive big data processing, analytics, and complex ETL jobs. These advanced capabilities of Amazon EMR exceed the simple requirements of PII masking for machine learning models. As a result, it becomes a less cost-efficient choice compared to more specialized data preparation tools, such as AWS Glue DataBrew, which are tailored for simpler data processing tasks without the additional features and associated costs of handling large-scale data processing.
    
    The option that says: **Load the dataset into Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler to encode the PII, then proceed with model training** is incorrect because this solution would not satisfy the requirement that the ML model should not use the PII. Encoding PII within Data Wrangler does not eliminate it. Instead, it merely alters its format, leaving the underlying sensitive information accessible and potentially usable by the ML model, which poses a risk of non-compliance with data privacy standards.
    

A company is currently provisioning an Amazon Redshift cluster as its primary data warehouse. They plan to do intensive data analysis on a separate Amazon Redshift cluster that utilizes the same live data from the primary Redshift cluster. The second Redshift cluster will only be used for 2 hours every 2 weeks for the new intensive data analysis procedure.

Which would be the MOST cost-effective solution for meeting these requirements?

- Run the `UNLOAD` SQL command on the primary Redshift cluster to unload all the data to an S3 bucket every 2 weeks. Then, create an Amazon Glue job to load data from the S3 bucket to the second Redshift cluster.
- **Use Amazon Redshift Serverless for the second Redshift cluster. Then, establish a data share from the primary Redshift cluster to the second Redshift cluster.**
    
    **Amazon Redshifts data sharing** provides two powerful capabilities:
    
    – Read-only access to data across multiple Amazon Redshift clusters.
    
    – Read live data without manually moving or copying data.
    
    Furthermore, this feature supports seamless sharing between provisioned and serverless clusters. This feature enables users to ensure consistent data availability across different teams and systems, improving collaboration and decision-making. Additionally, integrating serverless clusters in data sharing brings a layer of scalability and cost-effectiveness, which is particularly beneficial for testing environments.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/RedshiftDataShare_8JAN2024.png)
    
    **Amazon Redshift Serverless** enables the use of Redshift capabilities without the need to provision and manage Redshift clusters, which is ideal for workloads that are intermittent and short-lived, such as the bi-weekly, 2-hour intensive data analysis procedure as said in the scenario. By utilizing Redshift Serverless for the second cluster, the company only pays for the compute resources used during the analysis period, avoiding the costs associated with running a dedicated Redshift cluster 24/7. Additionally, **establishing a data share** from the primary Redshift cluster to the second serverless cluster allows for direct access to the live data without the need for data duplication or complex data pipeline setups, such as manual data unloads to S3, Glue jobs for data loading, or snapshot restoration processes.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use Amazon Redshift Serverless for the second Redshift cluster. Then, establish a data share from the primary Redshift cluster to the second Redshift cluster.**
    
    The option that says: **Run the `UNLOAD` SQL command on the primary Redshift cluster to unload all the data to an S3 bucket every 2 weeks. Then, create an Amazon Glue job to load data from the S3 bucket to the second Redshift cluster** is incorrect primarily because this approach would require significant data transfer and processing time, which is not at all efficient for an infrequently used cluster (only 2 hours every 2 weeks). Additionally, this solution incurs extra costs for data storage in S3 and the use of Amazon Glue. Lastly, this method does not support access to live data.
    
    The option that says: **Run the `UNLOAD` SQL command on the primary Redshift cluster to unload all the data to an S3 bucket every 2 weeks. Then, use the Amazon Redshift spectrum from the secondary Redshift cluster to access the data stored in Amazon S3** is incorrect. Just like the previous option, it involves unnecessary complexity and data movement. While Redshift Spectrum allows querying data in S3 without loading it into Redshift, regularly unloading to S3 is inefficient for this use case since data analysis on the second Redshift cluster occurs infrequently. Additionally, this method incurs additional costs for S3 storage and potentially for Spectrum queries. Lastly, just like the previous option, this method does not support access to live data.
    
    The option that says: **Generate manual snapshots of the primary Redshift cluster every 2 weeks, then restore these snapshots to the second Redshift cluster, ensuring that its configuration mirrors that of the primary Redshift cluster** is incorrect primarily because provisioning another production cluster means two times the cost. Furthermore, snapshotting and restoring a cluster is a very heavy operation, especially for short and infrequent usage (only 2 hours every 2 weeks). Lastly, just like the previous option, this method does not support access to live data.
    
- Run the `UNLOAD` SQL command on the primary Redshift cluster to unload all the data to an S3 bucket every 2 weeks. Then, use the Amazon Redshift spectrum from the secondary Redshift cluster to access the data stored in Amazon S3.
- Generate manual snapshots of the primary Redshift cluster every 2 weeks. Then, restore these snapshots to the second Redshift cluster, ensuring that its configuration mirrors that of the primary Redshift cluster.

A data engineer uses Amazon Athena to run nested `SELECT` queries for joining multiple tables. The data engineer wants to run additional queries on these results but finds the process challenging and time-consuming.

Which of the following would simplify the querying process for the team?

- **Create a view in Amazon Athena for each `SELECT` query.**
    
    In Amazon Athena, a view is a logical, not a physical table. It’s essentially a saved query that you can reference, just like a table. This is particularly useful when dealing with complex queries. In the given scenario, the data engineer has already prepared complex `SELECT` queries that join multiple tables for data aggregation. These queries are likely intricate and time-consuming to write and execute. By creating a view for each of these `SELECT` queries, the team can save the definitions of these complex queries. This means that they don’t have to write the entire query each time they want to use it; they can simply reference the view. This significantly simplifies the querying process.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/create-view-athena-030424.png)
    
    Furthermore, the team can run further queries against these views. In this context, the views act as virtual tables or composed queries on the underlying data. This is a more efficient approach as it reduces the time and computational resources required to run complex queries repeatedly. It also allows the team to focus on data analysis rather than data management.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create a view in Amazon Athena for each `SELECT` query.**
    
    The option that says: **Enable the Query Result Reuse feature in Amazon Athena** is incorrect. This feature would only speed up queries by caching the results of previous queries. It won’t inherently simplify the process of writing or managing complex queries.
    
    The option that says: **Create separate views in Amazon Athena for each table involved in the queries** is incorrect. This method would be more helpful if simplifying access to individual tables is the goal. However, the challenge is to simplify running queries against the results of a single complex query. This approach would still require complex joins and logic to be applied every time the data is queried.
    
    The option that says: **Use the Athena workgroup settings for query execution management** is incorrect. Workgroup is just a way for you to control query execution and access, set data usage limits, and configure query result locations. It won’t help in simplifying. While it’s an essential feature for managing Athena usage at scale, it doesn’t directly simplify the writing or structuring of complex queries.
    
- Enable the Query Result Reuse feature in Amazon Athena.
- Use the Athena workgroup settings for query execution management
- Create separate views in Amazon Athena for each table involved in the queries.

A data engineering team at a healthcare startup is working on a project to analyze patient health records. They have a significant amount of patient health records stored in an external cloud data warehouse. The team’s goal is to migrate these large datasets to Amazon Redshift for advanced analytics.

Which combination of steps would be the most efficient way to achieve this with MINIMAL effort? (Select TWO.)

- Use the Amazon Athena SQL query editor to transfer the data from the external cloud data warehouse to Amazon Redshift.
- Export the data from the external cloud data warehouse into Amazon S3 as flat delimited text files. Then, use the Amazon Redshift SQL query editor with the `COPY` command to transfer the data from Amazon S3 to Amazon Redshift.
- **Utilize the AWS Schema Conversion Tool (AWS SCT) to adapt the source schema and data definition language (DDL) script from the external cloud data warehouse to Amazon Redshift.**
- **Leverage AWS Glue Studio to transfer the data from the external cloud data warehouse to Amazon Redshift.**
- Use the Amazon Athena notebook to adapt the source schema and data definition language (DDL) script from the external cloud data warehouse to Amazon Redshift.
- **Evaluation**
    
    AWS SCT is a tool that significantly reduces the effort to migrate to Amazon Redshift by automatically converting the source schema and a majority of the custom code to a format compatible with Amazon Redshift. The converted code can be in the form of views, stored procedures, and functions.
    
    In this scenario, the team needs to migrate patient health records from an external cloud data warehouse to Amazon Redshift. The first step in this process is to ensure that the schema and DDL scripts of the external cloud data warehouse are compatible with Amazon Redshift. This is where AWS SCT comes in. By using AWS SCT, the team can automatically convert the source schema and DDL scripts to a format that Amazon Redshift can understand, thereby reducing the manual effort required in the migration process.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/transform_schema-030124.png)
    
    AWS Glue Studio is a visual interface for AWS Glue that simplifies the process of creating, running, and monitoring ETL jobs. ETL stands for Extract, Transform, and Load, which are the three main steps in data integration processes.
    
    In the given scenario, the team needs to migrate patient health records from an external cloud data warehouse to Amazon Redshift. After the schemas and DDL scripts have been converted using AWS SCT, the next step is to transfer the data itself. AWS Glue Studio can be used to create an ETL job that extracts the data from the external cloud data warehouse, transforms the data if necessary (for example, cleaning the data or converting data types), and then loads the transformed data into Amazon Redshift. This process is automated, which means the team can migrate large datasets with minimal effort.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Utilize the AWS Schema Conversion Tool (AWS SCT) to adapt the source schema and data definition language (DDL) script from the external cloud data warehouse to Amazon Redshift.**
    
    **– Leverage AWS Glue Studio to transfer the data from the external cloud data warehouse to Amazon Redshift.**
    
    The option that says: **Use the Amazon Athena notebook to adapt the source schema and data definition language (DDL) script from the external cloud data warehouse to Amazon Redshift** is incorrect because Amazon Athena is simply an interactive query service that makes it easy to analyze data in Amazon S3 using standard SQL. It is not designed to convert schemas or DDL scripts from one database to another.
    
    The option that says: **Use the Amazon Athena SQL query editor to transfer the data from the external cloud data warehouse to Amazon Redshift** is incorrect. It is true that Amazon Athena can be used to query data directly in S3, but this service is not designed to transfer data from an external cloud data warehouse to Amazon Redshift. Using AWS Glue Studio is a better option for easily transferring data to Redshift.
    
    The option that says: **Export the data from the external cloud data warehouse into Amazon S3 as flat delimited text files. Then, use the Amazon Redshift SQL query editor with the `COPY`command to transfer the data from Amazon S3 to Amazon Redshift** is incorrect because while this method could technically work, it would require significant manual effort and is not the most efficient way to transfer data from an external cloud data warehouse to Amazon Redshift. A more efficient way would be to use AWS Glue Studio or AWS SCT, which can automate the process and handle larger datasets more effectively.
    

A software company is rehosting a high-volume data-processing application running on-premises to AWS Lambda. Currently, the application has access to 200 GB of data stored on a local disk for its operations. The company prefers to migrate the application with minimal changes to the code.

Which approach would best meet these requirements?

- Transfer the reference dataset to an Amazon S3 bucket and set up a Lambda function for data interaction using Amazon S3 API.
- Implement a cluster and domain in Amazon OpenSearch Service and integrate data using Logstash. Modify the Lambda function to communicate with this environment for data access and manipulation.
- **Launch a new file system in the Amazon Elastic File System (Amazon EFS) console and upload the reference data. Create an access point and configure the Lambda function for data access.**
    
    **Amazon Elastic File System (Amazon EFS)** is an easy-to-use and scalable file storage solution for AWS Cloud services and on-premises resources. It can scale up to petabytes of data without affecting your applications and automatically adjusts its size as files are added or removed. This makes it a highly reliable and available storage option for a wide range of workloads and applications, including AWS Lambda functions.
    
    An **Amazon EFS access point** is a way to manage application access to shared datasets within an EFS file system. With access points, you can configure a user, group, and root directory for all file system requests made through that access point. This ensures that access control is enforced at the file system level.
    
    **AWS Lambda** enables serverless execution of code, scaling automatically to meet demand from a few requests to thousands per second.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA2-40-access-point-lambda-diagram-21Mar2024.jpg)
    
    Lambda functions can seamlessly mount an EFS file system to the function’s file system namespace using an access point. This setup allows Lambda functions to read and write files just as if they were on a local file system but with EFS’s scalability, flexibility, and performance. This method requires minimal changes to existing code that interacts with mounted volumes, making it the most suitable option for migrating data-intensive workloads with minimal code refactoring.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Launch a new file system in the Amazon Elastic File System (Amazon EFS) console and upload the reference data. Create an access point and configure the Lambda function for data access.**
    
    The option that says: **Temporarily store the reference dataset in the Lambda function’s `/tmp` directory** is incorrect. The `/tmp`directory in AWS Lambda provides a temporary storage space for the function’s execution environment. Data in this directory exists only for the duration of the function’s runtime and is then lost. Additionally, with its limited size (512 MB to 10 GB), it’s unsuitable for workloads that require access to 200 GB of data.
    
    The option that says: **Transfer the reference dataset to an Amazon S3 bucket and set up a Lambda function for data interaction using Amazon S3 API** is incorrect. While a Lambda function can read and write data to and from an S3 bucket, implementing this solution would require significant modifications to the existing code that accesses data through a mounted volume. Specifically, you would need to modify the code to use the S3 data API with the AWS SDK, which would not meet the requirement to minimize the number of code modifications.
    
    The option that says: **Implement a cluster and domain in Amazon OpenSearch Service and integrate data using Logstash. Modify the Lambda function to communicate with this environment for data access and manipulation** is incorrect. Amazon OpenSearch Service (successor to Amazon Elasticsearch Service) offers powerful search capabilities over large volumes of data. While it’s an excellent service for search and analytics, using it for data-intensive workloads that originally rely on mounted data volumes would necessitate substantial changes to the application logic, particularly for workloads not primarily focused on search or analytics.
    
- Temporarily store the reference dataset in the Lambda function’s `/tmp`directory.

A company uses an Amazon Kinesis data stream to manage its real-time data processing workloads. Recently, their consumer application on an EC2 instance started encountering `ProvisionedThroughputExceededException`errors.

The company must resolve this issue to ensure that the read data calls to the stream consistently succeed and that their real-time data processing is not disrupted.

Which actions could be taken to resolve the error? (Select TWO.)

- Enable the enhanced fan-out functionality on the stream.
- Activate the enhanced monitoring feature for the stream.
- **Increase the shard count of the Kinesis data stream.**
- Use a larger EC2 instance type for the Kinesis Data Streams application.
- **Implement exponential backoff and retry logic in the application.**
- **Evaluation**
    
    **Amazon Kinesis Data Streams** is a real-time data streaming service offered by Amazon Web Services (AWS). It enables the collection, processing, and analysis of large streams of data records in real-time. A Kinesis data stream is a collection of data records that are continually and sequentially added to over time, much like a stream. Each data record in the stream is composed of a sequence number, a partition key, and a data blob, which is an immutable sequence of bytes. It is capable of handling real-time data from hundreds of thousands of sources at a massive scale. It’s commonly used for scenarios such as collecting and processing log and event data, real-time analytics, and real-time metrics and reporting.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/kinesis-shard-012324.png)
    
    Amazon Kinesis Data Streams uses shards, which are essentially independent streams of data within a Kinesis data stream. Each shard has a fixed rate of data it can handle — 1 MB/sec data input and 2 MB/sec data output. If the data rate exceeds the capacity of the current shards, you will receive a
    
    ```
    ProvisionedThroughputExceededException
    ```
    
    error. Therefore, increasing the number of shards in your data stream can increase the throughput of your data stream and resolve this error. This is a process known as resharding, which involves splitting or merging shards to adjust the total capacity of the stream.
    
    When a `ProvisionedThroughputExceededException` error occurs, it means that the application is reading data too quickly from the stream. Implementing a backoff strategy, where the application waits for a short period before trying the request again, can help manage the rate of data retrieval and avoid exceeding the read-throughput limit. This is a common strategy for handling rate-limited APIs and services, and it’s particularly important when dealing with real-time data processing workloads where maintaining a steady flow of data is crucial.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Increase the shard count of the Kinesis data stream.**
    
    **– Implement exponential backoff and retry logic in the application.**
    
    The option that says: **Activate the enhanced monitoring feature for the stream** is incorrect because while enhanced monitoring provides additional metrics for the stream, it does not directly resolve the `ProvisionedThroughputExceededException` error. This error is related to the stream’s capacity, not its monitoring.
    
    The option that says: **Enable the enhanced fan-out functionality on the stream** is incorrect. While enhanced fan-out can improve the consumer-side experience by providing dedicated throughput per consumer, it does not directly address the overall capacity quotas of the data stream. It’s more about optimizing the consumption pattern rather than increasing the stream’s capacity to accept writes or aggregate reads without exceeding provisioned throughput.
    
    The option that says: **Use a larger EC2 instance type for the Kinesis Data Streams application** is incorrect because the size of the EC2 instance does not directly affect the capacity of the Kinesis data stream. The `ProvisionedThroughputExceededException` error is related to the stream’s capacity, not the size of the EC2 instance running the application.
    

A logistics firm analyzes its shipment data daily. The firm plans to execute a data processing job using Apache Spark to calculate delivery performance and subsequently notify its operations team. The unprocessed data is stored on Amazon S3, and the computed statistics, which will be analyzed using SQL, will also be stored on Amazon S3.

Which steps should the team take to fulfill these requirements while minimizing operational overhead? (SELECT TWO.)

- Set up a cron job in Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (MWAA) with Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAGs) to execute the Spark job, initiate an AWS Glue crawler, and send an alert to the operations team through Amazon SNS.
- **Create an Amazon EventBridge scheduled rule to trigger the AWS Step Functions workflow every day.**
- Use Amazon EventBridge Scheduler to run the Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAGs) every day.
- **Create an AWS Step Functions workflow with task states for running the Spark job in an Amazon EMR Serverless application, initiating an AWS Glue crawler, and publishing an Amazon SNS message to inform the operations team.**
- Create a Lambda function that imports the unprocessed data into an Amazon Redshift cluster. Create a second Lambda function to execute the Spark job using the Amazon Redshift integration for Apache Spark. Orchestrate the Lambda functions in an AWS Step Functions workflow.
- **Evaluation**
    
    Amazon EventBridge is a serverless event bus service that enables you to easily connect applications together using data from your applications, integrated Software-as-a-Service (SaaS) applications, and AWS services. It allows you to route real-time data streams to different endpoints and trigger workflows in a decoupled fashion. When integrated with AWS Step Functions, it can trigger complex workflows on a schedule, such as daily data processing jobs.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/eventbridge-022924.png)
    
    In this scenario, a scheduled EventBridge rule initiates a workflow in AWS Step Functions, orchestrating a series of tasks. First, it runs an Apache Spark job on an EMR Serverless application, processing data without requiring server management. Next, AWS Glue crawls the processed data, registering its schema in the AWS Glue Data Catalog for SQL querying with services like Athena. Finally, upon successful completion, Amazon SNS notifies the operations team. This approach utilizes serverless services to reduce operational overhead.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Create an Amazon EventBridge scheduled rule to trigger the AWS Step Functions workflow every day.**
    
    **– Create an AWS Step Functions workflow with task states for running the Spark job in an Amazon EMR Serverless application, initiating an AWS Glue crawler, and publishing an Amazon SNS message to inform the operations team.**
    
    The option that says: **Create a Lambda function that imports the unprocessed data into an Amazon Redshift cluster. Create a second Lambda function to execute the Spark job using the Amazon Redshift integration for Apache Spark. Orchestrate the Lambda functions in an AWS Step Functions workflow** is incorrect. While Redshift is a powerful data warehousing solution, it’s not designed to execute Spark jobs.
    
    The option that says: **Set up a cron job in Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (MWAA) with Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAGs) to execute the Spark job, initiate an AWS Glue crawler, and send an alert to the operations team through Amazon SNS** is incorrect because while Amazon MWAA can schedule and orchestrate workflows, it’s more complex and requires more operational overhead than AWS Step Functions. The question asks for a solution that minimizes operational overhead, making this option less suitable.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon EventBridge Scheduler to run the Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAGs) every day** is incorrect. While Amazon EventBridge Scheduler is capable of triggering various AWS resources such as Lambda functions, it does not directly manage or execute Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAGs) associated with Apache Airflow workflows. Apache Airflow uses its scheduler to manage DAG execution within its environment.
    

A data engineer has developed an Extract, Load, Transform (ETL) process that loads data from an S3 bucket into an Amazon Redshift cluster. The current process uses the `COPY` command to ingest data to the Redshift cluster. The company wants to improve the efficiency of its current process.

Which additional step should be implemented as part of the new ETL process?

- **Create a temporary staging table to hold the data for transformation.**
    
    A **COPY** command is the most efficient way to load a table. You can also add data to your tables using INSERT commands, though it is much less efficient than using COPY. The COPY command is able to read from multiple data files or multiple data streams simultaneously. Amazon Redshift allocates the workload to the cluster nodes and performs the load operations in parallel, including sorting the rows and distributing data across node slices.
    
    You can use temporary staging tables to hold the data for transformation. These tables are automatically dropped after the ETL session is complete. Temporary tables can be created using the `CREATE TEMPORARY TABLE` syntax, or by issuing a `SELECT … INTO #TEMP_TABLE`query. Explicitly specifying the `CREATE TEMPORARY TABLE` statement allows you to control the DISTRIBUTION KEY, SORT KEY, and compression settings to further improve performance.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/ETL3_22OCT2023.png)
    
    A temporary table acts like a normal table but has a lifetime of a single SQL session. The proper use of temporary tables can significantly improve the performance of some ETL operations. Unlike regular permanent tables, data changes made to temporary tables don’t trigger automatic incremental backups to Amazon S3, and they don’t require synchronous block mirroring to store a redundant copy of data on a different compute node. Due to these reasons, data ingestion on temporary tables involves reduced overhead and performs much faster.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create a temporary staging table to hold the data for transformation.**
    
    The option that says: **Load the data to an external table using Amazon Redshift Spectrum** is incorrect. Redshift Spectrum just offers a way for querying data directly on Amazon S3. It won’t help improve the efficiency of loading data into Redshift.
    
    The option that says: **Ingest data in bulk using the `UNLOAD` command** is incorrect because the UNLOAD command literally ‘unloads’ or moves the result of your Redshift query to Amazon S3 and not the other way around.
    
    The option that says: **Load data into the Amazon Redshift cluster with the `S3DistCp`command** is incorrect because the S3DistCp tool is primarily used in Amazon EMR, not in Redshift, to load data from Amazon S3 to HDFS.
    
- Load data into the Amazon Redshift cluster with the `S3DistCp` command.
- Load the data to an external table using Amazon Redshift Spectrum.
- Ingest data in bulk using the `UNLOAD`command.

A company ingests large amounts of data through an Amazon Kinesis Data Stream and stores it into an Amazon S3 data lake. Additionally, the company uses AWS Lambda to consume and process these incoming data. However, the ingestion process undergoes a highly variable and unpredictable volume.

This variability significantly increases the `IteratorAge` metric during peak times when the data stream experiences high volume. A data engineer must design a solution to improve performance when processing data from Kinesis Data Streams with Lambda.

Which solutions will meet these requirements? (Select THREE.)

- **Register the Lambda function as a consumer to the Kinesis Data Stream with enhanced fan-out.**
- **Add additional shards in the Kinesis Data Stream.**
- **Configure different parallelization factor settings to identify the optimal performance setting.**
- Use the provisioned capacity mode of the Kinesis Data Stream.
- Decrease the number of shards in the Kinesis Data Stream.
- Submit a support ticket to raise the default Lambda provisioned concurrency limit.
- **Evaluation**
    
    **Amazon Kinesis Data Streams** is a service designed for real-time data streaming and processing. It captures and stores large volumes of data, allowing applications to process and analyze data immediately instead of waiting until all data is collected. Kinesis Data Streams are composed of shards, each with a capacity limit for read and write operations. **AWS Lambda** can directly process data from Kinesis Data Streams. When a Kinesis Data Stream triggers a Lambda function, it processes a batch of records from the stream. Lambda functions can scale automatically with the number of shards in a Kinesis stream; each Lambda invocation processes data from a single shard. The **Parallelization Factor** in AWS Lambda determines how many concurrent invocations per shard are made. Adjusting this factor can optimize the processing of records and improve throughput. Lastly, The **Enhanced Fan-Out** feature of Amazon Kinesis provides a dedicated throughput of 2 MB/sec for read operations per shard for each registered consumer, as opposed to the shared throughput of 2 MB/sec across all consumers (e.g. EC2 Instances and Lambda Functions) in standard mode.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Kinesis_8JAN2024.png)
    
    In the context of handling variable and unpredictable data volumes with Amazon Kinesis Data Streams and AWS Lambda, **adding more shards** to the data stream increases its capacity to handle larger volumes of data by enabling higher throughput and parallel processing capabilities, which is crucial for managing peak loads. **Adjusting the parallelization factor** allows for fine-tuning the number of records processed concurrently by Lambda, optimizing performance by balancing the workload across more function instances, which enhances throughput during high-volume periods. **Enhanced fan-out** provides a dedicated throughput for Lambda consumers, ensuring faster and more reliable data delivery with reduced latency, which is essential for real-time processing needs. Together, these strategies effectively address the challenges of processing large and fluctuating data volumes, ensuring that the system can scale to meet demand while minimizing processing delays, as reflected by lower `IteratorAge` metrics.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Add additional shards in the Kinesis Data Stream.**
    
    **– Configure different parallelization factor settings to identify the optimal performance setting.**
    
    **– Register the Lambda function as a consumer to the Kinesis Data Stream with enhanced fan-out.**
    
    The option that says: **Use the provisioned capacity mode of the Kinesis Data Stream** is incorrect mainly because switching to this capacity mode does not increase performance. Simply using provisioned capacity mode without increasing the number of shards does not inherently address the issue of handling high volume and variability in data ingestion.
    
    The option that says: **Submit a support ticket to raise the default Lambda reserved concurrency limit** is incorrect. The context above did not state anything about the Lambda function nearing its service limit. While it can ensure that a certain level of Lambda resources is always available for your function, it doesn’t inherently improve the processing of high volumes of data from Kinesis.
    
    The option that says: **Decrease the number of shards in the Kinesis Data Stream** is incorrect. Like the previous option, the context did not state anything about the Lambda function nearing its service limit. While it can reduce the function’s startup time, it doesn’t directly improve the processing capability for high volumes of data from Kinesis Data Streams.
    

A data engineering team is tasked with migrating and continuously replicating data from an on-premises Microsoft SQL Server database to Amazon RDS for SQL Server. The team intends to use AWS Database Migration Service (AWS DMS) for both initial migration and subsequent data change synchronization. The objective is to implement a solution that requires minimal manual effort.

Which combination of actions should the team take to fulfill these requirements? (Select TWO.)

- **Create an AWS DMS task in Full load plus `CHANGE DATA CAPTURE (CDC)` mode.**
- Create an AWS Datasync task to run one-time transfers for migration of the on-premises SQL Server.
- Use the AWS Schema Conversion Tool (SCT) to convert the source database schema to be compatible with the target database.
- Create an AWS DMS task in `CHANGE DATA CAPTURE (CDC) only` mode.
- **Turn on the full transaction log settings on the on-premises SQL Server.**
- **Evaluation**
    
    **AWS Database Migration Service (AWS DMS)**allows you to migrate databases to AWS easily and securely. It supports migrations between on-premises and AWS databases and between different AWS database services. The source or target of the migration must be on AWS.
    
    An AWS DMS task can be created to capture ongoing changes from the source data store during the data migration process. Additionally, a task can be configured to capture ongoing changes after completing the initial full-load migration to a supported target data store. This process, known as ongoing replication or change data capture (CDC), is employed by AWS DMS when replicating ongoing changes from a source data store. The CDC process works by collecting changes to the database logs using the database engine’s native API.
    
    There are two types of ongoing replication tasks available in AWS DMS:
    
    **Full load plus Change data capture (CDC)** – The task migrates existing data and then updates the target database based on changes to the source database.
    
    **Change data capture (CDC) only** – The task migrates ongoing changes after you have data on your target database.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA2-42-aws-dms-diagram-from-aws-030124.png)
    
    There are [different prerequisites](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/dms/latest/userguide/CHAP_Source.SQLServer.html) that must be configured in the source database to allow Change Data Capture (CDC) to take place. Most commonly, transaction logs need to be enabled, which depends on the specific database being used. For example, with Microsoft SQL Server as the source, full transaction logging must be turned on to enable CDC. On the other hand, if the source is a MySQL database, binary logging must be enabled first before CDC can be utilized.
    
    In the scenario, since the requirement is to conduct an initial migration from the on-premises Microsoft SQL Server database to Amazon RDS for SQL Server, followed by capturing ongoing changes thereafter, an AWS DMS task that runs in the Full load plus CDC (Change Data Capture) mode must be used. This mode ensures that the initial full load migration is performed, and subsequent data changes are continuously replicated to the target database.
    
    Hence, the answers are:
    
    **– Turn on the full transaction log settings on the on-premises SQL Server.**
    
    **– Create an AWS DMS task in Full load plus `CHANGE DATA CAPTURE (CDC)` mode.**
    
    The option that says: **Create an AWS Datasync task to run one-time transfers for migration of the on-premises SQL Server** is incorrect. AWS DataSync is mainly used for transferring files and objects between on-premises storage and AWS storage services, not for migrating databases. Also, one-time transfers would not fulfill the requirement for subsequent data change synchronization after the initial migration.
    
    The option that says: **Use the AWS Schema Conversion Tool (SCT) to convert the source database schema to be compatible with the target database** is incorrect. AWS SCT is not required in this case since the source and target databases are both SQL Server instances, and no schema conversion is necessary.
    
    The option that says: **Create an AWS DMS task in `CHANGE DATA CAPTURE (CDC) only` mode**is incorrect. This option can work if the initial full load migration has already been completed by another method. However, please note that the scenario requires a solution for both initial migration and subsequent data change synchronization.
    

A data engineer is looking to improve the efficiency of their data processing workflows on AWS, aiming to decrease the overall processing time. The solution involves executing multiple tasks concurrently within their AWS Step Functions managed workflow.

Which AWS Step Functions state should the data engineer utilize to meet this requirement?

- **`Parallel`**
    
    In AWS Step Functions, the `Parallel` state is used to create parallel branches of execution in your state machine. AWS Step Functions automatically transitions to the next state after all branches of the `Parallel` state complete their tasks. This means that the `Parallel` state is the one to check to ensure all parallel processes have been completed successfully before moving on.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/step-functions-parallel-032524.png)
    
    A `Parallel` state causes AWS Step Functions to execute each branch, starting with the state named in that branch’s `StartAt` field, as concurrently as possible and wait until all branches terminate (reach a terminal state) before processing the `Parallel` state’s `Next` field.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **`Parallel.`**
    
    **`Fail`** is incorrect because the `Fail` state in AWS Step Functions is used to terminate the state machine with a failure. It does not have the functionality to check the completion of parallel tasks.
    
    **`Choice`** is incorrect because the `Choice` state simply allows for branching logic based on input data. While it can direct the workflow to different execution paths based on specified conditions, it does not support the concurrent execution of tasks.
    
    **`Succeed`** is incorrect. This state is just used to terminate the execution of a state machine successfully. It does not have the functionality to check the completion of parallel tasks.
    
- `Choice`
- `Fail`
- `Succeed`

A company uses an Amazon OpenSearch Service cluster for log analytics. Due to regulatory compliance, the company must store a large amount of index data for an indefinite period of time. The data is accessed infrequently, but when accessed, it needs to be available immediately. The company also wants to minimize costs as much as possible.

Which solution would meet the requirements?

- Move the index in a Cold storage.
- **Add UltraWarm nodes on the cluster and move the index to it.**
    
    UltraWarm nodes in Amazon OpenSearch Service utilize Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) along with associated caching solutions to enhance performance. UltraWarm provides a significantly lower cost per GiB for read-only data that is queried less frequently and doesn’t require the same performance level as hot storage. Although data in UltraWarm can’t be modified, it can be moved to the hot storage tier for modifications before being moved back.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/open-search-data-storage-022924.png)
    
    When determining UltraWarm storage needs, only the size of the primary shards is considered. When querying for the list of shards in UltraWarm, both primary and replicas are listed. However, both shards are placeholders for the same, single copy of the data, which resides in Amazon S3. The durability of data in Amazon S3 eliminates the need for replicas, and Amazon S3 abstracts away any operating system or service considerations. In the hot tier, accounting for one replica, 20 GB of index uses 40 GB of storage. In the UltraWarm tier, it’s billed at 20 GB.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Add UltraWarm nodes on the cluster and move the index to it.**
    
    The option that says: **Store the index in an OR1 storage** is incorrect because. OR1 instances keep a copy of the data in both local (Amazon EBS) and remote storage (Amazon S3). This contrasts with UltraWarm nodes, which are optimized to store data primarily in remote storage to reduce costs. This makes OR1 instances more expensive compared to UltraWarm.
    
    The option that says: **Move the index in a Cold storage** is incorrect. Although Cold storage is the most cost-effective for long-term storage of rarely accessed data, it requires a reattachment process to the cluster for the data to become accessible. This introduces delays in data availability, contradicting the requirement for data to be available immediately when accessed.
    
    The option that says: **Use Index State Management (ISM) to schedule the deletion of the index data** is incorrect. ISM is a feature in Amazon OpenSearch Service that automates routine index management tasks. It can be used to move data between different storage classes (hot, UltraWarm, and cold) based on a schedule or delete stale data. Since there is no definite period for when to delete or continue storing the data, applying ISM isn’t the right solution for this scenario.
    
- Use Index State Management (ISM) to schedule the deletion of the index data.
- Store the index in an OR1 storage

A company is hosting its application in Amazon EC2 instances. These EC2 instances connect to an Amazon RDS for Microsoft SQL Server database. The nature of the application is that it stores and processes sensitive and confidential data in its database. They aim to minimize the possibility of exposing their database credentials.

Which of the following solutions will meet this requirement?

- Utilize the AWS IAM Identity Center to authenticate to the Amazon RDS for Microsoft SQL Server database.
- **Utilize AWS Secrets Manager to store and automatically rotate credentials every 30 days.**
    
    **AWS Secrets Manager** offers direct integration with Amazon RDS. Not only does it store database credentials automatically, but it can also rotate these credentials automatically. This service provides a secure and centralized way to handle sensitive information like database credentials.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/SecretsManager_10JAN2024.jpg)
    
    **AWS Secrets Manager** provides a centralized service to manage secrets like database credentials securely, allowing applications on EC2 instances to retrieve them dynamically. Its built-in support for automatic rotation of secrets ensures that database credentials are regularly updated without manual intervention, significantly reducing the potential for unauthorized access. This approach not only enhances security by automating the credential lifecycle management but also aligns with best practices for securing access to databases, like Amazon RDS for Microsoft SQL Server, by minimizing the static configuration of sensitive information on the instances and instead securely retrieving credentials as needed.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Utilize AWS Secrets Manager to store and automatically rotate credentials every 30 days.**
    
    The option that says: **Utilize the AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) database authentication to safely authenticate the EC2 instances to the Amazon RDS for Microsoft SQL Server database** is incorrect. Although this option seamlessly authenticates compute resources to Amazon RDS, the AWS IAM database authentication does not support Amazon RDS for Microsoft SQL Server. IAM authentication only works with MariaDB, MySQL, and PostgreSQL.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize the Advanced Parameters from the AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store to support credential rotation every 30 days** is incorrect primarily because the AWS Systems Manager (AWS SSM) Parameter Store does not support credential rotation. Although values can be encrypted through AWS KMS, once the credentials are compromised, AWS SSM Parameter Store cannot automatically rotate these credentials.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize the AWS IAM Identity Center to authenticate to the Amazon RDS for Microsoft SQL Server database** is incorrect primarily because AWS IAM Identity Center is not used to manage database credentials or directly authenticate databases. This service is used for user identity management and access control across AWS services.
    
- Utilize the Advanced Parameters from the AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store to support credential rotation every 30 days.
- Utilize the AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) database authentication to safely authenticate the EC2 instances to the Amazon RDS for Microsoft SQL Server database.

A data engineering team has recently implemented an ETL workload that leverages various AWS services. Given the complexity and scale of their operations, the team now seeks a solution that will enable them to generate custom reports of their costs and usage across these services. The goal is to analyze spending habits and find areas to cut costs.

Which solution will meet this requirement?

- **Utilize AWS Cost Explorer to create and retrieve customizable reports that analyze AWS costs and usage data.**
    
    **AWS Cost Explorer** is a tool designed to provide a comprehensive view of your AWS costs and usage over time, helping you better manage your AWS resources. It provides the following benefits:
    
    **Custom reporting** – You can create custom reports to analyze your cost and usage data at different levels of granularity, like total costs across all accounts or specific costs by service, region, etc. This allows you to gain insights into your spending patterns.
    
    **Cost optimization** – Cost Explorer provides visibility into your usage at different levels, which helps you identify opportunities to optimize costs, like reducing the usage of underutilized resources.
    
    **Trend analysis** – With Cost Explorer, you can analyze cost and usage trends over multiple years at monthly granularity. This helps understand fluctuations and plan budgets accordingly.
    
    **Resource level data** – It provides cost attribution down to the individual resource level, like EC2 instances, allowing you to pinpoint expensive resources consuming the most costs.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/dea2-58-image-from-aws-docs.jpg)
    
    Cost Explorer would be a good fit for the data engineering team as it would allow them to generate customized reports on costs and usage of various AWS services used in their ETL workload. This will help them gain insights into spending patterns and identify opportunities to optimize costs, like rightsizing resources or reducing the usage of underutilized ones.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Utilize AWS Cost Explorer to create and retrieve customizable reports that analyze AWS costs and usage data.**
    
    The option that says: **Implement AWS Budgets to set custom cost and usage budgets that alert when thresholds are exceeded** is incorrect because AWS Budgets primarily focuses on monitoring and controlling AWS costs by setting budgets and alerts rather than generating detailed reports on costs and usage patterns.
    
    The option that says: **Deploy Amazon QuickSight for visualizing and analyzing AWS costs and usage data through dashboards** is incorrect because, although Amazon QuickSight can be used for visualization and analysis, it would require the data to be imported and processed, making it a more complex solution compared to directly using AWS Cost Explorer for cost analysis.
    
    The option that says: **Configure Amazon CloudWatch to monitor and alert on AWS service usage and associated costs** is incorrect because Amazon CloudWatch is primarily a monitoring service for AWS cloud resources and applications, not tailored for detailed cost and usage reporting.
    
- Deploy Amazon QuickSight for visualizing and analyzing AWS costs and usage data through dashboards.
- Implement AWS Budgets to set custom cost and usage budgets that alert when thresholds are exceeded.
- Configure Amazon CloudWatch to monitor and alert on AWS service usage and associated costs.

A data engineering team is enhancing the observability of an application deployed in an Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS) cluster.

The team wants to centralize the collection of low-level system metrics and logs from various microservices and enable near real-time visualization of these data for better insight and quicker response times.

What solution will satisfy these requirements with the LEAST amount of operational overhead?

- **Collect metrics and logs from the microservices using Amazon CloudWatch Container Insights. Configure a Data Firehose subscription filter to stream the logs to an Amazon OpenSearch Service cluster.**
    
    **Amazon CloudWatch Container Insights** is a service that monitors, troubleshoots, and alarms on containerized applications and microservices. It collects metrics and logs from EKS, ECS, and Kubernetes clusters and provides automatic dashboards in the CloudWatch console for end-to-end visibility.
    
    **Data Firehose** is a fully managed service that delivers real-time streaming data to destinations like S3, Redshift, and OpenSearch Service. A subscription filter in Firehose allows you to selectively filter logs from CloudWatch Logs and deliver them to the configured destination.
    
    The **Amazon OpenSearch Service** is a fully managed service that makes it easy to deploy, operate, and scale OpenSearch implementations. It provides enhanced security, availability, integration with other AWS services, and lower costs compared to managing your own OpenSearch cluster. Furthermore, it enables the visualization of log data through Kibana dashboards.
    
    ![Diagram of the given correct solution.](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA2-41-cw-insights-Diagram-022924.jpg)
    
    The data engineering team can use Amazon CloudWatch Container Insights to collect metrics and logs from the microservices running in the various EKS clusters. They can configure a Firehose stream to transport the logs from Container Insights to Amazon OpenSearch Service. OpenSearch Service is a fully managed service that makes it easy to deploy, operate, and scale for log analytics. It allows indexing and visualizing the logs from Container Insights for near real-time search and analysis. This removes the operational overhead of managing the infrastructure. The team can use OpenSearch Dashboards or Kibana for visualization and Grafana for dashboards and alerts, providing a single pane of glass for monitoring across all their clusters and enabling improved troubleshooting capabilities.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Collect metrics and logs from the microservices using Amazon CloudWatch Container Insights. Configure a Data Firehose subscription filter to stream the logs to an Amazon OpenSearch Service cluster.**
    
    The option that says: **Configure an Amazon Kinesis Agent to channel the logs from the microservices to Kinesis Data Streams. Relay the logs to Amazon DynamoDB with AWS Lambda for storage and analysis** is incorrect. This option does not provide an integrated solution for real-time visualization of query results. While it captures logs and uses AWS Lambda for processing, DynamoDB is primarily a NoSQL database suited for storage and not for the real-time visualization of logs and metrics.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon CloudWatch Application Insights to aggregate metrics and logs from the microservices. Set up an AWS Lambda subscription filter to push the log data to an Amazon OpenSearch Service cluster** is incorrect. CloudWatch Application Insights is primarily designed for monitoring applications rather than being a direct solution for aggregating metrics and logs from microservices. It doesn’t offer a built-in way to collect and visualize Kubernetes metrics or logs specifically. Furthermore, using AWS Lambda as an intermediary to deliver logs to Amazon OpenSearch introduces additional latency and complexity. AWS Lambda functions add an extra processing step, which can result in delays, especially under high throughput or when the function needs to scale.
    
    The option that says: **Channel logs from microservices to Data Firehose, then relay the logs to OpenSearch Service for enhanced search and analysis** is incorrect. While this option involves Amazon OpenSearch Service for search and analysis, it misses the integration with Amazon CloudWatch Container Insights, designed explicitly for monitoring containerized applications and provides essential metrics and logs crucial for observability.
    
- Configure an Amazon Kinesis Agent to channel the logs from the microservices to Kinesis Data Streams. Relay the logs to Amazon DynamoDB with AWS Lambda for storage and analysis.
- Use Amazon CloudWatch Application Insights to aggregate metrics and logs from the microservices. Set up an AWS Lambda subscription filter to push the log data to an Amazon OpenSearch Service cluster.
- Channel logs from microservices to Data Firehose, then relay the logs to OpenSearch Service for enhanced search and analysis.

A data engineering team configures an AWS Glue crawler in the `Tutorial's` AWS account to catalog VPC flow logs stored in an Amazon S3 bucket within the `Dojo`‘s account. The goal is to ensure the AWS Glue crawler in the `Tutorial`‘s account can access and catalog data seamlessly, focusing on integrating AWS services for cross-account resource access.

Which combination of actions should be taken to fulfill these requirements? (Select THREE.)

- Establish a Direct Connect link between the `Tutorials` and `Dojo`‘s accounts to ensure private connectivity for the AWS Glue crawler to access the S3 bucket.
- Utilize AWS Resource Access Manager (AWS RAM) in the `Dojo`‘s account to share the S3 bucket with the `Tutorial`‘s account, facilitating cross-account access.
- **Update the AWS Glue crawler’s configuration in the `Tutorial`‘s account to target the S3 bucket containing the VPC flow logs in the `Dojo`‘s account.**
- Create a VPC Endpoint for S3 in `Dojo`‘s account to streamline access between the AWS Glue crawler in the `Tutorial`‘s account and the S3 bucket.
- **Implement a bucket policy on the S3 bucket in the `Dojo`‘s account that explicitly permits the AWS Glue crawler’s IAM role in the `Tutorial`‘s account access to the bucket.**
- **Configure an IAM role in the `Tutorial`’s account with permissions to access the S3 bucket in the `Dojo`’s account and associate this role with the AWS Glue crawler.**
- **Evaluation**
    
    AWS Glue is a completely managed service for ETL (extract, transform, load), simplifying the process for users to prepare and import their data for analytical purposes. The AWS Glue crawler can be configured to access data stores in different AWS accounts by using appropriate permissions and configurations. Cross-account access in AWS involves configuring resource-based policies and IAM roles to grant the necessary permissions across accounts.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Cross-account_Granting_Bucket_Permissions_1March2024%20(1).png)
    
    The administrator of Account A assigns a bucket policy that allows cross-account access, enabling Account B to execute certain operations on the bucket. It is important to recognize that the administrator of Account B gains these permissions by default. Subsequently, the administrator of Account B applies a user policy to an account user, passing on the rights obtained from Account A. Following this, the user in Account B tests the permissions by accessing a file in the bucket that is controlled by Account A.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
