    **– Update the AWS Glue crawler’s configuration in the `Tutorial`‘s account to target the S3 bucket containing the VPC flow logs in the `Dojo`‘s account.**
    
    **– Implement a bucket policy on the S3 bucket in the `Dojo`‘s account that explicitly permits the AWS Glue crawler’s IAM role in the `Tutorial`‘s account access to the bucket.**
    
    **– Configure an IAM role in the `Tutorial`’s account with permissions to access the S3 bucket in the `Dojo`’s account and associate this role with the AWS Glue crawler.**
    
    The option that says: **Utilize AWS Resource Access Manager (AWS RAM) in the `Dojo`‘s account to share the S3 bucket with the `Tutorial`‘s account, facilitating cross-account access** is incorrect because the AWS RAM service is primarily used for sharing AWS resources across accounts, but it does not directly facilitate access for AWS Glue crawlers to S3 buckets in another account.
    
    The option that says: **Create a VPC Endpoint for S3 in `Dojo`‘s account to streamline access between the AWS Glue crawler in the `Tutorial`‘s account and the S3 bucket** is incorrect because VPC Endpoints simply facilitate private connections between VPCs and AWS services within the same account and do not directly address cross-account access for AWS Glue crawlers.
    
    The option that says: **Establish a Direct Connect link between the `Tutorials` and `Dojo`‘s accounts to ensure private connectivity for the AWS Glue crawler to access the S3 bucket** is incorrect because, in this scenario, Direct Connect is only used for establishing a dedicated network connection from your on-premises data center to AWS and does not directly facilitate cross-account access for AWS Glue crawlers to S3 buckets.
    

A company that provides a popular music streaming service is experiencing slow query performance when their data team tries to analyze user listening habits. The data team needs to perform complex queries on large tables containing billions of rows of data, which include user demographics, song metadata, and user activity logs. The current infrastructure is struggling to handle the load, causing delays in generating insights.

Which of the following would meet the requirements?

- Use the built-in Aggregate View.
- Set up a Custom scalar user-defined function (UDF) using either a SQL SELECT clause or a Python program.
- **Implement a Materialized View in Amazon Redshift.**
    
    **Materialized views** in Amazon Redshift provide a way to handle complex queries on large datasets. A materialized view contains a precomputed result set, based on an SQL query over one or more base tables. This means that the results of the query are calculated and stored when the view is created, rather than being calculated at query time. This can significantly speed up query performance on large datasets.
    
    Materialized views are especially useful for speeding up queries that are predictable and repeated. Instead of performing resource-intensive queries against large tables (such as aggregates or multiple joins), applications can query a materialized view and retrieve a precomputed result set. For example, consider the scenario where a set of queries is used to populate dashboards, such as Amazon QuickSight. This use case is ideal for a materialized view, because the queries are predictable and repeated over and over again.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/materialized-view-012524.png)
    
    In addition, Amazon Redshift can automatically rewrite these queries to use materialized views, even when the query doesn’t explicitly reference a materialized view. This improves query performance because many computation steps can be skipped and the precomputed results returned directly. Unlike a simple cache, many materialized views can be incrementally refreshed when DML changes are applied on the underlying (base) tables and can be used by other similar queries, not just the query used to create the materialized view.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Implement a Materialized View in Amazon Redshift.**
    
    The option that says: **Use the built-in Aggregate View** is incorrect. Aggregate view is a feature in Amazon DynamoDB, not Amazon Redshift. They represent all the tables in a data model, including table column names, sample data, and all global secondary indexes associated with the table. This does not inherently improve the performance of complex queries on large datasets.
    
    The option that says: **Set up a Custom scalar user-defined function (UDF) using either a SQL SELECT clause or a Python program** is incorrect. While user-defined functions in Amazon Redshift can be used to encapsulate complex operations, they do not inherently improve the performance of querying large datasets. A UDF executes on a single row and then returns a single value. It doesn’t benefit from parallel execution and can’t return a table or a result set.
    
    The option that says: **Integrate Amazon ElastiCache to cache query results** is incorrect. Amazon ElastiCache is an in-memory key-value store that sits between your application and the data store (database) that it accesses. It can be used for caching to improve application and database speed. However, it is not designed to handle complex queries on large datasets like the ones mentioned in the scenario. It is more suited for scenarios where the same data is being read repeatedly.
    
- Integrate Amazon ElastiCache to cache query results.

A retail chain compiles daily transaction records from multiple store locations in a Comma-Separated Values (CSV) format and uploads them to Amazon S3. A data engineer uses Amazon Athena to generate an analysis of these records.

Only a subset of columns is typically queried, yet Athena scans the entire table each time. The data engineer must create a solution to reduce the volume of data involved in a query.

Which approach would meet the requirements?

- **Organize the S3 files into a `/year/month/day` structure. Then, create a partitioning schema via the `PARTITIONED BY` clause in the `CREATE EXTERNAL TABLE` command.**
    
    **Amazon Athena** is a user-friendly service that uses standard SQL queries to analyze data stored in Amazon S3. With Athena, you can analyze data in S3 without the need to load it into a separate database.
    
    Partitioning data in Amazon S3 and utilizing Amazon Athena for querying can significantly reduce the amount of data scanned, lowering costs. Partitioning enables Athena to scan only the relevant partitions of data for a query rather than the entire dataset. This method is highly recommended for large datasets stored in S3, where queries often target a subset of the data based on specific columns like date, region, or department.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA2-42-AMAZON%20ATHENA-SCREENSHOT-030124.jpg)
    
    By organizing the CSV files in an S3 bucket with a `/year/month/day` structure, you can create partitions based on these fields. When creating an external table in Athena, The `CREATE EXTERNAL TABLE` statement defines the table structure, columns, and data types. The `EXTERNAL`keyword specifies that the table is external to Athena and points to the data location in S3.
    
    The `PARTITIONED BY` clause can be used to define a partitioning schema. This divides the table horizontally based on partition columns like date, product, etc.
    
    When queries use partition columns in the filter criteria, Athena can prune partitions to skip irrelevant data locations and reduce the amount of data scanned.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Organize the S3 files into a `/year/month/day` structure. Then, create a partitioning schema via the `PARTITIONED BY` clause in the `CREATE EXTERNAL TABLE` command.**
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue Schema Registry to strip any unused columns** is incorrect. AWS Glue Schema Registry is primarily used for managing and controlling data schemas. It does not have the capability to remove or strip columns from the actual data stored in Amazon S3 or other data sources.
    
    The option that says: **Execute the query in an Athena workgroup and apply a specified per-query control limit** is incorrect. Per-query control limits just set a boundary on the total amount of data that can be scanned. It won’t reduce the amount of data processed by Athena. If the limit is set too low, it may result in incomplete or incorrect query results, as the query may be terminated prematurely before scanning the necessary data.
    
    The option that says: **Divide the dataset into multiple compressed Gzip files, each approximately 1 GB in size, before storage** is incorrect. While compression can reduce storage costs, Athena still needs to scan the entire compressed dataset.
    
- Use AWS Glue Schema Registry to strip any unused columns.
- Divide the dataset into multiple compressed Gzip files, each approximately 1 GB in size, before storage.
- Execute the query in an Athena workgroup and apply a specified per-query control limit.

A data analyst is working with a large dataset stored in Amazon Redshift. The analyst needs to perform a complex data processing task that requires a custom function. The function will be used as part of a SQL query. The analyst wants to accomplish this with the least amount of effort and overhead.

Which of the following options should the analyst choose?

- Create a custom function using AWS Glue, register it in the Glue Data Catalog, and then access it from Redshift.
- Use Amazon EMR to process the data and then import the results into Redshift.
- Integrate Amazon Athena with Redshift using a custom UDF via the Athena Query Federation SDK.
- **Create a User-Defined Function (UDF) directly in Redshift.**
    
    Amazon Redshift, a fully managed, petabyte-scale data warehouse service, allows you to create custom scalar UDFs using either a SQL SELECT clause or a Python program. Once created, the UDF is stored in the database and is available for any user with sufficient privileges to run.
    
    Running a custom scalar UDF in Amazon Redshift is as straightforward as running existing Amazon Redshift functions. This means you don’t need to learn new ways of invoking functions, saving you time and effort.
    
    ![Redshift User-Define Functions](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/redshift-udf1-011024.png)
    
    For Python UDFs, you can even import your own custom Python modules. This allows you to leverage pre-existing code and libraries, further enhancing the power and flexibility of your UDFs.
    
    ![Redshift User-Defined Functions](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/redshift-udf2-011024.png)
    
    Moreover, UDFs in Redshift provide more control and flexibility over data processing workflows and potentially improve performance by reducing the amount of data transferred between the database and client application. This is because the computations are done within the database itself, rather than transferring data to an external location for processing.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create a User-Defined Function (UDF) directly in Redshift.**
    
    The option that says: **Integrate Amazon Athena with Redshift using a custom UDF via the Athena Query Federation SDK** is incorrect. While it is possible to create custom UDFs in Amazon Athena using the Athena Query Federation SDK, integrating these UDFs with Amazon Redshift would require additional effort and overhead. You would need to write the UDF in Java, package it into a JAR file, deploy it using AWS Lambda, and then manage the integration between Amazon Athena and Amazon Redshift. This process primarily involves multiple steps and services, making it more complex and time-consuming compared to creating a UDF directly in Amazon Redshift.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon EMR to process the data and then import the results into Redshift** is incorrect. Amazon EMR is just a cloud-native big data platform that allows you to process large amounts of data quickly and cost-effectively. However, using Amazon EMR to process the data and then importing the results into Amazon Redshift would require additional effort and overhead. You would need to set up an EMR cluster, write the processing code, run the job, and then import the results into Amazon Redshift.
    
    The option that says: **Create a custom function using AWS Glue, register it in the Glue Data Catalog, and then access it from Redshift** is incorrect. While AWS Glue allows you to create custom functions and register them in the AWS Glue Data Catalog, accessing these functions from Amazon Redshift would require additional steps and overhead. You would need to write the function code, register it in the AWS Glue Data Catalog using the `aws glue create-user-defined-function` command in the AWS CLI, and then configure Amazon Redshift to access the function.
    

An organization uses Amazon Redshift to store daily transaction data. To ensure compliance, the organization needs to track and log the daily load status of Redshift tables to determine if data has been successfully loaded. A data engineer is tasked with updating an Amazon DynamoDB table with these load statuses for auditing purposes.

Which of the following solutions should the data engineer do to meet these requirements?

- Enable AWS CloudTrail management events for Redshift and use these events to trigger an AWS Lambda function that writes the table load statuses to the DynamoDB table.
- **Configure AWS CloudTrail to capture Redshift API calls. Create an Amazon EventBridge rule to match Redshift events from CloudTrail Events and invoke an AWS Lambda function that updates the DynamoDB table with the load statuses.**
    
    **Amazon CloudTrail** is a service that helps you enable governance, compliance, operational auditing, and risk auditing of your AWS account. It provides a comprehensive log of actions taken within your AWS environment, including API calls made through the AWS Management Console, AWS SDKs, Command-line tools, and other AWS services.
    
    An **Amazon EventBridge rule** is a set of instructions that define how to process and route events in your AWS environment. Rules match incoming events and send them to targets for processing. You can create rules that respond to events recorded by CloudTrail, allowing you to automate responses to specific API calls or changes in your AWS resources.
    
    An **AWS Lambda function** is a of code that runs in response to events and automatically manages the underlying compute resources. It allows you to run code without provisioning or managing servers. Various AWS services, like EventBridge, can trigger Lambda functions, enabling you to build serverless applications and automate processes in response to events in your AWS environment.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-cloudtrail-eventbridge-lambda-04-10-2024.png)
    
    To monitor specific table activities or load statuses within Amazon Redshift, **EventBridge** is effective for capturing API call events from CloudTrail and triggering downstream actions. **CloudTrail** records Redshift API calls, which can then be leveraged by **EventBridge** rules to match and trigger a Lambda function. This Lambda function can subsequently update DynamoDB with real-time load statuses.
    
    CloudTrail’s integration with EventBridge provides the ability to capture Redshift-specific events, allowing the data engineer to build an automated pipeline for updating the DynamoDB table with relevant load statuses. The use of EventBridge ensures that only Redshift-related CloudTrail events trigger the Lambda function, making this setup both efficient and targeted.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Configure AWS CloudTrail to capture Redshift API calls. Create an Amazon EventBridge rule to match Redshift events from CloudTrail Events and invoke an AWS Lambda function that updates the DynamoDB table with the load statuses.**
    
    The option that says: **Enable AWS CloudTrail management events for Redshift and use these events to trigger an AWS Lambda function that writes the table load statuses to the DynamoDB table** is incorrect because while CloudTrail typically logs Redshift API calls, without EventBridge, it lacks a filtering mechanism that can match specific events to trigger Lambda functions effectively.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Redshift Event Notifications to monitor the table activities** is incorrect because Redshift Event Notifications are primarily designed for cluster-level alerts and events, such as failures or performance issues, making them insufficient for the compliance tracking of specific table load statuses.
    
    The option that says: **Set up Amazon CloudWatch to monitor the Redshift cluster and configure an alarm that triggers an AWS Lambda function to write the statuses of table loads into the DynamoDB table** is incorrect because CloudWatch monitors Redshift cluster metrics rather than specific API activities. CloudWatch alarms would not directly track specific table load statuses in the context required.
    
- Set up Amazon CloudWatch to monitor the Redshift cluster and configure an alarm that triggers an AWS Lambda function to write the statuses of table loads into the DynamoDB table.
- Use Amazon Redshift Event Notifications to monitor the table activities.

A data engineering team in a large organization is looking for a cost-effective solution to handle their non-urgent data integration workloads. These workloads include tasks such as testing and one-time data loads, which do not require fast job startup times or consistent runtimes. They are considering a solution that runs on spare compute capacity instead of dedicated hardware, which can lead to variable start and run times.

Which of the following would be the most suitable for this scenario?

- AWS Glue Development Endpoint
- AWS Glue Standard
- AWS Glue Streaming ETL
- **AWS Glue Flex**
    
    **AWS Glue Flex** is designed for non-urgent data integration workloads that do not require fast job startup times or consistent runtimes. It runs on spare compute capacity instead of dedicated hardware, which can lead to variable start and runtimes but is more cost-effective.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/flex-execution-aws-glue-010924.png)
    
    With AWS Glue Flex, jobs run on spare compute capacity in AWS instead of dedicated hardware. This means that the start and runtimes of jobs using Flex can vary because spare compute resources aren’t always readily available and can be reclaimed during the run of a job. However, this approach is more cost-effective for non-urgent workloads.
    
    Regardless of the execution option used, AWS Glue jobs have the same capabilities, including access to custom connectors, visual authoring interface, job scheduling, and Glue Auto Scaling. The Flex execution class is available for AWS Glue 3.0 Spark jobs.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **AWS Glue Flex.**
    
    **AWS Glue Standard** is incorrect because it is designed for time-sensitive tasks that require fast job startup times and consistent runtimes, achieved through the allocation of dedicated resources, making it more expensive and less flexible.
    
    **AWS Glue Development Endpoint** is incorrect because it is specifically built for active development and testing of ETL scripts, not for running production workloads.
    
    **AWS Glue Streaming ETL** is incorrect because this service is primarily used for streaming ETL jobs, which require real-time processing. It’s not designed for non-urgent workloads that can tolerate variable start and runtimes.
    

A company uses a Lambda function to retrieve objects from an S3 bucket in the same AWS account. The current IAM policy for the Lambda execution role is as follows:`{"Version": "2012-10-17","Statement": [{"Effect": "Allow","Action": ["s3:Get*","s3:Put*"],"Resource": "*"}]}`

This policy is overly permissive and does not adhere to the principle of least privilege.

The company needs to modify the IAM policy to ensure that the Lambda function has only the necessary permissions to interact with the S3 bucket.

Which of the following modifications would be the most appropriate?

- Replace the Action field with `"Action": ["s3:PutObject"]` and the Resource field with `"Resource": ["arn:aws:s3:::bucket-name/*"]`.
- Add the `Action": ["s3:GetObjectVersion"]` to the Action field. Replace the Resource field with `"Resource": ["arn:aws:s3:::bucket-name/*"]`.
- Add `"Action": ["s3:ListBucket"]`to the Action field. Replace the Resource field with `"Resource": ["arn:aws:s3:::bucket-name"]`.
- **Replace the Action field with `"Action": ["s3:GetObject"]` and the Resource field with `"Resource": ["arn:aws:s3:::bucket-name/*"]`.**
    
    AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) is a web service that helps you securely control access to AWS resources. When a principal makes a request in AWS, the AWS enforcement code checks whether the principal is authenticated (signed in) and authorized (has permissions). You manage access in AWS by creating policies and attaching them to IAM identities or AWS resources. Policies are JSON documents in AWS that, when attached to an identity or resource, define their permissions.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/s3-getobject-012224.png)
    
    The `s3:GetObject` action grants permission to retrieve objects from an S3 bucket. This action adheres to the principle of least privilege, which is a security practice that restricts access rights for users to the bare minimum permissions they need to perform their work.
    
    When you specify the `s3:GetObject` action in the IAM policy, the Lambda function is allowed to retrieve objects from the specified S3 bucket. The `"Resource": ["arn:aws:s3:::bucket-name/*"]` part of the policy specifies the bucket and objects the policy applies to.
    
    This policy is appropriate for a Lambda function that needs to retrieve objects from an S3 bucket, as it grants only the necessary permissions and no more. This is in line with the principle of least privilege.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Replace the Action field with `"Action": ["s3:GetObject"]`and the Resource field with `"Resource": ["arn:aws:s3:::bucket-name/*"]`.**
    
    The option that says: **Replace the Action field with `"Action": ["s3:PutObject"]` and the Resource field with `"Resource": ["arn:aws:s3:::bucket-name/*"]`** is incorrect because the `s3:PutObject` action allows only for writing or uploading objects to the S3 bucket, which is unnecessary for the Lambda function’s purpose of retrieving objects from the S3 bucket.
    
    The option that says: **Add `"Action": ["s3:ListBucket"]` to the Action field. Replace the Resource field with `"Resource": ["arn:aws:s3:::bucket-name"]`** is incorrect. While this is a step towards least privilege, it does not specifically allow retrieving objects with `s3:GetObject`, which is required for the Lambda function to perform its task. Moreover, `s3:ListBucket` is applied at the bucket level, not the object level, and does not alone fulfill the requirement to retrieve objects.
    
    The option that says: **Add the `Action": ["s3:GetObjectVersion"]` to the Action field. Replace the Resource field with `"Resource": ["arn:aws:s3:::bucket-name/*"]`** is incorrect. This policy would simply allow the function to retrieve different versions of an object in a versioned bucket. While this can be necessary for some use cases, it is not mentioned as a requirement in the scenario. Furthermore, the modification does not restrict actions to only those needed and includes additional permissions not specified as necessary.
    

A company is moving its AWS Glue DataBrew project to a new AWS account managed by a new data engineering team. This requires stopping DataBrew activities in the existing account and removing certain configurations that enabled Glue DataBrew to connect to the company’s Amazon RDS databases, EC2 instances, and other VPC resources.

The new data engineering team is responsible for cutting off the previous DataBrew connection. Moreover, the team must thoroughly understand the appropriate procedures to ensure a seamless and secure transition.

Which of the following should the data engineering team implement to satisfy the given requirement?

- **Detach the security group from all associated VPC resources and delete any security group rules within the VPC that reference the security group.**
    
    **Security groups** are crucial to Amazon Web Services (AWS) network security, especially for resources deployed in a Virtual Private Cloud (VPC). It acts as a virtual firewall for instances, allowing control of inbound and outbound traffic at the instance level. Unlike network access control lists (ACLs), which operate at the subnet level, security groups are directly associated with individual resources such as Amazon EC2 instances and AWS Glue DataBrew services that work within a VPC.
    
    A security group’s rules are responsible for the traffic allowed to and from resources associated with it. When a new instance or service is launched within a VPC, it can be related to one or more security groups, which then govern traffic flow to and from the resource. Managing these security groups effectively is crucial for maintaining the security and integrity of the resources within a VPC.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws_glue_data_catalog_rds_connection_17MAR2024.png)
    
    For the scenario described, the task is removing a security group associated explicitly with AWS Glue DataBrew operations as part of a migration to a new AWS account. This process involves a few critical steps to ensure the removal does not disrupt existing operations or compromise security. First, detaching the security group from any resources within the VPC is necessary. This step ensures that no active resources are left unprotected or inadvertently exposed due to the security group’s removal. Following this, it’s also essential to delete any rules within the security group that reference other security groups or resources within the VPC, ensuring a clean and orderly removal process.
    
    By integrating AWS Glue DataBrew with a Virtual Private Cloud (VPC), you can securely access and process data from VPC’s protected environment. This requires configuring security groups and VPC endpoints to control traffic flow and facilitate private connections. Proper network configuration is essential for maintaining secure and efficient workflows using DataBrew’s visual interface for data cleaning and normalization tasks.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Detach the security group from all associated VPC resources and delete any security group rules within the VPC that reference the security group.**
    
    The option that says: **Reconfigure the VPC to utilize a new security group for DataBrew connectivity before deleting the old one** is incorrect because reconfiguring to a new security group does not fulfill the requirement to properly detach and manage dependencies of the existing security group before its deletion.
    
    The option that says: **Disable deletion protection on the security group in AWS Security Hub to allow its removal** is incorrect. Deletion protection is a feature that applies only to AWS resources, such as EC2 instances and RDS databases, to prevent accidental deletion. Security groups, in general, don’t have deletion protection and can be deleted once they are no longer associated with any resources.
    
    The option that says: **Delete the Elastic Network Interface (ENI) associated with AWS Glue DataBrew before removing the security group** is incorrect. Deleting an ENI is not enough to remove the associated security group. Additionally, deleting an ENI could disrupt network connectivity for services that rely on it, resulting in unintended consequences beyond the scope of securely managing security groups.
    
- Delete the Elastic Network Interface (ENI) associated with AWS Glue DataBrew before removing the security group.
- Disable deletion protection on the security group in AWS Security Hub to allow its removal.
- Reconfigure the VPC to utilize a new security group for DataBrew connectivity before deleting the old one.

A company operates two Amazon Redshift clusters. The company’s Analytics Team uses the first cluster to derive critical business insights, while the Reporting Team uses the second cluster for report generation.

The Reporting team is tasked with publishing near real-time reports on an Amazon QuickSight dashboard. To achieve this, they require joining data from their cluster with specific datasets in the Analytics Team’s cluster.

Which solution will satisfy these criteria with the LEAST impact on the Analytic’s team cluster?

- Configure an AWS Database Migration Service replication with the Analytics team's cluster as the source and the Reporting team's as the target.
- In the Analytics team's cluster, create a materialized view of the required tables and give the Reporting team access.
- **Configure Amazon Redshift Data Sharing with the Reporting team's cluster as the consumer.**
    
    Amazon Redshift is a fully managed, petabyte-scale data warehouse service in the cloud. The Amazon Redshift service manages all of the work of setting up, operating, and scaling a data warehouse. These tasks include provisioning capacity, monitoring and backing up the cluster and applying patches and upgrades to the Amazon Redshift engine.
    
    Amazon Redshift can also securely share read access to live data across Amazon Redshift clusters, workgroups, AWS accounts, and AWS Regions without manually moving or copying the data with **Amazon Redshift data sharing**. Since the data is live, all users can see the most up-to-date and consistent information in Amazon Redshift as soon as it’s updated.
    
    **Amazon Redshift data sharing Use cases:**
    
    – Supporting different kinds of business-critical workloads
    
    – Enable seamless collaboration across teams and business groups for broader analytics, data science, and cross-product impact analysis.
    
    – Delivering data as a service
    
    – Sharing data between environments
    
    – Licensing access to data in Amazon Redshift
    
    ![https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Redshuft Data Sharing .png](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Redshuft%20Data%20Sharing%20.png)
    
    Amazon Redshift data sharing allows you to securely and easily share data for read purposes across different Amazon Redshift clusters without the complexity and delays associated with data copies and data movement. Data can be shared at many levels, including schemas, tables, views, and user-defined functions, providing fine-grained access controls that can be tailored for different users and businesses that all need access to the data.
    
    With this feature, you can seamlessly share live data between clusters. This option suits the requirement in the scenario, as the shared data will be made available to the consumer as quickly as possible. It will ensure transactional consistency and provide up-to-date and consistent data views to the Reporting team.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Configure Amazon Redshift Data Sharing with the Reporting team’s cluster as the consumer.**
    
    The option that says: **Configure an AWS Database Migration Service replication with the Analytics team’s cluster as the source and the Reporting team’s as the target** is incorrect. While this method can replicate data from one Redshift cluster to another, this approach involves moving data continuously, which can add unnecessary load and complexity to the Analytics team’s cluster. This could potentially impact the performance of the analytics operations, especially if the data volumes are large.
    
    The option that says: **In the Analytics team’s cluster, create a materialized view of the required tables and give the Reporting team access** is incorrect. Creating a materialized view in the Analytics team’s cluster could potentially impact the cluster’s performance since it requires additional storage and computational resources to maintain the view, especially if the data changes frequently.
    
    The option that says: **In the Reporting team’s cluster, create an Amazon Redshift Spectrum table using the unloaded copy of data from the Analytics team’s cluster to an Amazon S3 bucket** is incorrect. Even though this will allow the Reporting team to query the same data from the Analytics cluster, it still will not satisfy the requirement, as unloading data to an S3 bucket could lead to delays in data availability for real-time reporting. Moreover, the unload operation imposes an additional workload on the Analytics team’s cluster.
    
- In the Reporting team’s cluster, create an Amazon Redshift Spectrum table using the unloaded copy of data from the Analytics team's cluster to an Amazon S3 bucket.

An e-commerce company is planning to launch a new product line and wants to analyze customer reviews of similar products from their competitors. They have collected a large amount of review data. The company wants a solution that can help its data analysts, who have varying levels of technical skills, to clean, normalize, and analyze this data without worrying about infrastructure management.

Which AWS service would be the most suitable for this task?

- Use Amazon QuickSight for data analysis and visualization.
- **Use AWS Glue DataBrew for data cleaning and normalization.**
    
    AWS Glue DataBrew is a visual data preparation tool that makes it easy for data analysts and data scientists to clean and normalize data to prepare it for analytics and machine learning. It offers a wide selection of pre-built transformations to automate data preparation tasks, all without the need to write any code. You can automate filtering anomalies, converting data to standard formats, and correcting invalid values, and other tasks. After your data is ready, you can immediately use it for analytics and machine learning projects.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/aws-glue-databrew-sample-1.jpg)
    
    Since AWS Glue DataBrew is serverless, you do not need to provision or manage any infrastructure to use it. With AWS Glue DataBrew, you can easily and quickly create and execute data preparation recipes through a visual interface. The service automatically scales up or down based on the size and complexity of your data, so you only pay for the resources you use.
    
    Therefore, the correct answer is: **Use AWS Glue DataBrew for data cleaning and normalization.**
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue ETL jobs for data transformation** is incorrect because AWS Glue ETL jobs are used for extracting, transforming, and loading data. While they can be used for data transformation, they do not provide the visual interface for data cleaning and normalization that DataBrew does.
    
    The option says:  **Use AWS Step Functions to orchestrate data processing tasks** is incorrect. While possible, this solution brings unnecessary complexity. AWS Step Functions is commonly used for coordinating multiple AWS services.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon QuickSight to visualize and clean the data** is incorrect. Amazon QuickSight is primarily a business intelligence (BI) tool. While it does have some data preparation capabilities, it is not designed specifically for data cleaning and lacks the wide range of pre-built transformations, such as data normalization, deduplication, and data type conversion, that AWS Glue DataBrew offers.
    
- Use AWS Glue ETL jobs for data transformation.
- Use AWS Step Functions to orchestrate data processing tasks.

A multinational corporation streams real-time financial transaction data to a Kinesis data stream for third-party financial auditors to analyze. The schema and format of the data received by each auditor may vary due to their individual auditing standards and requirements. The corporation wants to efficiently manage and control schema changes across its entire data stream.

Which option would be most effective in fulfilling these requirements?

- Apply Kinesis Producer Library (KPL) to verify entries against an Amazon DynamoDB table's schema description.
- Use AWS Glue ETL jobs to transform the data stream according to the schema changes.
- Store the schema definition in Amazon S3 and use AWS Lambda to validate records against it.
- **Set up both producers and auditors to validate records against a schema definition stored in AWS Glue Schema Registry.**
    
    AWS Glue Schema Registry is a central repository for storing and managing schema definitions for your data. It helps ensure that all records in your data stream conform to a defined format and structure.
    
    To use the schema registry, you first define the schema for your data. Once the schema is defined, you can configure your producers and consumers to validate records against the schema definition using AWS Glue Schema Registry APIs or through the Kinesis Producer Library (KPL) and Kinesis Client Library (KCL) libraries. This means that any time a new record is added to the data stream, it will be checked against the schema to ensure it conforms to the defined format and structure. If the record does not meet the schema requirements, it can be rejected or transformed to conform to the schema before it is added to the data stream.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/kds-glue-schema-registry-sample.jpg)
    
    By using AWS Glue Schema Registry, you can streamline the process of managing and controlling schema changes for your data. This can help you avoid the need for defensive coding and coordination between teams, improve the quality of your data, reduce the risk of downstream application failures, and take advantage of integration with multiple AWS services. Additionally, the schema registry allows you to version each schema, giving you the freedom to evolve your schemas while maintaining reliability.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Set up both producers and auditors to validate records against a schema definition stored in AWS Glue Schema Registry.**
    
    The option that says: **Apply Kinesis Producer Library (KPL) to verify entries against an Amazon DynamoDB table’s schema description** is incorrect. Kinesis Producer Library (KPL) is used to efficiently send data to a Kinesis data stream. While KPL does aggregate and compress data before sending it to Kinesis Data Streams, it does not inherently validate records against a schema definition. Moreover, storing the schema definition in a DynamoDB table would not provide the centralized schema management and versioning capabilities that AWS Glue Schema Registry offers.
    
    The option that says: **Store the schema definition in Amazon S3 and use AWS Lambda to validate records against it** is incorrect because although this approach is technically feasible, it would not be efficient. It would require triggering a Lambda function for every record in the data stream, which could be costly and may not scale well with high-volume data streams. Furthermore, this approach does not provide a built-in way to manage and control schema changes.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue ETL jobs to transform the data stream according to the schema changes** is incorrect because AWS Glue ETL jobs are used to prepare and load data for analytics, not for real-time schema validation or management in a data stream. While Glue ETL jobs can transform data based on a defined schema, they do not provide real-time schema validation for producers and consumers of a Kinesis data stream.
    

A data engineer uses Amazon Athena to run SQL queries against data stored in Amazon S3. They need a solution to enforce settings across all queries to ensure they use the same query results location in Amazon S3.

Which approach can the team use to enforce these settings?

- Use Service Control Policies (SCPs)
- **Use Amazon Athena Workgroups**
    
    **Amazon Athena Workgroups** allows you to separate your query execution across different teams, workloads, and applications or even run them in isolation from other queries. Workgroups provide a way to track query-related metrics, set limits to manage costs and enforce settings across all queries within the workgroup. Each workgroup has its own query history, metrics, data usage, and performance statistics, allowing for better resource allocation and cost management.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/athena-create_workgroup-021324.gif)
    
    Workgroups also help you simplify the onboarding of new users to Athena. By selecting **Override client-side settings**, you enforce a predefined configuration on all queries within a workgroup. Users no longer have to configure a query results output location or S3 encryption keys. These settings default to the parameters defined for the workgroup where those queries execute. Additionally, each workgroup maintains a unique query history and saved query inventory, making queries easier for you to track down.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use Amazon Athena Workgroups.**
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon S3 Bucket Policies** is incorrect because this primarily focuses on managing permissions on the S3 bucket itself. It does not provide a way to enforce settings across all Athena queries in a specific environment.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS IAM Roles** is incorrect because this only applies in managing access to AWS resources, not Athen queries.
    
    The option that says: **Use Service Control Policies (SCPs)** is incorrect because SCPs are only designed to manage permissions in AWS Organizations and do not have the capability to enforce settings like query results location in Amazon S3.
    
- Use AWS IAM Roles
- Use Amazon S3 Bucket Policies

A data engineer is working on a project that involves processing large datasets using Python. The project requires a service that can handle distributed processing over multiple nodes with the least amount of operational overhead. The service should also be capable of handling both batch and real-time data processing tasks.

Which of the following AWS services would be the most suitable for this task?

- **AWS Glue for Ray**
    
    **AWS Glue for Ray** is a job type in AWS Glue, a serverless data integration service. It’s designed to scale AI and Python workloads. AWS Glue for Ray allows you to scale your Python workloads in a distributed cluster in a multi-node environment. This is particularly useful when you have large datasets that can’t fit on a single instance.
    
    Ray is an open-source unified compute framework that originated as a project at RISELab in UC Berkeley. It’s designed to scale AI and Python workloads. If your application is written in Python, you can scale it with Ray in a distributed cluster. Ray is Python native and can be combined with the AWS SDK for pandas to prepare, integrate, and transform your data for running your data analytics and ML workloads.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/aws-glue-ray-011524.jpg)
    
    You can use AWS Glue for Ray with Glue Studio Notebooks, SageMaker Studio Notebook, or a local notebook or IDE of your choice. This makes it a flexible and powerful tool for scaling Python workloads.
    
    **AWS Glue for Ray consists of two major components:**
    
    1. Ray Core – The distributed computing framework.
    2. Ray Dataset – The distributed data framework based on Apache Arrow.
    
    When running a Ray job, AWS Glue provisions the Ray cluster for you and runs these distributed Python jobs on a serverless auto-scaling infrastructure. This means you don’t have to worry about managing the underlying infrastructure, and you can focus on writing and running your Python workloads.
    
    Developers like Python for its ease of use and rich collection of built-in data processing libraries. They want to use familiar Python primitive type for processing large datasets. AWS Glue for Ray helps data engineers process large datasets using Python and popular Python libraries. AWS Glue for Ray uses Ray.io, an open-source unified compute framework that helps scale Python workloads from a single node to hundreds of nodes. AWS Glue for Ray is serverless, so there is no infrastructure to manage.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is **AWS Glue for Ray.**
    
    **AWS Lambda** is incorrect. It ****is a serverless computing service that lets you run your code without provisioning or managing servers. While it’s great for running small, independent tasks in response to cloud events (like changes to data in an S3 bucket or updates to a DynamoDB table), it may not be the best choice for processing large datasets using Python. AWS Lambda has a maximum execution time of 15 minutes per request, which might not be sufficient for processing large datasets.
    
    **Amazon EMR (Elastic MapReduce)** is incorrect. EMR is a cloud-based big data platform for processing large datasets using popular frameworks such as Apache Spark and Hadoop. While it’s powerful and flexible, it requires a fair amount of setup and management, which might not be ideal for a Python-based data processing task.
    
    **AWS Glue DataBrew** is incorrect because AWS Glue DataBrew is a visual data preparation tool that makes it easy to clean and normalize data for analytics and machine learning. It’s a great tool for data cleaning and exploration, but it’s not specifically designed for processing large datasets using Python.
    
- Amazon EMR (Elastic MapReduce)
- AWS Glue DataBrew
- AWS Lambda

A data engineer wants to analyze terabytes of data stored in an Amazon Redshift cluster. The data engineer must be able to run queries directly on the cluster without the need for complex ETL jobs or setting up additional data infrastructure.

Which feature of Amazon Athena will allow this direct access to the data source?

- Amazon Athena Capacity Reservations
- **Amazon Athena Federated Query**
    
    **Amazon Athena Federated Query** allows you to run SQL queries across data stored in relational, non-relational, object, and custom data sources. Athena uses data source connectors that run on AWS Lambda to run federated queries. A data source connector is a piece of code that can translate between your target data source and Athena.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/athena-federated-query-032824.png)
    
    Amazon CloudWatch Logs, Amazon DynamoDB, Amazon DocumentDB, and Amazon RDS, and JDBC-compliant relational data sources such MySQL, and PostgreSQL under the Apache 2.0 license. You can also use the Athena Query Federation SDK to write custom connectors.
    
    You can use the Amazon Athena Console or the AWS Serverless Application Repository to choose, configure, and deploy a data source connector to your account. After you deploy data source connectors, they are associated with a catalog you can specify in SQL queries. You can combine SQL statements from multiple catalogs and span multiple data sources with a single query.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Amazon Athena Federated Query.**
    
    The option that says: **Amazon Athena Query editor** is incorrect. This is simply the web interface provided by Athena for running queries. While it’s essential for interacting with Athena, the query editor itself doesn’t provide the functionality to query external data sources like Redshift without ETL processes.
    
    The option that says: **Amazon Capacity Reservations** is incorrect. The main purpose of provisioned capacity is to give a predictable query performance, not to extend Athena’s data access capabilities across different storage systems.
    
    The option that says: **Amazon Athena Workgroups** is incorrect because workgroups in Amazon Athena are primarily used to separate query execution between teams, workloads, applications, or users. They help manage query-related resources but do not enable querying across different data sources.
    
- Amazon Athena Workgroups
- Amazon Athena Query editor

A company has a complex ETL pipeline that involves multiple AWS Glue jobs. Each job performs tasks such as data extraction, cleaning, and loading data into a Redshift data warehouse for analytics. The company wants to orchestrate these jobs into a single workflow with minimal operational overhead.

Which AWS resource would be the most suitable for this use case?

- AWS Step Functions state machine
- AWS Batch
- **AWS Glue workflows**
    
    **AWS Glue workflows** provide a visual interface for orchestrating complex extract, transform, and load (ETL) activities involving multiple crawlers, jobs, and triggers. Each workflow manages the execution and monitoring of all its jobs and crawlers. As a workflow runs each component, it records execution progress and status. This provides you with an overview of the larger task and the details of each step.
    
    You can create a workflow from an AWS Glue blueprint or manually build a workflow a component at a time using the AWS Management Console or the AWS Glue API. Triggers within workflows can start both jobs and crawlers and can be fired when jobs or crawlers are complete. By using triggers, you can create large chains of interdependent jobs and crawlers.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/glue-workflow-021924.png)
    
    In addition to triggers within a workflow that defines job and crawler dependencies, each workflow has a start trigger. There are three types of start triggers: Schedule, On-demand, and EventBridge event. With this trigger type, AWS Glue can be an event consumer in an event-driven architecture.
    
    This would require the least operational overhead as it is fully managed and designed for this exact use case.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **AWS Glue workflows.**
    
    The option that says: **AWS Step Functions state machine** is incorrect because this option takes more work. When a Glue job is initiated from a Step Function state machine, there isn’t an immediate response indicating job completion. Instead, you’d have to implement custom logic within the state machine to continuously poll the status of a job. This is not the case with AWS Glue Workflow, where you can simply use the built-in conditions to manage running jobs in sequence or parallel.
    
    The option that says: **AWS Batch** is incorrect because it is intended for running batch computing jobs. It is not designed for orchestrating ETL jobs or managing data workflows.
    
    The option that says: **Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (Amazon MWAA)** is incorrect because, while Amazon MWAA provides a managed environment for running Apache Airflow workflows, it is not primarily designed for orchestrating Glue jobs. It is more general-purpose and may require more manual configuration and maintenance for ETL workflows compared to AWS Glue.
    
- Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (Amazon MWAA)

A development team is currently managing numerous AWS Glue jobs through the AWS Glue Console. They aim to host the jobs’ source code in a repository to efficiently track code changes. Additionally, they require changes made in the repository to be automatically reflected in the Glue jobs.

Which of the following will meet the requirements with the LEAST amount of development overhead?

- Use GitHub for version control and AWS CodePipeline for continuous integration and delivery.
- Use GitLab for version control and AWS CodeBuild for continuous integration and delivery.
- **Use GitHub for version control and use the AWS Glue Studio’s version control feature for syncing changes.**
    
    AWS Glue is a serverless, scalable data integration service that simplifies the discovery, preparation, and combination of data from various sources for analytics, machine learning, and application development. It allows developers to version control their ETL scripts and workflows with its integration with Git, an open-source version control system.
    
    This integration works for all AWS Glue job types, both visual and code-based, and offers built-in integration with GitHub. It also facilitates the use of automation tools like Jenkins and AWS CodeDeploy to deploy AWS Glue jobs. AWS CodePipeline, on the other hand, is a fully managed continuous delivery service that helps automate the release process, enabling rapid release of new features.
    
    This combination meets all the requirements stated in the scenario, providing version control for ETL scripts and workflows, automating the detection of new code commits, running automated tests, and deploying successful builds to the AWS account.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/aws-glue-git-011624.png)
    
    If you have remote repositories and want to manage your AWS Glue jobs using your repositories, you can use AWS Glue Studio or the AWS CLI to sync changes to your repositories and your jobs in AWS Glue. When you sync changes this way, you’re pushing the job from AWS Glue Studio to your repository or pulling from the repository to AWS Glue Studio.
    
    With Git integration in AWS Glue Studio, you can:
    
    – Integrate with Git version control systems, such as GitHub, GitLab, and Bitbucket
    
    – Edit AWS Glue jobs in AWS Glue Studio, whether you use visual jobs or script jobs, and sync them to a repository
    
    – Parameterize sources and targets in jobs
    
    – Pull jobs from a repository and edit them in AWS Glue Studio
    
    – Test jobs by pulling from branches and/or pushing to branches utilizing multi-branch workflows in AWS Glue Studio
    
    – Download files from a repository and upload jobs into AWS Glue Studio for cross-account job creation
    
    – Use your automation tool of choice (for example, Jenkins, AWS CodeDeploy, etc.)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use GitHub for version control and use the AWS Glue Studio’s version control feature for syncing changes.** Existing integration between Glue Studio and Git eliminates the need to manage multiple services or learn new tooling. This significantly reduces development time and effort.
    
    The option that says: **Use GitHub for version control and AWS CodePipeline for continuous integration and delivery** is incorrect. Although this option addresses the version control requirement with GitHub, it introduces additional layers of complexity. You’d typically need to configure AWS CodePipeline, adding more steps and potential points of failure. It’s more overhead compared to directly using AWS Glue Studio’s built-in feature.
    
    The option that says: **Use GitLab for version control and AWS CodeBuild for continuous integration and delivery** is incorrect. While this solution is possible, it would just require additional configuration to ensure changes made in the repository are automatically reflected in the AWS Glue jobs. This additional configuration might involve scripting or integration efforts to deploy changes to AWS Glue, leading to increased development overhead.
    
    The option that says: **Use Bitbucket and AWS CodePipeline for continuous integration and delivery** is incorrect. Similar to the other incorrect option, while this is valid, it may require additional configuration and overhead to deploy the changes made in the repository to AWS Glue.
    
- Use Bitbucket and AWS CodePipeline for continuous integration and delivery.

An e-commerce company has a vast amount of data stored in Amazon S3 buckets. The data is in various formats and is updated frequently. The company needs to orchestrate an ETL pipeline to transform and load this data into Amazon Redshift for further analysis. The solution should be able to handle changes in the data schema and should be easily manageable.

Which AWS service is best suited for these requirements?

- **AWS Glue Workflows**
    
    Extract, transform, and load (ETL) orchestration is a common mechanism for building big data pipelines. Orchestration for parallel ETL processing requires the use of multiple tools to perform a variety of operations. To simplify the orchestration, you can use AWS Glue workflows.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/aws-glue-workflow-021224-1.png)
    
    AWS Glue workflows provide a visual and programmatic tool to author data pipelines by combining AWS Glue crawlers for schema discovery and AWS Glue Spark and Python shell jobs to transform the data. A workflow consists of one or more task nodes arranged as a graph. Relationships can be defined, and parameters passed between task nodes to enable you to build pipelines of varying complexity. You can trigger workflows on a schedule or on-demand. You can track the progress of each node independently or the entire workflow, making it easier to troubleshoot your pipelines.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **AWS Glue Workflows.**
    
    The option that says: **AWS Step Functions** is incorrect. Step Functions is a viable option as it can use the AWS SDK to call Glue APIs. However, compared to a Glue Workflow, which is specifically designed for orchestrating Glue Crawlers and Glue Jobs, Step Functions is less straightforward. For example, when you run a crawler in Step Functions, you’re responsible for monitoring its status and implementing logic to poll whether the Crawler has completed before proceeding to the next step. In contrast, Glue Workflows handle monitoring of Jobs and Crawlers automatically.
    
    The option that says: **Amazon Data Firehose** is incorrect because it is only designed for streaming data into destinations such as Amazon S3, Amazon Redshift, and Amazon OpenSearch Service. It is not an ETL tool and does not handle data transformations or schema changes efficiently.
    
    The option that says: **Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (Amazon MWAA)**is incorrect because, while it can be used to manage complex ETL workflows, it primarily serves as a platform to programmatically author, schedule, and monitor workflows. It does not have the same level of integration with AWS Glue or the ability to handle changes in the data schema as effectively as AWS Glue workflows. It is more suited for custom workflows that require a high level of customization and flexibility.
    
- Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (Amazon MWAA)
- Amazon Data Firehose
- AWS Step Functions

A data engineering team is collecting log data from their website and storing it in Amazon S3 in JSON format. They need to transform and analyze this data to understand user behavior on the site. Some transformations needed are calculating daily active users, average session duration, and error rates. The processed data needs to be loaded into Amazon Redshift every day for analysis. The team has been using AWS Glue for their ETL jobs, but they are facing challenges with the development and testing of ETL scripts, which is slowing down their process.

Which of the following can the team leverage to improve the development and testing aspect of their ETL process?

- **AWS Glue Interactive Sessions**
    
    **AWS Glue Interactive Sessions** is a feature that allows developers to rapidly build, test, and run data preparation and analytics applications. It provides a programmatic and visual interface for building and testing Extract, Transform, and Load (ETL) scripts for data preparation.
    
    Interactive sessions run Apache Spark analytics applications and provide on-demand access to a remote Spark runtime environment. AWS Glue transparently manages serverless Spark for these interactive sessions. This means developers can focus more on writing the transformation logic than managing the infrastructure.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/glue-interactive-sessions-011624.png)
    
    Interactive sessions are flexible, allowing developers to build and test applications from the environment of their choice. Developers can create and work with interactive sessions through the AWS Command Line Interface and the API.
    
    For visual authoring and testing of notebook scripts, developers can use Jupyter-compatible notebooks. Interactive sessions provide an open-source Jupyter kernel that integrates almost anywhere that Jupyter does, including integrating with IDEs such as PyCharm, IntelliJ, and VS Code. This enables developers to author code in their local environment and run it seamlessly on the interactive sessions’ backend.
    
    Using the interactive sessions API, customers can programmatically run applications that use Apache Spark analytics without having to manage Spark infrastructure. Developers can run one or more Spark statements within a single interactive session.
    
    Therefore, AWS Glue Interactive Sessions provides a faster, cheaper, and more flexible way to build and run data preparation and analytics applications.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **AWS Glue Interactive Sessions.**
    
    **AWS Glue Data Catalog** is incorrect because it primarily serves as a persistent metadata store, but it does not directly aid in the development and testing of ETL scripts, which is the team’s main challenge.
    
    **AWS Glue Streaming ETL service** is incorrect because it is mainly used for real-time data preparation and loading. While it can be useful for continuous ingestion pipelines, it doesn’t directly address the challenge of developing and testing ETL scripts.
    
    **AWS Glue DataBrew** is incorrect because it is primarily a visual data preparation tool that enables users to clean and normalize data without writing any code. While it can help with data cleaning and normalization, it doesn’t directly address the development and testing of ETL scripts.
    
- AWS Glue Data Catalog
- AWS Glue DataBrew
- AWS Glue Streaming ETL service

A data engineer is deploying an application that requires low latency and high IOPS to AWS. The application writes temporary state data to disk during processing. The team must ensure that this data persists through instance terminations.

How can the data engineer meet this requirement?

- **Deploy on an EC2 instance that uses an instance store-backed Amazon Machine Image (AMI). Attach a secondary Amazon EBS volume for storing the temporary data. Leave the default settings upon creation.**
    
    In an EC2 instance that uses an instance store-backed Amazon Machine Image (AMI), the instance store serves as the root volume. EC2 Instance Store Volumes provide block-level storage directly attached to the host computer. This close integration with the host hardware enables the instance store to deliver exceptionally high IOPS, with certain instance types achieving millions of IOPS that exceed what EBS volumes can offer. This makes instance stores particularly suited for high-performance computing tasks, real-time processing of big data, and other I/O-intensive applications where speed is critical.
    
    However, one of the inherent limitations of instance store volumes is their ephemeral nature; the data stored on them is lost if the instance is stopped, terminated, or if the underlying physical machine fails. This characteristic makes instance store volumes unsuitable for storing data that needs to persist beyond the lifecycle of a single instance.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/amazon-ec2-instance-store-with-additional-ebs.jpg)
    
    To overcome this limitation and ensure data persistence, you can attach an additional Amazon EBS volume to the EC2 instance. EBS provides persistent block storage that can be detached from one instance and attached to another, or retained independently of any instance lifecycle. By configuring the application to write data that needs to persist to the EBS volume, you can protect this information against instance termination or failure.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Deploy on an EC2 instance that uses an instance store-backed Amazon Machine Image (AMI). Attach a secondary Amazon EBS volume for storing the temporary data. Leave the default settings upon creation.**
    
    The option that says: **Deploy on an EC2 instance that uses an instance store-backed Amazon Machine Image (AMI). Leave the default settings upon creation** is incorrect. Data on instance store volumes is ephemeral and will be lost if the instance is stopped, terminated, or fails. This does not meet the requirement for data persistence through instance terminations.
    
    The option that says: **Deploy on an EC2 instance with an Amazon Machine Image (AMI) that uses an EBS volume as the root. Leave the default settings upon creation** is incorrect. Although EBS volumes are persistent and can survive instance terminations, by default, the root volume for an EBS-backed AMI is terminated as well when the instance terminates.
    
    The option that says: **Deploy on an EC2 instance with an EBS-backed Amazon Machine Image (AMI). Attach a secondary Amazon EFS volume for storing the temporary data. Leave the default settings upon creation** is incorrect. While EFS is a valid solution for a storage solution, it’s generally not optimized for scenarios requiring high IOPS and low latency compared to EBS volumes.
    
- Deploy on an EC2 instance with an EBS-backed Amazon Machine Image (AMI). Attach a secondary Amazon EFS volume for storing the temporary data. Leave the default settings upon creation.
- Deploy on an EC2 instance that uses an instance store-backed Amazon Machine Image (AMI). Leave the default settings upon creation.
- Deploy on an EC2 instance with an Amazon Machine Image (AMI) that uses an EBS volume as the root. Leave the default settings upon creation.

A corporation stores large amounts of data in an on-premises data center. The corporation wants to have a copy of this data in Amazon S3 for further analysis. The replication process must run periodically to ensure data in the S3 bucket is up-to-date with the latest data from the on-premises data center.

Which AWS service is most suitable for meeting this requirement?

- **AWS DataSync**
    
    AWS DataSync is a secure, online service that automates and accelerates moving data between on-premises and AWS Storage services. DataSync can copy data between Network File System (NFS) shares, Server Message Block (SMB) shares, Hadoop Distributed File Systems (HDFS), self-managed object storage, AWS Snowcone, Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) buckets, Amazon Elastic File System (Amazon EFS) file systems, Amazon FSx for Windows File Server file systems, Amazon FSx for Lustre file systems, Amazon FSz for OpenZFS file systems, and Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP file systems.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/datasync-021524.png)
    
    With AWS DataSync, you can move data easily between S3 buckets for one-time migrations or periodic scheduled transfers, reducing operational overhead and infrastructure costs. DataSync is a fully managed solution that enables the transfer of data between S3 buckets with just a few clicks in the console, and without managing any additional infrastructure.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **AWS DataSync.**
    
    The option that says: **AWS Glue** is just a fully managed extract, transform, and load (ETL) service that makes it easy to prepare and load your data for analytics. It’s not specifically designed for moving data between on-premises data center and AWS.
    
    The option that says: **AWS Storage Gateway** is incorrect because AWS Storage Gateway is designed for hybrid cloud storage, enabling on-premises applications to seamlessly use AWS cloud storage. While it integrates well with Amazon S3, it’s better suited for continuous and real-time access to cloud storage rather than periodic replication for analysis purposes.
    
    The option that says: **AWS Snowball** is incorrect because AWS Snowball is primarily designed for physically transferring large amounts of data to AWS but is not suitable for ongoing periodic updates or automated data replication.
    
- AWS Glue
- AWS Storage Gateway
- AWS Snowball

A data engineering team is working on a new project that involves processing and analyzing a large amount of data. The data is collected from various sources and is expected to grow exponentially.

The team needs a solution that can handle this scale of data and provide efficient querying capabilities. They also need to ensure centralized data governance and access control.

Which combination of AWS services would best meet the team’s requirements? (Select TWO.)

- Utilize Amazon DynamoDB to store data and Amazon QuickSight to create visualizations and perform ad-hoc analysis of this data.
- **Utilize Amazon S3 to store data and Amazon Athena for running analysis.**
- **Utilize Lake Formation to centralize data access and permissions.**
- Utilize Amazon Redshift to store and analyze a large amount of data and AWS Glue to prepare the data (ETL operations) for analytics.
- Utilize AWS Glue Data Catalog to centralize data access and permissions.
- **Evaluation**
    
    Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) is storage for the internet. You can use Amazon S3 to store and retrieve any amount of data at any time, from anywhere on the web.
    
    Amazon Athena is a serverless, interactive analytics service built on open-source frameworks that support open-table and file formats. Athena provides a simplified, flexible way to analyze petabytes of data where it lives. Analyze data or build applications from an Amazon Simple Storage Service (S3) data lake and 30 data sources, including on-premises data sources or other cloud systems using SQL or Python. Athena is built on open-source Trino and Presto engines and Apache Spark frameworks, with no provisioning or configuration effort required.
    
    AWS Lake Formation is a managed service that makes it easy to set up, secure, and manage your data lakes. Lake Formation helps you discover your data sources and then catalog, cleanse, and transform the data. You can use Lake Formation to secure the data and ingest it into an Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) data lake.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/athena-lake-s3-030724-2.png)
    
    Amazon S3 is a scalable object storage service that can handle large amounts of unstructured data, which is ideal for the team’s requirement of handling an exponentially growing amount of data.
    
    Amazon Athena is a serverless interactive query service that makes it easy to analyze data in Amazon S3 using standard SQL. It can handle large-scale datasets, which aligns with the team’s need for efficient querying capabilities.
    
    AWS Lake Formation is a service that makes it easy to set up, secure, and manage data lakes. It provides centralized data governance and access control, which is one of the team’s requirements.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Utilize Amazon S3 to store data and Amazon Athena for running analysis.**
    
    **– Utilize Lake Formation to centralize data access and permissions.**
    
    The option that says: **Utilize Amazon DynamoDB to store data and Amazon QuickSight to create visualizations and perform ad-hoc analysis of this data** is incorrect. While DynamoDB is excellent for handling large amounts of data, it’s primarily a NoSQL database service used for key-value and document data structures. It might not be the best choice for complex analytical queries.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize Amazon Redshift to store and analyze a large amount of data and AWS Glue to prepare the data (ETL operations) for analytics** is incorrect because while Amazon Redshift is indeed a powerful data warehousing solution, it’s primarily designed for structured data and might not be the best fit for handling unstructured or semi-structured data that could come from various sources in this scenario. AWS Glue, while great for ETL operations, doesn’t provide the storage or querying capabilities needed for this scenario. It’s more about data preparation rather than data storage or querying.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize AWS Glue Data Catalog to centralize data access and permissions.**  is incorrect. Glue Data Catalog just serves as a centrailize metadata store. By itself, it’s not capable of managing data access permissions or governance.
    

An e-commerce company stores massive transaction data as .csv files in an Amazon S3 bucket. The data is processed using Amazon Athena to generate various reports. However, as the volume of data grows, the processing time increases. The company’s data engineer must expedite the data processing time.

Which of the following solutions meets the requirements?

- **Convert the .csv files to a columnar format like Apache Parquet or ORC and use Snappy compression to reduce the size of the data.**
    
    Apache Parquet and ORC are columnar storage formats that are optimized for fast retrieval of data and used in AWS analytical applications.
    
    Columnar storage formats have the following characteristics that make them suitable for using with Athena:
    
    *– Compression by column, with compression algorithm selected for the column data type* to save storage space in Amazon S3 and reduce disk space and I/O during query processing.
    
    *– Predicate pushdown* in Parquet and ORC enables Athena queries to fetch only the blocks it needs, improving query performance. When an Athena query obtains specific column values from your data, it uses statistics from data block predicates, such as max/min values, to determine whether to read or skip the block.
    
    *– Splitting of data* in Parquet and ORC allows Athena to split the reading of data to multiple readers and increase parallelism during its query processing.
    
    To convert your existing raw data from other storage formats to Parquet or ORC, you can run CREATE TABLE AS SELECT (CTAS) queries in Athena and specify a data storage format as Parquet or ORC, or use the AWS Glue Crawler.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/athena-ctas-040424-1.png)
    
    Snappy is a compression/decompression library that provides reasonable compression rates while still maintaining good performance during decompression. When used in conjunction with a columnar format like Parquet or ORC, Snappy can help to significantly reduce the size of the data, which in turn improves read efficiency and reduces data processing time.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Convert the .csv files to a columnar format like Apache Parquet or ORC and use Snappy compression to reduce the size of the data**.
    
    The option that says: **Convert the .csv files to a columnar format like Avro and use Snappy compression to reduce the size of the data** is incorrect because, while Avro is a useful data serialization system, it is not as efficient as Parquet or ORC for columnar data storage. Avro is primarily used for data serialization, and it doesn’t provide the same level of query performance as Parquet or ORC for columnar data.
    
    The option that says: **Use Gzip compression on the .csv files to improve data retrieval speed** is incorrect. Although Gzip compression can reduce file size for quicker data retrieval, it is not the most efficient way to speed up data processing in Athena. Gzip is a row-based compression, meaning Athena would still need to decompress and read through entire files to process queries, which does not significantly improve processing time compared to columnar formats.
    
    The option that says: **Convert the .csv files to a JSON format and use Snappy compression to reduce the size of the data** is incorrect.  This option might reduce the overall data size, but JSON is not an optimized format for query performance in  Athena. JSON is a row-oriented data format that does not support the efficient columnar data retrieval that is possible with formats like Parquet or ORC.
    
- Convert the .csv files to a JSON format and use Snappy compression to reduce the size of the data.
- Convert the .csv files to a columnar format like Avro and use Snappy compression to reduce the size of the data.
- Use Gzip compression on the .csv files to improve data retrieval speed.

A large e-commerce company has a data lake stored in Amazon S3, which contains a vast amount of customer transaction data in JSON format. The company wants to analyze this data to gain insights into customer behavior and improve its marketing strategies.

Amazon Athena is chosen for querying the data due to its serverless nature. However, the data engineering team is having difficulties dealing with JSON data, especially when they need to pivot it for analysis.

How should a solution be designed to enable efficient querying of the JSON data in the S3 data lake using Amazon Athena, considering the need to pivot the data?

- Load the JSON data from S3 into Amazon Redshift, then use the `PIVOT`function in Redshift to pivot the data.
- Use AWS Lambda to parse the JSON data and store it in DynamoDB. Then, use Athena to query the data in DynamoDB.
- Convert the JSON data into CSV format using AWS Glue, then create a table in Athena to query the data. Use SQL queries with `GROUP BY` and `CASE`statements to pivot the data.
- **Utilize the `JSON SerDe` library in Athena to parse the JSON data and create a table in Athena. Then, use SQL queries with `CASE` statements to pivot the data.**
    
    Amazon Athena is a serverless interactive query service that makes it easy to analyze data in Amazon S3 using standard SQL. It’s designed to handle large-scale datasets and can execute queries in parallel, making it a powerful tool for data analysis. In the context of the question, the company is dealing with JSON data in an S3 data lake and wants to pivot this data for analysis. Athena uses libraries called SerDes (Serializer/Deserializer) to interpret the input data format. For JSON data, Athena uses the `org.openx.data.jsonserde.JsonSerDe` library, also known as `JSON SerDe`. This library allows Athena to interpret the JSON data, parse it into a structured format that can be queried using SQL.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/athena-JsonSerDe-040224.png)
    
    Once the JSON data is parsed using the `JSON SerDe` library, a table can be created in Athena. This table represents the structured format of the JSON data and can be queried directly using SQL. Pivoting data is a common operation in data analysis where you transform the data from a long format to a wide format. In SQL, this can be achieved using `CASE` statements. A `CASE` statement lets you create new columns based on conditions in the data. This way, you can pivot the data based on your needs. This approach leverages the serverless nature of Athena, which means you don’t have to worry about managing infrastructure or scaling resources.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Utilize the `JSON SerDe` library in Amazon Athena to parse the JSON data and create a table in Athena. Then, use SQL queries with `CASE` statements to pivot the data.**
