    The option that says: **Load the JSON data from S3 into Amazon Redshift, then use the `PIVOT` function in Redshift to pivot the data** is incorrect because while it’s possible to load data from S3 into Redshift and use the `PIVOT` function, this would involve additional data transfer and storage costs. Moreover, it doesn’t utilize the serverless nature of Athena which can directly query data in S3.
    
    The option that says: **Convert the JSON data into CSV format using AWS Glue, then create a table in Athena to query the data. Use SQL queries with `GROUP BY` and `CASE` statements to pivot the data** is incorrect because it involves an additional step of converting JSON data into CSV format using AWS Glue. This might not be necessary as Athena can directly query JSON data using the `JSON SerDe` library.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Lambda to parse the JSON data and store it in DynamoDB. Then, use Athena to query the data in DynamoDB** is incorrect. While this could work, loading the data in DynamoDB adds an unnecessary step in the workflow. Athena can directly query the data stored in Amazon S3 without any data movement involved.
    

A global e-commerce company is experiencing rapid growth and expanding its operations to multiple regions worldwide. The company’s data is stored in various database engines, including MySQL, PostgreSQL, and MongoDB. The data engineering team is tasked with creating a unified view of all these data sources for analysis.

Which solution would meet this requirement with the LEAST operational effort?

- Use Amazon EMR to run big data frameworks like Apache Spark or Hadoop over the data in these databases.
- Use AWS Glue to transfer data from each database to Amazon Redshift and query the data using SQL.
- **Leverage Amazon Athena Federated Query to deploy and configure the appropriate data source connectors for each database engine and then run queries using Amazon Athena.**
    
    Amazon Athena Federated Query is a feature of Amazon Athena, AWS’s interactive query service, that makes it easy to analyze data in Amazon S3 using standard SQL. Federated Query extends Athena’s capabilities by allowing users to perform SQL queries across multiple data sources beyond Amazon S3. This includes a wide range of databases and services, both relational and non-relational, such as MySQL, PostgreSQL, Amazon DynamoDB, and others, without having to move the data into a single repository.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/athena-federated-query-032824.png)
    
    Connectors must be created using AWS Lambda functions to enable Federated Query in Amazon Athena. These connectors serve as intermediaries, allowing Athena to execute SQL queries on a variety of data sources by translating the queries into the target data source’s native query language. AWS offers built-in connectors for several common databases, simplifying integration. For data sources without a pre-built connector, custom connectors can be developed. This approach allows querying nearly any data store directly from Athena, enabling complex analysis across various datasets without data movement or duplication.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Leverage Amazon Athena Federated Query to deploy and configure the appropriate data source connectors for each database engine and then run queries using Amazon Athena.**
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue to transfer data from each database to Amazon Redshift and query the data using SQL** is incorrect. Transferring data to Amazon Redshift requires additional steps for data loading and maintenance, making it more complex than using Athena Federated Query.
    
    The option that says: **Run custom code using an AWS Lambda function to extract data from databases. Store the extracted data in Amazon DynamoDB. Use Amazon Athena for querying** is incorrect. Although AWS Lambda functions can be typically used to extract data, storing it in DynamoDB and then querying with Athena adds unnecessary complexity. This approach does not leverage the direct querying capability of Athena Federated Query, which is designed to query data directly from various data sources.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon EMR to run big data frameworks like Apache Spark or Hadoop over the data in these databases** is incorrect because Amazon EMR is primarily used for processing large amounts of data using popular big data frameworks. This approach would allow for complex data processing and analysis across multiple sources. However, it’s more complex and resource-intensive than necessary for simply creating a unified view of existing databases.
    
- Run custom code using an AWS Lambda function to extract data from databases. Store the extracted data in Amazon DynamoDB. Use Amazon Athena for querying.

A multinational corporation is planning to migrate its on-premises PostgreSQL database to Amazon RDS for PostgreSQL. Since the company runs critical operations on the database, the migration must be completed with minimize downtime. The company also wants to ensure data consistency by validating the migrated data.

Which AWS service would be the most suitable for this scenario?

- AWS Snowball
- AWS DataSync
- AWS Glue
- **AWS Database Migration Service**
    
    **AWS Database Migration Service (AWS DMS)** is a web service you can use to migrate data from your database that is on-premises, on an Amazon Relational Database Service (Amazon RDS) DB instance, or in a database on an Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) instance to a database on an AWS service. These services can include a database on Amazon RDS or a database on an Amazon EC2 instance. You can also migrate a database from an AWS service to an on-premises database. You can migrate between source and target endpoints that use the same database engine, such as from an Oracle database to an Oracle database. You can also migrate between source and target endpoints that use different database engines, such as from an Oracle database to a PostgreSQL database.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/aws-dms-postgresql-040324.png)
    
    You can use AWS DMS to migrate databases to the AWS Cloud quickly and securely. Your source database remains fully operational during the migration, which minimizes downtime for applications that rely on the database. You can reduce the migration time by using AWS DMS to create a task that captures ongoing changes after you complete an initial full-load migration through a process called change data capture (CDC).
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **AWS Database Migration Service.**
    
    **AWS Snowball** is incorrect because it is solely designed for large-scale data transfers. It does not have the capability to perform database migration or data validation.
    
    **AWS Glue** is incorrect because it is primarily an extract, transform, and load (ETL) service. It is used for preparing and loading data for analytics, but it does not cater to the specific needs of database migration or data validation.
    
    **AWS DataSync** is incorrect because it is merely a data transfer service. It can help automate and accelerate data movement between on-premises storage systems and AWS storage services. However, it does not support database migration.
    

A data engineer maintains multiple ETL jobs within AWS Glue. Among these jobs, there’s a specific non-urgent task that processes large amounts of data. The data engineer must optimize the cost of running this job without compromising its performance.

Which of the following actions would be the most cost-effective?

- Use spot instances for the job.
- Use AWS Glue STANDARD execution for the job.
- Use a smaller data processing unit (DPU).
- **Use AWS Glue Flex for the job.**
    
    **Flex** allows you to optimize your costs on your non-urgent or non-time sensitive data integration workloads such as testing and one-time data loads. With Flex, AWS Glue jobs run on spare compute capacity instead of dedicated hardware. The start and run times of jobs using Flex can vary because spare compute resources aren’t readily available and can be reclaimed during the run of a job.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/glue-flex-021424.png)
    
    Regardless of the run option used, AWS Glue jobs have the same capabilities, including access to custom connectors, visual authoring interface, job scheduling, and Glue Auto Scaling. With the Flex execution option, customers can optimize the costs of their data integration workloads by configuring the execution option based on the workloads’ requirements, using the standard execution option for time-sensitive workloads and Flex for non-urgent workloads. The Flex execution class is available for AWS Glue 3.0 Spark jobs.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use AWS Glue Flex for the job.**
    
    The option that says: **Use a smaller data processing unit (DPU)** is incorrect. Using a smaller DPU might reduce costs, but it could compromise the job’s performance, given the large amount of data being processed. DPUs provide processing power and memory for AWS Glue jobs. If the DPU size is too small, the job might not have enough resources to process large amounts of data efficiently.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue STANDARD execution for the job** is incorrect because while STANDARD execution could be used for the job, it’s less cost-effective than AWS Glue Flex for non-time-sensitive jobs. STANDARD execution uses dedicated resources to run your jobs, which can provide consistent performance. However, this comes at a higher cost compared to AWS Glue Flex, which uses spare computing capacity.
    
    The option that says: **Use spot instances for the job** is incorrect. Spot instances allow users to purchase unused EC2 capacity at reduced rates, which can lead to significant savings. However, AWS Glue jobs do not directly support the use of EC2 spot instances since Glue manages its own compute resources and does not expose underlying EC2 configurations.
    

A data engineering team needs an efficient method to analyze historical data for a market research project. The data resides in various AWS services, including Amazon RDS for PostgreSQL, Amazon DynamoDB, and Amazon S3, along with logs stored in Amazon CloudWatch. The team plans to execute a one-time query that requires joining this data.

What is the MOST cost-effective solution that the data engineering team can implement to meet this requirement?

- Implement AWS Lambda functions to transfer data from Amazon RDS for PostgreSQL, DynamoDB, and CloudWatch to S3, and then analyze using Amazon Athena.
- Configure Amazon Redshift Spectrum to query data in DynamoDB, Amazon RDS for PostgreSQL, and S3, with a separate process for CloudWatch Logs analysis.
- **Utilize Amazon Athena Federated Query to join and analyze data across Amazon RDS for PostgreSQL, DynamoDB, S3, and CloudWatch Logs.**
    
    **Amazon Athena Federated Query** enables running SQL queries across data stored in multiple data sources without extracting or loading the data into Amazon S3. This feature allows querying data directly from its original location, whether it resides in databases, data warehouses, data lakes, or custom data sources.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/dea3-45-amazon-athena-federated-query-diagram2-022624.jpg)
    
    Leveraging Athena’s Federated Query feature to access and join data from multiple sources directly from Amazon RDS for PostgreSQL, DynamoDB, S3, and CloudWatch Logs minimizes the cost by avoiding unnecessary data movement. It simplifies the architecture by leveraging a single query interface for diverse data sources, meeting efficient and cost-effective analysis requirements.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Utilize Amazon Athena Federated Query to join and analyze data across Amazon RDS for PostgreSQL, DynamoDB, S3, and CloudWatch Logs.**
    
    The option that says: **Deploy Amazon EMR with Apache Hadoop to process data from DynamoDB, Amazon RDS for PostgreSQL, and S3, and use Amazon CloudWatch Logs Insights for log analysis** is incorrect. This approach involves provisioning and managing an EMR cluster, which can be more expensive and has greater overhead compared to using Athena Federated Query, especially for a one-time job.
    
    The option that says: **Implement AWS Lambda functions to transfer data from Amazon RDS for PostgreSQL, DynamoDB, and CloudWatch to S3, and then analyze using Amazon Athena** is incorrect because it introduces unnecessary complexity and potential costs by requiring all data to be moved to S3 before analysis, which Athena Federated Query can do without.
    
    The option that says: **Configure Amazon Redshift Spectrum to query data in DynamoDB, Amazon RDS for PostgreSQL, and S3, with a separate process for CloudWatch Logs analysis** is incorrect because Redshift Spectrum can only query data in S3 and does not support direct querying of CloudWatch Logs. Moreover, having a separate process for CloudWatch Logs analysis would complicate the overall solution and increase costs.
    
- Deploy Amazon EMR with Apache Hadoop to process data from DynamoDB, Amazon RDS for PostgreSQL, and S3, and use Amazon CloudWatch Logs Insights for log analysis.

A data engineering team has a data pipeline that uses Amazon EMR for big data processing and stores intermediary results in Amazon S3. Part of the workflow involves transferring processed data to Amazon Redshift for analytics and business intelligence purposes. The team uses AWS Step Functions to orchestrate this process. However, when the Step Functions state machine is executed, the EMR jobs fail.

Which of the following should be taken to troubleshoot and resolve this issue?

- Check the VPC flow logs and settings of the security group cluster to ensure no connectivity issues prevent the Amazon EMR cluster from connecting to any downstream or upstream services.
- Set up auto-scaling policies for the EMR cluster to adjust its size and compute capacity in response to the volume of data being processed.
- **Check the IAM permissions associated with the state machine’s IAM role. Ensure it has the required permissions to run Amazon EMR jobs.**
    
    AWS Step Functions need appropriate IAM permissions to interact with other AWS services, such as Amazon EMR. If the Step Functions state machine lacks the necessary permissions to initiate or manage EMR clusters, the jobs would fail to start or execute properly. Checking and updating IAM permissions and resources specific to the EMR clusters could resolve issues related to access and authorization.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/step-functions-permissions-032524.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Check the IAM permissions associated with the state machine’s IAM role. Ensure it has the required permissions to run Amazon EMR jobs.**
    
    The option that says: **Check the EMR metrics published in Amazon CloudWatch to gain insights into the status of the EMR cluster** is incorrect. This option would only help in understanding the behavior of the cluster during its operation rather than solving an issue related to the triggering of jobs by Step Functions.
    
    The option that says: **Check the VPC flow logs and settings of the security group cluster to ensure no connectivity issues prevent the Amazon EMR cluster from connecting to any downstream or upstream services** is incorrect. While connectivity issues can indeed cause failures, this option is less likely if the primary cause is the failure of EMR jobs when triggered by Step Functions. Connectivity problems would typically manifest as network-related errors or failures in interactions between services, not necessarily causing the EMR job itself not to run when initiated by Step Functions.
    
    The option that says: **Set up auto-scaling policies for the EMR cluster to adjust its size and compute capacity in response to the volume of data being processed** is incorrect because it addresses a concern (scaling of Amazon EMR clusters) that is unrelated to the primary issue described. The main problem is the failure of the AWS Step Functions state machine to initiate Amazon EMR jobs. This failure is an orchestration issue, not a scaling or resource capacity issue within the EMR clusters. Auto-scaling configurations would not rectify or address the inability of Step Functions to start EMR jobs.
    
- Check the EMR metrics published in Amazon CloudWatch to gain insights into the status of the EMR cluster.

A company plans to process large amounts of data stored in Amazon S3. The data is in various formats, such as CSV, JSON, and Parquet. The company needs a solution that can handle the complexity of processing vast amounts of data and provide the flexibility to use popular distributed frameworks such as Apache Spark, Hadoop, Presto, and Flink.

What service would be best suited to fulfill the requirements?

- Amazon Redshift
- AWS Glue
- Amazon Athena
- **Amazon Elastic MapReduce (Amazon EMR)**
    
    Amazon EMR is a managed cluster platform that simplifies running big data frameworks, such as Apache Hadoop and Apache Spark, on AWS to process and analyze vast amounts of data. Using these frameworks and related open-source projects, you can process data for analytics purposes and business intelligence workloads. Amazon EMR also lets you transform and move large amounts of data into and out of other AWS data stores and databases, such as Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) and Amazon DynamoDB.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/amazon-emr-021924.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Amazon Elastic MapReduce (Amazon EMR)**
    
    **Amazon Redshift** is a fast, fully managed, petabyte-scale data warehouse service that makes it simple and cost-effective to analyze all your data using your existing business intelligence tools. However, it is not designed to process large amounts of data stored in various formats such as CSV, JSON, and Parquet, nor does it provide the flexibility to use popular distributed frameworks such as Apache Spark, Hadoop, Presto, and Flink.
    
    **AWS Glue** is a fully managed extract, transform, and load (ETL) service that makes it easy for users to prepare and load their data for analytics. While AWS Glue can technically run Spark jobs and process data in various formats, it’s less suitable for big data processing than Amazon EMR, which provides built-in support for popular distributed frameworks like Apache Spark, Hadoop, Presto, and Flink.
    
    **Amazon Athena** is simply an interactive query service that makes it easy to analyze data in Amazon S3 using standard SQL. It’s not designed for big data processing.
    

A company wants to conduct real-time analysis of streaming data from various sources. The system must be fault-tolerant to ensure reliability and continuous operation. The company intends to continuously aggregate data from the most recent 5-minute period to gain actionable insights.

Which of the following options would meet the requirements with the LEAST development effort?

- Configure an Apache Kafka with custom Java applications for real-time data processing.
- **Launch an Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink to analyze streaming data. Use a sliding window query to aggregate data.**
    
    With Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink, you can use Java, Scala, Python, or SQL to process and analyze streaming data. The service enables you to author and run code against streaming sources and static sources to perform time-series analytics and feed real-time dashboards and metrics.
    
    You can build applications in Managed Service for Apache Flink with the language of your choice using open-source libraries based on Apache Flink. Apache Flink is a popular framework and engine for processing data streams.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-DEA3-57-Managed-Apache%20Flink.png)
    
    Managed Service for Apache Flink provides the underlying infrastructure for your Apache Flink applications. It handles core capabilities like provisioning compute resources, AZ failover resilience, parallel computation, automatic scaling, and application backups (implemented as checkpoints and snapshots). You can use the high-level Flink programming features (such as operators, functions, sources, and sinks) in the same way that you use them when hosting the Flink infrastructure yourself.
    
    For handling large volumes of streaming data with requirements for real-time analysis, fault tolerance, and the capability to perform diverse aggregation tasks over time windows, Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink is a robust solution. This service is specifically designed for processing and analyzing streams of data in real time, offering the scalability, flexibility, and fault tolerance needed for such tasks. Apache Flink’s support for different types of windowing queries, including sliding windows, and tumbling windows. Sliding window aggregations allow for analyzing data across overlapping time windows, which is ideal for continuous monitoring and analysis no longer than one hour. This window query is suitable for continuous, real-time analysis over the most recent period. Moreover, the tumbling windows are fixed-sized, non-overlapping intervals of time. Each event in a data stream belongs to exactly one window. This window is ideal for scenarios where you need to perform an operation over a chunk of data collected within a regular interval, and each interval is considered independent. For example, counting the number of events every minute.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Launch an Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink to analyze streaming data. Use a sliding window query to aggregate data.**
    
    The option that says: **Configure an Apache Kafka with custom Java applications for real-time data processing** is incorrect because managing a Kafka cluster and developing custom applications to process the data is more complex and time-consuming than using a managed service.
    
    The option that says: **Enable the Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink Studio for SQL-based real-time analytics. Aggregate data using a tumbling window query** is incorrect because the tumbling window aggregates data over non-overlapping, fixed-sized time intervals. This does not fulfill the requirement to aggregate over the most recent 5-minute periods continuously.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize Amazon Kinesis Data Streams with AWS Lambda for real-time data processing over large, distributed data streams**is incorrect. Although AWS Lambda can process data from Kinesis Data Streams, this setup is not as straightforward and complicated to implement compared to a dedicated streaming service like Flink. Moreover, Lambda functions have an execution duration limit per request that is not suitable for continuous long-term processing of streaming data.
    
- Enable the Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink Studio for SQL-based real-time analytics. Aggregate data using a tumbling window query.
- Utilize Amazon Kinesis Data Streams with AWS Lambda for real-time data processing over large, distributed data streams.

A team of data engineers is developing an ETL (Extract, Transform, Load) pipeline that will use an Amazon S3-based data lake as its source. Sensitive information has been discovered during data exploration.

To ensure data privacy and protection, the data engineering team must implement a mechanism that can identify and redact the Personally Identifiable Information (PII) before the data is ingested.

Which combination of steps should be implemented to meet these requirements with the LEAST amount of development effort? (Select TWO.)

- **Create a Glue job in AWS Glue Studio and use the Detect PII transform to identify and redact columns containing PII.**
- Implement an Amazon Data Firehose stream with AWS Lambda for PII redaction.
- **Leverage AWS Step Functions to orchestrate the ingestion and processing workflow of the dataset.**
- Use AWS Macie for PII identification and data redaction.
- Create a rule in AWS Glue Data Quality to determine and redact the PII.
- **Evaluation**
    
    **AWS Glue Studio** offers a no-code visual interface for data integration tasks, including the detection and redaction of PII from datasets before they are ingested into a data lake. This approach minimizes the operational effort as it leverages AWS’s managed services to handle both the detection of sensitive information and the orchestration of data workflows. AWS Glue’s PII transform feature automatically identifies and masks or redacts sensitive data, ensuring that any PII present in the source data is appropriately handled before it reaches the S3 data lake.
    
    **AWS Step Functions** can then be used to orchestrate the workflow, controlling the flow of data from its source through the AWS Glue Studio for PII redaction and finally into the Amazon S3 data lake. This service provides a robust mechanism for managing complex data-processing workflows with minimal manual intervention, reducing operational burden.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA3-41-pii-and%20step-function-diagram-3-030124.png)
    
    In this scenario, the company can use AWS Glue Studio to visually create a Glue job to identify and redact PII from datasets. AWS Glue Studio includes a built-in Detect PII transform that the company can use right away. Glue Studio will automatically generate the necessary code for the job. Once done, you can include the Glue job as part of the pipeline orchestrated by an AWS Step Functions workflow.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Create a Glue job in AWS Glue Studio and use the Detect PII transform to identify and redact columns containing PII.**
    
    **– Leverage AWS Step Functions to orchestrate the ingestion and processing workflow of the dataset.**
    
    The option that says: **Implement an Amazon Data Firehose stream with AWS Lambda for PII redaction** is incorrect. While feasible, this approach requires custom development and management of a Lambda function for PII detection and redaction, potentially increasing operational effort and complexity.
    
    The option that says: **Create a rule in AWS Glue Data Quality to determine and redact the PII** is incorrect because AWS Glue Data Quality primarily focuses on monitoring data quality over time rather than performing data transformation tasks like PII redaction.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Macie for PII identification and data redaction** is incorrect because AWS Macie is primarily a data security and data privacy service focused on identifying and protecting sensitive data in AWS. While it can identify PII, it doesn’t directly support redaction or data transformation as part of an ETL process like AWS Glue Studio.
    

A data engineer is working on a project that involves processing and transforming large amounts of data. The data is stored in various formats and locations. The notebooks are based on Jupyter Notebooks, a popular open-source web application that allows you to create and share documents that contain live code, equations, visualizations, and narrative text.     AWS Glue Studio Notebook also support real-time data processing. This is particularly useful when data is sourced from IoT devices, change data capture (CDC) services, and streaming services such as Amazon Kinesis and Apache Kafka.

An industrial enterprise uses IoT sensors to monitor operations in its factory sectors. Data from these sensors is published to a data stream managed by Amazon Kinesis Data Streams. The enterprise wants to monitor operational metrics on a dashboard in real-time. The dashboard should update in real-time to ensure minimal latency.

Which solution will achieve this requirement with the least latency?

- Process the sensor data using Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink. Configure an Amazon Data Firehose stream to ingest processed data directly into Amazon Timestream. Utilize Amazon QuickSight to create dashboards drawing data from the Timestream database.
- Use Amazon Data Firehose to send sensor data from the stream to an S3 Bucket. Create an Amazon S3 event notification to invoke an AWS Lambda function upon the creation of new objects. Make use of the Lambda function to process and insert the data into Amazon Redshift. Build an Amazon QuickSight dashboard using Amazon Redshift as the source.
- Replace the data stream with Amazon Managed Streaming for Apache Kafka (MSK) and ingest the result into an Amazon S3 bucket. Build an Amazon QuickSight dashboard using Amazon S3 as the source.
- **Utilize Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink for real-time data processing. Configure an Apache Flink connector to output data into an Amazon Timestream database, and then use this database to power an Amazon Managed Grafana dashboard for visualization.**
    
    **Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink** is a powerful platform that enables real-time data processing. Data Engineers can use this service to analyze streaming data using Java, Scala, Python, or SQL. It simplifies the management of Apache Flink applications by handling the underlying infrastructure, such as compute resources, automatic scaling, and application backups. Moreover, the service enables you to quickly author and run powerful SQL code against streaming sources to perform time-series analytics, feed real-time dashboards, and create real-time metrics.

This provides powerful tools for creating dynamic dashboards to monitor and analyze data. This integration allows for real-time monitoring and analysis, making it suitable for various applications, including IoT, financial analytics, and operational monitoring.
