Create an Amazon S3 event notification to invoke an AWS Lambda function upon the creation of new objects. Make use of the Lambda function to process and insert the data into Amazon Redshift. Build an Amazon QuickSight dashboard using Amazon Redshift as the source** is incorrect. The process of storing data in S3, invoking Lambda, and then loading it into Redshift introduces latency, making it less ideal for the requirement of minimal latency.
    
    The option that says: **Process the sensor data using Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink. Configure an Amazon Data Firehose stream to ingest processed data directly into Amazon Timestream. Utilize Amazon QuickSight to create dashboards drawing data from the Timestream database** is incorrect. Although Data Firehose can consume data from a data stream and deliver it to a chosen destination, it does not support Amazon Timestream as a destination.
    
    The option that says: **Replace the data stream with Amazon Managed Streaming for Apache Kafka (MSK) and ingest the result into an Amazon S3 bucket. Build an Amazon QuickSight dashboard using Amazon S3 as the source** is incorrect. This method introduces additional latency through the use of S3 as an intermediary storage, which is not optimal for real-time operational monitoring.
    

A data engineer is developing a Python-based data processing service. The service must be exposed as private REST APIs using Amazon API Gateway. The data engineer must ensure the service can handle a steady load with quick, low-latency responses.

How can the service be deployed in the LEAST amount of effort?

- Implement the service for API Gateway on an Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS) cluster.
- Deploy the service on Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS). Configure API Gateway to route requests to an EKS cluster.
- **Create AWS Lambda functions with provisioned concurrency for the service and configure API Gateway to invoke the functions.**
    
    In Lambda, concurrency is the number of in-flight requests that your function is currently handling. There are two types of concurrency controls available:
    
    **Reserved concurrency** – This represents the maximum number of concurrent instances allocated to your function. When a function has reserved concurrency, no other function can use that concurrency. Configuring reserved concurrency for a function incurs no additional charges.
    
    **Provisioned concurrency** – This is the number of pre-initialized execution environments allocated to your function. These execution environments are ready to respond immediately to incoming function requests. Configuring provisioned concurrency incurs additional charges to your AWS account.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/aws-lambda-concurrency-022024.png)
    
    When you invoke a Lambda function, the invocation is routed to an execution environment to process the request. When a function has not been used for some time, when you need to process more concurrent invocations, or when you update a function, new execution environments are created. The creation of an execution environment takes care of installing the function code and starting the runtime. Depending on the size of your deployment package, and the initialization time of the runtime and of your code, this can introduce latency for the invocations that are routed to a new execution environment. This latency is usually referred to as a “cold start”. For most applications, this additional latency is not a problem. For some applications, however, this latency may not be acceptable.
    
    When you enable Provisioned Concurrency for a function, the Lambda service will initialize the requested number of execution environments so they can be ready to respond to invocations.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create AWS Lambda functions with provisioned concurrency for the service and configure API Gateway to invoke the functions.**
    
    The option that says: **Deploy the service on AWS Lambda functions with SnapStart. Integrate the functions with API Gateway** is incorrect because SnapStart is designed to improve cold start performance primarily for functions that are not invoked frequently. From the given scenario, it requires high and steady load handling with quick execution, SnapStart may not provide the same level of readiness and performance consistency as provisioned concurrency, which is explicitly designed to keep functions warm and ready to execute.
    
    The option that says: **Deploy the service on Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS). Configure API Gateway to route requests to an EKS cluster** is incorrect. Setting up an EKS cluster involves managing Kubernetes, which is complex and requires a significant amount of setup and operational management, even with AWS managing the Kubernetes infrastructure. This option would require much more effort compared to the serverless options.
    
    The option that says: **Implement the service for API Gateway on an Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS) cluster** is incorrect. While Amazon ECS is a fully managed container orchestration service, it still requires more setup and management effort compared to AWS Lambda. Thus, it’s not the least effort compared to AWS Lambda.
    
- Deploy the service on AWS Lambda functions with SnapStart. Integrate the functions with API Gateway.

A company uses Amazon S3 as a data lake to store data from its application. However, upon reviewing the data, there is a lot of duplicate information, such as customers who have signed up more than once, products that have accidentally added more than once, and so forth. The data engineer must find a way to detect and eliminate these duplicate information.

Which solution must the engineer implement to satisfy the requirement with the LEAST operational overhead?

- Create an ETL job written in Python. Import the Python `recordlinkage`library and use it for data deduplication.
- **Create an ETL job written in AWS Glue. Utilize `FindMatches` machine learning transform for data deduplication.**
    
    **AWS Glue** is a serverless data integration service that makes it easy for analytics users to discover, prepare, move, and integrate data from multiple sources. It can be used for analytics, machine learning, and application development. AWS Glue consolidates major data integration capabilities into a single service. These include data discovery, modern ETL, cleansing, transforming, and centralized cataloging.
    
    You can use AWS Glue to find matching records across a dataset (including ones without identifiers) by using the new **FindMatches ML Transform**, a custom machine learning transformation that helps you identify matching records.
    
    The FindMatches transform enables you to identify duplicate or matching records in your dataset, even when the records do not have a common unique identifier and no fields match exactly. This will not require writing any code or knowing how machine learning works. FindMatches can be useful in many different problems, such as:
    
    **Matching customers**: Linking customer records across different customer databases, even when many customer fields do not match exactly across the databases (e.g., different name spelling, address differences, missing or inaccurate data, etc).
    
    **Matching products**: Matching products in your catalog against other product sources, such as product catalog against a competitor’s catalog, where entries are structured differently.
    
    **Improving fraud detection**: Identifying duplicate customer accounts, determining when a newly created account is (or might be) a match for a previously known fraudulent user.
    
    **Other matching problems**: Match addresses, movies, parts lists, etc. In general, if a human being could look at your database rows and determine that they were a match, there is a really good chance that the FindMatches transform can help you.
    
    Each FindMatches transform must be taught what should be considered a match and what should not be considered a match. You teach your transform by adding labels to a file and uploading your choices to AWS Glue.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/AWS_BLOG_AWS_Glue_labelling_BJL_23032024.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create an ETL job written in AWS Glue. Utilize `FindMatches`machine learning transform for data deduplication.**
    
    The option that says: **Create an ETL job written in Python. Import the Python `recordlinkage`library and use it for data deduplication** is incorrect. Although this solution can perform data deduplication, it still requires significant operational overhead, as you would need to create custom code written in Python. The Python Record Linkage Toolkit is a library to link records in or between data sources. The toolkit provides most of the tools needed for record linkage and deduplication. It can resolve the issue, but complexity will arise in coding and when you’re going to deal with unstructured data.
    
    The option that says: **Create an ETL job written in Python. Import the Pandas library and use `DataFrame.drop_duplicates()` function for data deduplication.** is incorrect. Even though this solution can remove duplicate information, it still remains inadequate as it cannot really distinguish the names or products the same way humans differentiate them. It can only completely delete duplicate rows of data or information with the same values. For example, it will detect John James and Johnny James as different names, even though they usually belong to the same person.
    
    The option that says: **Create an ETL job written AWS Glue. Import the Python dedupe library and use it for data deduplication** is incorrect because while it can satisfy the requirement, it will still require a significant amount of custom coding. The Find Matches machine learning Transform will not require writing code or knowing how machine learning works. You only must edit a CSV file to identify which matches or does not (labeling) from a sample record. Hence, considering the operational overhead, Find Matches is the best answer.
    
- Create an ETL job written AWS Glue. Import the Python dedupe library and use it for data deduplication.
- Create an ETL job written in Python. Import the Pandas library and use `DataFrame.drop_duplicates()`function for data deduplication.

An organization collects daily user interaction and app performance logs from its mobile app. Initially, raw logs are transformed by AWS Glue for Spark Jobs and then stored in an Amazon S3 bucket.

Currently, the data in the S3 bucket is stored without any partitioning, causing the Glue jobs to scan the entire dataset during each run.

The company’s Data Engineer has noticed a gradual slowdown in the Amazon QuickSight Dashboard’s query performance over time. Upon investigation, the engineer determined that the long duration of the Glue Jobs is the root cause.

What combination of actions should the Data Engineer consider to improve query performance? (Select TWO.)

- **Arrange the data within the S3 bucket into partitions by year, month, and day.**
- Enhance the Glue Jobs query performance by changing the execution class to Flex.
- Modify the storage class of the S3 bucket to S3 Express One Zone.
- **Modify the Glue Job worker type configuration to a larger instance type.**
- Incorporate the DynamicFrame schema class within your Apache Spark-based Glue Jobs.
- **Evaluation**
    
    **AWS Glue** is a serverless data integration service that makes it easy for analytics users to discover, prepare, move, and integrate data from multiple sources. With AWS Glue, you can discover and connect to more than 70 diverse data sources and manage your data in a centralized data catalog.
    
    You define jobs in AWS Glue to accomplish the work that’s required to extract, transform, and load (ETL) data from a data source to a data target.
    
    You can improve performance just by reducing the amount of data loaded into your Spark cluster for each data source. Consider loading only the data that you need.
    
    To reduce the amount of data loaded into your job when reading from Amazon S3, consider **file size, compression, file format, and file layout (partitions)** for your dataset.
    
    – Keep the file size of inputs and outputs within a moderate range (for example, 128 MB). Files that are too small or too large can cause issues.
    
    – If your S3 objects are in the hundreds of megabytes, consider compressing them.
    
    – Use a columnar format. Apache Parquet and Apache ORC are popular columnar data formats. Parquet and ORC store data efficiently by employing column-based compression, encoding and compressing each column based on its data type.
    
    – By storing your S3 data to objects in different paths based on how the data will be used, you can efficiently retrieve relevant data. In this scenario, the data is currently stored without partitions, causing AWS Glue jobs to scan the entire dataset on each run. By partitioning your data, you can restrict the amount of data scanned by each downstream analytics application, improving performance and reducing cost.
    
    ![Work Type instance](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/AWS_BLOG_AWS_Glue_worker_type_instance_BJL_16032024.png)
    
    If your job is taking too much time, but executors are consuming sufficient resources, and Spark is creating a large volume of tasks relative to available cores, consider scaling cluster capacity. You can scale cluster capacity by using the following options:
    
    – Enable AWS Glue Auto Scaling
    
    – Scale-out: Increase the number of AWS Glue workers
    
    – Scale up: Use a larger worker type
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Arrange the data within the S3 bucket into partitions by year, month, and day.**
    
    **– Modify the Glue Job worker type configuration to a larger instance type.**
    
    The option that says: **Modify the storage class of the S3 bucket to S3 Express One Zone** is incorrect. Although using this storage class can improve latency, it will still not resolve the slow query performance. With this approach, you will deploy your Amazon S3 bucket in the same availability zone as your resources, which will improve the latency. However, it is not mentioned in the scenario if there is a latency issue. This will not resolve the slow performance issue.
    
    The option that says: **Incorporate the DynamicFrame schema class within your Apache Spark-based Glue Jobs** is incorrect. While this can potentially optimize the jobs’ performance, it is not guaranteed to solve the issue. DynamicFrames are only well-suited for working with semi-structured data formats. It can handle data that includes nested structures or varying schemas more efficiently than traditional data representations. However, the scenario did not mention the complexity of the data characteristics. If your data is already well-structured and does not contain nested structures or varying schemas, the benefits of DynamicFrames may be less pronounced. More is needed if the issue is due to resource constraints or loading a large volume of data.
    
    The option that says: **Enhance the Glue Jobs query performance by changing the execution class to Flex** is incorrect because Flex execution is simply more suitable for non-urgent or non-time-sensitive data integration workloads, such as testing and one-time data loads. The jobs are running daily, making this solution unsuitable. With Flex, AWS Glue jobs run on spare compute capacity instead of dedicated hardware. The start and run times of jobs using Flex can vary. This solution is for optimizing cost, not for enhancing performance. It could even abruptly stop data processing when AWS takes back the resources.
    

A Data Engineer is tasked with integrating a large volume of customer data into an Amazon S3 data lake. The data is currently stored in an on-premises data center. In compliance with strict company policies, it’s mandatory to detect and redact any Personally Identifiable Information (PII) from these records before they are ingested into the data lake.

Which of the following options best meets these requirements?

- Use AWS Glue’s FindMatches ML Transform to detect sensitive data.
- Use AWS Glue’s built-in classifiers for detecting sensitive data.
- Use Amazon Macie to detect sensitive data.
- **Use AWS Glue’s Sensitive Data Detection feature.**
    
    AWS Glue’s Sensitive Data Detection feature is specifically designed to detect sensitive data such as PII. It uses pattern matching and machine learning to automatically recognize personally identifiable information (PII) and other sensitive data at the column or cell level.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/detect-sensitive-date-010924.png)
    
    You can choose how you want the data to be scanned. AWS Glue provides two options for scanning data – detecting PII in each row or detecting the columns that contain PII data. When you choose to detect fields that contain PII, you can reduce costs and improve performance by sampling a portion of rows.
    
    You can also choose the PII entities to detect. If you choose Detect PII in each cell, you can choose from one of three options: All available PII patterns, Select categories, or Custom.
    
    When you choose to detect fields that contain PII, you can specify the percentage of rows to sample and the percentage of rows that contain the PII entity in order for the entire column to be identified as having the PII entity.
    
    The Detect PII transform provides the ability to detect, mask, or remove entities that you define or are pre-defined by AWS. This enables you to increase compliance and reduce liability.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use AWS Glue’s Sensitive Data Detection feature.**
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue’s FindMatches ML Transform to detect sensitive data** is incorrect because AWS Glue’s FindMatches ML Transform is primarily used for finding duplicate records, not for detecting sensitive data.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue’s built-in classifiers for detecting sensitive data** is incorrect because AWS Glue’s built-in classifiers are primarily used for inferring the schema of data, not for detecting sensitive data.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Macie to detect sensitive data** is incorrect because, while Amazon Macie is indeed a fully managed data security and data privacy service that uses machine learning and pattern matching to discover and protect sensitive data, it does not inherently have the capability to redact or remove the detected sensitive data.
    

A data engineering team is tasked with improving the performance and reliability of their Amazon Redshift database. During their analysis, they want to focus on identifying and resolving issues that could potentially lead to query failures, suboptimal query performance, or any other alerts that might indicate problematic database operations.

Which of the following Redshift system table views would be the most suitable for the given scenario?

- Get the query information from the `STL_USAGE_CONTROL` table view.
- Get the query information from the `STL_WLM_QUERY` table view.
- **Get the query information from the `STL_ALERT_EVENT_LOG` table view.**
    
    Records an alert when the query optimizer identifies conditions that might indicate performance issues. Use the STL_ALERT_EVENT_LOG view to identify opportunities to improve query performance. A query consists of multiple segments, and each segment consists of one or more steps.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/STL_ALERT_EVENT_LOG-022324.png)
    
    STL_ALERT_EVENT_LOG is visible to all users. Superusers can see all rows; regular users can see only their own data.
    
    You can use the STL_ALERT_EVENT_LOG to identify potential issues in your queries, then follow the practices in Tuning query performance to optimize your database design and rewrite your queries.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Get the query information from the `STL_ALERT_EVENT_LOG`table view.**
    
    The option that says: **Get the query information from the `STL_WLM_QUERY` table view** is incorrect because it primarily records information about query execution such as time spent on the CPU, number of rows read or written, and disk space used for spills. While it can be useful for performance tuning, it does not directly provide information about alerts or events that might indicate problematic database operations.
    
    The option that says: **Get the query information from the `STL_USAGE_CONTROL` table view** is incorrect because it only logs changes in the WLM configuration and when queries are paused or canceled due to WLM rules. It can be useful for understanding how WLM configurations affect query execution, but it does not directly log alert events or issues that could lead to query failures.
    
    The option that says: **Get the query information from the `STL_QUERY_METRICS` table view** is incorrect because it simply provides a snapshot of the current query queue. It can be useful for understanding the current load on the system, but it does not directly provide information about alerts or events that might indicate problematic database operations.
    
- Get the query information from the `STL_QUERY_METRICS` table view.

A multinational corporation has a requirement to share certain data stored in an Amazon S3 bucket with external partners. However, the data contains Personally Identifiable Information (PII), which needs to be redacted before sharing. The company wants to retrieve redacted versions of the data from S3.

Which option would satisfy the requirements?

- **Create an Amazon S3 Object Lambda access point for the S3 bucket. Then, configure the access point to invoke an S3 Object Lambda function that redacts PII.**
    
    When using Amazon S3 for shared datasets for multiple applications and users to access, it’s important to restrict privileged information, such as personally identifiable information (PII), to only authorized entities. For example, when a marketing application uses some data containing PII, it might need to first mask PII data to meet data privacy requirements. Also, when an analytics application uses a production order inventory dataset, it might need to first redact customer credit card information to prevent unintended data leakage.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/s3-object-lambda-020124.jpg)
    
    In this scenario, the corporation can use S3 Object Lambda to dynamically redact PII from objects upon retrieval from S3. By creating an S3 Object Lambda access point attached to their bucket, the corporation ensures that when data is requested, the Lambda function intercepts the retrieval process to modify the content, specifically to redact PII, before the data reaches the requester. This method means that the original data remains intact within the S3 bucket, and there is no need to maintain separate copies of the data.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create an Amazon S3 Object Lambda access point for the S3 bucket. Then, configure the access point to invoke an S3 Object Lambda function that redacts PII.**
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Athena to query the data stored in Amazon S3 and retrieve only non-PII data from the objects** is incorrect because Amazon Athena is a query service that allows you to analyze data stored in Amazon S3 using SQL. It does not have the capability to dynamically redact PII data from objects in S3 when they are retrieved. Athena can primarily query and retrieve data, but it cannot modify or redact the data itself.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Macie to discover, monitor, and protect sensitive data** is incorrect because Amazon Macie is a security service that uses machine learning to automatically discover, classify, and protect sensitive data like PII. While it can identify and monitor access to PII, it does not have the capability to dynamically redact PII from the objects when they are retrieved. Macie is primarily focused on discovery and classification.
    
    The option that says: **Create an S3 event notification to trigger an AWS Lambda function that redacts PII as new data is uploaded. Share the redacted copies with the partners** is incorrect because as this approach involves processing data as it’s uploaded, not dynamically at retrieval time. It would require storing a separate, redacted copy of the data, which doesn’t align with the requirement for dynamic redaction upon retrieval. This method only handles redaction at upload, not at the time of retrieval.
    
- Use Amazon Athena to query the data stored in Amazon S3 and retrieve only non-PII data from the objects.
- Create an S3 event notification to trigger an AWS Lambda function that redacts PII as new data is uploaded. Share the redacted copies with the partners.
- Use Amazon Macie to discover, monitor, and protect sensitive data.

A company is working on a project that references third-party datasets from various business partners. The datasets are hosted in Amazon S3 buckets. The company’s data engineering team must recommend a method for accessing and integrating these datasets into its existing data pipeline.

Which of the following methods meets the requirements for the LEAST operational overhead?

- Use Amazon Glue to crawl the datasets and store the metadata in Amazon Athena for querying.
- **Share the third-party datasets through AWS Data Exchange and access them using the `GetDataSet` API.**
    
    AWS Data Exchange is a service that helps AWS customers easily find, subscribe to, and use third-party data in the cloud.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/AWS%20Data%20Exchange%20Diagram-020924.png)
    
    When you have access to an AWS Data Exchange dataset, you can use compatible AWS or partner analytics and machine learning services to extract insights from it. This makes AWS Data Exchange a powerful tool for integrating diverse datasets into an existing data pipeline for comprehensive analysis and insights, which is exactly what the data engineering team in question needs.
    
    Moreover, AWS Data Exchange provides options for both subscribers and data providers. It eliminates the need for data providers to build and maintain any data delivery and entitlement infrastructure. Anyone with an AWS account can create and send data grants to data receivers. Therefore, utilizing API calls for the retrieval and integration of third-party datasets from AWS Data Exchange is the most direct and efficient method for the team’s needs.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Share the third-party datasets through AWS Data Exchange and access them using the `GetDataSet` API.**
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Glue to crawl the datasets and store the metadata in Amazon Athena for querying** is incorrect because while Amazon Glue can indeed crawl datasets and store metadata, it’s primarily used for preparing and loading data for analytics. In the context of the question, the team needs to access and integrate third-party datasets from AWS Data Exchange. Amazon Glue does not provide a direct method for this. It would require additional steps to export the data from AWS Data Exchange and import it into Amazon Glue, which is not the most efficient or direct method for the team’s needs.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Kinesis to stream the datasets in real-time into the data pipeline** is incorrect because Amazon Kinesis is designed for real-time streaming data. It’s an excellent service when you need to collect, process, and analyze real-time data to get timely insights and react quickly to new information. However, in this scenario, the team needs to access and integrate diverse datasets that are hosted on various platforms within the AWS ecosystem, not stream real-time data. Therefore, using Amazon Kinesis would not meet the team’s requirements.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize API calls for the retrieval and integration of third-party datasets from AWS DataSync** is incorrect because AWS DataSync is just a data transfer service that simplifies, automates, and accelerates moving data between on-premises storage systems and AWS Storage services, as well as between AWS Storage services. While it’s useful for transferring large amounts of data over the internet or AWS Direct Connect, it does not provide a direct method for accessing and integrating third-party datasets from AWS Data Exchange. The team would need to first transfer the data from AWS Data Exchange to an AWS Storage service and then use AWS DataSync to move the data, which is not the most direct or efficient method for their needs.
    
- Utilize API calls for the retrieval and integration of third-party datasets from AWS DataSync.
- Use Amazon Kinesis to stream the datasets in real-time into the data pipeline.

A company is developing a new data analysis application. The application needs to retrieve and process data from an Amazon S3 bucket. The data access patterns are complex and vary based on the analysis being performed. The company wants to use a service that allows them to use their own code to process and transform the data as it is being retrieved from Amazon S3.

Which AWS service should the company use to meet these requirements?

- AWS Lambda
- AWS Glue
- **Amazon S3 Object Lambda**
    
    **Amazon S3 Object Lambda** allows you to add your own code to Amazon S3 GET, LIST, and HEAD requests to process and modify data as it is being returned to an application. This enables the use of custom code to transform the data returned by these requests in various ways, such as filtering rows, dynamically resizing and watermarking images, or redacting confidential data. This service is powered by AWS Lambda functions, which means your code runs on fully managed AWS infrastructure. This eliminates the need to create and store derivative copies of your data or to run proxies, with no changes required to your applications.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/ObjectLamdaDiagram-020224.png)
    
    S3 Object Lambda operates by using AWS Lambda functions to automatically process the output of standard S3 `GET`, `LIST`, or `HEAD`requests. You can author and run your own custom Lambda functions, allowing you to tailor the data transformation to your specific use cases. Once you configure a Lambda function, you attach it to an S3 Object Lambda service endpoint, known as an Object Lambda Access Point. When a request is sent to your Object Lambda Access Point, Amazon S3 automatically calls your Lambda function. Any data retrieved using an S3 `GET`, `LIST`, or `HEAD` request through the Object Lambda Access Point is then returned as a transformed result back to the application.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Amazon S3 Object Lambda.**
    
    The option that says: **AWS Glue** is incorrect. While AWS Glue does provide capabilities for data transformation, it does not allow for processing data as it is being retrieved from S3. It is more focused on preparing and combining data for analytics.
    
    The option that says: **AWS Lambda** is incorrect. A Lambda function alone does not provide the functionality to process and transform data during its retrieval from S3 without integrating with other services like S3 Object Lambda.
    
    The option that says: **Amazon S3 Storage Lens** is incorrect because S3 Storage Lens is just a cloud-storage analytics feature that you can use to gain organization-wide visibility into object-storage usage and activity. It does not allow for processing data as it is being retrieved from S3. It’s more focused on providing insights about storage usage and helping you optimize costs.
    
- Amazon S3 Storage Lens

A company is preparing to deploy an Amazon EMR cluster, utilizing Apache Spark for data extraction, transformation, and loading (data ETL). The cluster must sustain the company’s performance under enduring workloads, and high reliability is of the utmost importance. The cluster must store data persistently.

Which storage and computing will satisfy the requirements MOST cost-effectively?

- Utilize the local file system as the storage and spot instances for computing.
- **Utilize Elastic Map Reduce File System (EMRFS) as the storage and Graviton instances for computing.**
    
    **Amazon EMR** (previously called Amazon Elastic MapReduce) is a managed cluster platform that simplifies running big data frameworks, such as Apache Hadoop and Apache Spark, on AWS to process and analyze vast amounts of data. Using these frameworks and related open-source projects, you can process data for analytics purposes and business intelligence workloads.
    
    Based on the EMR Workflow, planning the storage is an essential first step in configuring Amazon EMR.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/AWS_DOC_EMR_Workflow_BJL_03032024.png)
    
    You can choose from a variety of file systems to store input data, output data, and log files:
    
    **Hadoop Distributed File System (HDFS)** – HDFS is a distributed, scalable, and portable file system for Hadoop. HDFS distributes the data it stores across instances in the cluster, storing multiple copies of data on different instances to ensure that no data is lost if an individual instance fails. One advantage is that it’s fast; a disadvantage is that it’s ephemeral storage which is reclaimed when the cluster ends. It’s best used for caching the results produced by intermediate job-flow steps.
    
    **EMR File System (EMRFS)** – EMRFS is an implementation of the Hadoop file system used for reading and writing regular files from Amazon EMR directly to Amazon S3. EMRFS provides the convenience of storing persistent data in Amazon S3 for use with Hadoop.
    
    **Local file system** – The local file system refers to a locally connected disk. When you create a Hadoop cluster, each node is created from an Amazon EC2 instance that comes with a preconfigured block of pre-attached disk storage called an instance store. Data on instance store volumes persists only during the lifecycle of its Amazon EC2 instance.
    
    For computing, **AWS Graviton** processors are custom-designed by AWS to enable the best price performance for workloads in Amazon EC2. Amazon EC2 instances powered by AWS Graviton2 processors provide up to 40% better price performance over comparable fifth-generation x86-based instances for a wide variety of workloads.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Utilize Elastic Map Reduce File System (EMRFS) as the storage and Graviton instances for computing.** EMRFS is the most suitable file system for storing persistent data. At the same time, Graviton instances are the best choice in terms of cost-effectiveness while not sacrificing performance.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize Hadoop Distributed File System (HDFS) as the storage and x86 instances for computing** is incorrect. While HDFS is fast and can perform better, it is unsuitable for storing persistent data. HDFS uses temporary storage, available only during the cluster’s life cycle. x86 instances are inferior to Graviton as Amazon EC2 instances powered by AWS Graviton2 processors provide up to 40% better price performance over comparable fifth-generation x86-based instances for various workloads.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize the local file system as the storage and spot instances for computing**is incorrect because of unreliability. Data on a local file system only persists at the lifecycle of the EC2 instance and will be deleted after EC2 instance termination. The company will also run enduring workloads that make spot instances unsuitable because spot instances can be interrupted when EC2 reclaims the instance to repurpose capacity. While this option can be cheaper, there are better options than this one, considering high reliability and performance.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize Amazon Elastic File System (EFS) as the storage and spot instances for computing** is incorrect. While EFS is suitable for many use cases, it is generally more expensive than S3 for data-intensive applications like Spark on EMR. Also, the use of spot instances, although cost-effective, could pose a risk of interruption, which may affect the reliability of the system.
    
- Utilize Hadoop Distributed File System (HDFS) as the storage and x86 instances for computing.
- Utilize Amazon Elastic File System (EFS) as the storage and spot instances for computing.

A data engineering team in a large organization is looking for a cost-effective solution to handle their non-urgent data integration workloads. These workloads include tasks such as testing and one-time data loads, which do not require fast job startup times or consistent runtimes. They are considering a solution that runs on spare compute capacity instead of dedicated hardware, which can lead to variable start and run times.

Which of the following would be the most suitable for this scenario?

- AWS Glue Streaming ETL
- AWS Glue Development Endpoint
- AWS Glue Standard
- **AWS Glue Flex**
    
    **AWS Glue Flex** is designed for non-urgent data integration workloads that do not require fast job startup times or consistent runtimes. It runs on spare compute capacity instead of dedicated hardware, which can lead to variable start and runtimes but is more cost-effective.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/flex-execution-aws-glue-010924.png)
    
    With AWS Glue Flex, jobs run on spare compute capacity in AWS instead of dedicated hardware. This means that the start and runtimes of jobs using Flex can vary because spare compute resources aren’t always readily available and can be reclaimed during the run of a job. However, this approach is more cost-effective for non-urgent workloads.
    
    Regardless of the execution option used, AWS Glue jobs have the same capabilities, including access to custom connectors, visual authoring interface, job scheduling, and Glue Auto Scaling. The Flex execution class is available for AWS Glue 3.0 Spark jobs.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **AWS Glue Flex.**
    
    **AWS Glue Standard** is incorrect because it is designed for time-sensitive tasks that require fast job startup times and consistent runtimes, achieved through the allocation of dedicated resources, making it more expensive and less flexible.
    
    **AWS Glue Development Endpoint** is incorrect because it is specifically built for active development and testing of ETL scripts, not for running production workloads.
    
    **AWS Glue Streaming ETL** is incorrect because this service is primarily used for streaming ETL jobs, which require real-time processing. It’s not designed for non-urgent workloads that can tolerate variable start and runtimes.
    

A data engineer is working on a project that involves extracting data from an Amazon S3 bucket, which is accessed through an S3 VPC Gateway endpoint. The engineer’s task is to process this data with an AWS Glue job and then save the processed data in an Amazon Redshift database.

Despite setting up the IAM role with the right permissions for the Glue job, the engineer encounters a connection error with Amazon S3 when attempting to execute the job.

How can the engineer resolve the issue?

- **Ensure that the VPC’s route table has routes for the Amazon S3 VPC gateway endpoint.**
    
    In an Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (VPC), a route table contains a set of rules, called routes, that are used to determine where network traffic is directed. Each subnet in your VPC must be associated with a route table; the table controls the traffic for the subnet. A subnet can only be associated with one route table at a time, but you can associate multiple subnets with the same route table.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/route-table-030724.png)
    
    An Amazon S3 VPC Gateway Endpoint is a gateway that you specify as a target for a route in your route table for traffic destined to Amazon S3. When you create an endpoint, you can attach an endpoint policy to it that controls access to the service to which you’re connecting. Therefore, ensuring that the VPC’s route table has routes for the Amazon S3 VPC gateway endpoint is crucial.
    
    In practice, you’d have to assign the CIDR ranges for Amazon S3 as a destination in the route table of your VPC. In the AWS Management Console, these are represented by a prefix list, so you won’t have to enumerate them manually. Select the gateway endpoint as the target. This way, traffic destined for S3 will be routed directly through the gateway endpoint, enhancing both security and performance by keeping the traffic within the Amazon network and not exposing it to the public internet.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Ensure that the VPC’s route table has routes for the Amazon S3 VPC gateway endpoint.**
    
    The option that says: **Modify the AWS Glue security group to permit inbound traffic from the Amazon S3 VPC gateway endpoint** is incorrect. AWS Glue does not directly communicate with S3 via security groups. AWS Glue and Amazon S3 communicate over the AWS network, and access is managed through IAM roles and VPC endpoint configurations, not security group settings.
    
    The option that says: **Set up an S3 bucket policy to specifically allow the AWS Glue job to access the S3 bucket** is incorrect. The scenario already mentions that the IAM role has the right permissions for the Glue job. This implies that the necessary permissions for accessing S3 from Glue have been granted. The issue at hand is related to the network connectivity (via VPC endpoint) rather than the absence of access permissions.
    
    The option that says: **Configure the AWS Glue job to use a different AWS region** is incorrect. Changing the AWS region where the AWS Glue job runs would not inherently solve a connectivity issue caused by VPC routing or endpoint configuration mistakes. This action could introduce unnecessary complexity, including data transfer costs and latency
    
- Configure the AWS Glue job to use a different AWS region.
- Modify the AWS Glue security group to permit inbound traffic from the Amazon S3 VPC gateway endpoint.
- Set up an S3 bucket policy to specifically allow the AWS Glue job to access the S3 bucket.

A retail company uses an Amazon Redshift cluster to store daily sales data. Several tables within this cluster are updated daily with new sales records. The company wants to automate the monitoring process, which verifies whether the tables are successfully updated daily or if any updates are still pending. Additionally, it should be accurately reflected in an Amazon DynamoDB table.

Which of the following options would be the most appropriate way to load the status of Redshift tables automatically? (SELECT TWO)

- Utilize an EC2 instance to run a cron job that checks the Redshift table statuses and updates the DynamoDB table accordingly.
- **Create a Lambda function that leverages Amazon Redshift API to query the status of each table’s data load in the Redshift cluster. Load the status recording in the Amazon DynamoDB table.**
- Set up a direct connection between Redshift and DynamoDB using a VPC peering connection to sync table statuses automatically.
- **Configure an Amazon EventBridge rule to trigger the Lambda function at a specific time each day.**
- Enable Redshift's built-in integration with DynamoDB to automatically sync the table statuses without the need for additional AWS services or scripting.
- **Evaluation**
    
    AWS Lambda is a serverless computing service that runs code in response to events and automatically manages the computing resources required by that code. It is ideal for executing small pieces of code (functions) in response to events. Amazon Redshift is a fully managed, petabyte-scale data warehouse service in the cloud. It allows users to run fast, complex queries against vast amounts of data. Amazon DynamoDB is a key-value and document database that delivers single-digit millisecond performance at any scale, making it a great choice for keeping real-time status updates. Lastly, Amazon EventBridge is a serverless event bus that makes it easy to connect applications together using data from your apps, integrated Software-as-a-Service (SaaS) applications, and AWS services.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/DEA3-34.png)
    
    AWS Lambda can interact with Amazon Redshift to check the status of the tables and then update the Amazon DynamoDB table accordingly. This eliminates the need for managing servers or runtime environments, focusing solely on the code that monitors and updates the table statuses. Amazon EventBridge can be used to trigger this Lambda function at regular intervals, ensuring the process is automated and consistent.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Create a Lambda function that leverages Amazon Redshift API to query the status of each table’s data load in the Redshift cluster. Load the status recording in the Amazon DynamoDB table.**
    
    **– Configure an Amazon EventBridge to trigger the Lambda function at a specific time each day.**
    
    The option that says: **Enable Redshift’s built-in integration with DynamoDB to automatically sync the table statuses without the need for additional AWS services or scripting** is incorrect because there is no such built-in feature or direct integration that allows Amazon Redshift to automatically sync its table statuses with DynamoDB.
    
    The option that says: **Set up a direct connection between Redshift and DynamoDB using a VPC peering connection to sync table statuses automatically** is incorrect. While VPC peering can facilitate communication between two services within AWS, it does not inherently provide a method for data synchronization or the automatic updating of table statuses. The syncing of data would still require a custom implementation, such as a script or an application, to handle the logic and data transfer between Redshift and DynamoDB. Thus, simply setting up a VPC peering connection does not address the automation or the logic required to sync the table statuses.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize an EC2 instance to run a cron job that checks the Redshift table statuses and updates the DynamoDB table accordingly** is incorrect. While you can use EC2 instance to run a cron job, it introduces additional complexity and management overhead. An Amazon EC2 instance would need to be provisioned, configured, and maintained. The cron job would require a script to interact with both Redshift and DynamoDB, necessitating ongoing maintenance and monitoring to ensure its continuous operation.
    

A multinational corporation has customer data from various countries stored in an Amazon S3 bucket. The data analysts in the team are assigned to specific countries and should only have access to the customer data from their respective countries. The corporation wants to enforce strict access policies to ensure data privacy and compliance.

Which of these options would most efficiently and securely manage and control access to customer data while ensuring compliance with the corporation’s policies?

- **Incorporate the S3 bucket as a data lake location within AWS Lake Formation. Utilize the granular security features of Lake Formation, such as row-level security, to enforce the corporation’s access policies effectively.**
    
    **AWS Lake Formation** is a fully managed service that helps you build, secure, and manage data lakes, and provide access control for data in the data lake. AWS Lake Formation row-level permissions allow you to provide access to specific rows in a table based on data compliance and governance policies. If you have large tables storing billions of records, you need a way to enable different users and teams to access only the data they are allowed to see. Row-level access control is a simple and performant way to protect data while giving users access to the data they need to perform their job. Lake Formation provides centralized auditing and compliance reporting by identifying which principals accessed what data, when, and through which services.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/data-lakes-row-level-02-07-24.png)
    
    If you have large fact tables storing billions of records, you need a way to enable different users and teams to access only the data they’re allowed to see. Row-level access control is a simple and performant way to protect data while giving users access to the data they need to perform their jobs.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Incorporate the S3 bucket as a data lake location within AWS Lake Formation. Utilize the granular security features of Lake Formation, such as row-level security, to enforce the corporation’s access policies effectively.**
    
    The option that says: **Establish individual tables for each country’s customer data within the Amazon S3 bucket. Grant access to each data analyst specifically based on the country they are responsible for** is incorrect because it only provides a rudimentary level of access control. While creating separate tables for each country’s data might seem like a good idea, it doesn’t provide the necessary granularity for access control. AWS recommends using IAM policies for access control. Sharing a table with multiple users can lead to data leaks if not managed properly.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue to catalog the data in the S3 bucket and set up separate databases for each country. Provide access to each analyst based on the country that the analyst serves** is incorrect because it primarily focuses on the physical location of the data, which does not directly relate to access control or compliance with the corporation’s policies. While storing data in AWS Regions close to the customers can improve data access speed and reduce latency, it doesn’t help with access control.
    
    The option that says: **Import the customer data into Amazon Redshift. Construct a distinct view for each country’s data. Generate separate IAM roles for each country to provide precise access to the respective country’s data. Assign the corresponding roles to the analysts based on their country of service** is incorrect because it simply moves the data to a different service (Amazon Redshift) without providing a comprehensive solution for enforcing access policies at a granular level. While Amazon Redshift allows for the creation of views and the assignment of IAM roles, it doesn’t provide the same level of fine-grained access control as AWS Lake Formation.
    
- Use AWS Glue to catalog the data in the S3 bucket and set up separate databases for each country. Provide access to each analyst based on the country that the analyst serves.
- Import the customer data into Amazon Redshift. Construct a distinct view for each country’s data. Generate separate IAM roles for each country to provide precise access to the respective country’s data. Assign the corresponding roles to the analysts based on their country of service.
- Establish individual tables for each country’s customer data within the Amazon S3 bucket. Grant access to each data analyst specifically based on the country they are responsible for.

A corporation with a data lake managed by AWS Lake Formation seeks to automate its manual, time-consuming data quality checks. They need a solution that integrates with AWS, reduces the effort from days to hours, and allows them to define rulesets for table evaluation.

What solution would be recommended for this scenario?

- Utilize Amazon Athena for querying and assessing the data quality in your AWS data lake.
- **Create AWS Glue data quality rules and define rulesets to evaluate your table.**
    
    **AWS Glue Data Quality** is a feature of AWS Glue that provides a managed, serverless experience to help you evaluate and monitor the quality of your data. It is built on top of the open-source DeeQu framework.
    
    AWS Glue Data Quality works with Data Quality Definition Language (DQDL), which is a domain-specific language that you use to define data quality rules. It offers non-coders an easy way to set up data quality rules. These personas include data stewards and business analysts.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/aws-glue-data-quality-010924.png)
    
    It uses machine learning to detect anomalies and hard-to-detect data quality issues, and it provides a Data Quality score that gives an overview of the health of your data. This allows corporations with large AWS data lakes to automate their manual, time-consuming data quality checks
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create AWS Glue data quality rules and define rulesets to evaluate your table.**
    
    The option that says: **Implement AWS Lake Formation security policies to automate data access management in your AWS data lake** is incorrect because, while AWS Lake Formation does provide security policies, it doesn’t directly automate data quality checks. It’s more about managing access to the data. The actual data quality checks would still need to be implemented separately.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize Amazon Athena for querying and assessing the data quality in your AWS data lake** is incorrect because, while AWS Athena can be used for assessing data quality, it doesn’t inherently automate the process or allow for the definition of rulesets for table evaluation.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon QuickSight to visualize and analyze the data in your AWS data lake** is incorrect because while Amazon QuickSight is a business intelligence service that can be used to visualize and analyze data, it doesn’t directly provide functionality for data quality checks. It’s more about data visualization and analysis.
    
- Use Amazon QuickSight to visualize and analyze the data in your AWS data lake.
- Implement AWS Lake Formation security policies to automate data access management in your AWS data lake.

A Data Engineer must create an orchestration workflow for transforming large volumes of data files using AWS Step Functions. The objective is to optimize performance by running a set of transformation processes to all data files in parallel.

How can the engineer meet these requirements?

- **Use the `Parallel` state to execute the transformation processes simultaneously on all data files.**
    
    **AWS Step Functions** is a serverless orchestration service that lets you integrate with AWS Lambda functions and other AWS services to build business-critical applications. Through Step Functions’ graphical console, you see your application’s workflow as a series of event-driven steps.
    
    Step Functions is a serverless orchestration service that lets you define an application workflow as a series of event-driven steps. Each step in the workflow is called a state. You most commonly use states, such as Task, Choice, Parallel, and Map, to define your workflows.
    
    These are the types of State:
    
    – A `Task` state `("Type": "Task")`represents a single unit of work performed by a state machine. A task performs work by using an activity or an AWS Lambda function, by integrating with other supported AWS services, or by invoking a third-party API, such as Stripe.
    
    – A `Choice` state `("Type": "Choice")` adds conditional logic to a state machine.
    
    – A `Wait` state `("Type": "Wait")`delays the state machine from continuing for a specified time. You can choose either a relative time, specified in seconds from when the state begins, or an absolute end time, specified as a timestamp.
    
    – A `Succeed` state `("Type": "Succeed")` stops an execution successfully. The `Succeed` state is a useful target for `Choice` state branches that don’t do anything but stop the execution.
    
    – A `Fail` state (“Type”: “Fail”) stops the execution of the state machine and marks it as a failure, unless it is caught by a `Catch`block.
    
    – Use the `Map` state to run a set of workflow steps for each item in a dataset. The `Map` state’s iterations run in parallel, which makes it possible to process a dataset quickly.
    
    – The `Parallel` state `("Type": "Parallel")` can be used to add separate branches of execution in your state machine.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/AWS_Step_functions_Parallel_state_BJL_17032024.png)
    
    *Example Use Case of Parallel State. The parallel state can run multiple executions simultaneously to optimize performance.*
    
    In the scenario, you can use the `Parallel`state to execute multiple transformation processes simultaneously rather than sequentially. For example, let’s say you have a dataset comprising numerous JSON files that need to be transformed through a series of steps, such as normalization and deduplication before they are loaded into a data warehouse.
    
    By using the `Parallel` state, you can design your Step Functions workflow to split the dataset into individual files or batches of files and process these in parallel. Each branch of the parallel state can perform the necessary transformations on its assigned file or batch. This could involve invoking AWS Lambda functions or Glue jobs that execute the specific transformation logic.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use `Parallel`state to execute the transformation processes simultaneously on all data files.**
    
    The option that says: **Use the `Choice` state to make decisions within the workflow based on specified conditions** is incorrect because the `Choice` state is primarily used for adding conditional logic (if-else) on a state machine. Based on the scenario, the engineer needs a state to run simultaneous transformations, not to make decisions based on conditions.
    
    The option that says: **Use the `Map` state to iterate over each data file and apply the transformation individually** is incorrect. Using the `Map` state is a straightforward and efficient solution if each file requires the same transformation. However, if there are variations in the transformation process, the `Parallel`state offers more flexibility and control over workflow execution.
    
    The option that says: **Use the `Wait` state to pause the workflow for a specified duration before proceeding with the next steps** is incorrect because it will not help optimize performance. The Wait state is often only helpful in scenarios where you have to wait for an asynchronous process to be completed before advancing to subsequent steps.
    
- Use the `Choice` state to make decisions within the workflow based on specified conditions.
- Use the `Map` state to iterate over each data file and apply the transformation individually.
- Use the `Wait` state to pause the workflow for a specified duration before proceeding with the next steps.

A data engineering team at a media company is developing a new feature for their application that allows users to upload videos. Once a video is uploaded to their Amazon S3 bucket, a process must be triggered immediately to transcode it for different devices. The team has decided to use the `s3:ObjectCreated` action as the event trigger.

Which of the following options is the most efficient way to set up this architecture?

- Use AWS Lambda to continuously poll the S3 bucket for new files and trigger the transcoding service when a new file is found.
- **Set up S3 Event Notifications to send a message to an AWS Lambda function whenever a new object is created in the bucket, and then have the Lambda function trigger the transcoding service.**
    
    Amazon S3 provides a useful feature called ‘Event Notifications’, which enables you to receive notifications when specific events occur in your S3 bucket. To use this feature, you need to create a notification configuration that specifies the events you want to track and the destinations where you want to receive the notifications.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/s3-event-notification-2-020124.png)
    
    AWS Lambda can be used to process event notifications from Amazon Simple Storage Service. By configuring notification settings on a bucket, Amazon S3 can send an event to a Lambda function when an object is created or deleted. To enable this functionality, you must grant Amazon S3 permission to invoke a function using the function’s resource-based permissions policy.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/s3-event-notification-020124.png)
    
    Using S3 Event notifications to trigger a Lambda function eliminates the need to continuously poll the S3 bucket for new files or involve additional services. The `s3:ObjectCreated` event ensures that the Lambda function is triggered immediately when a new file is uploaded. The Lambda function then invokes or initiates the transcoding service, leading to prompt video processing.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Set up S3 Event Notifications to send a message to an AWS Lambda function whenever a new object is created in the bucket, and then have the Lambda function trigger the transcoding service.**
    
    The option that says:**Configure Amazon S3 to publish an event with the video’s S3 path to an Amazon SQS queue when a new file is uploaded. Then, have the transcoding service poll the queue** is incorrect. While possible, this requires implementing additional polling logic within the transcoding service to continuously check the queue for new messages. With AWS Lambda, you can directly handle the S3 event without the need for an intermediary queue.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Lambda to continuously poll the S3 bucket for new files and trigger the transcoding service when a new file is found** is incorrect because it’s inefficient. Continuously polling the S3 bucket would consume unnecessary resources and might not trigger the transcoding service as quickly as the `s3:ObjectCreated` event.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Kinesis Data Streams to stream the data from the S3 bucket to AWS Lambda, and then have Lambda trigger the transcoding service when a new file is detected** is incorrect because it’s overkill for this scenario. While Kinesis Data Streams can be used with S3 and Lambda for real-time data processing, it’s not necessary for this use case. The `s3:ObjectCreated` event can directly trigger a Lambda function, which is a straightforward and more efficient solution.
    
- Use Amazon Kinesis Data Streams to stream the data from the S3 bucket to AWS Lambda, and then have Lambda trigger the transcoding service when a new file is detected.
- Configure Amazon S3 to publish an event with the video's S3 path to an Amazon SQS queue when a new file is uploaded. Then, have the transcoding service poll the queue.

A tech startup is currently utilizing Amazon Athena for their data analytics operations, using SQL queries for ETL tasks via the Create Table As Select (CTAS) feature. They have recently decided to transition to Apache Spark due to its capabilities in handling large datasets across distributed clusters.

Which feature of Amazon Athena would allow the startup to use Apache Spark for their data analytics operations?

- Athena query settings
- Athena Data Catalog
- Athena data source
- **Athena workgroup**
    
    An Athena workgroup is a feature of Amazon Athena that allows for the grouping of queries. By default, each account has a primary workgroup, and the default permissions allow all authenticated users access to this workgroup. Each workgroup that you create shows saved queries and query history only for queries that ran in it. This separates your queries from other queries within an account and makes it more efficient for you to locate your own saved queries and queries in history.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/athena-workgroup-041924.png)
    
    When a workgroup is enabled with Apache Spark, it allows the use of Apache Spark with Amazon Athena for data analytics operations. This is particularly useful for organizations that need to process large datasets across distributed clusters, as it allows them to leverage the advanced data processing capabilities of Apache Spark within the Amazon Athena environment. You can set up workgroup-wide settings and enforce their usage by all queries that run in a workgroup. The settings include query results location in Amazon S3, expected bucket owner, encryption, and control of objects written to the query results bucket. When you enforce workgroup-wide settings, all queries run in this workgroup use workgroup settings. This happens even if their client-side settings may differ from workgroup settings.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Athena workgroup.**
    
    **Athena Data Catalog** is incorrect because Athena Data Catalog is just a central location where you can store and retrieve table metadata across all your data stored in Amazon S3. It does not provide the capability to use Apache Spark with Amazon Athena.
    
    **Athena data source** is incorrect because Athena data sources refer to the databases and tables that Athena uses to run queries. While they are an important part of using Athena, they do not enable the use of Apache Spark with Amazon Athena.
    
    **Athena query settings** is incorrect because Athena query settings allow you to manage query-related configurations in Amazon Athena. They do not enable the use of Apache Spark with Amazon Athena.
    

A data engineering team uses Lambda functions to execute various data processing tasks. Recently, the team has developed a custom Python library that abstracts common data transformations tailored to their workload.

The team wants to implement this library into their existing Lambda functions to enhance their data processing capabilities.

Which solution would meet the requirements in the LEAST amount of effort?

- **Use AWS Lambda Layers to package the custom library and reference this layer in the Lambda functions.**
    
    When building serverless applications, it is quite common to have code shared across Lambda functions. This code can be your custom code used by more than one function or a standard library you add to simplify the implementation of your business logic.
    
    Instead of having to package and deploy this shared code together with all the functions using it, you can put common components in a ZIP file and upload it as a Lambda Layer. Your function code doesn’t need to be changed and can reference the libraries in the layer as it would normally do.
    
    Layers can be versioned to manage updates, each version is immutable. When a version is deleted or permissions to use it are revoked, functions that used it previously will continue to work, but you won’t be able to create new ones.
    
    In the configuration of a function, you can reference up to five layers, one of which can optionally be a runtime. When the function is invoked, layers are installed in `/opt` in the order you provided. Order is important because layers are all extracted under the same path, so each layer can potentially overwrite the previous one. This approach can be used to customize the environment. For example, the first layer can be a runtime, and the second layer can add specific versions of the libraries you need.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/lambda-layers-diagram-020924.png)
    
    Lambda Layers allow you to package common code (such as custom Python scripts) separately from your Lambda functions. You can share the same layer across multiple functions, reducing redundancy.  Updates to the Python scripts can be done in one place (the layer) and automatically propagate to all functions using it. Custom logic is centralized in the layer, promoting consistency.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use AWS Lambda Layers to package the custom library and reference this layer in the Lambda functions.**
    
    The option that says: **Package the Lambda functions together with the custom library and upload them to an Amazon S3 bucket. Update each Lambda function’s code using the S3 bucket as the source location** is incorrect. While technically possible, this approach requires repackaging a Lambda function with the custom library upon any update to the library. This process is time-consuming and prone to errors, especially as the number of Lambda functions grows.
    
    The option that says: **Store the custom library in an Amazon EFS volume and configure the Lambda functions to mount the EFS volume to access the custom Python library** is incorrect. This method involves configuring network file system access permissions and can lead to increased latency, as Lambda functions must access the library over the network. It’s an over-engineered solution for the task of library sharing, which can be more efficiently accomplished with Lambda layers.
    
    The option that says: **Assign a common alias to all Lambda functions. Invoke each Lambda function by specifying the alias associated with it** is incorrect. Assigning aliases to Lambda functions is useful for versioning and routing traffic, but it doesn’t directly address code reusability or centralization of custom scripts. Aliases are primarily used to manage different versions of a function.
    
- Package the Lambda functions together with the custom library and upload them to an Amazon S3 bucket. Update each Lambda function’s code using the S3 bucket as the source location.
- Store the custom library in an Amazon EFS volume and configure the Lambda functions to mount the EFS volume to access the custom Python library.
- Assign a common alias to all Lambda functions. Invoke each Lambda function by specifying the alias associated with it.
